abstract_pt: "Ao longo do século XXI, a tecnologia tem evoluído a uma velocidade exponencial,\
  \ sendo integrada na rotina diária e assumindo um papel crítico no nosso quotidiano.\
  \ A resultante proliferação de dispositivos digitais levantou novos desafios relacionados\
  \ com a integração de equipamentos tecnológicos heterogéneos em ambientes físicos\
  \ complexos e de larga escala. Os Sistemas Ciberfísicos abordam estas situações,\
  \ propondo interligar os mundos cibernético e físico. Embora estes sistemas sejam\
  \ predominantemente conhecidos em processos industriais, recentemente têm recebido\
  \ interesse no setor da energia, em particular na geração, transmissão, fornecimento\
  \ e consumo de energia elétrica.\r\n\r\nAo longo das últimas décadas, um crescimento\
  \ sustentável das necessidades elétricas tem sido observado a nível mundial, para\
  \ o qual a atual infraestrutura da rede elétrica se tem revelado incapaz de assegurar\
  \ um adequado e estável fornecimento de energia. Mais ainda, a atual taxa de exploração\
  \ de combustíveis fósseis e outras fontes poluentes de energia, até agora responsáveis\
  \ pela maior parte da geração mundial, também aumentou consideravelmente. Para além\
  \ de ameaçar a extinção destas fontes, a sua exploração é também responsável por\
  \ impactos em fenómenos de alterações climáticas, capacidade de transmissão e distribuição,\
  \ bem como uma gestão adaptativa e em tempo-real da infraestrutura da rede elétrica.\r\
  \n\r\nPor forma a lidar com a emergente crise energética, a rede elétrica tradicional\
  \ tem sido transformada numa infraestrutura inteligente e totalmente automatizada,\
  \ denominada rede elétrica inteligente. De entre as suas características, a capacidade\
  \ de supervisão em tempo-real, monitorização, coordenação adaptativa, e resiliência,\
  \ são destacadas. Ao abrigo da atual revolução digital, e consequente crescimento\
  \ exponencial do consumo e proliferação de geração renovável, a sua coordenação\
  \ é crítica.\r\n\r\nA presente dissertação enquadra-se nestes esforços de coordenação.\
  \ Melhorias na gestão energética de residências inteligentes, através do escalonamento\
  \ ótimo de equipamentos elétricos, são propostas. Estes incluem electrodomésticos\
  \ frequentemente operados pelos residentes, como máquinas de lavar roupa e loiça,\
  \ e outros equipamentos, como baterias ou sistemas de bombas elétricas. Períodos\
  \ de menor consumo e preços da rede mais reduzidos, bem como de maior geração elétrica\
  \ local, são explorados para reduzir a fatura elétrica e do desconforto dos residentes.\
  \ Uma casa inteligente protótipo é considerada, suportada por dados históricos reais\
  \ de consumo e previsões de geração local. Aplicada a este caso de estudo, foi desenvolvida\
  \ uma estratégia adaptativa baseada em otimização matemática para o escalonamento,\
  \ no horizonte de uma semana, de máquinas de lavar roupa, secar roupa e lavar loiça,\
  \ uma bomba de água elétrica e um sistema de bateria. Nesta estratégia, o escalonamento\
  \ é realizado em todos os dias da semana, considerando horizontes progressivamente\
  \ mais reduzidos. Para cada novo dia do horizonte, o escalonamento é formulado como\
  \ uma instância de um problema de escalonamento não preemptivo de máquinas, recorrendo\
  \ a estratégias de programação linear para obter soluções de escalonamento. De forma\
  \ a considerar a imprevisibilidade do consumo elétrico e da geração em ambientes\
  \ residenciais, é utilizada uma ferramenta de modelação probabilística baseada em\
  \ processos Gaussianos para modelação destas fontes de incerteza, para cada problema\
  \ de escalonamento considerado.\r\n\r\nEsta ferramenta foi validada em problemas\
  \ de escalonamento de equipamentos elétricos em habitações, tendo sido aplicada\
  \ no planeamento, ao longo de uma semana, de equipamentos elétricos controláveis,\
  \ em 30 semanas dispersas ao longo de um ano. Nas simulações realizadas, a competitividade\
  \ desta estratégia, com respeito a critérios de custo de energia e desconforto dos\
  \ residentes, é demonstrada, em comparação com abordagens do estado da arte, em\
  \ particular com horizonte recidivo e escalonamento para o dia seguinte. Uma modificação\
  \ adicional à estratégia desenvolvida é também proposta para abordar limitações\
  \ relacionadas com a acomodação de violações de restrições de operação dos equipamentos\
  \ e das cargas adicionais, fora da sua utilização-padrão. Enquanto que as limitações\
  \ do primeiro tipo resultam de consumo ou geração mais extremo, a realização de\
  \ cargas adicionais é motivada pela ocorrência de eventos não expectáveis, como\
  \ eventos sociais. Em simulações adicionais, todas as violações de restrições foram\
  \ detetadas e acomodadas com êxito. Mais ainda, todas as cargas adicionais foram\
  \ planeadas com sucesso, sem comprometer a viabilidade das soluções anteriormente\
  \ determinadas.\r\n\r\nA presente dissertação contribui com estratégias para o escalonamento\
  \ de equipamentos em habitações, validadas com sucesso num caso de estudo protótipo\
  \ de uma residência, recorrendo a dados reais, as quais poderão apresentar um impacto\
  \ tangível na resolução de problemas de escalonamento com incerteza em Sistemas\
  \ Cíberfísicos."
abstract_en: "Throughout the 21st century, technology has advanced at exponential\
  \ speed, spreading into our daily routines and becoming critical in almost any everyday\
  \ life domain. The resulting proliferation of digital devices is raising challenges,\
  \ concerning the integration of a variety of heterogeneous technologies in complex\
  \ and large-scale physical environments. Cyber-Physical Systems target such situations,\
  \ proposing to bridge the cyber and physical worlds. Even though these systems are\
  \ predominantly known in industrial processes, they have recently gained interest\
  \ in the energy sector, in particular in the generation, transmission, supply and\
  \ consumption of electrical energy.\r\n\r\nOver the last few decades, a sustainable\
  \ worldwide growth of electrical energy needs has been observed, to which the existing\
  \ power grid infrastructure was proven incapable of ensuring adequate and stable\
  \ supply to all customers. In addition, the exploitation rate of fossil fuels and\
  \ other pollutant energy sources, so far responsible for most of the worldwide power\
  \ generation, also skyrocketed, threatening the depletion of these sources and having\
  \ a significant impact on anthropogenic climate change, power grid transmission\
  \ and distribution capacity, along with adaptive and real-time management of the\
  \ grid infrastructure.\r\n\r\nTransformations to the traditional power grids have\
  \ been proposed to address this energy crisis, turning them into intelligent and\
  \ fully automated “smart” grids. Among the main characteristics of these new-generation\
  \ power grids, their real-time supervision, monitoring, adaptive asset coordination,\
  \ and resilience, are highlighted. In light of the on-going digital revolution,\
  \ the exponentially increasing demand, renewable generation, and the coordination\
  \ of these two sources needs to be properly addressed.\r\n\r\nThis thesis is framed\
  \ within the above mentioned coordination efforts. Improvements on the rationalisation\
  \ of electrical energy in residential smart homes are sought by means of optimal\
  \ scheduling of controllable appliances. These include electrical devices commonly\
  \ operated by household residents, such as clothes washing or dishwasher machines,\
  \ and other equipments, such as batteries or electrical pump systems. The periods\
  \ of lower demand and cheaper utility tariffs, as well as of higher local microgeneration,\
  \ are exploited to minimise, under explicit constraints, the overall electricity\
  \ bill and the resident discomfort. A prototype smart home case study, supported\
  \ by real-world historical power demand records and local microgeneration forecasts,\
  \ is considered. An adaptive mathematical optimisation-based framework is developed\
  \ for week-ahead scheduling of a clothes washing machine, tumble dryer, dishwasher,\
  \ electrical pump and a battery system. In this framework, daily rescheduling is\
  \ carried out under progressively smaller scheduling horizons. For each new day,\
  \ appliance allocation is formulated as an instance of a non-preemptive machine\
  \ scheduling problem, with common linear programming solvers used to obtain optimal\
  \ scheduling solutions. In order to account for the unpredictability of household\
  \ demand and generation over the scheduling horizon, a Gaussian process-based probabilistic\
  \ formulation is adopted to model these sources of uncertainty, within the scope\
  \ of each scheduling problem.\r\n\r\nThe proposed methodology is validated in the\
  \ context of household appliance scheduling problems. It is applied for week-ahead\
  \ allocation of controllable appliances, considering 30 separate weeks covering\
  \ a one-year period. Simulation results show competitive electricity bill and resident\
  \ discomfort performance, in comparison to some of the state of the art approaches,\
  \ namely standard receding horizon and day-ahead scheduling strategies. A modification\
  \ to the developed framework is also proposed to address identified limitations\
  \ concerning the accommodation of violations of operational constraints, and extra\
  \ operation of controllable loads, outside of typical usage. While the former occur\
  \ as a result of more extreme demand or generation realisations, the latter are\
  \ motivated by unexpected events, such as social gathering events. In additional\
  \ simulations, all constraint violations are successfully detected and avoided.\
  \ Moreover, successful allocation of extra controllable loads, without compromising\
  \ operation feasibility, is also observed.\r\n\r\nThis dissertation contributes\
  \ with household appliance scheduling strategies, successfully validated on a prototype\
  \ smart home environment and real-world demand data, which is expected to have a\
  \ positive impact in solving scheduling problems with uncertainty in Cyber-Physical\
  \ Systems."

UNIVERSITY OF COIMBRA
Abstract
Faculty of Sciences and Technology
Department of Informatics Engineering
PhD in Informatics Engineering
Adaptive Supervisory Framework for Cyber-Physical Systems
Optimal Scheduling of Smart Home Components
by Joaquim Pedro Bento Gonçalves PratasLeitão
Throughoutthe 21st century, technologyhasadvancedatexponentialspeed, spread-
ing into our daily routines and becoming critical in almost any everyday life domain.
The resulting proliferation of digital devices is raising challenges, concerning the inte-
gration of a variety of heterogeneous technologies in complex and large-scale physical
environments. Cyber-Physical Systems target such situations, proposing to bridge the
cyber and physical worlds. Even though these systems are predominantly known in in-
dustrial processes, they have recently gained interest in the energy sector, in particular
in the generation, transmission, supply and consumption of electrical energy.
Over the last few decades, a sustainable worldwide growth of electrical energy
needs has been observed, to which the existing power grid infrastructure was proven
incapable of ensuring adequate and stable supply to all customers. In addition, the
exploitationrateoffossilfuelsandotherpollutantenergysources, sofarresponsiblefor
most of the worldwide power generation, also skyrocketed, threatening the depletion
of these sources and having a signiﬁcant impact on anthropogenic climate change,
power grid transmission and distribution capacity, along with adaptive and real-time
management of the grid infrastructure.
Transformations to the traditional power grids have been proposed to address this
energy crisis, turning them into intelligent and fully automated “smart” grids. Among
the main characteristics of these new-generation power grids, their real-time supervi-
sion, monitoring, adaptive asset coordination, and resilience, are highlighted. In light
of the on-going digital revolution, the exponentially increasing demand, renewable
generation, and the coordination of these two sources needs to be properly addressed.
This thesis is framed within the above mentioned coordination eﬀorts. Improve-
ments on the rationalisation of electrical energy in residential smart homes are sought
by means of optimal scheduling of controllable appliances. These include electrical
devices commonly operated by household residents, such as clothes washing or dish-
washer machines, and other equipments, such as batteries or electrical pump systems.
Theperiodsoflowerdemandandcheaperutilitytariﬀs, aswellasofhigherlocalmicro-
generation, are exploited to minimise, under explicit constraints, the overall electricity
bill and the resident discomfort. A prototype smart home case study, supported by
real-world historical power demand records and local microgeneration forecasts, is
considered. An adaptive mathematical optimisation-based framework is developed
for week-ahead scheduling of a clothes washing machine, tumble dryer, dishwasher,
electrical pump and a battery system. In this framework, daily rescheduling is carried
out under progressively smaller scheduling horizons. For each new day, appliance allo-

x
cation is formulated as an instance of a non-preemptive machine scheduling problem,
with common linear programming solvers used to obtain optimal scheduling solutions.
In order to account for the unpredictability of household demand and generation over
the scheduling horizon, a Gaussian process-based probabilistic formulation is adopted
to model these sources of uncertainty, within the scope of each scheduling problem.
The proposed methodology is validated in the context of household appliance
scheduling problems. It is applied for week-ahead allocation of controllable appli-
ances, considering 30 separate weeks covering a one-year period. Simulation results
show competitive electricity bill and resident discomfort performance, in comparison
to some of the state of the art approaches, namely standard receding horizon and
day-ahead scheduling strategies. A modiﬁcation to the developed framework is also
proposed to address identiﬁed limitations concerning the accommodation of violations
of operational constraints, and extra operation of controllable loads, outside of typical
usage. While the former occur as a result of more extreme demand or generation
realisations, the latter are motivated by unexpected events, such as social gathering
events. In additional simulations, all constraint violations are successfully detected
and avoided. Moreover, successful allocation of extra controllable loads, without com-
promising operation feasibility, is also observed.
This dissertation contributes with household appliance scheduling strategies, suc-
cessfully validated on a prototype smart home environment and real-world demand
data, which is expected to have a positive impact in solving scheduling problems with
uncertainty in Cyber-Physical Systems.
Keywords: Cyber-Physical Systems, Energy Management, Uncertainty-Aware
Scheduling, Smart Homes, Supervision.

Abordagem de Supervisão Adaptativa para Sistemas Ciber-Físicos
Escalonamento Ótimo de Componentes de Casas-Inteligentes
por Joaquim Pedro Bento Gonçalves PratasLeitão
Ao longo do século XXI, a tecnologia tem evoluído a uma velocidade exponencial,
sendo integrada na rotina diária e assumindo um papel crítico no nosso quotidiano.
A resultante proliferação de dispositivos digitais levantou novos desaﬁos relacionados
comaintegraçãodeequipamentostecnológicosheterogéneosemambientesfísicoscom-
plexos e de larga escala. Os Sistemas Ciberfísicos abordam estas situações, propondo
interligar os mundos cibernético e físico. Embora estes sistemas sejam predominan-
temente conhecidos em processos industriais, recentemente têm recebido interesse no
setor da energia, em particular na geração, transmissão, fornecimento e consumo de
energia elétrica.
Ao longo das últimas décadas, um crescimento sustentável das necessidades elétri-
cas tem sido observado a nível mundial, para o qual a atual infraestrutura da rede
elétrica se tem revelado incapaz de assegurar um adequado e estável fornecimento
de energia. Mais ainda, a atual taxa de exploração de combustíveis fósseis e out-
ras fontes poluentes de energia, até agora responsáveis pela maior parte da geração
mundial, também aumentou consideravelmente. Para além de ameaçar a extinção
destas fontes, a sua exploração é também responsável por impactos em fenómenos de
alterações climáticas, capacidade de transmissão e distribuição, bem como uma gestão
adaptativa e em tempo-real da infraestrutura da rede elétrica.
Por forma a lidar com a emergente crise energética, a rede elétrica tradicional
tem sido transformada numa infraestrutura inteligente e totalmente automatizada,
denominada rede elétrica inteligente. De entre as suas características, a capacidade
de supervisão em tempo-real, monitorização, coordenação adaptativa, e resiliência,
são destacadas. Ao abrigo da atual revolução digital, e consequente crescimento expo-
nencial do consumo e proliferação de geração renovável, a sua coordenação é crítica.
A presente dissertação enquadra-se nestes esforços de coordenação. Melhorias
na gestão energética de residências inteligentes, através do escalonamento ótimo de
equipamentos elétricos, são propostas. Estes incluem electrodomésticos frequente-
mente operados pelos residentes, como máquinas de lavar roupa e loiça, e outros
equipamentos, como baterias ou sistemas de bombas elétricas. Períodos de menor
consumo e preços da rede mais reduzidos, bem como de maior geração elétrica local,
são explorados para reduzir a fatura elétrica e do desconforto dos residentes. Uma casa
inteligente protótipo é considerada, suportada por dados históricos reais de consumo
e previsões de geração local. Aplicada a este caso de estudo, foi desenvolvida uma

xii
estratégia adaptativa baseada em otimização matemática para o escalonamento, no
horizonte de uma semana, de máquinas de lavar roupa, secar roupa e lavar loiça, uma
bomba de água elétrica e um sistema de bateria. Nesta estratégia, o escalonamento
é realizado em todos os dias da semana, considerando horizontes progressivamente
mais reduzidos. Para cada novo dia do horizonte, o escalonamento é formulado como
uma instância de um problema de escalonamento não preemptivo de máquinas, recor-
rendo a estratégias de programação linear para obter soluções de escalonamento. De
forma a considerar a imprevisibilidade do consumo elétrico e da geração em ambientes
residenciais, é utilizada uma ferramenta de modelação probabilística baseada em pro-
cessos Gaussianos para modelação destas fontes de incerteza, para cada problema de
escalonamento considerado.
Esta ferramenta foi validada em problemas de escalonamento de equipamentos
elétricos em habitações, tendo sido aplicada no planeamento, ao longo de uma semana,
de equipamentos elétricos controláveis, em 30 semanas dispersas ao longo de um ano.
Nas simulações realizadas, a competitividade desta estratégia, com respeito a critérios
de custo de energia e desconforto dos residentes, é demonstrada, em comparação com
abordagens do estado da arte, em particular com horizonte recidivo e escalonamento
para o dia seguinte. Uma modiﬁcação adicional à estratégia desenvolvida é também
proposta para abordar limitações relacionadas com a acomodação de violações de
restriçõesdeoperaçãodosequipamentosedascargasadicionais, foradasuautilização-
padrão. Enquanto que as limitações do primeiro tipo resultam de consumo ou geração
mais extremo, a realização de cargas adicionais é motivada pela ocorrência de eventos
não expectáveis, como eventos sociais. Em simulações adicionais, todas as violações
de restrições foram detetadas e acomodadas com êxito. Mais ainda, todas as cargas
adicionais foram planeadas com sucesso, sem comprometer a viabilidade das soluções
anteriormente determinadas.
A presente dissertação contribui com estratégias para o escalonamento de equipa-
mentos em habitações, validadas com sucesso num caso de estudo protótipo de uma
residência, recorrendo a dados reais, as quais poderão apresentar um impacto tangível
na resolução de problemas de escalonamento com incerteza em Sistemas Cíberfísicos.
Palavras-Chave: Gestão de Energia, Escalonamento com Incerteza, Residências
Inteligentes, Sistemas Cíberfísicos, Supervisão.

Acknowledgements
The successful completion of this thesis would not have been possible without the
support of a group of people to whom I owe proper acknowledgment.
First of all, I would like to express my sincere gratitude to Prof. Alberto Cardoso,
Prof. Bernardete Ribeiro and Prof. Paulo Gil, for giving me this opportunity and
accepting the challenge of having me as their student. Throughout this journey, their
guidance and teachings have been invaluable, always challenging me to expand my
horizons, knowledge and critical thinking. I am conﬁdent these, and many more
other teachings, have deeply contributed to make me a better researcher and person.
In addition to my supervisors, I also want to thank Prof. Carlos Fonseca, for his
valuable inputs and patience in the many discussions concerning the mathematical
optimisation problems addressed in this work.
The research that led to this thesis would also have not been possible without
the funding and support provided by the Portuguese Foundation for Science and
Technology (FCT), Human Capital Operational Program (POCH) and the European
Union (EU), under the Ph.D. grant SFRH/BD/122103/2016, and by the Centre for
Informatics and Systems of the University of Coimbra (CISUC).
I would have also not been able to come this far without my colleagues and friends,
those who have always been there and the ones I met during this journey. I want to
thank each and every one of them for all their support, discussions and overall good
times shared in the last ﬁve years.
Last, but certainly not the least, I would like to give a very special thanks to my
family, in particular my parents and sister, who put up with me everyday, love me,
believe in me, support me and, overall, make me a better person. I certainly would
not have been able to do this without you!

xix
Notation
The notation adopted throughout this dissertation is intended to be consistent, intu-
itive and as coherent as possible with that adopted in the diﬀerent ﬁelds related to
this work. In each chapter, symbols and notation conventions are presented as they
are adopted, being their meaning and role in the discussion clariﬁed.
In the formulations of the mathematical optimisation problems instantiated in
this work (see Chapters 3 and 5, along with Appendices A and B), a distinction
between scalars, vectors, matrices and tensors is considered. Scalars are represented
by lowercase letters, such asx, while a vector is represented in bold letters, such asy.
On its turn, a matrix is denoted byˆ zand, ﬁnally, a tensor is denoted by¯ w. Moreover,
sets are denoted by a special calligraphic font, e.g.U denotes a set.

Chapter 1
Introduction
This chapter introduces the doctoral thesis work. Primarily, the main motivation of
this thesis is discussed in Section 1.1. Secondly, in Section 1.2, the problem statement
of this research work is presented in detail, along with its central research questions.
The main outcomes of this dissertation are presented in Section 1.3, followed by a list
of scientiﬁc publications produced within the scope of this thesis. Finally, Section 1.4
outlines the remainder of the document.
1.1 Motivation
Throughout the21st century, technology has spread into our daily routines, becoming
critical in almost any everyday life domain, in such a way that originated a new rev-
olution. Within this technological breakthrough, embedded technology has become
ever more present in people’s lives, raising challenges concerning the integration of
distinct and heterogeneous digital devices in complex and large-scale physical envi-
ronments (Lv et al., 2021). One important challenge refers to the need for equipments
to have communication, perception, intelligence and reasoning capabilities. Cyber-
Physical Systems (CPSs), formally deﬁned in Deﬁnition 1.1.1, ﬁt well within these
challenges and needs, proposing to bridge the cyber and physical worlds, closing the
gaps between these two (distinct) realities, and creating opportunities to extend the
existing knowledge in these ﬁelds (Lee et al., 2020). Since their initial conceptualisa-
tion, CPSs have been strongly linked with industrial processes, due to the inherent
importance of human–machine interactions and adaptive supply chains, in these en-
vironments. Nevertheless, CPSs have also recently been targeted for other areas, in
particular the energy sector.
Deﬁnition 1.1.1.(Rajkumar et al., 2010) “Cyber-Physical Systems are physical and
engineered systems whose operations are monitored, coordinated, controlled and inte-
grated by a computing and communication core”.
For many years, electrical power grids have been responsible for delivering power
in a single direction, from utility generators to demand-side entities. As the instal-
lation of power grids became more widespread, and electrical devices proliferated to
demand-side entities, an overall sustained increase in customer demand could not be
stopped. In order to address the corresponding supply stress, observed across power
grids worldwide, interventions to the existing infrastructures were carried out, such as
deploying additional power stations and higher-capacity power lines. However, in the
early 21st century, it became clear that the previously reported growth in customer
demand was not going to slow down. On the contrary, an exponential increase of
electrical energy needs was predicted, calling for further enhancements to the grid in-
frastructure and novel technological advances, capable of coping with novel, and more
intense, demand patterns. Research on new-generation electrical appliances, metering

Chapter 1. Introduction 2
equipment and infrastructure has been carried out, targetting adaptive monitoring
and control of power demand, generation and transmission. Simultaneously, growing
environmental concerns and the anticipation of the depletion of fossil fuels (Hussain
et al., 2021) have boosted generation from renewable energy sources (RESs), and
their subsequent integration in the existing grid infrastructure. While growing RES
generation is already starting to be observed worldwide1,2, fossil fuels still remain
the primary generation source for electrical energy (Solarin et al., 2021). It should
also be mentioned that, in many sources, particularly in non-scientiﬁc literature, the
terms electricity and energy are used interchangeably when, in fact, they have diﬀer-
ent meanings. In the domain of power systems, electricity constitutes one component
of total energy consumption, being the others the transport and the heating.
In Figure 1.1, worldwide electricity generation is presented, from 1985 to 2019, for
each of its main sources. Coal is clearly highlighted has the main source of electricity,

having been so over the course of the last 34 years, followed by gas and hydropower.
Moreover, according to Figure 1.1, in 2019, around 62.22% of worldwide electricity
generation was still ensured via heavy-carbon emission sources, namely coal, gas and
oil. A rapid growth of RESs, in particular solar and wind, is also noted in recent years,
being aligned with sustainable energy movements. On the other hand, a decreasing
trend in oil-based electricity generation is reported, being in 2019 only responsible
for around 4% of electricity generation worldwide. It should, nonetheless, be noted
that Figure 1.1 is concerned with worldwide electricity generation. Naturally, diﬀerent
generation shares per source are registered for diﬀerent countries, as a result of their
individual adoption of green policies, or lack thereof. For example, according to data
from (Petroleum, 2020), during 2019, 99.96% of electricity generation of Saudi Arabia
was ensured via gas and oil. In the opposite of the spectrum, countries like Norway
are strongly dependent on clean and renewable sources, such as hydropower (which

c8cf5e1127ce$$File$$pt$$1.pdf, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 1. Introduction 3
accounted for 93.67% of electricity generation in 2019). Other countries like Portu-
gal have successfully committed to proliferating generation from RESs, despite still
ensuring a portion of consumed electricity via fossil fuel-based sources. According to
data from Redes Energéticas Nacionais (REN)3, a company responsible for managing
the main energy infrastructure networks of Portugal, in 2020, 59% of the country’s
demand was supplied from RES generation. Within RESs, wind and hydropower
ensured around 50% of the power demand in Portugal, during 2020.
In light of the proliferation of digital devices, the increasing growth of customer
demand, and sustainable energy movements, many countries are subjecting their ex-
isting power grids to a digital revolution. It includes several disruptive promises such
as: (i) transition from an electromechanical system to a digital system; (ii) two-way
communication mechanisms and protocols; (iii) real-time infrastructure monitoring;
and (iv) distributed power generation, not only from conventional carbon sources –
such as oil, gas and coal – but also from RESs and other low-carbon sources. In
their ﬁnal form, traditional power grids are replaced by intelligent and fully auto-
mated “smart” grids. These new-generation power grids are aligned with the concept
of a CPS presented in Deﬁnition 1.1.1. In a smart grid, the electrical energy demand
from each individual customer is continuously monitored and coordinated with other
customers and supply-side entities. Grid generation and power transmission needs
to be continuously adapted and controlled, via digital signals issued in existing two-
way communication channels. Moreover, the entire grid system must be resilient to
adverse and unpredicted events, such as external grid attacks, power transmission
failures, or sudden peaks or drops in renewable or traditional generation power pro-
duction. As such, load coordination and fast reaction to adverse events constitute the
key characteristics of smart grids, which ensure stable operation and power supply.
Within these two attributes, load coordination has received considerable attention, in
particular the scheduling of electrical loads in residential buildings. This popularity
is mainly due to the fact that residential buildings represent a signiﬁcant share of
worldwide demand (Figueiredo et al., 2020).
1.2 Problem Statement & Research Questions
This research work is framed within the smart grid paradigm shift. It follows sustain-
able and eﬀective load coordination movements, addressing the problem of improving
the rationalisation of the electrical energy in residential buildings. As stated in the
previous section, the fact that only residential buildings are considered, and not of
other types, is due to their signiﬁcant share of worldwide demand.
In a nutshell, building energy rationalisation is enabled in two ways, namely via
consumption reduction or consumption shifting (Bradac et al., 2015; Leitão et al.,
2020). A more detailed discussion of these techniques is performed in Chapter 2.
In consumption reduction, improved rationalisation of electrical energy is promoted
by means of the acquisition of more energy-eﬃcient appliances, and shutting down
of electrical devices not currently in use. On the other hand, consumption shifting
proposes to change demand patterns, deferring the operation of certain appliances
to periods of cheaper power or higher RESs generation. The fact that consumption
reduction is strongly dependent on the acquisition of new, more energy-eﬃcient, ap-
pliances reduces its popularity in the context of households. The often greater cost
3https://www.ren.pt/files/2021-03/2021-03-29134252_7a820a40-3b49-417f-a962-
6c4d7f037353$$7319a1b4-3b92-4c81-98d7-fea4bfefafcd$$b4f0f693-3d69-48be-b0c8-
c8cf5e1127ce$$File$$pt$$1.pdf, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 1. Introduction 4
of buying new appliances, in comparison to the smaller immediate electricity bill sav-
ings they enable, mean a longer return on investment. In addition, simply reducing
the demand from individual appliances only constitutes a temporary solution to this
problem. In light of predicted exponential growth of power demand, long-term energy
rationalisation is only possible by changing individual consumption behaviours. These
include demand coordination across the grid, and the exploitation of RESs, both at
demand-side and supply-side of the grid. Clearly, based on the above arguments,
consumption shifting is the most viable technique to change power demand patterns.
The research problem addressed in this work is, therefore, stated as follows:
Research Problem. In the sequel of the smart grid paradigm shift, demand-side build-
ings and supply-side entities are becoming more and more heterogeneous, dynamic, and
energy-dependent. Observed and expected growing trends of these behaviours of smart
grid subsystems are threatening the proper continuous monitoring of the smart grid
infrastructure, and its overall optimisation. Thus, eﬀective coordination of demand-
side and supply-side grid entities is required, providing beneﬁts to both entities. In this
context, particular attention should be devoted to demand-side residential buildings, as
they currently represent a signiﬁcant share of the worldwide power demand.
From this research problem, and the above discussed topics, the following general
research question (RQ) is deﬁned.
RQ. How can the energy usage of new-generation smart homes be improved?
Based on the above presented statements, which are further developed in Chap-
ter 2, within the scope of this work, the rationalisation of electrical energy in smart
homes is approached from a consumption shifting perspective. Therefore, the cen-
tral question of this work is to determine the optimal management and operation of
electrical appliances, within a smart home. From this general research question, the
following additional, and more concrete, research questions are formulated:
• RQ 1. How should smart home electrical appliance operation be managed, in
ordertopromoteenergyrationalisation, whileaddressingoperationalconstraints
and maintaining resident comfort within acceptable levels?
• RQ 2. How to properly model the diﬀerent sources of uncertainty related to
the electrical energy needs of a smart home?
• RQ 3. How to integrate uncertainty sources within state of the art energy
rationalisation mechanisms?
• RQ 4. How to manage diﬀerent electrical appliances, monitor household de-
mand and operational needs, and ensure a proper coordination with the smart
grid? Moreover, how can time-varying constraints and other unexpected events,
aﬀecting the management of smart home electrical appliances, be taken into
consideration?
1.3 Research Outcomes
This research work addresses the problem of improving the rationalisation of the elec-
trical energy in residential buildings, via optimal scheduling of electrical appliances.
In order to tackle this problem, a novel mathematical optimisation-based framework
is proposed, which is iteratively extended as each one of the above presented research
questions are addressed. A number of contributions of this work are presented in Sec-
tion 1.3.1. These contributions, and other initially pursued research directions, have
also been documented in the published scientiﬁc literature covered in Section 1.3.2.

Chapter 1. Introduction 5
1.3.1 Contributions
This research contributes to answer the challenges addressed by the research questions
presented in the previous section. Speciﬁcally, a total of three signiﬁcant contributions
are made in the context of this research.
The ﬁrst contribution concerns the development of a mathematical optimisation-
based framework to manage electrical loads in smart homes. It targets week-ahead
scheduling of domestic appliances, and works by planning the operation of the con-
trollable appliances for each new day of the week. At each day, a horizon that covers
the period from the current day of the week to the last day of the week is considered.
Progressively smaller horizons are considered as the week advances. Such character-
istic yields the compressive nature of this framework, which ultimately motivates the
choice of its name. It should also be noted that, within the scope of this work, the
term appliances is used to refer not only to electrical devices commonly operated by
household residents, such as clothes washing or dishwasher machines, but also others
less frequently found in households or managed by residents, such as batteries or elec-
trical pump systems, just to name out a few. A preliminary version of this framework
is proposed in Chapter 3. In this proposal, the non-controllable household demand
and microgeneration are estimated over given scheduling horizons, respectively based
on representative proﬁles and forecasts. Regarding the generation forecasts, they are
determined based on weather forecast information and models of photovoltaic (PV)
panels, installed at the home.
The second contribution is motivated by a limitation of the preliminary framework
proposal. As notedinSection3.4, problemuncertainties, relatedwithnon-controllable
household demand and PV microgeneration over the scheduling horizon, are explicitly
discarded from each scheduling problem considered. This severely hinders the adop-
tion of risk assessment and contingency planning measures which, in turn, limits the
accommodation of severe (non-controllable demand and microgeneration) uncertainty
realisations. In order to address this limitation, the second contribution of this re-
search work is the development of probabilistic models to describe the non-controllable
household power demand and PV microgeneration. The development of these proba-
bilistic models is detailed in Chapter 4. Separate probabilistic non-parametric Gaus-
sian process (GP) regression-based models describe the non-controllable household
demand and PV microgeneration. Other contributions were also identiﬁed, in the
form of novel GP covariance functions (see Section 4.2.3) and heuristics to select
individual models with superior predictive performance (see Section 4.2.6).
The third contribution focuses on the integration of problem uncertainties, namely
in terms of the non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration, within a week-
ahead allocation of controllable appliances. It is presented in Chapter 5, in the form
of an extension to the framework introduced in Chapter 3. This extension includes
the incorporation of probability distributions, modelling the non-controllable demand
and the PV microgeneration, within the daily appliance scheduling. Likewise Chap-
ter 3, progressively smaller scheduling horizons are considered for each new day of the
week. Probability distributions are also exploited to provide feasibility thresholds.
Finally, contributions concerning the integration of the proposed framework within
smart homes are also noticed. In Section 5.5, modiﬁcations to the framework pro-
posed at the beginning of Chapter 5 are performed to ensure continuous smart home
monitoring and controllable load rescheduling, whenever required. As demonstrated
in Section 5.5, the novel framework successfully avoids the violation of operational
constraints, either as a result of extreme uncertainty realisations, or due to the occur-
rence of unexpected events, which require the operation of additional loads.

These publications can be categorised according to their respective results and
contributions. Two categories are identiﬁed. The ﬁrst contains publications with pre-
liminary results, encompassed within the broader topics of CPSs. These include J3,
C2, C6, C7, BC1, O2, O3 and O4. As for the second category, it includes publi-
cations J1, J2, C3, C4, C5 and O1, whose contributions present a narrower scope,
explicitly framed within the outcome of this research.
Concerning publications in the ﬁrst category, in BC1, O2 and O4, contributions
to supervisory control and resilience enhancement in CPSs are presented. These pub-
lications, and their respective contributions, have been performed during preliminary
studies on the broader CPS topic. A narrower scope was, subsequently, promoted,
investigating the management of electrical energy and water resources in residential
environments. Urban water consumption patterns are investigated in J3 and C6,
whereas O3 forecasts residential power demand using a capsule network-based tech-
nique. On its turn, a preliminary application for monitoring and prediction of power
and water demand in residential environments is covered in C1. As for C2 and C7,
they demonstrate the application of two software development tools extensively used
in this research, namely IPython and Jupyter Notebooks, within educational contexts.
As for the second category, management of electrical energy in smart homes is
introduced in O1, and complemented by a survey on home energy management sys-
tems (HEMSs), presented in J2. In this survey, an aggregated and uniﬁed perspective
on commonly managed household appliances, scheduling objectives, constraints and
security issues is presented. Publications C4, C5 and J1 study the optimal scheduling
of electrical appliances in a prototype smart home. While C4 and C5 propose meta-
heuristic techniques for domestic appliance scheduling, in C3, a preliminary strategy
based on mathematical optimisation techniques is introduced, and ﬁnalised in J1.

Chapter 1. Introduction 8
1.4 Thesis Outline
This document is organised in six chapters and two complementary appendices. In
Chapter 1, the doctoral thesis work is introduced, covering its main motivation, and
research problem, questions and outcomes.
Following the general introduction of this chapter, a comprehensive outlook on
HEMSs and closely-related topics is provided in Chapter 2. The chapter begins with
an introduction to the smart grid paradigm shift, and to the integration of HEMSs
within these new-generation power grid systems. Popular approaches to saving energy
in buildings are also discussed. The presented techniques pave the way for a categori-
sation of commonly managed household appliances, along with decision-making trends
adopted to address residential load uncertainties. Concerning energy savings in build-
ings, this topic is further discussed from the point of view of the coordination of the
household electrical appliances. In this sense, common scheduling techniques, criteria
and constraints are reviewed. Finally, HEMSs and smart grid security aspects are
covered, namely the main attack vectors and defence strategies.
Chapter 3 builds upon the main ﬁndings documented in Chapter 2, regarding the
popular approaches to energy saving in buildings, in particular with respect to control-
lable appliance scheduling. This chapter addresses the problem of week-ahead schedul-
ing of household appliances, for the rationalisation of electrical energy. In this chapter,
a mathematical optimisation-based framework is proposed to address known issues of
popular techniques within the HEMS scheduling literature. Mathematical support to
the proposed framework, in the form of lower and upper bounds for the two schedul-
ing criteria considered, is provided in Appendices A.1 and A.2. The novel proposal
is validated against two established techniques in the appliance scheduling literature,
whose detailed formulation is provided in Appendices A.3 and A.4. This is carried
out for a smart home prototype. In this scenario, characteristic non-controllable de-
mand is modelled by real-world historical records, while microgeneration is considered
available by means of PV panels, and, ﬁnally, local energy storage is carried out via a
battery system. The week-ahead scheduling of a set of electrical appliances, namely an
electrical water pump, and clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines,
is carried out over a total of 30 diﬀerent weeks, covering a one-year period. The
operation of the controllable appliances is presented and discussed for each strategy.
Furthermore, an in-depth comparison against typical and ideal appliance allocation
strategies is carried out. Finally, the chapter concludes with a synthesis of its main
ﬁndings. Within the scope of this chapter, the fact that all sources of uncertainty
are discarded constitutes a major limitation of the proposed strategy. As noted in
Section 3.4, this limitation led the way to the remainder research, in which explicit
modelling and integration of problem uncertainties within the proposed mathematical
optimisation framework is investigated.
Following the ﬁndings of Chapter 3, a probabilistic modelling of the two sources of
uncertainty, in the previously considered week-ahead appliance scheduling problem, is
carried out in Chapter 4. In this chapter, probabilistic modelling of non-controllable
household power demand and PV microgeneration is performed, based on historical
demand records and generation forecasts. Generation forecasts are determined based
on historical weather forecast information and PV panel models. Within the scope
of this chapter, non-controllable demand and generation are modelled via separate
probabilistic non-parametric GP regression-based models. This chapter begins with
a brief contextualisation of probabilistic modelling, followed by a summary of the
literature on building power demand and generation forecasting. Motivated by the
main ﬁndings of the literature review, GP regression and quantile regression (QR)

Chapter 1. Introduction 9
techniques are highlighted. As a result, a theoretical background on these two tech-
niques is provided, being QR taken as the comparative benchmark for the developed
GP demand and generation models. After the initial contextualisation and theoretical
background, the methodology adopted in the development of GP and QR predictive
models is covered. This includes a description of the collected historical datasets,
exploratory data analysis, pre-processing, and input representation. In the case of
GP models, distinct covariance functions, including novel proposals, are presented.
In addition, performance criteria, a cross-validation (CV)-based framework for model
evaluation, and heuristics guiding the selection of GP and QR conﬁgurations with
superior predictive performance, are also discussed. After assessing the predictive
performance of the diﬀerent GP and QR conﬁgurations, the above mentioned heuris-
tics are employed to select one GP and one QR conﬁguration, for each data source,
with the best overall performance. The selected GP and QR conﬁgurations are further
evaluated on a separate test dataset, which has not been previously shown to any of
the models. Demand and generation forecasts resulting from the selected GP and QR
models are contextualised within the published literature, in which the same tech-
niques have also been considered to model power demand and power generation. The
chapter concludes with a consolidated overview, performed in Section 4.4, highlighting
its contributions and future work directions.
Chapter 5 builds up on the contributions of both Chapters 3 and 4. In this
chapter, a new mathematical optimisation-based framework for a week-ahead smart
home appliance scheduling is proposed. This framework extends the original proposal
presented in Chapter 3, explicitly modelling uncertain household non-controllable de-
mand and microgeneration via appropriate probability distributions. In this chapter,
the smart home prototype scenario and controllable appliances, considered in Chap-
ter 3, are revisited. The scheduling of the controllable appliances is carried out over
the same set of 30 weeks as considered before, for both the novel proposal and two
state of the art variants presented in Chapter 3. The probabilistic modelling of both
non-controllable demand and generation motivated changes in the formulation of the
mathematical optimisation problems, considered within the novel strategy. In this
case, the expected value of an objective function composed of two conﬂicting criteria is
considered. Supported by the content of the Appendix B.1, which provides the math-
ematical foundations for deriving this expected value, a scenario-based approximation
is carried out. Moreover, the probabilistic modelling of the non-controllable demand
and microgeneration also motivated the deﬁnition of probabilistic constraints, which
provide probabilistic guarantees concerning the feasibility of the scheduling solutions
obtained. Further mathematical support for the formulation of these constraints is
provided in Appendix B.3, while lower and upper bounds for each scheduling crite-
rion are derived in Appendix B.2. Likewise for the original framework proposed in
Chapter 3, detailed formulations of the two variants of the state of the art schedul-
ing strategies considered in this chapter are provided, respectively, in Appendices B.4
and B.5. The operation of the controllable appliances is presented and discussed,
for each strategy, for a number of uncertainty realisations. An in-depth comparison
against typical and ideal appliance allocation strategies is also carried out, being a
high computational burden reported for both the novel strategy and its state of the
art variants. This has motivated the adoption of scenario reduction techniques, reduc-
ing the underlying computational load by discarding the most redundant realisations.
The impact of scenario reduction within the studied household appliance scheduling
problem is assessed by comparing the allocation of the controllable appliances with
the original scenario set, and resulting from diﬀerent reduced scenario sets. The inte-
gration of the framework proposed in this chapter within the prototype smart home

Chapter 1. Introduction 10
case study is, subsequently, addressed. An extended strategy is derived, in which
household demand and generation are continuously monitored, and the operation of
the controllable appliances is rescheduled upon the violation of any constraint. In
Appendices B.6 and B.7, detailed formulations of the underlying mathematical opti-
misation problem instances considered within this extended strategy are presented.
The chapter concludes with a statement of its main ﬁndings.
Chapter 6 concludes this dissertation, presenting the main outcomes of the un-
derlying research work. It summarises the main contributions of each chapter, and
provides a consolidated overview of this document. Finally, reﬂections on possible
additional research directions and future work paths are also discussed.

11
Chapter 2
State of the Art
The digital revolution carried out throughout the21st century has promoted an in-
crease in the number of digital devices available for individual use, along with their
penetration in our daily routines. This has raised several challenges, related with
the integration of many heterogeneous embedded devices in complex and large-scale
physical environments. A sustained growth in worldwide electricity needs and con-
sumption has been observed, as a result of this digital revolution, threatening the
adequate and stable power supply in existing power grid infrastructures.
In recent years, the on-going digital revolution has also been accompanied by
growing concerns regarding the unsustainable exploitation rates of the major elec-
tricity sources, and their negative environmental impacts. Indeed, for many years,
fossil fuels have remained the main source of the electricity consumed worldwide, ac-
counting for around 59% of the primary generation in the United States in 20201.
Their continuously unsustainable exploitation rates and strong environmental impact
– boosting global warming (Wood, 2020) and the number of diseases such as allergic
rhinitis, asthma, cancer, adverse birth outcomes and even preterm birth (Jauniaux
and Grigg, 2020) – have motivated investments in clean and renewable alternatives,
such as biofuels, hydroelectric, solar and wind (Wood, 2020). While renewable alter-
natives address the main challenges of fossil fuel-based generation, their integration in
existing power grids also raises its own challenges, in particular concerned with their
high power output variability.
In the sequel of the technological advances presented during the21st century, a
new paradigm shift for power grids has emerged, in the form of the so-called smart
grids. Under this new paradigm, traditional power grids undergo a complete transfor-
mation, transitioning from electromechanical systems to digital and fully automated
infrastructures. Two-way communication mechanisms between demand and supply
entities are also supported, along with continuous and adaptive monitoring, resilience,
and intelligent and real-time decision-making. CPSs, formalised in Deﬁnition 1.1.1,
are framed within this context, proposing to bridge the gap between the cyber and
physical worlds. They do this by enhancing existing physical devices with commu-
nication, perception, intelligence and reasoning capabilities. These systems provide,
via the discussed smart grid enhancements, the technological support for continuous
monitoring, adaptation and coordination of demand, generation and grid transmis-
sion. Within the scope of smart grids, coordination of demand-side loads has assumed
a critical role. When done properly, it not only contributes to ensure grid transmis-
sion capacity limits, but also to enable coordination between demand and generation,
in particular of renewable sources. In addition to promoting stable power supply,
the reduction of operation costs, to both the utility and individual customers, is also
sought, by taking this criteria into consideration when planning grid operation.
1https://www.eia.gov/outlooks/aeo/pdf/AEO_Narrative_2021.pdf, accessed on: 06 Septem-
ber 2021.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 12
In its sequel, a growing adoption of demand-side management (DSM) and demand
response (DR) programs in residential environments has been observed. In this con-
text, DSM and DR programs have been carried out to coordinate demand among grid
entities, ensuring stable supply, and even load distribution. The need for the coordina-
tion of distinct grid assets became even more important with the increased penetration
of renewable generation, mostly due to its highly variable output. In recent years, a
growing interest in HEMSs has also been observed. These systems target eﬃcient en-
ergy management, and provide the means for automated and intelligent smart home
appliance management, with both environmental and ﬁnancial beneﬁts for residents.
The conceptualisation of HEMSs involves several aspects, namely their deﬁnition,
characterisation, architecture and underlying purpose in household environments.
In this chapter, a comprehensive outlook on HEMSs and closely-related topics
is provided. The chapter begins with an introduction to the smart grid paradigm
shift (Section 2.1), followed by a contextualisation of HEMSs within the smart grid,
and popular building energy saving approaches (Section 2.2). A categorisation of the
existing literature regarding commonly managed household appliances is presented
in Section 2.3, along with decision-making trends adopted to address residential load
uncertainties. Section 2.4 is devoted to the coordination of household appliances,
covering common scheduling techniques, criteria and constraints. Finally, a discussion
onHEMSsandsmartgridsecurityisperformedinSection2.5, whileanaggregatedand
summarised overview of the topics discussed in this chapter is presented in Section 2.6.
It must also be noted that the contents of this chapter have, to a considerable extent,
been covered in a published literature review (Leitão et al., 2020).
2.1 Smart Grid
An electrical or power grid can be described as an interconnected network system,
responsible for delivering electricity from producers to consumers (Fang et al., 2012).
The current power grid was conceived many decades ago, when customer demand was
signiﬁcantly smaller. In designing the conventional power grid, power generation was
localised around communities, in order to reduce transmission costs and losses. The
interaction between the diﬀerent entities in the grid, namely utility and customers,
is limited and unidirectional. In this scenario, the utility is responsible for ensuring
adequate power generation and customer distribution. Moreover, customers are billed
according to their individual power consumption, and generally in a monthly basis.
One-way communication mechanisms constitute a major limitation of traditional
power grids, preventing a proper accommodation of ever-changing and rising power
demand, observed in recent years. In conventional power grids, customers have no way
of informing the utility of their electricity needs, nor the utility can adequately moni-
tor and react to sudden demand peaks. In recent years, a digital transformation of the
traditional power grid has been carried out, under the so-called smart grid paradigm
shift. Conventional power grids are being replaced by a new-generation of intelligent,
highly reliable and fully automated grids (Leitão et al., 2020). Two-way communi-
cation between the utility and customers is featured, supporting bidirectional ﬂow
of information and power. Improved eﬃciency, ﬂexibility and reliability is promised
via real-time self-monitoring and self-healing mechanisms, in addition to optimised
demand and supply-side resource management (Yan et al., 2013). In Figure 2.1, typ-
ical structures of both traditional and smart electric power grids are illustrated. In
Table 2.1, the main diﬀerences between the traditional and smart power grid infras-
tructures are presented (Farhangi, 2010; Ourahou et al., 2020).

Two-way communication is supported by advanced metering equipment (Makhad-
meh et al., 2019). Novel smart meters measure the customer demand in real-time,
and communicate it to the utility. Demand-side appliance operation is coordinated
with the grid, leveraging global infrastructure information, in particular during criti-
cal demand periods. The following key characteristics of the smart grid support the
attainment its primary objectives (Longe, 2016; Makhadmeh et al., 2019):
• Proliferation of advanced metering equipment, supporting two-way communica-
tion between consumers and the utility.
• Integration of distributed and renewable energy generation at both supply and
demand-sides of the grid.
• Adaptive, reliable, secure and resilient infrastructure, featuring self-monitoring,
self-conﬁgurable and self-healing characteristics.
2https://www.flaticon.com, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 14
• Lower electricity expenses for consumers, achieved via optimal resource man-
agement between producers and consumers, and increased penetration of clean
and renewable energy sources.
The integration of RESs in the power grid is expected at both demand and supply-
sides. As a result, customers may choose to exploit local microgeneration for self-
consumption, or inject it in the grid. RES generation must be coordinated with the
existing power plants and customer demand, in order to reduce the deployment of
conventional and more expensive secondary generators. Load deferment also plays an
important contribution, shifting appliance usage away from peak hours. Additional
challenges to a normal operation of the grid are still raised by RES integration, such
as addressing the higher variability and uncertainty of its generation, along with the
added computational burden of coordinating more devices. Finally, power generated
at conventional sources or RES generators remains routed to consumers through power
lines, switches and transformers. Any break in grid distribution, caused by adverse
weather, device malfunction or sudden demand changes, just to name out a few, must
be automatically identiﬁed by the smart grid, and assigned an alternative route.
Along with the grid infrastructure, interventions in demand-side buildings are also
requiredinordertoensurethesatisfactionoftheoperationalrequirementsofthesmart
grid. A similar paradigm shift is identiﬁed for buildings, paving the way to smart
building technologies. A particularly relevant revolution concerns the adoption of
HEMSs, whichnotonlypromiseeﬃcientappliancemanagement, butalsocoordination
with the utility and local microgeneration.
2.2 Home Energy Management Systems
Over the last decade, improvements in smart grid technology have promoted the
migration of these disruptive technological solutions to diﬀerent types of buildings.
Residential buildings have been subject of signiﬁcant interest from academia and prac-
titioners, particularly from the point of view of energy rationalisation. In light of this
recent interest, the way to a new generation of residential buildings has been paved,
under the so-called smart home paradigm shift. Smart homes oﬀer better quality of
life and energy rationalisation, through context-aware, self-adaptive, supervisory and
real-time monitoring, enabling early identiﬁcation of residential needs and preferences,
as well as autonomous decision-making, usually by means of appliance coordination.
HEMSs are encompassed within the smart home paradigm shift. These systems
target eﬃcient energy management by means of domestic load coordination. More
precisely, HEMSs decide when to turn certain devices on or oﬀ, in order to explore low
demand and cheaper electricity periods. The operations of the HEMSs are supported
by advanced metering infrastructure (AMI) devices, remote control and automation
mechanisms, as well as two-way communication between customers and the utility.
Communication mechanisms are vital to utility companies, enabling a reduction in
the uncertainty of future demand, and the coordination of generation over the grid.
The importance of this coordination between demand-side and supply-side entities
is further highlighted by the recent proliferation of generation from RESs. Wind and
solarPV-basedgenerationiswidelypopularatbothconsumerbuildingsandacrossthe
grid; however, this type of power generation is characterised by highly unpredictable
and variable power output, due to weather dynamics. High uncertainty in RES power
output threatens supply stability and safety, under dynamic customer demand. In
order to address these challenges, power companies have relied on communication,

Chapter 2. State of the Art 15
monitoring and self-adaptive decision-making mechanisms, so as to perform hierarchi-
cal demand coordination and accommodate changing operation conditions. Examples
of changing operation conditions include variations in demand and generation needs,
updates to grid prices or meteorological forecasts, just to name out a few.
2.2.1 HEMS Characterisation
The general architecture of a HEMS is illustrated in Figure 2.2. A HEMS comprises
the following four main components:
(a) Sensing and measuring devices– used to measure physical quantities, e.g. tem-
perature and light, detect motion or collect detailed appliance consumption
information, among others. These devices are often namedsmart meters, due
to their support for two-way communication between the HEMS and the utility.
(b) Smart appliances – consist of typical household devices (e.g., dishwasher or
air conditioning units) and energy generation devices (e.g., PV panels or wind
turbines), enhanced with computing and communication capabilities, that are
heavily explored for remote control of their operation. It is common for smart
appliances to communicate with a central platform within the HEMS, which is
responsible for processing measured data and coordinate appliance operation.
(c) User interface – enabling residents to interact with the HEMS by displaying
monitoring information, such as energy expenses. In addition, residents can
also specify preferences regarding comfort parameters, priorities among appli-
ances to be scheduled or criteria to be met. Common interfaces include touch
screen devices or mobile applications, although less user-friendly alternatives,
e.g. computer terminal, are also available.
(d) Central platform – receives smart meter information and schedules electricity
usages, e.g. via an optimisation-based approach. A common choice of scheduling
criterion is the electricity bill, although the comfort of residents, the reduction
of peak demand and of green house gas (GHG) emissions are also common.
Two approaches can be considered to regulate HEMS operations in coordina-
tion with the power company and other grid customers, namely centralised and dis-
tributed (Celik et al., 2018). Within centralised HEMS, load scheduling and deploy-
ment tasks are conducted by the utility. This entity is responsible for aggregating
individual building consumption-related information, such as sensor data, appliance
operation needs and constraints. Resource coordination is often performed by means
of massive constrained optimisation problems, with a resulting heavy computational
load. Under this topology, the above mentioned HEMSs central platform is no longer
responsible for load scheduling tasks, being instead restricted to the aggregation, pro-
cessing and inference of sensor data.
Contrastingwithcentralisedmethodologies, indistributedHEMSs, gridoperations
are coordinated through cooperation among several independent decision-making en-
tities. A compromise between individual goals must, therefore, be achieved without
aﬀecting the stability of the power supply, or other critical impositions. Communi-
cation between all entities involved is more frequent, even though less sensitive infor-
mation is exchanged, for privacy and security reasons. For example, the minimum
required power levels tend to be transmitted, instead of the explicit appliance opera-
tion needs (Mondal et al., 2017). Within distributed load management, the underlying
computational burden is split among all players. As such, the individual computa-
tional load is lower, making these approaches more attractive for the coordination of

2020), under Creative Commons 4.0 license4.
distinct grid assets and household appliances. For these reasons, distributed strate-
gies are more popular for the coordination of groups of buildings (Chavali et al., 2014;
Mondal et al., 2017; Celik et al., 2018) than at an individual household level (Wei
et al., 2015). In the latter case, individual agents are often assigned to manage single
appliance operations. Despite agent-based and game-theory-based approaches (Celik
et al., 2016; Mondal et al., 2017; Celik et al., 2018) being natural choices to model
individual HEMSs and grid entities in this process, mathematical optimisation tech-
niques(Chavalietal.,2014;Weietal.,2015;Huetal.,2018)havealsobeenconsidered.
Both centralised and distributed HEMSs seek continuous monitoring and coordi-
nation of physically separated equipments. Therefore, it comes as no surprise that
communication technologies constitute a major driver of HEMSs. In (Longe, 2016),
local, wide and ﬁeld area networks are highlighted to address smart grid communica-
tion challenges. While the adoption of wireless communication, such as WiFi (Jiang
et al., 2018) and ZigBee (Byun et al., 2012; Srivas et al., 2020), is growing, wired
communication is still considered in both residential and industrial contexts. Within
wired communication technologies, Ethernet (Zhang et al., 2010) and power line com-
munication (Son et al., 2010; Nassar et al., 2012) are commonly exploited. In addition,
hybrid technologies combining, e.g. power line communication and ZigBee (Han et al.,
2014) or WiFi (Olvera-Irigoyen et al., 2012), have also been discussed (Longe, 2016).
The growing trend towards demand coordination also calls for communication
and security protocols capable of addressing the critical vulnerabilities of HEMSs
and the smart grid. To this end, communication technologies must be backed up by
security protocols, which prevent unauthorised system access, attacks and minimise
transmission and other operation failures (He and Yan, 2016; Mrabet et al., 2018).

Chapter 2. State of the Art 17
Security aspects of both HEMSs and the smart grid fall out of the scope of this section,
being discussed in Section 2.5.
2.2.2 Energy Management in Buildings
Consumption reduction and consumption shifting are the two predominant strate-
gies to improve building energy rationalisation and reduce the electricity bill (Bradac
et al., 2015). Consumption reduction promotes the acquisition of energy-eﬃcient
appliances and turning oﬀ of unnecessary loads. On the other hand, consumption
shifting addresses the underlying energy management problem by deferring appliance
use to periods of cheaper electricity. The popularity of consumption shifting within
residential buildings has been acknowledged (Bradac et al., 2015; Leitão et al., 2020),
mostly as it requires cheaper and less intrusive interventions. Nonetheless, demand-
side appliance coordination requires the solution of complex scheduling problems, as
device operation is often restricted by physical and preferences constraints, billing
scheme, local power generation and baseline demand. DSM and DR programs have
been proposed for demand-side load regulation.
DSMisabroadspectrumﬁeld, comprisingacollectionoftechniquestoimproveen-
ergy rationalisation and reduce the overall energy bill at consumption-side (Palensky
and Dietrich, 2011). Owing to the fact that the DSM-related literature is somewhat
disperse, there is no general consensus regarding the classiﬁcation and categorisation
of the underlying techniques. In (Palensky and Dietrich, 2011), DSM techniques are
categorised based on their timing and customer impact, namely (i) Energy Eﬃciency
(EE), (ii) Time of Use (TOU), (iii) DR, and (iv) Spinning Reserve. EE techniques
permanently enhance energy use by choosing energy-eﬃcient appliances, improving
building design and construction, and increasing end-user awareness and behavioural
changes. While both TOU and DR promote load balancing by means of appliance
coordination and shifting, in TOU, the household demand is billed at diﬀerent prices
based on the time of the day. On the other hand, DR promotes changes in electricity
use as a response to smart grid events, such as grid price updates. Spinning reserve
techniques promote communication among demand-side devices, in order to adjust
demand based on grid frequency. In (Lampropoulos et al., 2013), a diﬀerent categori-
sation has been proposed, splitting the underlying techniques into (i) EE, (ii) DR,
and (iii) Strategic Load Growth (SLG). In comparison to the categorisation proposed
in (Palensky and Dietrich, 2011), the main novelty of this categorisation are SLG
techniques. According to the cited authors, SLG techniques aim to change the load
shape by forcing demand to increase or decrease in certain periods.
DR refers to real-time changes in electricity use at demand-side, carried out as a
result of changes in grid prices or incentive payments. These are often carried out
with the goal of lowering the grid demand during speciﬁc periods of time (Chen et al.,
2018). Real-time load adjustment is crucial in DR, addressing unexpected grid de-
mand peaks and variable RES power generation, and contributing to short-term grid
balancing (Liu, 2017). In DR literature there is also no general consensus regarding
the categorisation of the underlying techniques. In (Palensky and Dietrich, 2011), DR
techniques are categorised intomarket and physical techniques. Market DR imposes
static or dynamic billing, along with ﬁnancial incentives, encouraging load shifting to-
wards speciﬁc periods of time. Conversely, physical DR is focused on external smart
grid signals, issued by the utility in the sequel of infrastructure failures or mainte-
nance, and aiming to reduce or remove demand at a given period. In (Lampropoulos
et al., 2013), a more exhaustive categorisation of DR is performed, splitting DR tech-
niques into: (i) frequency-based, (ii) direct control over utility equipment, (iii) direct

Chapter 2. State of the Art 18
control over end-use equipment, (iv) price-based, (v) market-based, and (vi) model-
based. This categorisation is presented in Figure 2.3.Frequency-basedtechniques turn
demand-side devices on or oﬀ based on frequency-based mechanisms.Direct control
over either the utility or end-use equipment aims to control grid assets, such as trans-
formers and feeders, or demand-side appliances. For utility equipment, the adoption
of voltage reduction and protection relays is highlighted in (Lampropoulos et al.,
2013), while protection fuses and clock-based controllers are considered at demand-
side. Price-based techniques are focused on the adoption of billing schemes such as
TOU, real-time pricing (RTP) or critical-peak pricing (CPP), to reshape demand-side
loads. Conversely, inmarket-based, demand-side resources are explicitly incorporated
into electricity markets. Model-based predictive control (MPC) is the most popular
model-based technique, with alternatives such as machine learning-based also being
the subject of recent research interest (Leitão et al., 2020).

Figure 2.3: Demand-Side Management and Demand Response cat-
