Commons 4.0 license5.
More recently, in (Zhou et al., 2016) and (Chen et al., 2018), DR is split intoprice-
based and incentive-based. Both categories share a focus on minimising the electricity
bill via scheduling of electrical devices. While price-based techniques are focused on
adjusting loads to dynamic grid prices, incentive-based techniques rely on determinis-
tic and time-invariant policies, such as direct load control, to promote load reduction.
In the sequel of the integration of RESs and distributed storage systems in modern
power grids, AMI, dynamic billing policies and automated DSM and DR also need to
be properly incorporated. Owing to distinct, and often conﬂicting, utility and cus-
tomer objectives, extensive research on multi-objective optimal resource management,
notably MPC (Bianchini et al., 2016; Khanmirza et al., 2016), linear programming
(LP), non-linear programming (NLP)6 (Stluka et al., 2011; Amini et al., 2015; Yahia
and Pradhan, 2018), and genetic algorithms (GAs) (Neves and Silva, 2015; Hu and
Xiao, 2018; Talha et al., 2018) is required.
2.2.3 Renewable Energy Integration
Motivated by the smart grid digital revolution and worldwide green initiatives, policies
to signiﬁcantly reduce GHG emission and increase the share of renewable energy over
the next decades have been promoted in countries such as the U.S., U.K. and Portugal,
5https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.
6In this document, the abbreviation NLP is considered within an optimisation scope, and therefore
refers to non-linear programming optimisation problems. It should not be confused with the same
abbreviation considered within the language processing ﬁeld, where NLP refers to natural language
processing.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 19
just to name out a few7. These movements aim to replace pollutant and non-renewable
sources, such as fossil-fuels, with clean and renewable alternatives.
RESs are distinguished from conventional generation sources based on their de-
pendence on natural resources, such as sunlight, wind, biomass or geothermal energy.
Unlike conventional sources, exploration of these resources does not contribute to
global warming and the increase of the carbon footprint. RES generation power out-
put is also dependent on natural phenomena, often imposing spatial constraints to
generator deployment, and limiting the regulation of their power output. As such,
RES generation is further characterised by high power output uncertainty.
Given the high variability and uncertainty of RES power output, ensuring sta-
ble and adequate power supply with these sources is a challenging task. This holds
true, not only for individual homes, where RES microgeneration often targets self-
consumption, but also for utility-side RES generation. While supply stress and GHG
emissions can be alleviated via RES generation, unexpected adverse weather can sud-
denly drop its power output. In these cases, conventional generators must be de-
ployed to make up for the lost generation. Despite eﬀorts to integrate RES in smart
homes and the power grid, several challenges are still identiﬁed, sharing the following
ideas (Kumar et al., 2019; Tsai et al., 2020; Hosseini et al., 2020; Sinsel et al., 2020):
• Quality and stability– Concerning requirements for uninterruptible power sup-
ply, voltage and current stability. Challenges belonging to this category include:
–Increased ﬂickering, harmonic distortions and system voltage, caused by
variable RES power output. These disturbances contribute to shorter
equipment lifetime, trips and appliance damage.
–Unreliable shutdown during blackouts, as generation may persist in areas
disconnected from the main network. This constitutes a safety and grid
maintenance hazard.
–InsuﬃcientRESgenerationtomatchthecurrentdemand, leadingtosystem
voltage loss, and violating supply stability requirements.
–Lower short-circuit power level of RES generators, which contributes to
increased voltage instability.
–Need for coordinated planning and dispatching of energy networks in geo-
graphically distinct locations.
• Flow – Concerning eﬃcient power transmission and distribution. Challenges
belonging to this category include:
–Lack of controlled RES generation, potentially leading to violations of
transmission constraints, and overloading of transmission lines.
–Increasedriskofshort-circuitandvoltagetrips, duetovariableRESoutput.
–Inadequate protection design against highly dynamic load ﬂows, such as
those resulting from RESs.
• Balance – Comprehending demand and supply matching, for which the following
challenges are raised:
–Increased risk of demand and supply mismatch, due to higher uncertainty
of RES generation, and restricted on-demand dispatching. The viability
of RES generation as a backup source, exploited to balance unforeseen
outages of other sources, is further threatened by its limited generation
ﬂexibility. This motivates the use of conventional generation sources, such
as fossil-fuels, as backup options.
7Source: Renewables 2020 Global Status Report. Available athttps://www.ren21.net/reports/
global-status-report, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 20
–Sub-optimalmanagementofthegridinfrastructure, duetoinaccuratemod-
elling and forecasting of RES generation.
–Increased computational burden of generation modelling, resulting from
ﬁtting individual models to diﬀerent generators and locations.
–Need for improved demand and supply coordination across the grid, ad-
dressing the high uncertainty of RES generation.
• RES placement– Consisting of spatial requirements for the installation of RES
generators. The suitability of any given new location involves several factors,
namely: (i) renewable resource quality; (ii) transmission capacity; (iii) land use
and other geographical and geological aspects; (iv) zoning and permitting; and
(v) enforced policies.
• Energy Storage– The integration of energy storage systems (ESSs), usually in
the form of batteries, in the smart grid infrastructure is gaining popularity. ESSs
can be exploited to store generation surplus, in particular originated in RESs.
Nonetheless, the integration of these devices also raises challenges on its own,
namely with respect to low eﬃciency, reduced expected lifetime, and the overall
added complexity stemming from coordinating many devices (Tsai et al., 2020).
Solution techniques addressing the integration of RESs within smart grids have
also been proposed. In (Sinsel et al., 2020), approaches are categorised based on two
criteria, namely technology deployment –centralisedvsdistributed – and the contribu-
tion to system ﬂexibility –ﬂexibilityvs grid technologies. Distributed technologies are
implemented at diﬀerent grid locations, leading to a more distributed system, while
centralised alternatives remain restricted to a single, central, regulating entity. On
the other hand, ﬂexibility technologies contribute to system ﬂexibility by promoting
active power generation and consumption, while grid technologies are more related
with speciﬁc applications. According to (Sinsel et al., 2020), solution technologies are
divided in four groups:
• Distributed and Flexibility technologies
–Modiﬁcations to renewable generators, in order to improve eﬃciency and
power output, e.g. solar tracking in PV panels.
–Distributed deployment of renewable and conventional generators across
diﬀerent locations in the grid, in order to promote peak load shaving. Con-
cerning renewable generators, small-scale deployment at demand-side is
also considered, reducing building demand through self-consumption.
–Distributed DSM and DR programs, with the goal of coordinating demand-
side loads with current and predicted generation.
–Improved demand and renewable generation models, supporting enhanced
grid infrastructure management.
• Distributed and Grid technologies
–Expansion and reinforcement of the grid infrastructure, e.g. by installing
and upgrading transmission cables, overhead lines and transformers.
–Novel protection design and strategies meeting the requirements of RES
generators. New equipment installation, such as limiters, is also considered.
–Distributed voltage management, placed alongside RES generators.
–Remote monitoring of power networks and electric status estimation.
–Distributed voltage and frequency controllers, to address the increased RES
generator voltage and frequency variability.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 21
• Centralised and Flexibility technologies
–Modiﬁcations to primary generator equipment, enabling remote monitoring
and power output regulation.
–Harmonic ﬁlters, deployed to ﬁlter harmonic distortions of RES generators.
–Centralised deployment of ESSs, responsible for accommodating demand
peaks and sudden drops in generation from RES or conventional sources.
–Centralised DSM and DR for large-scale coordination of load and genera-
tion throughout the grid.
–Centralised control and management frameworks for optimal integrated
energy network dispatch.
• Centralised and Grid technologies
–Improved modelling of demand and renewable generation, with the same
purpose as in distributed technologies, but considering centralised mod-
elling.
–Reinforcement and expansion of the transmission grid infrastructure, car-
ried out to address the bidirectional ﬂow of renewable energy generation.
–Large-scale monitoring of the grid infrastructure.
–Centralised voltage and frequency control techniques, such as high-voltage
direct current transmission or enhanced centralised power ﬂow monitoring
and control mechanisms.
–Centralised RES de-loading via, e.g., droop control, inertia control and
de-loading control, as highlighted by (Kumar et al., 2019).
2.2.4 HEMSs Overview
HEMSs comprehend a growing trend concerning the management of domestic ap-
pliances, paving the way to a new generation of smart homes. These buildings are
characterised by automated and intelligent monitoring and control of their electrical
appliances. Within this paradigm shift, energy eﬃciency, lower bill and proliferation
of clean and renewable energy sources are promised. As illustrated in Figure 2.2,
HEMSs are composed of interconnected components in continuous communication,
such as smart appliances, user interfaces and a central platform. Sensing devices are
used to measure physical quantities, either from the environment or individual smart
appliances. The latter possess computing and communication capabilities to enable
remote monitoring and control of their operation. This supervision is carried out by
the central platform, which coordinates household loads according to speciﬁc criteria,
usually via an optimisation approach.
HEMSs operate in one of two ways: (i) seeking to reduce the overall demand,
by turning oﬀ unnecessary loads or promoting more energy-eﬃcient appliances; or
(ii) ﬁnding adequate periods in which appliance operation would reduce the weekly
electricity bill, increase the penetration of RESs, or other criteria. The popularity
of consumption shifting in residential buildings is undeniable, and is most notori-
ously carried out in the form of DSM and DR programs. HEMS appliance scheduling
requires proper coordination of demand with other customers and the grid. This
is typically ensured by two-way communication devices, such as smart meters, and
achieved via centralised or distributed approaches. In centralised-based management,
a single entity is responsible for coordinating the demand across the grid. This entity
leverages relevant customer sensor data, appliance operation needs, and constraints.
As problem data is gathered in a single entity, the optimality of the computed solu-
tions is, generally, guaranteed. Nonetheless, concerns regarding the underlying high

Chapter 2. State of the Art 22
computational load, associated with solving large-scale non-linear constrained optimi-
sation problems, and privacy issues, due to the exchange of private information over
the grid, are noted. In the case of distributed approaches, cooperation between inde-
pendent decision-makers addresses the above mentioned computational burden issue.
Moreover, as the original problem is split across multiple decision-makers, the ex-
change of conﬁdential data is smaller in volume and frequency. Nevertheless, the fact
that only incomplete problem informations available does not allow for the optimality
of the underlying solutions to be guaranteed.
2.3 Household Appliances
Residential and non-residential buildings are composed of several electrical devices,
with individual characteristics and needs in terms of consumption and usage proﬁles.
In order to address the resulting increasing heterogeneity of devices, HEMSs need to
cope with changing requirements and new challenges. In this subsection, household
electrical appliances are discussed from the point of view of the HEMS management.
Several categories for electrical appliances are proposed, and the appliances most
commonly managed byHEMSs are identiﬁed, basedon recent surveys of theliterature.
Within smart appliances managed by HEMSs, particular attention is given to electric
vehicles (EVs) for their recent, and growing, popularity. Finally, current decision-
making trends carried out to address residential load uncertainties are also covered.
2.3.1 Appliance Categorisation
Many distinct categorisations of household electrical appliances can be performed,
namely based on their dimension, use frequency, protection against electric shocks,
just to name out a few. Within this section, and by extension the remainder of this
document, electrical appliances are categorised based on how they are managed by a
HEMS. In essence, any given appliance may or may not support the coordination of
its operation by a HEMS, as suggested by (Batista and Batista, 2018). In the pro-
posed categorisation, controllable appliances are distinguished fromnon-controllable
ones. Controllable appliances are electrical devices whose operation can be scheduled
over a given time horizon by a HEMS. On the other hand,non-controllable appli-
ances do not contemplate such operation coordination. Broader categorisations have,
nonetheless, been considered to address distinct appliance operation cycles and, con-
sequently, HEMS management. In (Beaudin and Zareipour, 2015), household devices
are categorised in the following classes:
(a) Uncontrollable loads – whose operation can in no way be modiﬁed or rescheduled
by a HEMS. This class comprehends appliances that provide an added value to
residents, and which are, sometimes, completely controlled by them. This is the
case, e.g., of tv sets, computers or sound systems.
(b) Curtailable loads – whose energy consumption can be adjusted mid-operation,
usually by tuning appliance settings, with no subsequent compensation and little
impact to the comfort of the residents. One example of such appliances concerns
dimming indoor artiﬁcial illuminance as a function of daylighting.
(c) Uninterruptible loads – once started, must run a complete cycle. Hence, ap-
pliances considered in this class only allow their corresponding starting time to
be rescheduled by either the HEMS or residents. Common examples include
dishwasher, clothes washing or dryer machines.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 23
(d) Interruptible loads – whose operation can be interrupted at any time, and subse-
quently resumed. It is common for a HEMS to model appliances of this class as
devices with constant consumption, easing the formalisation and solution of the
underlying scheduling problems. Common examples of such appliances include
rechargeable devices like plug-in hybrid EVs.
(e) Regulating loads – which must maintain their operation state as close as possible
toagivenreferencevalue, usuallydeﬁnedbytheresidentsortheHEMS.Heating,
ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) systems are examples of appliances
commonly considered in this class.
(f) Energy Storage – used to store energy for subsequent use when needed, com-
prising appliances such as external batteries.
Aposteriorcategorisationproposedin(Zhouetal.,2016)distinguishes schedulable,
whose operation can be modiﬁed or scheduled by a HEMS, fromnon-schedulable appli-
ances. In addition, authors of (Samadi et al., 2013; Batista and Batista, 2018) propose
analogous controllable and non-controllable classes, while elastic and inelastic loads
are likewise proposed in (Bayram and Ustun, 2017). This variety of categorisation
schemes is presented to illustrate that no categorisation scheme is globally accepted.
Moreover, this lack of consensus is also present among followers of the categorisation
scheme proposed in (Beaudin and Zareipour, 2015), which diverge on which appliances
to be assigned to each class. Refrigerators are a common example of these divergences,
due to their relatively short cycling characteristics. At hourly resolution, their oper-
ation cannot be adjusted, and thus they are regarded as uncontrollable devices (Yu
et al., 2013; Hassan et al., 2013). On the other hand, at lower time resolutions, they
can be classiﬁed as either interruptible (Adika and Wang, 2014a,c) or uninterruptible
loads (Samadi et al., 2013; Silvente and Papageorgiou, 2017), depending on whether
or not their consumption is adjustable. Finally, they can also be considered as regu-
lating loads, as they seek to maintain their indoor temperature as close as possible to
a predeﬁned reference value.
In (Leitão et al., 2020), an exhaustive list of appliances most commonly controlled
by a HEMS is presented. In this list, the clothes washing, tumble dryer, dishwasher,
plug-in EVs and air conditioning units stand out from other appliances equally impor-
tant for comfort and lifestyle, such as personal computers and various entertainment
systems. As stated in (Leitão et al., 2020), this ﬁnding is expected as these appliances
represent a high share of household power consumption.
2.3.2 Electric Vehicle
Inthelastfewyears, EVshavebeenthesubjectofsigniﬁcantandgrowinginterestfrom
academia and practitioners alike (Saldaña et al., 2019), providing a more eﬃcient and
less polluting alternative to conventional internal combustion engines (ICEs) (Poul-
likkas, 2015). The environmental beneﬁts of EVs constitute one of the main drivers of
its wide adoption in urban communities, along with novel transportation paradigms
such as carsharing (Liao et al., 2020).
A large-scale adoption of EVs raises important challenges, with respect to the man-
agement of the power grid infrastructure, supply and stability (Saldaña et al., 2019).
Given the substantial power requirements of EVs, their wide adoption is expected to
substantially increase customer demand and grid stress, in particular peak demand.
In (Carmichael et al., 2021), the eﬀects of unmanaged EV charging in residential de-
mand are predicted to almost double the household evening peak demand. Naturally,
if carried out simultaneously across diﬀerent households, unmanaged EV charging

Chapter 2. State of the Art 24
poses a threat to grid supply and its operational stability. Among the proposed ap-
proaches to address these challenges, the reinforcement of the grid infrastructure and
the development of enhanced EV charging mechanisms are highlighted in (Soares
et al., 2018). The reinforcing the grid infrastructure often represents expensive and
intrusive interventions, and is carried out to enable the grid to increase their power
generation and transmission. Conversely, novel EV charging mechanisms seek not
only to optimise EV charging times, but also to exploit their batteries as small-scale
storage and generator devices. The main diﬀerence between grid infrastructure rein-
forcements and novel EV charging mechanisms is concerned with the ﬂow of electricity
between the grid and the vehicle, which is classiﬁed as unidirectional in the former
case, and bidirectional in the latter (Saldaña et al., 2019).
Unidirectional EV Charging
In unidirectional EV charging, EVs are treated as regular household electrical ap-
pliances. When the vehicle needs to be charged, it is plugged into an outlet which
provides it with an unidirectional ﬂow of electricity, from the grid to the vehicle. As
with other electrical appliances, unidirectional EV charging can be further split into
uncontrolled or controlled charging (Leitão et al., 2020). Uncontrolled, or “dumb”,
charging follows the traditional use of any domestic appliance, where residents de-
termine when, and for how long, the vehicle is charged. In this situation, the grid
has no prior knowledge of EVs charging cycles, and is more susceptible to unexpected
demand peaks, in particular when multiple vehicles are simultaneously plugged-in.
On the other hand, in controlled, or smart, charging, EV charging is coordinated with
other appliances and the grid, in order to minimise peak demand and grid stress.
In (Saldaña et al., 2019), unidirectional EV charging is categorised into centralised
and decentralised. In centralised methodologies, charging of diﬀerent EVs is coordi-
nated by a single entity, which aggregates information from diﬀerent devices and
locations throughout the grid, and optimises the charging of the EV at a global level.
On the other hand, in decentralised EV charging, each EV is responsible for the co-
ordination of its charging cycles with other EVs. This task is accomplished by an
EV charging management system. Centralised EV charging solutions can be further
categorised into (Leitão et al., 2020): (i) aggregator-based, (ii) distributor system op-
erator (DSO) based, and (iii) multi-agent-based. While in aggregator and DSO-based
strategies, vehicles from users with common interests are managed by the same entity,
in multi-agent-based strategies, groups of independent decision-makers are considered,
being the charging and discharging of EVs coordinated to maximise individual goals,
with minimal impact on the preferences of each decision-maker, and in the overall
stability of the grid infrastructure.
Bidirectional EV Charging
Unlike in unidirectional EV charging, bidirectional EV charging considers electricity
ﬂowfromthegridtothevehicleandvice-versa. ThisparadigmextendstheroleofEVs,
from another electrical appliance to mobile energy storage systems and generators, as
they are permitted to inject power into the grid by discharging their batteries. EVs
can, therefore, be exploited to accommodate unexpected peaks in customer demand
and drops in generation output, the latter often resulting from the high variability
of renewable generation. When grid generation is suddenly required to increase, in
order to accommodate sudden demand-side peaks, EVs can be used as local generators
which supply the required demand. By doing so, customer demand is ﬂattened, and

Chapter 2. State of the Art 25
generation stress is reduced. Conversely, unexpected grid generation surpluses can
also be accommodated by not fully charged EVs, parked at residential buildings or
other appropriate locations.
In (Saldaña et al., 2019), bidirectional EV charging is categorised into vehicle-
to-grid (V2G), vehicle-to-building (V2B) and vehicle-to-home (V2H). In this cate-
gorisation, V2G works as previously described, with available EVs exploited as both
small-scale storage systems and microgenerators. Both V2B and V2H are presented
as variants of V2G, only restricted to a given building or home. EV charging and
discharging can also be coordinated via centralised or decentralised solutions, with
aggregator-based, DSO-based and multi-agent-based being, again, considered in the
same way as in unidirectional EV charging. Finally, proper billing mechanisms must
also be established, in order to compensate individual customers, in the case of EV
grid injection, and the grid, in the case of EV charging.
2.3.3 Residential Load Uncertainty
Load management and appliance coordination, performed by a HEMS, are strongly
inﬂuenced by the baseline power demand and other external factors, such as the local
microgeneration and the utility grid prices. Considering that the true future values of
demand and generation cannot be predicted without error, appliance coordination is
performedinthepresenceofuncertaininformation. Themainsourcesofuncertaintyin
a HEMS are: (i) baseline power demand; (ii) appliance operation needs; (iii) appliance
consumption; (iv) local microgeneration; and (v) utility grid prices. One possible
way to address these uncertainties consists in completely removing them from the
decision-making process. In this case, load planning is entirely based on current and
past data. On the other hand, stochastic, robust, chance-constrained, (stochastic)
dynamic programming, (stochastic) fuzzy optimisation (Beaudin and Zareipour, 2015)
and machine learning-based (Molina-Solana et al., 2017; Sutton and Barto, 2018;
Powell, 2019) approaches have been highlighted for their explicit incorporation of
uncertain information within the decision-making process.
Concerning stochastic programming, problem uncertainties are modelled as ran-
dom variables, and the expected value of the objective function of the problem is
minimised. On its turn, the solutions of a robust optimisation problem must be feasi-
ble for uncertainty realisations with stronger (worse) problem impact. The deﬁnition
of probabilistic or chance constraints leads to solutions complying with the problem
constraints with a certain probability level. As such, these constraints only need
to be veriﬁed for a (usually substantial) subset of uncertainty realisations. Stochas-
tic dynamic programming is closely related with multistage stochastic programming,
computing an optimal solution for the expected value of the objective function of
the problem, under a probabilistic state-space model. Finally, in stochastic fuzzy
optimisation, uncertainties are replaced with non-crisp (fuzzy) values, in order to
compute approximate solutions, with a certain conﬁdence level. Among these tech-
niques, stochastic programming and robust optimisation emerge as the two most pop-
ular (Maggioni et al., 2015; Ning and You, 2019; Kazemzadeh et al., 2019), followed
by chance-constrained programming, which is often integrated in both frameworks.
With respect to machine learning-based techniques, the reinforcement learning
(RL)frameworkhasbeenproventobeextremelyeﬀective, inproblemswheredecisions
must be repeatedly made in the presence of uncertain information. As an example,
this technique has recently been shown to consistently beat top players not only in
one the most challenging board games – Go (Silver et al., 2016), characterised by
an enormous search space and diﬃculty in evaluating individual board positions and

Chapter 2. State of the Art 26
moves – but also in the highly complex and dynamic Starcraft game (Vinyals et al.,
2019) – where information perceived by each player is imperfect.
The remainder of this subsection further elaborates on the key ideas of highlighted
mathematical optimisation-based techniques, i.e. stochastic programming, robust op-
timisation and chance-constrained programming. As for RL-based techniques, addi-
tional discussion is presented in Section 2.4.1. The reader is directed to referenced
literature for an in-depth coverage of each technique.
Stochastic Programming
Decision-making via stochastic programming-based approaches is carried out for the
expected value of the objective function, under probabilistic modelling of the uncer-
tainties of the problem, described by random variables (Chen et al., 2013). Thus,
ﬁtting a probability distribution to a given process is crucial for stochastic program-
ming, and generally represents a high computational burden (Maggioni et al., 2015).
This is often exacerbated by the fact that the uncertainty sources are commonly based
on real-world observations, which follow no particular distribution and for which the
collection of large volumes of data is diﬃcult. In (2.1), a generic formulation of a
stochastic programming problem is presented (de Mello and Bayraksan, 2014).
min
x∈X
E (G0 (x, ϵ))
subject to
E (Gk (x, ϵ))≤ 0, k= 1,...K s
(2.1)
whereGk denotes an extended real-valued function, which takes as inputs the decision
vector x ∈ X ⊆Rnx and a randomly distributed uncertainty vector ϵ ∈ Ξ, for
k = 1,...,K s. It is assumed that ϵ has a known distributionP, and expectations
E(·) in (2.1) are well-deﬁned, computed with respect to the distribution ofϵ, and
ﬁnite for allx∈X . Finally, the functionsGk (x, ϵ) may not depend onϵ, at which
point a deterministic constraintGk (x)≤ 0 is deﬁned, for a givenk = 1,...,K s.
In some cases, namely when the distributionP is discrete, analytical expressions
for the expectations deﬁned in both the objective function and the constraints of (2.1)
can be derived. As a result, deterministic problem formulations can be obtained, and
addressed by existing solvers. In most real-world cases, the probability distribution
P, which describes uncertainty sources, is either continuous or has an extremely large
number of realisations. In these cases, it can be impossible, or extremely computa-
tionally expensive, to compute the above expectation.
In the related literature, sampling techniques have emerged to approximate the
probability distributionP and, consequently, the above expectations (de Mello and
Bayraksan, 2014). Within sampling techniques, interior and exterior sampling ap-
proaches are highlighted. In interior sampling techniques, the sampling procedure is
explicitly integrated in the algorithms employed to solve the stochastic programming
problem, e.g. stochastic branch and bound. On the contrary, in exterior sampling
techniques, these two procedures are decoupled. Monte Carlo sampling is one of the
most popular examples. In (2.2), a Monte Carlo sampling-based approximation of the
expectation E (Gk (x, ϵ)) is presented (de Mello and Bayraksan, 2014).

in replacing the original problem described in (2.1) with a sample approximation,
is often referred to as thesample average approximation (SAA) method (de Mello
and Bayraksan, 2014). In SAA, a large number of samples is desired, in order to
improve the underlying approximation. Sampling a large number of samples may
represent a computational bottleneck, in particular if the sampling is carried out from
conditional distributions. Authors of (Chinchilla and Hespanha, 2019) address these
limitations by proposing a methodology to construct bounds for the expected value of
scalar functions of random vectors. As such, the minimisation of the original expected
value is replaced by the minimisation of the computed bounds. As explicit integration
or sampling is not required in the proposed method, the computational eﬃciency of
common solvers can still be exploited to determine problem solutions.
Even though the convergence of SAA has been extensively studied, in some cases,
it may be more advantageous to adopt an iterative or sequential sampling approach,
such as the stochastic approximation, in which optimisation and sampling procedures
are alternated. Most sequential approaches to stochastic programming problems con-
sider that decisions are made iteratively, and accompanied by state observations that
reveal the realisations of the uncertainty variables. Such problems are referred to
as two-stage stochastic programming problems, although they can be generalised to
multistage problems. Their underlying idea is that some decisions need to be made
prior to an observation of the current state of the environment, while others can
be postponed until after the uncertainty has been unfolded. The former comprise
the ﬁrst-stage decisions, whereas the latter correspond to the second-stage decisions.
In (2.4), the classical formulation of two-stage linear stochastic programming problems
is presented (Prékopa, 1995).

ϵ become available. After the realisations of ϵ have became available, recourse or
second-stage decisionsy are taken in order to adjust the behaviour of the system to
the observed uncertainty realisations.
Robust Optimisation
Robust optimisation approaches diﬀer from their stochastic counterparts, in the sense
that they make no prior assumptions concerning the uncertainties of the problem and
how they are modelled. Under robust optimisation frameworks, problem uncertain-
ties are assumed to belong to convex and bounded deterministic uncertainty sets.
Robust optimisation problems seek to ﬁnd solutions whose feasibility and optimality
are guaranteed for all uncertainty realisations within the deﬁned uncertainty set, while
achieving the best possible performance for worst-case realisations (Maggioni et al.,
2015; Sözüer and Thiele, 2016). The most general form for robust convex optimisation
is deﬁned in (2.6).
min
x∈X
f0 (x,u)
subject to
fi (x,u)∀u∈U , i= 1,...,m
(2.6)
where u represents uncertain variables belonging to the uncertainty setU, f0 (x,u)
the objective function, andfi (x,u) constraints of the problem, withi = 1,...,m . De-
cision variables are represented by a vectorx, with the setX representing the possible
values of this vector. Given that, in robust optimisation, feasible solutions are found
for every realisation inu∈U , and are optimal in the worst case, the mathematical
formulation in (2.6) leads to the so-calledmin-max robust approach (Buchheim and
Kurtz, 2016). Thismin-max robust optimisation problem is formulated in (2.7).

Robust optimisation was originally proposed to address decision-making in the
presence of uncertain information, in problems where probabilistic models are either
unavailable or intractable, or in problems in which infeasibility is not tolerable (Li and
Floudas, 2012; Sözüer and Thiele, 2016). In fact, this is the case in many problems
dealing with real-world data, for which reliable and reasonable estimates of probability

Chapter 2. State of the Art 29
distributions are hard to obtain, rendering the application of stochastic programming
techniques less attractive (Maggioni et al., 2015).
In comparison to stochastic programming, robust optimisation privileges solutions
with worse performance. The reason for this conservatism is due to the focus on
worst-case scenario realisations, penalising solution performance in the presence of less
extreme uncertainty realisations. On the other hand, robust optimisation approaches
often have a smaller computational load than their stochastic counterparts. This is
mainlyduetothefactthatmanystochasticprogrammingproblemsrequireintegration
over multiple dimensions, or approximation through large numbers of scenarios, which
results in high-dimension problems with high computational burden.
Within robust optimisation, the deﬁnition of the uncertainty set plays a crucial
role in problem tractability, solution feasibility and performance. Indeed, choosing
an extremely large uncertainty set can lead to over-conservative solutions, whereas
another that is excessively small may produce non-robust solutions (Duchi, 2018).
The structure of the uncertainty set, and its impact on the underlying problem, has
ben extensively studied in (Bertsimas and Sim, 2004; Ben-Tal et al., 2004; Li and
Floudas, 2012; Maggioni et al., 2015), with box, ellipsoidal, polyhedral sets, and their
respective intersections, having been discussed in (Li and Floudas, 2012).
The min-max problem, formalised in (2.7), comprises a traditional or static robust
optimisation problem, in which all decisions are made at once (“here-and-now”), prior
to any uncertainty realisation (Ning and You, 2019). However, such framework is
not well-suited for sequential decision-making problems, for which adaptive robust
optimisation (ARO) has been proposed. ARO has promoted two-stage and, more
generically, multistage adaptive robust problems, described in (2.8).

wherexcomprises ﬁrst-stage (“here-and-now”) decisions andyrepresents second-stage
(“wait-and-see”) decisions, the latter made after uncertainty realisations have been
observed. Moreover, cTx and bTy deﬁne, respectively, ﬁrst and second-stage costs,
while ˆAx≥b speciﬁes system dynamics, andΩ(x,u) restricts second-stage decisions.
Similarly to two-stage stochastic programming problems, second-stage decisions are
carried out in order to address feasibility issues and adjust system behaviour.
Chance Constraints
Chance-constrained optimisation is another powerful and widely employed decision-
making technique, in the presence of uncertain information. Similarly to stochastic
programming, uncertainties are described by random variables modelled by probabil-
ity distribution functions (Ning and You, 2019). In chance constraint programming,
probabilistic constraints ensure a minimum threshold for the probability of meeting
certain constraints. The generic formulation of a chance-constrained optimisation
problem is presented in (2.9).

Chapter 2. State of the Art 30
min

p (Gi (x, ϵ)≤ 0)≥ 1−θ, i= 1,...,m
(2.9)
where x∈X⊆ Rnx are decision variables,ϵ∈ Ξ is an uncertainty vector of random
variables, θ is a pre-speciﬁed risk level parameter, and probabilitiesp (Gi (x, ϵ)≤ 0)
are computed with respect to the distribution ofϵ. In (2.9), a single chance constraint
is deﬁned for each condition of interest, being represented by functionsGi (x, ϵ), for
i = 1,...,m . Alternatively, joint chance constraints can also be considered, deﬁn-
ing a threshold for the probability of meeting several constraints simultaneously, i.e.
p (Gi (x, ϵ)≤ 0, i= 1,...,m ), wherem is the number of simultaneous constraints.
Chance-constrained programming models are, in some cases, computationally in-
tractable. This is due to the calculations of individual probabilities, which require the
computation of multivariate integrals. These challenges motivated the development
of solution algorithms such as SAA (Luedtke and Ahmed, 2008), convex conservative
approximation schemes (Nemirovski and Shapiro, 2007), and sequential approxima-
tion (Hong et al., 2011). Furthermore, in (Charnes and Cooper, 1963), a reformula-
tion of probabilistic constraints is proposed for speciﬁc classes of convex programming
problems, exploiting mathematical manipulation and properties of probability density
functions (PDFs) to derive deterministic equivalent models of the original constraints.
The resulting equivalent problems are solved by common approaches, as demonstrated
in (van Ackooij et al., 2011; Grosso et al., 2014; Nikzad et al., 2019).
2.3.4 Summary
HEMSs propose to improve household energy rationalisation, bill reduction or other
criteria, through continuous monitoring and management of household demand and
appliances. Even though a uniﬁed categorisation of household appliances is not con-
sensual from a HEMS management perspective, two major types of appliances can
be identiﬁed. They include those supporting remote HEMS control and management,
named controllable appliances, and those whose operation cannot be inﬂuenced in any
way, comprising non-controllable appliances. Controllable appliances can be further
split into curtailable, regulating, uninterruptible loads and energy storage systems.
Supported by a recent literature review, washing machines, EVs and HVAC systems
have been identiﬁed as being more appealing for smart management and scheduling,
due to their operation mode, typical long program duration and power demand.
In recent years, growing research and general interest in EVs, and its related tech-
nologies, have emerged from academia and practitioners alike. In light of more eﬃcient
and environment-friendly alternatives to conventional ICEs, substantial penetration
of EVs in human lifestyles is expected. The considerable power requirements of EVs
pose serious challenges to the management and stability of the power grid infras-
tructure. In particular, as EVs are adopted at larger scales, customer demand and
grid stress are, inevitably, expected to increase. Interventions on the grid infrastruc-
ture and enhancements to EV charging mechanisms are highlighted to address such
challenges. While the former comprehends expensive and intrusive interventions, the
latter seeks to schedule EV charging in an optimal way, taking advantage of EVs
batteries to accommodate peaks in customer-side demand or grid generation. Within
these strategies, unidirectional charging is distinguished from bidirectional charging,
based on the ﬂow of electricity between the grid and a vehicle.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 31
HEMSs coordinate demand-side loads over a given future horizon, for which base-
line demand and generation can only be forecasted, and not be accurately determined.
In typical household scenarios, the main sources of uncertainty inﬂuencing the oper-
ation of a HEMS include the baseline power demand, the consumption of individual
appliances and their operation needs, the local power generation and the utility grid
prices. Naturally, the achieved energy rationalisation and bill reduction are strongly
inﬂuenced by how well these uncertainty sources can be modelled and forecasted.
This has motivated further exploration of proper mechanisms, in order to address
uncertainties in demand and generation. Within optimisation-based scheduling ap-
proaches, uncertainty is commonly addressed via stochastic programming or robust
optimisation. Stochastic programming requires probabilistic uncertainty modelling,
as decision-making is carried out for the expected criterion value. On the other hand,
in robust optimisation, a more conservative approach is adopted. In this case, prob-
lem uncertainties are modelled by convex bounded sets, and optimal solutions target
worst-case realisations, i.e. with worse impact to the problem. In addition to stochas-
tic programming and robust optimisation, chance-constrained programming has also
been highlighted. Probabilistic constraints are deﬁned to ensure that the probability
of meeting a given constraint is above a certain threshold. Thus, probabilistic uncer-
tainty modelling is also required for chance-constrained programming. The robustness
of the obtained solutions, with respect to the uncertainty realisations, can be increased
by considering higher thresholds for the probabilistic constraints. Nevertheless, the
underlying problems are often diﬃcult and computationally expensive to solve.
2.4 Appliance Coordination
In section 2.2.2, consumption shifting and reduction are distinguished among predom-
inant strategies to improve building energy rationalisation and reduce overall bill. The
popularity of consumption shifting is highlighted, according to which customer-side
power demand is managed by coordinating the operation of household electrical ap-
pliances. While consumption reduction is focused on reducing the overall demand,
consumption shifting searches for periods of time during which electrical energy can
be eﬃciently used. In this process, consumption shifting seeks to improve a given
criterion, usually the electricity bill. As such, scheduling techniques pose as natural
choices for household appliance coordination. Prior to their adoption and deploy-
ment in real-world scenarios, critical choices concerning which devices are managed,
scheduling criteria, constraints and techniques to employ must be made. This sec-
tion addresses appliance coordination by means of scheduling techniques, covering
preponderant techniques, criteria and constraints.
2.4.1 Scheduling Techniques
In the context of consumption shifting, appliance coordination can be seen from an
optimisation perspective. According to this viewpoint, the operation of each device is
planned over a future time horizon, by solving an instance of an optimisation prob-
lem, in which given criteria are minimised, subjected to physical and operational
constraints. Modelling of demand from non-controllable electrical devices, and local
microgeneration, if available, can also be considered in appliance operation planning.
Load shifting techniques can be classiﬁed according to ﬁve main categories, namely (i)
mathematical optimisation, (ii) heuristic-based, (iii) MPC, (iv) game theory, and (v)
machine learning (Al-Sumaiti et al., 2014; Shaikh et al., 2014; Beaudin and Zareipour,
2015; Bayram and Ustun, 2017; Molina-Solana et al., 2017).

Chapter 2. State of the Art 32
Mathematical Optimisation
LP problems are the most predominant mathematical optimisation technique consid-
ered for demand-side load coordination. They express scheduling criteria, objective
function and constraints by linear relationships, exploiting established algorithms (e.g.
branch and bound, simplex or interior point) to solve the individual problem instances,
with relatively low computational load. Common LP solvers and software packages
include GLPK8, CPLEX9 and GAMS10.
The discretisation of the time domain into equal-length slots is a common practice
among mathematical optimisation techniques (Ding et al., 2016; Chakraborty et al.,
2018). Vectors of binary decision variables, specifying the operation status of indi-
vidual devices during each slot of the horizon, are commonly deﬁned in this case. In
the resulting binary LP problem instances, a value of 1 in a given position of the
vector of decision variables signals that the appliance in question is working during
the corresponding slot. Conversely, a value of 0 in a given position of the vector of
decision variables means that the appliance is turned oﬀ. Integer variables can also be
considered, instead of binary vectors. In this case, the slot in which a given load starts
or ﬁnishes is speciﬁed. In the context of household appliance scheduling, real-valued
variables can also be considered, e.g. to specify the operation setting or intensity of a
given load. If both real-valued and integer decision variables are deﬁned, the resulting
problem is referred to as a mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) problem.
Time domain discretisation promotes less ﬂexible operation of controllable appli-
ances, see e.g. (Chen et al., 2013; Leitão et al., 2018c), as their start and end times are
restricted to multiples of the slot unit. By contrast, in continuous time formulations,
see e.g. (Hendrickx et al., 2014; Pinedo, 2016; Nagananda and Khargonekar, 2017),
the start or ﬁnish of a new controllable appliance load is modelled via real-valued
variables. In continuous time formulations, controllable appliance can, therefore, be
scheduled for any instant of the horizon. A larger number of problem variables is,
therefore, usually deﬁned in discrete time formulations, which contributes to the in-
crease of the underlying computational burden. Nonetheless, problem reformulations,
seeking to reduce the overall number of decision variables and constraints, have also
been studied (Ahmad et al., 2019a).
In (Amini et al., 2015), scheduling of domestic controllable appliances, from dis-
tinct customers in a residential compound, is considered via linear mathematical opti-
misation problems. The proposed optimisation problem results from a reformulation
of an original problem, and is carried out to reduce the high computational load of
standard optimisation methods. In (Silvente and Papageorgiou, 2017), day-ahead
(DA) management of power demand, wind generation and a storage system in a mi-
crogrid, is carried out via MILP-based mathematical optimisation, with the goal of
minimising the operation costs. In (Yahia and Pradhan, 2018), DA scheduling of a
total of 20 domestic appliances is performed, in order to minimise the household elec-
tricity bill and the resident discomfort. In (Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani, 2019),
a multi-objective MILP is employed for demand-side load management in a scenario
with multiple residential customers. In this work, the presence of battery ESSs is
considered. A total of 6 diﬀerent demand scenarios are simulated to investigate the
beneﬁts resulting from the integration of a battery for energy storage. In (Yahia
and Pradhan, 2020), DA scheduling of a total of 9 household appliances is carried
8https://www.gnu.org/software/glpk, accessed on: 06 September 2021.
9https://www.ibm.com/analytics/cplex-optimizer, accessed on: 06 September 2021.
10https://www.gams.com, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 33
out, based on collected real-world appliance operation data. A multi-objective for-
mulation is considered, minimising electricity bill, scheduling inconvenience and peak
load. In (Hammou Ou Ali et al., 2020), MILP problems are considered in DA ap-
pliance scheduling. In this work, electricity bill and customer discomfort criteria are
combined into a single objective function.
The works (Amini et al., 2015; Silvente and Papageorgiou, 2017; Yahia and Prad-
han, 2018, 2020; Hammou Ou Ali et al., 2020) share a common drawback concerning
the absence of non-controllable load modelling. By not taking into consideration the
non-controllable demand, the determined controllable appliance schedules may coin-
cide with peaks in non-controllable load. Such load deferment, based on incomplete
demand information, can produce sub-optimal performance. Moreover, local power
outages can also occur, as a result of violations of the contracted power limit. On its
turn, in(Leitãoetal.,2021), smarthomeelectricalappliancesarescheduledoveraone-
week horizon considering that non-controllable power and non-potable water demand
are modelled by a single representative proﬁle, which biases the resulting appliance
scheduling and performance. These limitations are addressed, e.g., in (Nan et al.,
2019), where a scenario-based modelling of uncertainties in non-controllable demand
is employed. In this work, a MILP-based approach is employed for DA scheduling of a
clothes washing, tumble dryer, air-conditioning, electric vehicle and light equipment.
Local energy storage and PV microgeneration are also considered, being allocation
carried out to reduce grid demand and daily electricity bill.
Within mathematical optimisation-based techniques, the scheduling criteria, ob-
jective function and constraints can also be expressed by non-linear relationships. In
comparisontoLP,morecomplexprobleminstancescanbemodelledbyNLPproblems,
but also at a higher computation cost. Binary and mixed-integer non-linear program-
ming (MINLP) problems can also be deﬁned, as for LP problems. Among common
NLP solvers, SCIP (Gleixner et al., 2018a,b), GAMS and LINGO11 are highlighted.
In (Stluka et al., 2011), demand and supply-side building infrastructures, in-
tegrated within a microgrid system, are managed via a MINLP-based approach.
At supply-side, unit commitment and economic dispatch problems are considered,
whereas the customer electricity bill is optimised. In (Althaher et al., 2015), DA
scheduling of deferrable, curtailable, thermal and critical domestic appliances is car-
ried out. Appliance operation times are determined by solving instances of MINLP
problems through an iterative rolling horizon procedure, carried out to account for
changes in utility tariﬀs. Diﬀerent case studies are considered, demonstrating the ef-
fectiveness of the proposed approach in minimising the electricity bill, while preserving
the comfort of the residents. In (Setlhaolo and Xia, 2016), demand-side load schedul-
ing and coordination of domestic controllable appliances are carried out among ﬁve
residential customers, with dedicated PV generation, storage systems and TOU tariﬀs.
In the cited work, a non-linear mathematical optimisation-based approach is adopted,
in order to minimise the customer electricity bill and the grid power consumption.
In addition to linear and non-linear programming techniques, dynamic program-
ming (DP) has also been considered. In DP, the optimisation problem is structured
into multiple stages. Each stage covers a given time instant, for which sequential and
independent scheduling decisions are made. DP methods are often considered when
solving stochastic programming problems and Markov Decision Processes (MDPs),
the latter also being popular within RL frameworks (Powell, 2019).
Explicit uncertainty handling in mathematical optimisation techniques is often
considered via stochastic programming or robust optimisation formulations, as noted
11https://www.lindo.com, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 34
in Section 2.3.3. Robust optimisation is often considered in critical scenarios, for
which system response and operation must be ensured, even in the presence of most
extreme events. On the other hand, stochastic programming optimises the expected
value of the objective function of the problem, considering all possible realisations
of the uncertainty, before making a decision. In comparison to robust optimisation,
signiﬁcantly higher computational load is expected from stochastic programming, due
to exploring all possible uncertainty realisations.
In the context of home energy management, robust appliance allocation is biased
towards scenarios of high non-controllable demand and low microgeneration. On the
other hand, overall better performance in the presence of diﬀerent demand and gen-
eration realisations is provided by the stochastic programming-based allocation. For
example, in (Chen et al., 2012), DA appliance scheduling is performed by a rolling two-
stage scenario-based stochastic MILP, considering uncertain grid tariﬀs. Stochastic
MILP problem instances are, instead, adopted in (Javadi et al., 2020) for DA domestic
appliance scheduling, considering uncertain PV generation output. In (Wang et al.,
2015), violations of the household comfort are minimised by a robust LP-based ap-
proach, while DA household appliance planning is performed in (Akbari-Dibavar et al.,
2020) via a hybrid stochastic-robust optimisation approach, considering uncertain PV
generation and utility tariﬀs.
Heuristics
Heuristics and metaheuristics comprehend valuable alternatives to mathematical op-
timisation methods, in high-dimensional and computationally demanding problems.
They are often preferred over mathematical optimisation methods, in problem in-
stances for which exact solutions cannot be computed in feasible time. This is the
case of nondeterministic polynomial time (NP-complete) problems, which cannot be
eﬃciently solved, despite any solution being veriﬁable in realistic, polynomial, time.
Heuristics and metaheuristics rely on high-level procedures to sample admissible so-
lutions, sacriﬁcing accuracy, precision and optimality, in detriment of speed. More-
over, these techniques make very few assumptions concerning the underlying problem,
therefore being suited for a variety of problems.
In this class of methods, GAs and swarm intelligence-based techniques stand out
from other heuristic-based alternatives, in household appliance scheduling problems.
In (Talha et al., 2018), demand-side load shifting is performed in a residential building
via GA and an artiﬁcial ﬁsh swarm algorithm. Authors of the cited work schedule
demand-side loads with the goal of minimising electricity bill and peak-to-average
load ratio (PALR). In (Hu and Xiao, 2018), GA-based heuristic search of non-linear
optimisation problems is carried out to manage the temperature set-points of air-
conditioning units. A reduction in the electricity bill and the peak power is reported,
whilemeetingtheresidentthermalcomfortrequirements. In(Pamulapatietal.,2020),
an evolutionary algorithm (EA) is employed to compute solutions from the Pareto
frontier, in a multi-objective optimisation problem (MOOP). The authors propose to
minimise both the electricity bill and the resident dissatisfaction over a single-day
horizon, by means of managing interruptible, uninterruptible and regulating loads.
Concerning techniques based on swarm intelligence principles, particle swarm op-
timisation (PSO) is highlighted as the most predominant choice. In (Molavi and
Ardehali, 2016), management of a thermal energy storage unit in an industrial build-
ing is considered. In the cited work, the performance of GA and PSO techniques is
compared over a total of four days. In (Leitão et al., 2018c), PSO is employed for DA
scheduling of one clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machine, along with

Chapter 2. State of the Art 35
an electrical pump system, in a prototype smart home environment. The same tech-
nique is reported in (Lin and Hu, 2018), where DA scheduling of diﬀerent appliances is
performed. Concerning scheduling goals, the electricity bill, customer dissatisfaction
and carbon dioxide emissions are considered in the cited work. Finally, both PSO and
rainﬂow metaheuristic algorithms are employed in (Rashid et al., 2020), for DA load
scheduling of individual smart homes, in a smart grid community. The application of
other heuristic and metaheuristic algorithms has also been considered in household
appliance scheduling problems, namely artiﬁcial bee colony (Leitão et al., 2018a),
tabu search (Morganti et al., 2009), diﬀerential evolution (Essiet et al., 2019), grey
wolf optimisation (Iqbal et al., 2019) or the polar bear algorithm (Iqbal et al., 2020).
Model Predictive Control
Within the scope of MPC techniques, coordination of electrical loads from domestic
appliances is addressed as a receding horizon (RH) optimal control problem. In MPC-
based appliance scheduling, an iterative procedure is considered. At each discrete
time instant, an observation of the current state of the system is performed, and a
horizon-dependent optimal control sequence is computed. From the computed control
sequence, only the ﬁrst action is implemented on the system, at which point the
procedure is repeated for the next time instant. MPC-based smart home appliance
coordinationismainlyfocusedonthemanagementofthermostaticallyregulatedloads,
such as HVAC systems.
The main drawbacks of MPC-based load shifting include the stability of the un-
derlying closed loop system, and its resulting optimal control. Moreover, signiﬁcant
modelling costs are also noted, resulting from the need to acquire system data and for
expert monitoring. As a result, the application of MPC to medium and large buildings
is limited (Shaikh et al., 2014).
In (Godina et al., 2018a,b), MPC-based management of a HVAC unit is consid-
ered in a smart home, equipped with a set of PV panels for local microgeneration.
In the proposed MPC-based framework, two conﬂicting criteria are speciﬁed, namely
the electricity bill and the indoor temperature comfort, resulting in a multi-objective
MPC formulation. In (Bianchini et al., 2019), management of a HVAC system, lo-
cated within a smart building, is carried out via a MPC-based strategy. Within the
case study considered in this work, thermal and electrical storage facilities, along with
PV generation, are contemplated. Overall, a single-day horizon is considered in the
proposed MPC strategy, with HVAC management being carried out with the goal of
minimising the overall electricity bill. Conversely, in (Sabegh and Bingham, 2019),
MPC is employed to ensure the optimal scheduling of a domestic refrigerator equip-
ment. On the other hand, in (Nagpal et al., 2020; Li and Roche, 2020), this strategy
is considered to manage a variety of household appliances, and to coordinate power
demand from multiple customers, in a microgrid environment.
Game Theory
Game theory-based approaches are usually employed within multi-agent frameworks,
where individual and independent agents are considered. Each agent is responsible
for ﬁnding a strategy that improves a given individual utility function, while also
being conditioned by the individual strategies and objectives of other agents (Bayram
and Ustun, 2017). Cooperative and non-cooperative games comprise the two main
categories of game theory approaches. In cooperative game theory, individual agents
collaborate with each otherto reach a commongoal. Onthe other hand, self-interested
and non-collaborative agents are considered in non-cooperative games.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 36
Cooperative games are more suited for appliance coordination in contexts of multi-
ple consumers, considering the improvement of criteria such as overall demand, usage
of grid resources or social welfare, just to name out a few (Deng et al., 2014). Game
theory-based approaches can also be incorporated in existing DSM programmes, as
in (Mohsenian-Rad et al., 2010), in order to promote load ﬂattening and sustainable
and eﬃcient grid resource use. In (Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani, 2019), coopera-
tive game theory is proposed to minimise the household electricity bill and the system
peak demand. Within the proposed approach, load shifting is carried out by solving
a MINLP problem instance, incorporating uncertain renewable power generation and
residential hourly load demand. On its turn, in (Jamil et al., 2019), a game theory-
based approach is integrated with metaheuristic algorithms, for periodic rescheduling
and coordination of household appliances. In the referenced work, a single criterion is
considered, consisting in the minimisation of the electricity bill. In (Latiﬁ et al., 2019),
an event-triggered game-theoretic strategy is proposed for demand-side power man-
agement. DA coordination of a smart microgrid system is considered, being individual
agents developed to model customers and retailers. Individual agents collaborate to-
gether to minimise the service provider costs and the demand-side electricity bill.
Machine Learning
For many years, utility companies have heavily relied on traditional control systems
andphysicalmodelling, inorderto monitorand manage thegrid infrastructure (Zhang
et al., 2018). In recent years, the Internet of Things (IoT) and smart grid technological
advances, havebeenresponsibleforaconsiderableboostintheproliferationofmachine
learning techniques in DSM and DR programs. In the sequel of the IoT and smart grid
digital revolutions, a new generation of digital sensors, enhanced with computing and
communication capabilities, has been integrated in the existing grid infrastructure.
This enabled the collection of large volumes of data across the grid. On its turn, the
availability of large volumes of data has motivated the adoption of machine learning
and data science techniques. These techniques aim to extract new information from
collecteddata, andareexploitedtoimprovethemanagementofthegridinfrastructure,
and the overall demand-side experience. Within the context of building and grid
management, machine learning-based techniques have been suggested, in particular
for forecasting of the appliance scheduling and power demand (Molina-Solana et al.,
2017; Zhang et al., 2018).
Concerning the scheduling of electrical appliances in smart homes, artiﬁcial neu-
ral networks (ANNs) and RL are highlighted. Among ANN topologies, feedforward
architectures are the most popular (Leitão et al., 2020), determining the appliance
operation times based on forecasts of future demand and generation, time of day and
occupancy information. Individual ANN structures can be trained for each control-
lable appliance, as in (Matallanas et al., 2012), although a single ANN can also be
trained to coordinate multiple electrical appliances (Hernandez et al., 2010). On the
other hand, RL comprises the existence of intelligent agents inserted in a given envi-
ronment. Agents learn iteratively how to perform a given task. At each iteration, an
observation of the current state of the environment is performed, the agent decides on
which action to perform, based on its goals and perceived environmental state, and
receives a reward as a result of the taken action (Sutton and Barto, 2018).
Within RL frameworks, the deﬁnition of a policy is usually comprehended within
individual agents. The policy deﬁned for each agent determines which action is taken
by the agent, at any given perceived state. The ultimate goal of the agent is, there-
fore, to ﬁnd a policy that maximises the expected cumulative reward. In searching

Chapter 2. State of the Art 37
for the optimal policy, an agent may rely on environmental and action reward models,
distinguishing model-based from model-free RL algorithms (Sutton and Barto, 2018).
In model-based RL, a model of the environment dynamics is learned, usually a MDP,
along with state and action-value functions. The learned model of the environment,
state function and action-value functions enables the agent to choose, at each discrete
time step and based on its current perceived state, the action with highest expected
reward (Schrittwieser et al., 2020). On the other hand, model-free RL exploits dif-
ferent algorithms to iteratively approximate the state and action-value functions, and
consequently to choose future actions (Sutton and Barto, 2018).
In many works, preference for model-free RL is justiﬁed by the challenges associ-
ated with accurate modelling of the environment. Q-learning (Watkins and Dayan,
1992) is one of the most popular model-free RL algorithms, having been considered
in household load scheduling under uncertain power demand and generation, in both
collaborative and non-collaborative frameworks (Kim and Lim, 2018; Koﬁnas et al.,
2018). The scalability of Q-learning is somewhat limited, owed to its discretisation of
both the agent state and the action space. Novel approaches have been researched, in
order to address this shortcoming, among which deep RL (DRL) is highlighted. DRL
combines RL with deep learning techniques, in order to estimate non-linear action-
value functions in inﬁnite action spaces (Mnih et al., 2015). In (Lee and Choi, 2020),
a hierarchical DRL algorithm is employed for the scheduling of smart home appli-
ances, considering local PV microgeneration and a battery storage system, in order
to minimise the electricity bill and the customer discomfort.
Within DRL, deep Q-learning and deep policy gradient (DPG) algorithms are
highlighted (Arulkumaran et al., 2017). Deep Q-learning extends Q-learning by esti-
mating the action-value function through a deep ANN, see e.g. (Ji et al., 2019; Mocanu
et al., 2019). On the other hand, DPG exploits deep learning techniques to estimate
the policy of each agent, as in (Chung et al., 2020).
Finally, considering building power demand forecast, predictive models have been
developed to model either (i) demand from speciﬁc appliances, such as HVAC, or (ii)
from the entire building (Ahmad et al., 2018). Forecasting of building power demand
and renewable generation can be regarded as a time-series forecast problem (van der
Meer et al., 2018a; Chinmoy et al., 2019). Within this scope, the application of
the following machine learning-based approaches has been highlighted (Molina-Solana
et al., 2017; Zhang et al., 2018; van der Meer et al., 2018b; Bourdeau et al., 2019):
(a) Conventional methods– including techniques such as time-series decomposition,
autoregressive moving average (ARMA), autoregressive integrated moving av-
erage (ARIMA), multiple linear regression (Yun et al., 2012) or ordinary least
squares regression (Dagnely et al., 2015).
(b) ANNs – in particular feedforward (Biswas et al., 2016), convolutional (Amaras-
ingheetal.,2017;Zhangetal.,2018), recurrent(Marinoetal.,2016;Zhangetal.,
2018) and restricted boltzmann machine (Taylor et al., 2011; Zhang et al., 2018)
topologies. Recurrent ANNs, in particular long short-term memory (LSTM),
yield the best prediction accuracy across diﬀerent regression tasks, powered by
a large number of layers, which compose most state of the art deep ANNs. In
addition to being black-box models, the training of these high complex deep
ANN architectures is also characterised by substantial computational load.
(c) Capsule networks– relying on the concept of a capsule, capable of learning fea-
turesthatareinvarianttodeformationsandothertransformations(Hintonetal.,
2011; Sabour et al., 2017). Concerning building energy consumption, in (Kim

Chapter 2. State of the Art 38
et al., 2018; Leitão et al., 2018b), capsule networks are employed to predict fu-
ture demand, based on past consumption information and other relevant input
data, such as time information.
(d) Support vector machines(SVMs) – have been applied to solve building energy
forecast problems in the form of support vector regression (SVR), see e.g. (Xue-
mei et al., 2009; Massana et al., 2015; Paudel et al., 2017). In these cited works,
the underlying prediction performance is shown to be superior to that of tradi-
tional techniques, and even to some ANN-based data-driven models.
(e) Gaussian process regression– comprising a non-parametric method based on
GPs (Rasmussen and Williams, 2006). Within a GP-based framework, a time-
series is represented as a collection of random variables with a joint Gaussian
distribution, completely speciﬁed by mean and covariance functions. Given a
training dataset, GPs deﬁne a distribution of functions, commonly Gaussian
distributed, and passing through the observations at the training points. The
mentioned training data is also explored to tune the parameters of the model,
considering a set of parameter values which maximises the log marginal likeli-
hood of the training data (Shepero et al., 2018).
(f) Segmentation and regression techniques– instead of using all available data to
train a machine learning-based model, approaches of this nature identify recur-
rent patterns in the collected data, and ﬁt individual models to the samples
belonging to each pattern. Their motivation is that similar samples will most
likely belong to the same patterns. Stemming from this motivation, the resulting
machine learning-based models are expected to be easier to train when similar
data subsets are considered, instead of for the entire (possibly highly heteroge-
neous) dataset. In (Xishuang Dong et al., 2017), a K-means clustering algorithm
is used to segment electricity readings on a hourly basis. Subsequently, a con-
volution neural network (CNN) model is exploited to approximate each cluster,
while, in (Yildiz et al., 2018), household electricity loads are predicted by us-
ing classiﬁcation and regression trees, together with a self-organising map-based
data clustering technique.
(g) Model ensembles– seeking to take advantage of the individual strengths of the
diﬀerent techniques, while mitigating their drawbacks. Examples include ran-
dom forests and boosting decision trees (Bourdeau et al., 2019). In the last few
years, model ensembles have gained considerable interest in modelling and fore-
casting of building energy consumption. In some cases, model ensembles have
been reported to be capable of outperforming individual models. Nevertheless,
their adoption has been hampered in part due to their associated complexity
and computational burden.
2.4.2 Criteria
In the following, the most commonly considered household appliance scheduling cri-
teria are listed (Bayram and Ustun, 2017):
1. Electricity bill – the most common scheduling objective. Indeed, minimising
the electricity bill comprises the main motivation for residential consumers to
defer their electricity loads, while taking into account the available tariﬀs and
the renewable microgeneration (Chavali et al., 2014; Anvari-Moghaddam et al.,
2015; Wu et al., 2016; Marzband et al., 2017; Leitão et al., 2018c; Golmohamadi
et al., 2019a,b; Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani, 2019; Lampropoulos et al.,
2019; Lu et al., 2020).

Chapter 2. State of the Art 39
2. Distribution system losses— being responsible for the dissipation of a fraction
of the generated power, due to Joule eﬀects in power lines and other equipment
across the grid, namely transformers. A common approach to accommodate this
problem is to deploy diﬀerent generation sources along the power line, which are
regulated by means of an optimal dispatch strategy (Sortomme et al., 2011;
Deilami et al., 2011; Miceli, 2013).
3. Peak load– utility companies are often interested in encouraging their customers
to reduce their peak load demand, or even to achieve a given proﬁle, as it
beneﬁts the management of the grid infrastructure (Adika and Wang, 2014b;
Bamdad et al., 2017; Liao et al., 2018; Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani, 2019;
Ahmad et al., 2019b; Lu et al., 2020). To this end, utility companies often
deﬁne individual targets, expressed as PALR, for customer loads. PALRs are
considered to promote a balanced use of the power grid resources. The closer this
ratio is to one, the ﬂatter the consumption load is throughout the day (Samadi
et al., 2013; Liu et al., 2014b).
4. Carbon emission– HEMSs can plan the operation of household appliances with
the goal of minimising the emissions of carbon and other GHGs (Bakker et al.,
2010; Saber and Venayagamoorthy, 2011). As in (Ahmad et al., 2019b), penalty
fees can be charged to individual customers, based on their GHG emission levels,
to promote a global reduction of the pollutant emissions at household level.
5. Customer comfort– in addition to the coordination of appliance usage, carried
out to reduce the overall bill or the pollutant emissions, the customer comfort
and other preferences can also be taken into account. Comfort parameters are
usually incorporated into the underlying scheduling problems in the form of
constraints. Nevertheless, when taken as scheduling criteria, they are often
complemented by additional criteria, such as the electricity bill. In (Verhelst
et al., 2012), management of air-to-water heat pumps connected to a residential
ﬂoor heating system is carried out, taking into account appliance usage and
comfort parameters. Moreover, in (Anvari-Moghaddam et al., 2015), a multi-
objective MINLP model is developed for optimal energy use in a smart home,
ensuring a meaningful balance between energy saving and comfortable lifestyle.
6. Social welfare – regarded as the balance between consumer beneﬁts and their
corresponding costs (Bayram and Ustun, 2017). HEMSs considering this goal
aim to improve social welfare of a community of consumers at global scale (Li
et al., 2011; Chen et al., 2014; Chavali et al., 2014).
Over the past decades, ﬂat rate-based pricing tariﬀs have been adopted by utility
companies. According to these tariﬀs, customers are charged a given static rate per
unit of electrical energy consumed. Recently, owing to the advances on smart metering
technology, dynamic billing schemes have become increasingly preponderant. Overall,
the most predominant tariﬀs reported in relevant literature include (Rasheed et al.,
2015; Bayram and Ustun, 2017):
(a) All-in-rate – a static billing strategy, in which a ﬁxed rate is charged to cus-
tomers, remaining unchanged throughout the day.
(b) TOU – according to which the day is divided into distinct periods. A ﬁxed rate
tariﬀ is adopted during each period. It is common for these periods, and their
respective tariﬀs, to change over time. Moreover, they can be determined based
on multiple criteria, such as the season of the year or the day of the week.
(c) CPP – deﬁning a peak rate to bill customers during critically overloaded pe-
riods. Both the peak rate and critical overload thresholds are deﬁned by the

Chapter 2. State of the Art 40
utility, based on total customer consumption. In the remainder of the time, this
approach becomes TOU.
(d) RTP –duringwhichadoptedtariﬀsareperiodicallyupdated, usuallyonahourly
or daily basis.
(e) Inclining Block Rate(IBR) – which considers a unit electricity rate, increasing
with the consumption in blocks of several hundred kWh. As IBR bills higher
demand at more expensive rates, even load distribution is, indirectly, promoted
by this billing strategy.
2.4.3 Constraints
Within household appliance scheduling problems, two main types of constraints are
identiﬁed, to restrict the operation times of electrical appliances. They compriseap-
pliance and comfort constraints. Appliance constraints comprise the modelling of the
individual devices, either through average non-varying consumption (Pedrasa et al.,
2010; Zhang et al., 2013; Ahmad et al., 2017; Leitão et al., 2018a,c; Golmohamadi
et al., 2019b), or independent consumption proﬁles assigned to each phase of the op-
eration cycle (Giorgio et al., 2011; Sou et al., 2011; Wu, 2012; Zhang et al., 2013;
Silvente and Papageorgiou, 2017). Average non-varying consumption is a far more
popular choice than the deﬁnition of individual consumption proﬁles, mainly due to
the underlying model simplicity and lack of detailed consumption proﬁle data for
controllable appliances.
On the other hand, the evaluation of resident comfort is a very complex task, as
a result of the subjectiveness and individual perception of comfort. In (Beaudin and
Zareipour, 2015; Bayram and Ustun, 2017), customer comfort assessment is classiﬁed
into: (i) inconvenience due to timing, related to the perception of discomfort, resulting
from operating certain appliances outside of their respective preferred time windows;
(ii) inconvenience due to appliance use, related to premature load interruption, in-
tensity reduction or failure to perform a given load; and (iii) inconvenience due to
appliance priorities, which refers to failure to respect precedences and priorities of
certain appliance over others.
HEMSs need to balance several distinct and often conﬂicting constraints. While
some of these constraints must always be satisﬁed, at the risk of major household
impact, others only need to be partially met. The degree to which a constraint may
not be satisﬁed distinguishes hard constraints from soft constraints. As one example,
the capacity of external batteries and contracted power are common examples of
hard constraints. Comfort constraints can, in many scenarios, be incorporated as soft
constraints. The reason why it is possible to treat them as soft constraints is because
a small resident discomfort is often acceptable, if it leads to a meaningful reduction
in the electricity bill. Constraints can be addressed in two distinct ways, namely by
being taken as additional objectives of the problem, leading to unconstrained multi-
objective problem instances. Alternatively, constraints can explicitly be considered in
constrained problem instances (Sardahi, 2016).
Multi-objective Transformation
As stated, in multi-objective transformations, the original constrained scheduling
problem is transformed into an unconstrained equivalent, by incorporating original
constraints as new problem objectives. As a result, a multi-objective scheduling prob-
lem is deﬁned. MOOPs can be solved by computing a representative approximation
of the Pareto front, usually via heuristic and metaheuristic-based techniques, such as

Chapter 2. State of the Art 41
EAs (Gong and Cai, 2008; Li et al., 2008; Venter and Haftka, 2009; Wang et al., 2010;
Reynoso-Meza et al., 2010; Salinas et al., 2013; Lu et al., 2016; Mahbub et al., 2016).
For some problem instances, computing a representative approximation of the
Pareto front can be computationally demanding. In these situations, more eﬃcient
alternatives have been sought. For many formulations, it is common to transform
the multi-objective problem into an equivalent single-objective instance, usually by
considering a weighted sum of all objectives (Setlhaolo and Xia, 2014; Setlhaolo et al.,
2014; Kinhekar et al., 2014; Setlhaolo and Xia, 2016; Yahia and Pradhan, 2018).
The bounded objective and physical programming methods are common solution
methods for single-objective problem transformations via weighted sum (Beaudin and
Zareipour, 2015). The former methods consider all but one objective as constraints,
which implies a stronger importance of the criterion remaining as the objective. On
the other hand, physical programming methods require a deeper knowledge of the
underlying problem in order to explicitly model trade-oﬀs among all objectives.
Explicit Constraint Handling
The way constraints are handled by HEMSs is strongly dependent on the individ-
ual scheduling strategies carried out. In MPC, LP or NLP frameworks, the support
for the speciﬁcation of constraints is typically natively provided. As a result, the
