feasibility of solutions obtained with these frameworks is ensured. In addition, soft
constraints can also be considered, usually by relaxing the constraint speciﬁcation by
means of a user-deﬁned infeasibility degree, or by removing the original constraint and
penalising deviations from it in the value of the objective function. Heuristic optimi-
sation methods were designed to solve unconstrained problems, implying the selection
of adequate constraint-handling techniques (CHTs) (Michalewicz and Janikow, 1991;
Coello, 2002; Mezura-Montes and Coello, 2011). In (Coello, 2002; Mezura-Montes and
Coello, 2011) CHTs are categorised into early (up to year 2000) and current (2000s-
2010s) CHTs. Even though these techniques target heuristic-based methods, some
can also be employed within other frameworks.
Within early CHTs, proposed techniques can be grouped into three main cate-
gories: (i) penalty functions, (ii) specialised operators, and (iii) separation of objec-
tivesandconstraints. Penaltyfunctionsprivilegethecomputationoffeasiblesolutions,
by penalising constraint violations in the criterion function. Penalty functions have
been extensively adopted, motivating distinct deﬁnitions such as static (Coello, 2002),
dynamic (Yokota et al., 1996), or penalties based on co-evolution (Coello, 2000) and
segregated GA (Riche et al., 1995) principles. Despite their ease of use and imple-
mentation, penalty terms assigned to the violations of individual constraints need to
be properly tuned, which comes at signiﬁcant eﬀort. The deﬁnition of specialised op-
erators refers mainly to the recombination and mutation operators. These operators
are speciﬁcally deﬁned within EAs and GAs, in order to ensure the feasibility of the
generated individuals, and to maintain the genetic diversity of the population. For
these reasons, the deﬁnition of these operators needs to be tuned for each particular
problem instance, therefore representing a signiﬁcant tuning eﬀort. Finally, techniques
such as co-evolution (Paredis, 1994) or feasibility rules (Powell and Skolnick, 1993)
propose to treat problem objectives and criteria separately.
During more recent periods, current trends on CHTs have been increasingly fo-
cused on problem-oriented modiﬁcations to the existing techniques, rather than on
the synthesis of novel approaches. Still, in (Mezura-Montes and Coello, 2011), six
categories of current CHTs are distinguished:

Chapter 2. State of the Art 42
(a) Stochastic ranking (SR) – a novel CHT proposal, originally designed to ad-
dress the shortcomings of poor tuning of penalty functions (Runarsson and Yao,
2000). SR quantiﬁes the degree of a constraint violation via penalty functions,
implementing an adaptive ranking of the candidate solutions according to the
value of their objective and penalty function. Pairs of feasible solutions are
compared based on the value of the objective function. If one or both solutions
are infeasible, a user-deﬁned probabilityPf determines whether comparisons are
carried out using the objective or penalty functions. SR has been widely applied
together with evolutionary strategies (Runarsson and Yao, 2000), DE (Zhang
et al., 2008; Liu et al., 2009) and ant colony optimisation (Leguizamon and
Coello, 2007; Fonseca et al., 2007).
(b) ϵ-Constrained method – another novel CHT proposal, according to which the
small infeasibility degrees are ignored, based on a thresholdϵ∈ R. This thresh-
old inﬂuences the selective pressure on the feasible solutions (Takahama et al.,
2005). Naturally, larger ϵ values promote the exploration of deeper infeasible
regions, while smaller values allow for fewer infeasible solutions, as a result of an
increase in the selective pressure. A careful tuning of this parameter is, there-
fore, required, in order to promote a balance between feasible solutions and
exploration of the search space. The use of theϵ letter should also be clariﬁed.
In stochastic programming literature, problem uncertainties are commonly rep-
resented by a vectorϵ (Section 2.3.3). Conversely, in theϵ-constrained method,
ϵ deﬁnes an infeasibility threshold, below which candidate solutions are still
treated as feasible. In order to maintain the original nomenclature (Takahama
et al., 2005), the threshold parameter is herein represented byϵ.
(c) Penalty functions– remaining a popular CHT, being the subject of novel contri-
butionsexploringco-evolutionandmulti-populationconcepts, suchasin(Coello,
2000; Tasgetiren and Suganthan, 2006; Mani and Patvardhan, 2009).
(d) Special operators– despite the active research towards problem-oriented tech-
niques, a proper categorisation of specialised techniques is hindered by the lack
of shared characteristics, as well as their integration with other techniques.
(e) Feasibility rules– following the principles of separation of objectives and con-
straints, feasibility rules have been widely employed in heuristic and metaheuris-
tic frameworks such as GA (Elsayed et al., 2011b), DE (Zielinski et al., 2008),
PSO (Wang and Cai, 2009) and artiﬁcial bee colony (ABC) (Karaboga and
Akay, 2011).
(f) CHT ensembles – in which multiple techniques are combined and applied to
the same problem. CTH ensembles are motivated by theno-free-lunch theo-
rem (Wolpert and Macready, 1997), exploring individual strengths of each tech-
nique and mitigating their own shortcomings. Examples include ensembles of
feasibility rules, penalty functions,ϵ-constrained and SR (Mallipeddi and Sug-
anthan, 2010; Tasgetiren et al., 2010; Elsayed et al., 2011a). Nonetheless, the
fact that many techniques are applied at the same time results in a signiﬁcant
computational load, which justiﬁes its limited adoption.
Aﬁnalgroupnotcoveredin(Mezura-MontesandCoello,2011)isthatofconstraint
metamodelling (Kramer, 2010), which focus on the development of metamodels target-
ting feasibility checking and prediction, constraint estimation and repair of infeasible
mutations (Li and Li, 2010) in black-box scenarios where constraint boundaries are
not explicitly provided.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 43
2.4.4 Summary
HEMSs often manage the building power demand by means of consumption shifting,
resorting to scheduling techniques to coordinate the building loads. Mathematical op-
timisation comprises the most popular choice for problems of this nature, in particular
for small and medium-sized instances, such as those resulting from the scheduling of
electrical loads in individual households. The popularity of the mathematical opti-
misation techniques is mainly due to their low computational burden and optimal-
ity guarantees. Heuristic-based techniques constitute valuable alternatives for large-
dimension problem instances. In comparison to mathematical optimisation, heuristics
and metaheuristics make up for their inferior solution performance with smaller com-
putational load, for large-dimension problem instances. As a result, heuristic-based
techniques are usually preferred in problems where mathematical optimisation cannot
operate in feasible time.
Machine learning-based techniques, in particular RL, are another popular alterna-
tive to mathematical optimisation. With machine learning, the popularity of model-
free RL in residential appliance scheduling problems is noted in recent years. The
absence of a model is an appealing characteristic of model-free RL, particularly in
real-world problems with signiﬁcantly high modelling costs. MPC is another popular
alternative, deﬁned as an advanced process control method, according to which a given
plant model is iteratively optimised, over a ﬁnite RH. Within the context of DSM-
based household load shifting, MPC targets thermostatically regulated loads, such
as HVAC systems or refrigerators. Despite its popularity in industrial control, the
signiﬁcant modelling costs, the stability issues of the underlying closed loop system,
and the sub-optimal performance, limit the application of MPC to medium and large
buildings. Finally, game theory-based techniques were also discussed, usually em-
ployed within multi-agent frameworks. Cooperative game theory was distinguished
from non-cooperative, based on collaboration between individual agents. Cooper-
ative techniques were highlighted in scenarios with multiple consumers, where an
improved rationalisation is achieved by cooperation and load coordination among
diﬀerent households. Nonetheless, these techniques can also be employed in single
households, in which case each agent is responsible for managing a single appliance.
The management of the household electrical loads is still mainly carried out with a
single operational goal, subjected to constraints of several natures. The electricity bill
is the most common criterion, followed by the resident comfort and carbon emissions.
When performed at grid level, other operational goals are considered, namely the
distribution losses, peak load and peak-to-average load reduction. These criteria are
more aligned with the operational goals and interests of the utility companies. With
respect to the appliance scheduling constraints, device and comfort restrictions were
distinguished, the former concerning the modelling of individual devices, whereas the
latter are related to timing, priorities and inconveniences in appliance use. HEMS can
address scheduling constraints by incorporating them directly in operational goals,
leading to a multi-objective transformation of the original problem, or by considering
appropriate constraint-handling methodologies. Penalty functions, namely adaptive
penalties, have remained one of the most popular explicit constraint-handling al-
ternatives over the years. Feasibility rules are another popular choice for heuristic
optimisation-based scheduling. In recent years, constraint metamodelling has also
emerged in the related literature.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 44
2.5 Smart Grid Security
The elements of the smart grid infrastructure are inevitably exposed to vulnerabili-
ties and threats, such as failures in equipment and transmission, or information pri-
vacy. These vulnerabilities may threaten the generation and stability of power supply
throughout the grid. This exposition of the grid infrastructure is further accentu-
ated by the smart grid paradigm shift, mostly due to the constant communication
between grid entities. As a result, extensive research from academia, governments
and industries has been carried out, in order to improve the resilience of the grid
infrastructure (Mrabet et al., 2018). Vulnerabilities at both physical and cyber spaces
of the smart grid have been identiﬁed (He and Yan, 2016). The vulnerabilities at the
physical level are centred around the stability of the power supply, and ensured by
coordinating the assets of the grid, which seek to address time-variance and demand
and generation uncertainties (Moslehi and Kumar, 2010). On the other hand, the
main challenges at cyber level are the lack of security features in most ﬁeld devices,
leaving them exposed to malicious external attacks (He and Yan, 2016).
In (He and Yan, 2016; Batalla et al., 2017; Mrabet et al., 2018; Kumar et al., 2019;
Zhang et al., 2021), smart grid attack vectors and corresponding counter measures
have been extensively reviewed. In the remainder of this section, the main ﬁndings of
above cited works are presented.
2.5.1 Attack Vectors
In (Zhang et al., 2021), smart grid attack vectors are summarised in three categories.
They are: (i) control signal attacks, (ii) measurement attacks, and (iii) control signal
measurement attacks. Control signal attacks target mission-critical devices, such as
automatic generation control, relays or smart inverters. Control signal attacks seek
to gain unauthorised access and control of these devices, in order to operate them at
the will of the attacker. On the other hand, measurement attacks are focused on the
manipulation of the sensor readings, along with compromising physical communica-
tion links. By doing so, the attackers can mask the actual system behaviour from the
system operators, or prevent the operators from issuing control signals to the actu-
ators. Finally, control-signal-measurement attacks comprise coordinated attacks on
both the control signals, measurement equipment and the communication infrastruc-
ture. A common practice among this type of attacks consists in damaging existing
grid infrastructure through control signal attacks, while masking the behaviour of the
system and preventing operator control via measurement attacks.
Control Signal Attacks
Smart grid generators, power lines and individual customers comprise the primary
targets of control signal attacks. This type of attack aims to disrupt the normal op-
eration of the grid, by issuing adulterated control signals to these devices (Mohajerin
Esfahani et al., 2010). In (Arani et al., 2019), aurora attacks, targeting synchronous
generator breakers and points of common coupling in microgrids, are examined. Au-
rora attacks aim to damage generators of the smart grid by injecting compromised
commands in these devices. Triggered malicious actions result in repeatedly opening
and re-closing of generator breakers, making these devices split out of synchronism
with the grid. When the main grid and the generator are not synchronised, high
torque and currents can be produced, which may ultimately damage the generator.
Pricing attacks have also been carried out. In this type of attack, illegal proﬁts are
made by attackers who have gained unauthorised access to the system, and exploited

Chapter 2. State of the Art 45
the price margin between on-peak and oﬀ-peak tariﬀs. One example of this type
of control signal attack is discussed in (Tan et al., 2013). According to the attack
described in the cited work, old and scaled electricity price information is sent to the
customers, in order to inﬂuence their power demand. This privileged information is
further exploited to increase the revenue of the attackers. In (He and Yan, 2016),
attackers buy additional electricity during oﬀ-peak tariﬀ periods. An attack on the
power grid is subsequently carried out, decreasing the availability of electrical energy,
and driving its price up. As a result, attackers are able to sell previously bought
extra electricity at a higher price, achieving an illegal proﬁt. In (Giraldo et al., 2017),
attacks on smart grid communication channels are carried out, in order to increase
the mismatch between demand and generation in the grid. Similarly, when demand
exceeds generation, electricity prices increase, allowing attackers to inject power into
the grid at a higher price. In (Zhang et al., 2020), the electricity market is manipulated
by injecting malicious bidding prices and power demand quantities at customer-side.
Under these attacks, the electricity consumption of individual customers is aﬀected,
disrupting overall grid distribution feeders operation.
Individual customers constitute another common target of control signal attacks.
In (McLaughlin et al., 2013), the lack of built-in security features in many customer-
side equipments is exploited by attackers for energy theft purposes. Exploitation of
customer-side security vulnerabilities has also targeted information leakage (Sankar
et al., 2013). Finally, malicious control signals have also been issued across large
clusters of customers, forcing their demand to increase at strategical time periods (Yi
et al., 2016; He and Yan, 2016). By doing so, transmission lines can be severely
damaged, threatening the stability of the power grid and the occurrence of power
outages. If carried out at large scale, it can result in a denial of service (DoS).
Measurement Attacks
The primary aim of measurement attacks is to compromise the sensing and commu-
nication infrastructure of the smart grid. Measurement attacks accomplish this goal
by masking the behaviour of the system and its response to on-going attacks. Inter-
diction of transmission lines, false data injection, manipulation of control signals, and
tripping of transformers and generators are often carried out, see e.g. (Yuan et al.,
2011; Sridhar and Govindarasu, 2014; Srikantha and Kundur, 2015).
Byzantine attacks against cognitive radio and mobile communication networks
have also been reported in the literature (Zhang et al., 2015). The goal of these attacks
is to compromise network nodes, which are posteriorly exploited to disrupt trusted
routing mechanisms. If carried out at a large scale, the performance of the network
will, inevitably, drop. Byzantine attacks are commonly carried out for vandalism
purposes or to exploit vulnerabilities in the existing communication infrastructure.
When vandalism is the only motivation for byzantine attacks, it is common to instruct
malicious nodes to report busy communication channels as being free, promoting an
overload of these channels. This can also be an eﬀective way of conducting a DoS
attack on a given communication infrastructure. On the other hand, the opposite
is carried out when attackers aim to exploit the existing infrastructure for their own
communications. In this case, malicious nodes are instructed to report free channels
as being busy. By doing so, these channels are not occupied by non-malicious nodes,
meaning that attackers can take advantage of low-traﬃc communication networks.
Attacks on automatic generation control (AGC) units have also been considered
in (Tan et al., 2017). These units are critical to ensure that the frequency of the power
system remains within acceptable operation bounds. However, frequency monitoring

Chapter 2. State of the Art 46
in many power grids has an important limitation, in the form of the time interval be-
tween frequency validation measurements, usually performed once every few minutes.
Thus, attackers exploit this time interval, manipulating sensor readings in order to
trigger unwanted AGC unit behaviour, and ultimately cause power imbalances. The
high automation degree of the AGC units means that successful measurement attacks
are diﬃcult to mitigate (Zhang et al., 2021).
In (Hines et al., 2017), unauthorised access to system topology information, via
measurement attacks, is reported. Once the grid topology information is obtained,
strategically vulnerable locations are identiﬁed and subjected to posterior attacks, e.g.
false data injection. In (Chen et al., 2018a), the injection of adulterated power ﬂow
measurementdataisexploitedtotriggerinadequateAGCresponse, withconsequences
in terms of power grid frequency stability.
More recently, in (Chen et al., 2019), machine learning, and more speciﬁcally
RL-based, techniques, have also been exploited to launch unidentiﬁable false data
injection attacks. In the cited work, a Q-learning-based blind false data injection
attack is carried out in a power grid system. In this attack, the attacker is assumed
to have very limited to no prior knowledge of the power system. It is also assumed
that the important parameters of the system, providing valuable information to the
attacker, cannot be estimated from system observations. Thus, in the cited work,
attacker actions are guided by an online learning strategy, which iteratively searches
for the optimal attack vectors. Another recent trend in measurement attacks are
global positioning system (GPS) spooﬁng attacks (Cui et al., 2019). In these attacks,
the attackers swap the signals of two geographically distinct locations, tricking grid
monitoring systems into detecting a disturbance at a given location, when in fact it
is taking place in a completely diﬀerent sections of the grid. All eﬀorts to address the
problem are unsuccessful, as the wrong area is targeted.
Control Signal Measurement Attacks
In (Zhang et al., 2021), two subcategories of control signal measurement attacks are
distinguished, namely line outage masking and Stuxnet-like attacks.
In line outage masking attacks, control signal attacks are carried out to disrupt
the electricity supply to the customers in the grid. At the same time, measurement
attacks are carried out to mask the resulting supply power shortage from the grid
infrastructure monitoring systems. In (Zhang et al., 2021), the well-known December,
2015 attack on the Ukrainian power grid is highlighted among the notable examples of
line outage masking attacks. In this attack, malicious attackers gained unauthorised
access to the supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) system of a regional
Ukrainian electricity distribution company. Attackers were then able to disconnect
generationsubstationsfromthemaingridforabout3hours, eﬀectivelystoppingpower
generation. While power generation was disrupted, power grid monitoring routines
were also being adulterated to mask the resulting power outage. In (Li et al., 2018),
two-step cyber attacks are proposed to mask line outages resulting from physical
attacks targetting the power grid. In the proposed scheme, a topology-preserving
attack is primarily carried out. Subsequently, a load redistribution attack is launched
to bypass state estimation-based line ﬂow monitoring mechanisms.
Stuxnet-like attacks are named after the so-called Stuxnet (Karnouskos, 2011).
This attack was ﬁrst uncovered in 2010, and is believed to have been responsible for
signiﬁcantly delaying and damaging the Iranian nuclear program. The attack targeted
SCADA systems, forcing an unintended operation of uranium centrifuges. Issued
malicious commands forced the centrifuges to spun too fast, and for long periods of

Chapter 2. State of the Art 47
time, damaging and destructing the materials under processing. While the attack was
taking place, false data injection attacks were being carried out to mimic a normal
operationofthesystem. In(Zhangetal.,2021), itisclaimedthatattacksofthisnature
can only be carried out on converged systems, where system states are expected to be
unchanged. Moreover, false data injection and replaying attacks must be carried out
to perfection, in order to avoid detection.
2.5.2 Defence Strategies
Distinct categorisations of smart grid defence strategies can be performed, namely
based on defence goals – such as prevention of future attacks or stopping of on-going
attacks –, nature of the technique or automation level, just to name out a few.
In (He and Yan, 2016), smart grid defence is approached from three levels of
counter-measures, namely (i) protection, (ii) detection, and (iii) mitigation. Within
this categorisation, protection mechanisms constitute the ﬁrst line of defence, aim-
ing to stop external attacks before they could occur. The most commonly deployed
protection mechanisms include the adoption of secure communication channels and
protocols, re-conﬁguration of the topological information and restriction of critical
information. The second line of defence is activated when above mentioned protec-
tion mechanisms fail to prevent attacks. The goal of detection counter-measures is,
thus, to detect malicious activities. To this end, intrusion detection systems (IDSs)
and intrusion prevention systems (IPSs) (Radoglou-Grammatikis and Sarigiannidis,
2019), physical watermarking of control inputs (Mo et al., 2015), and a variety of
signal processing (Manandhar et al., 2014) and data-driven techniques (Ding et al.,
2013; Ozay et al., 2016; Li et al., 2020) are commonly exploited. Finally, when all
the above fails and an attack cannot be stopped, mitigation counter-measures are de-
ployed. These techniques aim to accommodate the ongoing attacks and their eﬀects,
with optimisation-based approaches (Delgadillo et al., 2010; Zhao and Zeng, 2013)
and game-theory techniques (Sanjab and Saad, 2016; Chen et al., 2011; Ma et al.,
2013; Esmalifalak et al., 2013) being widely employed.
A diﬀerent categorisation scheme is proposed in (Zhang et al., 2021). In this
work, diﬀerent attack detection mechanisms are primarily highlighted, followed by a
categorisation of the state of the art defence approaches in cyber-physical smart grids.
Concerning the detection of smart grid attacks, temporally-relevant and spatially-
relevant approaches are highlighted in (Zhang et al., 2021). In temporally-relevant
methods, the current state of the system is estimated at each discrete time instant,
based on a prior estimated state, system models, measurement and control signals.
Smart grid attacks are, subsequently, detected based on system state estimations.
Among temporally-relevant approaches, Kalman ﬁlter and chi-squared-based state
estimators are highlighted (Simon, 2010; Weerakkody et al., 2014; Zhang et al., 2021).
On the other hand, in spatially-relevant approaches, smart grid attacks are detected
by exploiting correlations between diﬀerent sensor readings, collected in the same time
interval. In both temporally-relevant and spatially-relevant approaches, attacks are
detected based on the system state and measurement estimations. While temporally-
based attack detection exploits past information, current system state information is
exploited in spatially-relevant attack detection.
Finally, state of the art cyber-physical smart grid defence approaches are grouped
in a total of ﬁve distinct categories, namely (i) securing measurement sensors, (ii)
modelling and algorithmic enhancement, (iii) data-driven and signal processing ap-
proaches, (iv) moving target defence (MTD), and (v) watermarking.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 48
Securing measurement defence mechanisms are aimed at protecting the critical
smart grid infrastructure against control signal and measurement attacks. In (Bobba
et al., 2010), a validation method for critical sensor readings is proposed, primar-
ily addressing false data injection attacks. Protection against attacks of this type is
achieved via validation of sensor measurements and state information. In the pro-
posed approach, a trade-oﬀ between the protection level and the computation cost
stemming from verifying a large number of measurements and state information, nat-
urally, emerges. More recently, in (Silvestre et al., 2018), centralised and distributed
techniques are developed to monitor a subset of a smart grid. In this work, devel-
oped centralised and distributed detectors are responsible for monitoring the current
state of a portion of the grid, protecting it against information corruption attacks.
In (Zhao et al., 2018), a robust method against false data injection is proposed. In
this work, the validation of sensor measurement and state information is carried out
via statistical consistency assessment. In order to avoid the high computational bur-
den of validating a large volume of information, only readings from a limited group of
trusted power monitoring units are validated. Data redundancy is also promoted via
short-term forecasting (Chakhchoukh et al., 2014).
In the case of modelling and algorithmic enhancement methods, contributions
considered by (Zhang et al., 2021) in this category are mainly focused on improving
the existing detection methods. In (Liu et al., 2014a), the problem of detecting false
data injection attacks is approached as a matrix separation problem. This approach is
opposed to earlier works, see e.g. (Huang et al., 2011), where model-based approaches,
supported by adaptive cumulative sum techniques, are adopted. More recently, an
online anomaly detection method is proposed in (Ashok et al., 2018), which is focused
on broad attack scenarios, exploiting online load and generation forecasts, along with
real-time sensor information collected from power monitoring units.
Following the growing interest of machine learning and data-driven-based tech-
niques for smart grid attacks and load coordination, techniques of this nature have
also been exploited for smart grid protection purposes. In (Zhang et al., 2021), both
supervised and unsupervised learning techniques are reported. In (Ozay et al., 2016),
a ANN-based binary classiﬁcation of sensor readings and other system measurements
is carried out. The collected readings are processed in both batch or online settings,
being classiﬁed as either secured or attacked. Experimental analyses carried out in
the cited work showed a superior performance of ANN-based attack detection, in com-
parison to state vector estimation-based methods. In (Niu et al., 2019), smart grid
anomalies are detected based on the residuals between the observed and LSTM-based
forecasted measurements. In addition to ANN-based approaches, model-based (Mo
et al., 2014), game-theoretic (Vamvoudakis et al., 2014), Kalman ﬁlters (Manandhar
et al., 2014), RL-based (Chen et al., 2019) and generative adversarial networks (Li
et al., 2020) have also been reported.
MTD techniques are often employed in critical and emergency scenarios, when
other preventive and protection measures have failed to protect the system. This
category of techniques proposes to proactively change the conﬁguration of the system,
enhancing network security by means of reducing the attack surface and increasing the
uncertainty around the conﬁguration of the network system (Al-Shaer et al., 2013).
Implementation of MTD approaches in cyber-physical smart grids is a very complex
task. In this task, distributed ﬂexible AC transmission system (D-FACTS) devices are
exploited to promote changes to the system conﬁguration, and consequently invalidate
any existing knowledge of an attacker. Proper placement and operation of D-FACTS
devices is critical in this task. In (Zhang et al., 2021), an in-depth review of ﬁve MTD
strategies are discussed, covering both the placement and operation of these devices.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 49
Finally, watermarking defence approaches propose the use of watermarks in or-
der to identify the ownership and verify the integrity of sensor readings and control
signals. Despite the availability of tools such as cryptography and digital signatures,
physical watermarking remains superior against both physical attacks and malicious
users (Zhang et al., 2021). In short, physical watermarking can be seen as injecting
a known noise in the input of the system, which results in an expected system be-
haviour and output. When the correct watermark, i.e. noise, is not injected, diﬀerent
outputs and behaviours are observed in the system, which signal an attack. In (Mo
et al., 2015), physical watermarking is carried out to authenticate correct control op-
erations, whereas in (Satchidanandan and Kumar, 2017), it is extended into dynamic
watermarking, in order to address noisy measurement and highly dynamic systems.
More recently, in (Ferdowsi and Saad, 2018), a LSTM deep learning-based framework
is proposed to carry out dynamic watermarking of sensor readings within a IoT envi-
ronment. In this framework, the watermark is generated based on stochastic features
extracted from collected reading signals.
2.6 Synthesis
In this chapter, HEMSs were contextualised in the smart grid paradigm shift, and
discussed with respect to the improvement of the energy rationalisation in household
environments, achieved via appliance scheduling. HEMSs comprise a new generation
of systems which provide the means for automated and intelligent appliance control
within smart homes. These systems promise eﬃcient energy management and bill
reduction through coordination of domestic loads, shutting down unnecessary loads
and optimal load scheduling, exploring periods of cheaper electricity and higher local
generation to further reduce the electricity costs. They strongly rely on AMI, smart
appliances, meters and sensors, two-way communication protocols, among others.
The general architecture of a HEMS comprises sensing and measuring devices,
smart appliances, a user interface and a central platform, being all of the above
interconnected and in constant communication. Sensing devices measure physical
quantities such as the temperature or the current appliance consumption, just to
name out a few, and communicate their readings to a central platform for persistent
storage and posterior analysis. Smart appliances encompass a new generation of
devices, enhanced with computing and communication capabilities, which enable two
way-communication, and ultimately remote monitoring and control. User interfaces
can take the form of a web interface or computer terminal, and are used by household
residents to interact with the HEMS. Examples of resident–HEMS interactions include
querying the current and past consumption information, or specifying additional loads
or operational constraints over a given time horizon. Lastly, the central platform
receives and stores sensory information, and coordinates the household loads based
on established criteria. Its operation is heavily supported by two-way communication
with grid entities and, potentially, with other household environments.
Two main strategies to improve the energy rationalisation and reduce the elec-
tricity bill at household level were identiﬁed, namely consumption reduction and con-
sumption shifting. While the former proposes to turn oﬀ unnecessary loads and re-
place existing appliances with more energy-eﬃcient alternatives, the latter is focused
on shifting appliance loads in time, in order to explore energy-eﬃcient periods. The
popularity of consumption shifting at residential level is undeniable, mostly due to
its fewer and cheaper investments required, being often tackled from a scheduling
problem perspective in either centralised or distributed approaches.

Chapter 2. State of the Art 50
Household appliances managed by HEMSs can mainly be divided into controllable
and non-controllable devices, where the former allow for their operation to be man-
aged by an external entity, such as the HEMS, but the latter do not. Controllable
loads can be further split into curtailable, regulating, uninterruptible loads and energy
storage systems, based on their interaction with HEMSs. Supported by a recent liter-
ature review, washing machines, EVs and HVAC systems were distinguished as highly
appealing for smart management and scheduling, owed to their operation mode, typi-
cal long program duration and high consumption. In recent years, EVs have been the
subject of growing interest, mainly due to their promise of a more eﬃcient and less
polluting alternative to conventional ICEs. Their integration in urban communities
still poses relevant and serious challenges, stemming from their power requirements,
which are expected to substantially increase grid demand stress. To this end, research
into EV charging mechanisms capable of ensuring their safe and stable integration
in modern power grids has also been conducted. On the one hand, unidirectional
charging approaches treat EVs as any other residential electrical appliances, seeking
to coordinate charging schedules with other domestic appliances. On the other hand,
bidirectional charging is more complex, allowing electricity to ﬂow from the grid to
the vehicle and vice-versa. Such bidirectional electricity ﬂow allows EVs to act as
mobile energy storage systems and generators, accommodating unexpected customer
demand peaks and grid generation drops.
TheloadmanagementandappliancecoordinationcarriedoutbyHEMSsisstrongly
inﬂuenced by the baseline power demand, the utility grid prices and other external
factors, which can only be predicted to a certain degree of certainty. In this chapter,
stochastic programming and robust optimisation-based approaches were distinguished
to address these uncertainties in decision-making processes. Supported by probabilis-
tic uncertainty modelling, stochastic programming optimises the expected value of the
objective function, while robust optimisation improves the behaviour of the system as-
suming the worst-case uncertainty realisations. While morewell-rounded solutions are
obtained in the former techniques, the strict focus of the latter on extreme realisations
often results in sub-optimal performance under less severe events. Despite its beneﬁts,
the computational load of stochastic programming is considerable, mainly resulting
from the probabilistic modelling and approximation of the expected value of the ob-
jective function. Alternatives to both the stochastic and the robust techniques have
also been identiﬁed, ranging from chance-constrained programming to heuristic and
machine learning-based, in particular RL, approaches. In the case of heuristic and
machine learning-based techniques, they are more valuable in large-scale problems,
where the computational burden of mathematical approaches becomes too high.
Concerning the operational goals of HEMSs, the electricity bill emerges as the
most common criterion, followed by the customer comfort, carbon emissions or distri-
bution losses. With regards to the scheduling restrictions, appliance and comfort con-
straints are highlighted. Reported techniques either explicitly address them through
proper constraint-handling techniques, or treat these constraints as additional objec-
tives in the multi-objective problem reformulations. While mathematical optimisation
frameworks provide native support for explicit constraint-handling, the adoption of
penalty functions or feasibility rules is more common among other frameworks, namely
heuristic-based.
Finally, HEMS and smart grid security was covered, discussing critical vulnerabil-
ities in the form of attack vectors, and respective counter-measures. The main smart
grid attack vectors are categorised into control signal, measurement, and control signal
measurement attacks. While the control signal attacks are focused on gaining unau-
thorised access and control over the devices in the smart grid, measurement attacks are

Chapter 2. State of the Art 51
focused on manipulating sensor readings and compromising physical communication
links, in order to force a speciﬁc system behaviour or prevent an adequate response of
the system monitor to on-going attacks. Finally, control signal measurement attacks
combine the main ideas of both control signal and measurement attacks. Attacks as-
signed to this category rely on control signal attacks to adulterate system behaviour,
while compromising communication links in order to mask the actual behaviour of
the system and prevent an adequate response. Concerning HEMS and smart grid
defence approaches, they are grouped ﬁve categories. Securing measurement defence
mechanisms are aimed at protecting critical smart grid infrastructure against control
signal and measurement attacks. To this end, defence mechanisms in this category
have relied heavily on authentication and validation methods for collected sensor data
and system state information. The proposed algorithmic enhancements to the exist-
ing system models are mainly focused on anomaly detection and protection against
false data injection attacks. Within this context, a growing adoption and interest in
machine learning and data-driven-based techniques has also been observed. Among
noted defence techniques of this nature, the application of ANN-based, generative
adversarial networks, Kalman ﬁlters, RL and game-theoretic approaches has been
noted. MTD techniques diﬀer from the above mentioned alternatives, in the sense
that they are focused on proactively changing the conﬁguration of the system, in or-
der to increase the uncertainty of attackers around its current conﬁguration. Finally,
in watermarking defence approaches, the use of watermarks is promoted as a way to
certify both the ownership and integrity of sensor readings and issued control signals.

53
Chapter 3
Demand-Side Smart Home Load
Management
Overthelastdecades, technologicaladvanceshavepromotedanincreaseinthenumber
of electrical devices recurrently used in our daily lives. This proliferation of electrical
deviceshasalsocontributedtotheobservedexponentialgrowthofpowerdemand, par-
ticularly in the residential sector. These technological advances and demand growth
have also been accompanied by growing environmental concerns. Sustainable en-
ergy movements have been promoted, seeking to progressively replace conventional,
pollutant and non-renewable, fossil fuel-based generation with clean and renewable
alternatives, such as solar and wind.
The realisation of the smart grid digital revolution promises to address the chal-
lenges of the demand growth, technological advances and the integration of renew-
able generation sources. This new-generation system proposes to enhance the existing
powergridinfrastructurewithautonomous, intelligentandreal-timeloadmanagement
capabilities. Bidirectional customer–utility information and power ﬂow constitute the
two main foundations of the smart grid, playing a critical role in enabling demand-side
load coordination, stable, and even load distribution throughout its infrastructure.
In Chapter 2, the adoption of DSM and DR programs is highlighted for demand-
side load regulation and energy rationalisation, particularly in residential buildings.
Within these programs, the preferred application of LP and MILP mathematical
optimisation techniques is highlighted, along with RL frameworks (Section 2.4.1).
Concerning the scheduling horizon, DA-based controllable load scheduling is usually
favoured in detriment of longer scheduling horizons, namely one week (Yahia and
Pradhan, 2020; Duman et al., 2021). It is argued that, while single-day scheduling
horizons are aligned with daily dishwasher operation, and usage of HVAC or lighting
equipment, just to name out a few, they do not address the scheduling of clothes wash-
ing or tumble dryer loads, given that they are operated on a lower frequency, e.g. on
a weekly basis. Under DA-based scheduling, the allocation of the clothes washing or
tumble dryer loads in any day of the week is optional. The only exception concerns the
last day of the week, provided no previous scheduling has been performed. Taking into
consideration the popularity of the electricity bill criterion (Section 2.4.2), these loads
are likely delayed until the last day of the week, which may not be the most proﬁtable
choice. The adoption of longer, in particular weekly, horizons within RH frameworks
addresses this problem, by pondering the operation of these appliances in diﬀerent
days before a decision is made. Nevertheless, the adoption of weekly scheduling hori-
zons within standard RH strategies raises similar problems to DA-based allocation.
In standard RH allocation, a seven-day horizon is always considered. Moreover, as
the week advances, the start of the horizon is shifted forward one day. By doing so, a
portion of the next week is covered, over which the allocation of clothes washing and
tumble dryer loads remains optional.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 54
This chapter addresses the problem of week-ahead household appliance scheduling
for the rationalisation of the electrical energy via DSM-based techniques. A novel
mathematical optimisation-based CRH framework is proposed to address the known
issues of RH and DA alternatives. Similarly to standard RH, the controllable ap-
pliances are scheduled at the start of each new day of the week. Only the ﬁrst day
of the planned appliance operation is implemented. Progressively smaller scheduling
horizons are considered for each new day, which are obtained by shifting the start
of the horizon one day forward, while maintaining the last day ﬁxed. In the pro-
posed CRH approach, the daily scheduling of the controllable appliances is carried
out by solving an instance of a LP optimisation problem. The choice for a mathemat-
ical optimisation-based approach, in detriment of heuristic-based and other machine
learning-based frameworks, namely RL, is primarily due to the guarantees of feasible
and optimum solutions. For the speciﬁed parametrisation, criteria and constraints,
LP solvers provide a global optimum that veriﬁes the imposed constraints. On the
other hand, with both heuristic-based and RL-based techniques, only local optima are
found. Moreover, for small and medium-sized problem instances, the computational
eﬃciency of LP solvers remains unbeaten by heuristics and RL strategies.
The novel CRH-based approach is validated against the standard RH and DA
strategies, along with the ideal allocation and the typical appliance operation. The
assessment of the CRH strategy is carried out considering a prototype smart home,
with characteristic non-controllable demand modelled by real-world historical records.
In this household, the ability to generate electrical energy at a micro level is also
considered, through a set of PV panels. Adequate models of the PV panels and the
weather are considered to forecast the PV generation output. Moreover, it is assumed
that the household has the ability to store electrical energy, by means of a battery
system. Scheduling of four controllable appliances, namely clothes washing, tumble
dryer, dishwasher and electrical pump, is studied. The scheduling of these appliances
is carried out over the course of one week, for a total of 30 diﬀerent weeks covering a
one-year period. The main contributions of this chapter are summarised as follows:
• Novel CRH optimisation-based strategy for week-ahead home controllable ap-
pliance scheduling. The operation of controllable appliances is rescheduled at
the start of every new day of the week, considering updated representative de-
mand proﬁles and generation forecasts, determined from historical records. The
daily rescheduling accommodates the degradation of the weather forecast and
PV generation models, which is more noticeable for later in the scheduling hori-
zon. In addition to the degradation of the predictive models, other unexpected
events, such as additional operation of the controllable loads outside of their
typical usage, can also be accommodate by daily rescheduling.
• Comparison of the novel CRH scheduling approach against standard RH and DA
variants, which follow the state of the art in smart home appliance scheduling.
The performance of the appliance scheduling is assessed over 30 distinct weeks.
The operation of the controllable appliances is planned separately for each week,
and evaluated according to the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort
criteria. The performance of the CRH strategy is shown to be competitive with
that of DA and RH variants, validating this novel scheduling strategy.
• Comparison of the novel CRH scheduling approach against the ideal allocation
and the typical appliance operation. For each week, CRH schedules follow the
ideal appliance operation, albeit with more intense pump system ﬂow rate. A
gap to the performance of the ideal allocation strategy, according to the weekly

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 55
electricity bill criterion, is identiﬁed. As perfect prediction of the future non-
controllable demand and power generation is not possible, the electricity bill
gap is considered acceptable. Concerning the resident discomfort, no signiﬁcant
diﬀerence is observed between the CRH and the ideal allocation. In the case
of the typical operation of the controllable appliances, it is outperformed by
the novel CRH strategy, according to both the weekly electricity bill and the
resident discomfort criteria.
This chapter is organised as follows. In Section 3.1, the prototype smart home
environment considered in this chapter is introduced, followed by a presentation of
the CRH-based appliance scheduling strategy. In Section 3.2, a formal deﬁnition of
the individual MILP optimisation problem instances, formulated and solved under the
referred CRH approach, is provided. In Section 3.3, the CRH, RH and DA appliance
allocation is discussed, and the obtained domestic controllable appliance operation
schedules are compared against those resulting from the ideal allocation and the typ-
ical appliance operation. Finally, the main ﬁndings of this chapter are summarised
in Section 3.4. It must also be noted that the contents of this chapter have, to a
considerable extent, been covered in a published scientiﬁc article (Leitão et al., 2021).
3.1 Methodology
This section presents the prototype smart home considered throughout this docu-
ment, and the novel CRH approach for week-ahead household appliance scheduling.
The smart home environment is discussed in Section 3.1.1, comprising the techni-
cal speciﬁcations for each household electrical appliance, external water tank, battery
storage, and PV panels. As for the novel CRH strategy, it is presented in Section 3.1.2.
3.1.1 Case Study
A prototype of a single-family home, inhabited by four residents, is presented in Fig-
ure 3.1, whose power and water needs vary throughout the day. In addition to the
electrical appliances, the house is equipped with PV panels, a battery system, and an
external water tank. The PV panels are used for microgeneration, primarily targeting
self-consumption via domestic supply, followed by battery storage or grid injection.
As for the battery, it is sued for microstorage of the generation surplus of the PV pan-
els, and of power purchased from the power grid. The electrical energy stored in the
battery can be used for domestic supply or injected in the grid. Upon grid injection, a
ﬁnancial compensation, proportional to the injected power, is considered. Simultane-
ous PV microgeneration and battery storage is motivated by successful peak shaving
and self-consumption boosting (Pimm et al., 2018; Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani,
2019;Sallesetal.,2020). Concerningthewatertank, itisincludedtostorenon-potable
groundwater for domestic supply. The water tank is supplied with groundwater by
an electrical pump system. Groundwater stored in the tank lacks proper treatment,
being therefore unsuited for domestic consumption, showering or dishwasher machine
operation. Nevertheless, it can be used for toilet ﬂushing and clothes washing, which
promotes potable water savings (Kehoe and Chang, 2021).
The above described smart home is also assumed to be equipped with a HEMS,
which is responsible for monitoring the domestic demand and managing certain con-
trollable devices, namely a clothes washing machine, tumble dryer, dishwasher and
pump system. The HEMS is further responsible for coordinating the self-consumption
of the PV microgeneration, along with grid injection and the charging and discharg-
ing of the battery system. For each week, scheduling of a complete washer and dryer

cycle is considered, while one operation of the dishwasher machine must be scheduled
every day. As for the electrical pump, its operation is conditioned by the volume of
non-potable water stored in the tank. All controllable loads are modelled as unin-
terruptible loads, i.e. cannot be interrupted mid-operation and later resumed. The
clothes washing machine is aCandy GSV128T3S, operated on the Mix program, with
a duration of 105 minutes, and consumption of 0.98 kWh and 57 litres. The tumble
dryer is aBosch WTG85230EE, operated on theCotton program, with a duration of
203 minutes and consumption of 6.08 kWh. The dishwasher is aBosch SMS25AW05E,
operated on theNormal program, with a duration of 120 minutes and consumption
of 1.3 kWh. The battery system is modelled as in (Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani,
2019), considering a lead-acid battery with a capacity of 2.4 kWh. Finally, the elec-
trical pump system is characterised by a 30 l/h maximum ﬂow rate and 1 kW peak
power, and a maximum capacity of 334 l is considered for the external water tank.
The appliances not managed by the HEMS are treated as non-controllable ap-
pliances. When applicable, their respective power and non-potable water demand
are modelled to represent a possible real-world scenario for a family of four residents.
Non-controllable power demand is modelled by real-world historical aggregate records,
available in the Pecan Street Inc. Dataport dataset3. From this dataset, historical
aggregate records are retrieved from a single-family home, over a two-year period,
and at hourly basis. Concerning the non-potable water demand, only two sources are
considered, namely the toilet ﬂushes and the washing machine. Non-potable water
demand resulting from the operation of the clothes washing machine is modelled based
on the consumption of the selected program, and assuming an average non-varying
demand throughout the duration of the program. On the other hand, toilet ﬂushing
is modelled as in (Leitão et al., 2018a,c), through a Poisson distribution parametrised
by the Alliance for Water Eﬃciency (AWE)4 (Table 3.1).

where A is the area of the panels, I is the current total radiation on the panels,
nadd represents losses of the inverter and other equipment, andn is the conversion
eﬃciency, which is given by (3.3).
n =nc, STC· [1−ν (Ta−Tc, STC +D·I)] (3.3)
where nc, STC denotes the reference eﬃciency at standard test conditions (STCs),ν
the temperature coeﬃcient of the solar cells,Ta the current air temperature,Tc, STC
the temperature of solar cells at STCs, andD is the coeﬃcient for the irradiance
dependence. The parametrisation proposed in (Widen, 2011) is adopted in this case
study, considering a system with crystalline silicon solar cells and a standard inverter:
nadd = 0.8, nc,STC = 0.14, ν = 0.4%/oC, Tc,STC = 25oC, D = 0.028oC· m2·W−1.
The total solar radiation over the PV panels,I, is estimated as follows. First, the
total solar radiation over an inclined plane is estimated for a cloudless atmosphere, at
the desired coordinates, and using theParametrization Model C(Iqbal, 1983). Sub-
sequently, a deterministic linear model proposed in (Larson et al., 2016) is employed
to estimate the eﬀects of the cloud cover on the solar radiation. Using this model,
the estimated solar radiation over the PV panels, considering the cloud attenuation
eﬀects, is given byIclouds, and determined in (3.4).
Iclouds = (oﬀ+ (1−oﬀ)· (1−cc))·Icloudless (3.4)

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 58
where Iclouds denotes the current total radiation under cloudy skies,cc denotes the
fraction of the sky covered by clouds, or cloud cover index,Icloudless is the cur-
rent total radiation for a cloudless atmosphere (computed using the above mentioned
Parametrization Model C), andoﬀ= 0.35 is the fraction of the global radiation that
reaches the surface of the earth when cloud coverage is maximum. Cloud coverage
data is collected for each time instant from the Global Forecast System (GFS) model,
using thePVLIB Python library v0.6.0 (Holmgren et al., 2018).
Regarding the battery, its stored electrical energy is modelled through the so-called
state of charge(SOC), which is deﬁned as the ratio of the current battery capacity to
the nominal capacity (Lokeshgupta and Sivasubramani, 2019). Utility grid purchases
are billed according to a TOU tariﬀ, taking into account a publicly available rate
structure from a utility company. The same TOU tariﬀ is adopted for all seven days
of each week. As such, between 7 am and 11 pm, power is billed at0.2029 c.u./kWh
(currency unit per kilowatt-hour), while a lower tariﬀ is adopted from 0 am to 7 am,
during which power is billed at0.0969c.u./kWh. Concerning grid injection, a constant
income of 0.05 c.u./kWh is considered for all slots of all the seven days of each week.
Finally, the discomfort of the household residents, resulting from the deviation of
the operation of the clothes washing machine, the tumble dryer and the dishwasher
from their typical operation periods, is considered. Following the approach in (Pamu-
lapati et al., 2020), the resident discomfort is deﬁned separately for each controllable
appliance as 1−∑
i
ζx
i xi, where ζx
i is the percentage of operations of appliancex
carried out at sloti of the day, andxi is a binary value, taking the value 1 if, in the
proposed schedule, the appliancex is being operated during sloti of the day, and 0
otherwise. In this expression,ζx
i is computed by dividing the number of loads of the
appliance x carried out during the sloti of the day, by the total number of opera-
tions of the appliancex. As for the electrical pump, no speciﬁc discomfort model is
included, since there is no interaction between residents and the water pump.
3.1.2 Load Coordination Strategy
The novel CRH mathematical optimisation-based approach, proposed to address the
problem of week-ahead controllable appliance scheduling, is outlined in Algorithm 1.
LikewiseRH,theappliancesareinitiallyscheduledoveraone-weekhorizon, fromMon-
day to Sunday. The operation times of the controllable appliances are determined by
solving an instance of a MILP optimisation problem. Representative non-controllable
power and non-potable water demand proﬁles are determined over each respective
horizon, based on the historical aggregate records. The forecasts of the PV microgen-
eration are also retrieved over the same horizon. The ﬁrst day of the planned operation
is implemented, and the procedure is repeated at the start of the next day. At the start
of the second day of the week, a six-day horizon is considered, obtained by shifting the
ﬁrst day and maintaining the last day of the horizon unchanged (Tuesday–Sunday,
for the second day of the horizon). The new MILP optimisation problem instance is
solved considering the recalculated non-controllable (power and non-potable water)
demand proﬁles, together with the updated PV generation forecasts. The procedure
is sequentially repeated until the end of the corresponding week.
Even though compressive rolling horizons have been previously studied in the
literature, see e.g. (Chen et al., 2012), to the best of the author knowledge, this is the
ﬁrst proposal of a CRH optimisation-based approach to perform week-ahead appliance
scheduling. The inclusion of non-controllable demand proﬁles and microgeneration
forecasts further improves the robustness of the underlying controllable appliance
schedules, which has not been extensively considered in the literature.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 59
Algorithm 1Compressive Receding Horizon
for eachday of the weekdo
1. Deﬁne the horizon of the optimisation problem instance (day – Sunday)
2. Retrieve the non-controllable power and non-potable water demand proﬁles
from historical records
3. Retrieve the updated PV microgeneration forecasts
4. Instantiate the MILP problem formulation (Section 3.2)
5. Solve the MILP instance
6. Retrieve the appliance schedules for the ﬁrst horizon day
7. Implement the ﬁrst day of the controllable appliance operation
end for
By considering a ﬁxed-width horizon, from the second day of the week onwards,
both the current week and a part of the next week are covered in traditional RH. Tak-
ing into consideration the fact that, under standard RH approaches, the controllable
appliances are scheduled over the entire horizon, the reader is reminded of the draw-
backs concerning the optional scheduling of loads not operated every day. By planning
the operation of the controllable appliances over a given week, relying on progressively
smaller scheduling horizons, the typical weekly usage trends of some appliances are
properly addressed. Moreover, the daily rescheduling allows the PV generation fore-
cast errors, stemming from unreliable weather forecasts, to be addressed, adjusting
controllable load operation according to the updated forecasts. Other unexpected
events requiring the adjustment of the planned load operation, namely extra dish-
washer, clothes washing or tumble dryer loads, can also be accommodated via daily
rescheduling of the operation of the controllable appliances.
3.2 Optimisation Problem Formulation
Within the novel CRH proposal, the controllable loads are scheduled by solving, at
the start of each new day of the horizon, individual instances of a MILP optimisation
problem, whileconsideringtherepresentativenon-controllabledemandproﬁlesandthe
updated microgeneration forecasts. The MILP optimisation problems are formulated
as instances of non-preemptive machine scheduling problems. In this formulation,
the controllable appliances take the form of individual machines, whose operations
correspond to jobs that need to be scheduled over a given horizon.
All instances of theMILP optimisationproblem share mostly the sameparameters,
variables, objective and constraints, diﬀering on the values of certain parameters and
constraints. A discretisation of the time domain is performed, splitting the scheduling
horizon intoT hourly slots, numbered from 1 toT. According to this discretisation,
the ith slot covers the period from houri− 1 to hour i. In the remainder of this
section, vectors are distinguished from scalars, the former represented in bold letters.

and d0 = 1,...,d f. In addition, the number of slotsh = 24 in each day, and the
corresponding length of each scheduling horizon,T = (df−d0 + 1)·h, are deﬁned for
each instanceofthe optimisation problem. Thedurationof acompleteclothes washing

The parameterτw∈{ 0, 1} describes the need to wash and dry a load of clothes in

of the clothes washing machine over the tumble dryer is assumed, such that a new
dryer load starts in the slot immediately after the last washer slot. The parameters
ow ∈{ 0,...,l w− 1} and ot ∈{ 0,...,l t− 1} are deﬁned to ensure the continuity
of the clothes washing and the tumble dryer loads, respectively, at the start of the
current scheduling horizon. The parameterow speciﬁes the number of slots, from the
start of the horizon, assigned to the clothes washing machine. On the other hand, the
parameter ot speciﬁes the number of slots, from the start of the horizon, assigned to
the tumble dryer machine. On the ﬁrst optimisation problem instance, it is assumed
that ow = ot = 0, as overnight clothes washing or tumble dryer operation is not
considered at the beginning of any new week.

3.2.3 Criteria
For each day of the horizon, the controllable appliance scheduling minimises two
conﬂicting criteria, namely the electricity bill (EB) and the resident discomfort (RD).
The EB criterion is deﬁned in (3.5) based on TOU tariﬀs and grid injection incentives.
