Finally, it should be mentioned that, in (3.9), both the normalised weekly electricity
bill, EB∗ and the RD, are unitless, and deﬁned in the interval [0, 1]. As0≤ α≤ 1,
the expression in (3.9) is also constrained to the interval [0, 1]. Moreover, the pa-
rameterα reﬂects the relative importance of the weekly electricity bill criterion in the
objective function of the corresponding instance of the MILP optimisation problem.
Conversely,1−α reﬂects the relative importance of the resident discomfort criterion in
the objective function of the corresponding instance of the MILP optimisation prob-
lem. When smallerα weight parameter values are considered, higher importance to
the minimisation of the resident discomfort is assigned. Conversely, when higherα
weight parameter values are considered, stronger importance to the minimisation of
the weekly electricity bill is assigned.
3.3 Appliance Scheduling
The CRH approach proposed in Algorithm 1 is employed in the week-ahead appliance
scheduling, considering the minimisation of the weighted sum of the weekly electricity
bill and the resident discomfort. The allocation of the controllable loads is carried
out over 30 diﬀerent weeks, from March 2018 to February 2019, considering the smart
home environment and appliance operation needs described in Section 3.1.1. The
CRH-based appliance operation schedules are compared against the traditional RH,
DA, ideal allocation and typical appliance operation. The popularity of the traditional
RH and DA allocation in the appliance scheduling literature motivated the choice of
these two particular strategies, which provide benchmarks for assessing the perfor-
mance of the proposed CRH approach. As for the ideal allocation, it is chosen to
provide a lower bound for the scheduling performance, stemming from assuming prior
knowledge of the future non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration. As for the
typical allocation, it is chosen in order to compare the proposed CRH-based strategy
against the resident appliance operation, retrieved from the historical records.
In the case of the traditional RH, the operation of the controllable appliances is
scheduled for each new day of the week, over a seven-day horizon. Only the ﬁrst day

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 72
of the planned operation is implemented. The PV microgeneration forecasts and the
non-controllable power and non-potable water proﬁles cover a seven-day period. A
complete formulation of the instances of the MILP optimisation problem, considered
at each new day of the week under the standard RH, is presented in Appendix A.3.
As for DA scheduling, a single-day horizon MILP problem is formulated and solved
at the start of each new day of the week. Its PV microgeneration forecasts and
the non-controllable power and non-potable water proﬁles are obtained in the same
context of the traditional RH strategy, with the diﬀerence that only a single-day
horizon is considered, and the solution is implemented on the prescribed day. A
complete formulation of the instances of the MILP optimisation problem considered
at each new day of the week under the DA-based scheduling strategy is presented in
Appendix A.4. Concerning the ideal allocation, a single MILP instance is solved for
eachweek, consideringthereal-worldnon-controllabledemandandPVgenerationover
the corresponding week. Finally, no optimisation problems are considered in typical
appliance allocation, as device operation data is retrieved from historical records.
For each week, the non-controllable power demand is modelled as follows. Histor-
ical records are collected for the same week in the previous year, along with records of
thepreviousandsubsequent10weeks, stillinthepreviousyear. Asanexample, forthe
week starting on 18/02/2019, the historical records from 11/12/2017 to 30/04/2018
are considered. For each slot of the week, the85th percentile of these historical records
is taken as the representative proﬁle. The historical power demand records, used to
compute the representative demand proﬁle, are collected from the Pecan Street Inc.
Dataport dataset6, for a single-family home and the period of interest. As for the
non-controllable non-potable water demand, the corresponding representative proﬁle
is estimated by multiplying, for each slot of the horizon, the85th percentile of the
corresponding Poisson distributions by the average non-potable water volume per toi-
let ﬂush (Table 3.1). In Figure 3.2, the household power and the non-potable water
demnand proﬁles obtained for the ﬁrst horizon of one week are presented.
(a) Power demand proﬁle.
 (b) non-potable water consumption proﬁle.
Figure 3.2: Power (a) and non-potable water (b) demand proﬁles
obtained for the ﬁrst horizon of one week.
Concerning the PV microgeneration, its output is forecasted for each slot of the
horizon, for a PV area of 10 m2 with an inclination of 35 degrees towards the equa-
tor. These forecasts are computed based on the PV panel model and parametrisa-
tion in (Widen, 2011). The solar radiation is estimated according to (3.2)– (3.4),
based on the solar radiation modelParametrization Model C(Iqbal, 1983), the cloud
6https://dataport.cloud, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 73
coverage data collected from the Global Forecast System via thePVLIB Python li-
brary (Holmgren et al., 2018) v0.6.0, and a deterministic model which expresses the
cloud attenuation eﬀects on the solar radiation (Larson et al., 2016).
Finally, the preferences of the residents concerning the operation of the clothes
washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines are derived from one-year histori-
cal records. As an example, for the week starting on 18/02/2019, historical records
from 18/02/2018 to 17/02/2019 are considered. Following (Pamulapati et al., 2020)
and (3.7), the resident discomfort is deﬁned for each slot of the week, and separately
for the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines. As such, for each
element of the parameter vectorsζw, ζt and ζd, the percentage of the operations of
the corresponding appliance, performed by residents in each slot of the horizon, is only
computed based on the given one-year period. In Figure 3.3, the estimated preferences
of the residents are illustrated for one week, for the clothes washing, tumble dryer and
dishwasher machines. Darker colours represent more frequent appliance usage during
the respective slot and, therefore, higher preferences of the residents.
Figure 3.3: Daily controllable appliance preferences, estimated for
oneweek. Darkercoloursrepresenthigherappliancepreferencesduring
the respective slot.
Concerning the typical appliance allocation, for each of the 30 weeks, the operation
of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines is retrieved from the
respective historical household demand records. In the case of the electrical pump, its
typical operation is deﬁned as follows. When the volume of non-potable water stored
in the tank drops below 10% of the diﬀerence between its maximum and minimum
capacity, above the minimum tank volume, i.e. whenVi≤Vmin + 0.1 (Vmax−Vmin),
the underlying pump is turned on until the tank is full, i.e.Vi = Vmax. In these
expressions, Vi denotes the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank at the
end of sloti of the horizon, withi = 1,...,T . During the tank reﬁll, the pump is
set to run at full power, provided the contracted power limit is not exceeded. As for
the typical management of the battery storage system, a similar strategy is followed.
When the battery storage capacity drops below 10% of the diﬀerence between its
maximum and minimum SOC, above the minimum SOC, i.e. whenSOCi≤ SOCmin+
0.1 (SOCmax−SOCmin), the battery is charged until its capacity is full, i.e. SOCi =
SOCmax. In these expressions,SOCi denotes the battery SOC at sloti of the horizon,
withi = 1,...,T . During the charging of the battery, the maximum possible charging
power is deployed at each slot of the horizon, provided the contracted power limit
is not surpassed. Moreover, the available local microgeneration is favoured over grid
purchases during the charging of the battery system. Once the battery is fully charged,
its available power is favoured for domestic supply.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 74
For each of the 30 weeks, appliance allocation is carried out under each one of
the above mentioned strategies, namely the CRH, RH, DA and ideal allocation, for
ﬁve diﬀerent household electricity bill weight values: (i) strict minimisation of the RD
(α = 0); (ii) strict minimisation of the EB (α = 1); (iii) equal criteria importance
(α = 0.5); (iv)higherminimisationoftheRD,withoutcompletelydisregardingtheEB
(α = 0.25); and (v) higher minimisation of the EB, without completely disregarding
the RD (α = 0.75). All optimisation problems instantiated in every week, and for
each strategy, are solved using the Solving Constraint Integer Programs (SCIP) solver
v6.0.0 (Gleixner et al., 2018a,b), running on a Ubuntu 16.04 machine equipped with
504GBRAM,andadual2.2GHzIntelXeonSilver4214centralprocessingunit(CPU)
with 16.5MB cache.
3.3.1 Compressive Receding Horizon
The proposed CRH approach is employed for the week-ahead scheduling of the con-
trollable appliances, for each one of the 30 weeks considered. In each week, the
controllable appliances are scheduled separately for each electricity bill weight, i.e.
α∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1}. For each week and electricity bill weight, the same non-
controllable demand proﬁles and PV microgeneration forecasts are considered.
For α = 0, the operation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher
machines is scheduled for the periods of higher resident preferences. Across the 30
weeks, the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines are scheduled for diﬀerent days
of the week, coinciding with the on-peak tariﬀs. However, for diﬀerent weeks, they are
operated in diﬀerent days of the horizon. The clothes washing and tumble dryer loads
are always scheduled for the same slots of the day, coinciding with on-peak tariﬀs.
As such, the clothes washing machine is operated in slots 16 and 17 (3 pm to 5 pm),
whereas the tumble dryer is operated from slots 18 to 21 (5 pm to 9 pm). Regarding
the operation of the dishwasher machine, its nighttime operation, coincident with
oﬀ-peak tariﬀs, is preferred. The dishwasher machine is mainly operated in slots
3 and 4 (2 am to 4 am), albeit its start may be anticipated by one slot, in some
weeks. Concerning the electrical water pump, its operation does not inﬂuence the
resident discomfort criterion. As such, its only scheduling goals are to ensure suﬃcient
non-potable water storage in the tank, and maintain the total demand in each slot
below the contracted power limit. Intense and prolonged operation of the pump
system, immediately before high non-controllable non-potable water demand, is also
commonly observed. Considering that these periods coincide with on-peak tariﬀs, this
operation of the pump system contributes to an increase of the weekly electricity bill.
For α ∈ {0.25; 0.5}, the scheduling of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and
dishwasher machines follows the same pattern reported forα = 0. As such, forα∈
{0.25; 0.5}, the operation of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines coincides
with on-peak tariﬀs, whereas a nighttime operation is reported for the dishwasher. On
the other hand, forα = 0.75, the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines are no
longer strictly operated during the on-peak tariﬀ periods. Instead, for some weeks, the
washer and dryer loads are shifted to nighttime, from 0 am to 6 am. Moreover, the oﬀ-
peak allocation of the dishwasher machine is maintained, resulting in the optimisation
of both criteria. The operation of the pump system is shown to vary the most with
increasing α weights. As the minimisation of the electricity bill criterion becomes
more important, i.e. α increases, prolonged and intense on-peak pump activity is
replaced with smaller ﬂow rate and oﬀ-peak operation. Shorter pump operation with
lower ﬂow rate results in smaller power demand of the pump system, along with a
reduction of the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank. For largerα weights,

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 75
i.e. α = 0.75, smoother pump operation is responsible for decreasing the tank storage,
over the entire week, which remains closer to its minimum capacity bound.
Finally, forα = 1, the resident discomfort is excluded, and appliance allocation
is carried out exclusively to minimise the weekly electricity bill criterion. A more
extreme oﬀ-peak allocation is, therefore, observed. The operations of the clothes
washing and tumble dryer machines are entirely shifted to oﬀ-peak billing periods
and, in some days of some weeks, even span over two separate days. Resulting from
the oﬀ-peak shifting of the washer and dryer machines, the operation of the dishwasher
and the electrical pump system are also adjusted accordingly. Therefore, while the
operation of the dishwasher machine remains constrained to oﬀ-peak billing periods, it
is scheduled for diﬀerent slots of each day of the week, than those reported for smaller
α weights. Concerning the electric pump system, its operation is coordinated with the
remaining controllable appliances and non-potable water demand. The coordination
of the operation of the electrical pump system is carried out to minimise the weekly
electricity bill, while ensuring the feasibility of the appliance scheduling solutions.
Smaller pump ﬂow rates are, therefore, observed, and mostly shifted to oﬀ-peak slots.
The only exceptions to the oﬀ-peak operation of the pump system occur when the
speciﬁed minimum tank volume cannot be ensured exclusively during these periods.
In this case, the pump system is operated during on-peak billing periods, in order to
ensure the tank volume bounds. Finally, smaller pump ﬂow rates are reported on the
last day of each week. They stem from the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill
criterion, considered for thisα weight. The consequences of the smaller pump ﬂow
rates are twofold. First, smaller volume of non-potable is stored in the water, and the
power demand from the pump system is observed to be smaller during the last day
of each week. Second, the reported smaller power demand of the pump system also
contributes to a reduction of the weekly electricity bill.
For eachα weight, the planned operation of the controllable appliances is sub-
sequently simulated over each week, considering the actual non-controllable demand
and PV microgeneration. In all simulated weeks, an overﬂow of the water tank is
observed during some slots. It occurs when the volume of non-potable water stored
in the tank, computed according to (3.23), exceeds the imposed maximum capacity.
In order to avoid this phenomenon, and to promote the robustness of the controllable
appliance schedules, with respect to high non-controllable demand, a pruning strat-
egy is proposed to the electrical pump. According to this strategy, the planned pump
operation is discarded when the tank is full, reduced when the volume of non-potable
water stored in the tank would exceed the maximum capacity, or increased if the tank
storage drops below the prescribed minimum capacity. The observed overﬂow of the
tank is due to an overestimation of the non-controllable non-potable water demand,
carried out during the generation of the representative proﬁle.
In Figures 3.4 and 3.5, the eﬀects of the proposed pump pruning strategy in the
controllable demand and water tank volume are presented, forα = 0.5 and the week
with higher demand. The reader is recalled of the direct proportionality of the pump
system ﬂow rate and its power demand.
In Figure 3.5, the proposed pruning strategy is shown to avoid the tank overﬂow,
throughouttheentireweek. Moreover, ascanbeinferredfromFigure3.4, asubstantial
portion of the weekly household power demand, and consequently of the electricity
bill, stems from the operation of the electrical pump system. Therefore, two major
beneﬁts of the proposed pump pruning strategy are noted. First, it ensures that the
volumesofnon-potablewaterstoredinthetankremainwithintheestablishedcapacity
thresholds. Second, as the proposed strategy mainly reduces or removes the pump
system operation, it also contributes to the reduction of the weekly electricity bill.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 76
Figure 3.4: Original CRH controllable load demand (top) and after
pump system operation pruning (bottom), forα = 0.5. Image taken
from (Leitão et al., 2021), under the Creative Commons 4.0 license7.
Similar tank overﬂow behaviour is also observed for otherα weights, and overcame
by considering the proposed pump pruning strategy. Finally, it should be mentioned
that, given the simplicity of this strategy, its real-time implementation is feasible.
Unlike what was observed for the water tank, under-charging or over-charging
of the battery system is not observed for any week andα weight. In Figure 3.6,
the battery SOC is presented for the week with higher demand andα = 0.5. Two
main ﬁndings are drawn from Figure 3.6. First, about a single complete charge and
discharge cycle of the battery system is carried out per day of the horizon. Second,
nighttime, cheaper, grid tariﬀs are heavily exploited for the charging of this device,
except in the last day of the week, for which it is not charged once the minimum
SOC value is reached. Similar management of the battery system is observed for
diﬀerentα weights, except forα = 0. Forα = 0, microstorage is not exploited, and
the battery SOC value remains unchanged and equal to its initial value, throughout
each week. This is explained by the fact that, forα = 0, the resident discomfort is the
only scheduling criterion being considered. As the operation of the battery system
does not inﬂuence the discomfort of the residents, any feasible operation of this device
is accepted. In light of these statements, the fact that the chosen mathematical
optimisation solver privileges solutions with fewer nonzero variables further justiﬁes
the absence of the operation of the battery system.
3.3.2 Traditional Receding Horizon
The traditional RH-based allocation of the controllable appliances follows the same
trends reported for the CRH approach. Likewise for the CRH-based scheduling, the
7https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 77
Figure 3.5: Original CRH tank volume (top) and after pump system
operation pruning (bottom), forα = 0.5. Image taken from (Leitão
et al., 2021), under the Creative Commons 4.0 license8.
shifting of the clothes washing and tumble dryer loads is conditioned by the contribu-
tion of the minimisation of the electricity bill to the corresponding objective function.
As such, afternoon operation of the washer and dryer, along with nighttime dish-
washer scheduling, are observed fromα = 0 to α = 0.5, aligned with the preferences
of the residents. Nonetheless, for thisα weight range, the start of a clothes washing,
tumble dryer or dishwasher load is delayed by one hour in some weeks, in comparison
to the CRH-based allocation. Forα = 0.75, it is observed that the weekly loads of the
clothes washing and tumble dryer machines are more frequently shifted to oﬀ-peak
periods, in particular during the ﬁrst slots of each day. Forα = 1, the oﬀ-peak op-
eration of the controllable appliances prevails, while the overnight operation of the
clothes washing and tumble dryer machines is more frequent than for the CRH case.
No signiﬁcant diﬀerence in the operation of the pump system and the dynamics
of the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank is observed, in comparison to
CRH. Likewise CRH-based allocation, excessive operation of the pump system and the
overﬂow of the water tank are, once again, observed, resulting from an overestimation
of the non-controllable non-potable water demand. As with the CRH-based schedul-
ing, the proposed pump pruning strategy is shown capable of avoiding overﬂows of
the water tank. The management of the battery system is also shown to follow CRH
planning trends. In all days of the week, except in the last day, one complete charge
and discharge cycle of the battery system is performed. With respect to the manage-
ment of the batter system, the main diﬀerence between the CRH and the standard
RH strategies is concerned with the last day of the week. As reported in Section 3.3.1
for the CRH approach, the battery is not charged during the last day of the week.
On the other hand, in the traditional RH, nighttime battery charging is carried out in
the last day of the week. Indeed, while a single-day horizon is considered in the last
8https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 78
Figure 3.6: Original CRH battery SOC, forα = 0.5. Image taken
from (Leitão et al., 2021), under the Creative Commons 4.0 license9.
day of the week under CRH-based scheduling, a seven-day horizon is instead adopted
in standard RH. As such, battery recharging is ensured within a RH framework to
account for the household demand at the beginning of the next week. Because battery
charging takes place at nighttime (oﬀ-peak slots), all the required power is provided
by the grid, which increases the weekly electricity bill.
It should also be mentioned that, in traditional RH, the optional allocation of the
clothes washing and tumble dryer machines, during the second week, is considered.
The reason why this allocation is optional is due to the fact that only a portion of the
second week is covered in the horizon of the daily optimisation problems. The decision
to schedule these two loads during the second week is entirely dependent on the value
of theα weight considered in each instance of the optimisation problem. Forα = 1,
the two appliances are never scheduled during the second week. The reason for not
scheduling these two loads stems from the fact that, for thisα weight, only the weekly
electricity bill criterion is minimised. In this case, all optional loads of the controllable
appliances are discarded, in order to minimise the weekly electricity bill. Conversely,
when the resident discomfort is considered, either as the only criterion (α = 0), or
togetherwiththeelectricitybill( α∈{ 0.25, 0.5, 0.75}), theclotheswashingandtumble
dryer loads are scheduled for the second week. Indeed, forα∈{ 0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75}, the
smaller contribution assigned to the weekly electricity bill means that the electricity
bill increase, resulting from scheduling a clothes washing and tumble dryer load in
the second week, is inferior to the resident discomfort increase resulting from not
scheduling these two loads. As such, for theseα weights, it is more advantageous to
plan these loads one week in advance.
3.3.3 Day-Ahead Allocation
The main diﬀerence between the DA strategy and its CRH and standard RH counter-
parts is the allocation of the washer and dryer machines. For each day of the week, a
single-day horizon is speciﬁed in the DA strategy, in which the operation of the clothes
9https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 79
washing and tumble dryer machines is optional. If these appliances are scheduled for
any given day, their operation will not be planned in the following days of the same
week. On the other hand, the allocation of the clothes washing and the tumble dryer
loads is mandatory in the last day of the week, if these two appliances have not yet
been operated in past days of the same week.
From α = 0 to α = 0.5, in all 30 weeks, the weekly operation of the clothes
washing and tumble dryer machines is always scheduled for the ﬁrst day of the week.
This is carried out to minimise the resident discomfort in the ﬁrst day of the horizon,
because it is the criterion with higher contribution to the objective function, forα∈
{0; 0.25; 0.5}. Conversely, forα∈{ 0.75; 1}, the operation of the clothes washing and
the tumble dryer machines is deferred to oﬀ-peak slots of the last day of the week.
This deferment is carried out to minimise the weekly electricity bill which, for these
α weights, comprises the criterion with higher contribution to the objective function.
In a total of ﬁve weeks, exceptions to the above described allocations are noted. In
these weeks, the clothes washing and tumble dryer loads are operated in the ﬁrst
two days of the respective weeks. Concerning the dishwasher machine, its nighttime
operation, from 2 am to 4 am (slots 3 and 4), remains the norm for every day of every
week. Nonetheless, in few days of some weeks, the corresponding dishwasher load is
anticipated by one slot, operating from 1 am to 3 am (slots 2 and 3).
Concerning the electrical pump, the water tank and the battery, operation trends
similartotheCRH-basedschedulingareobserved. Thepumpsystemispredominantly
operated during the oﬀ-peak slots, being its ﬂow rate gradually decreased with higher
electricity billα weights. Excessive operation of the electrical pump system is, once
again, veriﬁed, resulting in an overﬂow of the water tank. Likewise CRH and RH,
this problem is addressed by adopting the proposed pump pruning strategy, which
proposes to discard the excessive operations of the pump. As for the battery system,
it is charged during the ﬁrst hours of each day, and subsequently discharged during
daytime. This behaviour follows CRH and RH schedules. Unlike in RH, no battery
charging is considered at the end of the last day of the week, as subsequent days are
not contemplated in the scheduling horizon.
3.3.4 Ideal Appliance Schedules
In the ideal allocation, the demand and generation of the household is assumed to
be known, in each week, prior to the scheduling of the controllable appliances. The
ideal allocation strategy exploits this knowledge to determine, for each α weight,
the operation schedules that result in the lower values of the objective function of
the problem. Two additional considerations must be made at this point. First, it
should be noted that, as the future demand and generation is assumed to be known in
advance, there is no need to reschedule the operation of the controllable appliances at
the start of each day of the week. Second, the underlying assumption of this strategy is
not feasible in practice, as future demand and generation cannot be predicted without
error. As such, for eachα weight, the performance reported for the ideal allocation
deﬁnes the lower bounds for the weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort criteria.
As with the CRH, RH and DA approaches previously discussed, for smallerα,
the controllable appliances are predominantly operated during the periods of higher
resident preference, in order to minimise the resident discomfort. As α grows, in
particular forα∈{ 0.75; 1}, the washer and dryer loads are shifted towards oﬀ-peak
billing periods, to reduce the weekly electricity bill. For theseα weights, the operation
of the dishwasher machine remains aligned with the residents preferred operation

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 80
times, which coincide with oﬀ-peak tariﬀs. For this reason, the operation of the
dishwasher machine remains mostly unchanged forα∈{ 0.75; 1}.
Concerning the electrical pump system, a similar trend to that reported in the
previous paragraph is also shown for the ideal allocation. For higherα weights, the
ﬂow rate of the pump system is coordinated with the clothes washing machine, in order
to pump the strictly necessary volumes that ensure the satisfaction of the minimum
volumes of the tank over the week. By doing so, both the power demand of the pump
system power and the household weekly electricity bill are minimised, and the volume
of non-potable water stored in the tank is guaranteed to remain within the established
capacity bounds. In other words, no water tank overﬂow occurs.
Finally, a similar management of the battery storage system is carried out. Like-
wise for previously discussed approaches, no battery charging or discharging is pro-
moted forα = 0. The reasons for this decision are twofold. First, the operation of the
battery system has no impact on the resident discomfort, which is the only scheduling
criterion considered forα = 0. Second, as the weekly electricity bill is not considered
for α = 0, the overall scheduling performance is not further improved by exploiting
any available storage. As higher α weights are considered, the battery system be-
comes increasingly more necessary for power storage for posterior domestic supply.
Therefore, charging and discharging of this device becomes more frequent. Nighttime
charging of the battery system, such that it is posteriorly discharged during the day
remains the norm for allα, exceptα = 0. When a substantial contribution is given to
the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill, i.e. forα∈{ 0.75; 1}, the coordination
of the battery system charging and discharging with the household demand and the
PV microgeneration is further enhanced. Likewise for the management of the pump
system, only the strictly necessary power is stored in the battery.
3.3.5 Typical Allocation
The typical appliance allocation is solely determined by the residents of the household.
As such, the minimisation of either the weekly electricity bill or the resident discomfort
is not considered, and therefore there is no need to specify anyα weight. For the
residential home considered in this work, controllable appliances are shown to be
operated by the residents at diﬀerent times of the day, in every day of the 30 weeks.
The cycles of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines remain predominantly
operated around noon, with few exceptions in some weeks, in which these appliances
are scheduled for later in the day, starting at around 6 pm. On the other hand, the
operation of the dishwasher machine is not shown to follow any concrete pattern. In
some days of some weeks, its nighttime operation is observed, following the operation
trends of the remaining strategies. In other days, a full load of dishes is, instead,
washed during the day, either at noon or during the afternoon.
Concerning the electrical pump system, its allocation is constrained to speciﬁc
periods of the day, contrasting with its operation determined with the CRH, RH, DA
and ideal allocation, where this device is operated throughout the day. In the typical
allocation, the water tank is also shown to be capable of ensuring the domestic non-
potable water supply over a two-day period, after which it must be reﬁlled via the
pump system. As such, around three complete tank reﬁlls are carried out over the
courseofanygivenweek. Theresultingintenseoperationoftheelectricalpumpsystem
is predominantly carried out at nighttime, exploiting oﬀ-peak tariﬀs to mitigate the
underlying impact on the weekly electricity bill. Nonetheless, it should be pointed out
that the tank can also be reﬁlled during the day, as long as the established threshold
for the minimum capacity of the tank is reached.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 81
As for the battery system, similar behaviour to that reported for the water tank
is observed. The main diﬀerence between the management of the two systems is the
recharging frequency of the battery. In comparison to hthe ousehold power demand
needs, the capacity of the battery is rather low. As such, its charging and discharging
are more frequent, amounting to around three complete daily charge–discharge cycles.
3.3.6 Load Deferment Analysis
In the previous subsections, similar scheduling solutions for the operation of the con-
trollable appliances were reported for the CRH, RH and DA strategies. For smaller
α weights, the controllable loads are shifted towards slots of higher preference of the
residents. While the resident discomfort criterion is improved by this allocation, it
leads, as a side eﬀect, to higher weekly electricity bill, due to the on-peak operation of
the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines. Conversely, when the minimisation of
the weekly electricity bill is favoured, in the form of higherα weights, all controllable
loads are shifted to oﬀ-peak periods. Oﬀ-peak load shifting results, in turn, in higher
resident discomfort, stemming from shifting the operation of the clothes washing and
tumble dryer machines away from its preferable slots.
Thefactthat, forhigh αweights, allcontrollableappliancesareshiftedtowardsoﬀ-
peak periods, also suggests insuﬃcient local PV generation surplus; otherwise, these
loads would had been exclusively scheduled for the periods of PV microgeneration. In
Figure 3.7, the non-controllable demand and local PV microgeneration of the house-
hold are presented over one week. It can be inferred from Figure 3.7 that local PV
generation is insuﬃcient to ensure the non-controllable demand, for almost the entire
week. Few slots of generation surplus are identiﬁed, in which the non-controllable
demand is surpassed by the local PV microgeneration. Nevertheless, the observed
generation surplus is small in value, and cannot supply a complete washer, dryer or
dishwasher load. In this scenario, controllable load demand supply is, inevitably, fully
ensured by the grid. As a result, the storage capacity of the battery system and
oﬀ-peak billing slots are leveraged to minimise the weekly electricity bill.
Figure 3.7: Non-controllable demand and local PV microgeneration
of the household, for one week.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 82
In Figure 3.8, the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort are presented
for all of the ﬁveα weights, and for the week with maximum demand. The reader
is again reminded that the typical appliance allocation is not inﬂuenced by any par-
ticular α weight. The performance of the typical appliance operation is, nonetheless,
presented for eachα weight for easier comparison against the other strategies. In
Figure 3.8: Electricity bill (top) and resident discomfort (bottom),
evaluated for all approaches, for the week with maximum demand,
and allα weights. Image taken from (Leitão et al., 2021), under the
Creative Commons 4.0 license10.
Figure 3.8, a trade-oﬀ between the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort
is shown. For the considered week, no signiﬁcant variation in the resident discomfort
is observed as a function of theα weights and the scheduling approaches, including
the ideal allocation. In order to justify the observed similar resident discomfort per-
formance, it should be noted that the resident discomfort criterion is only inﬂuenced
by the slot of the day in which the washer, dryer and dishwasher machines are oper-
ated. Indeed, according to the speciﬁcation of this criterion in (3.7), scheduling the
same appliance to the same slots of the day, across diﬀerent days, results in the same
resident discomfort. As presented in Sections 3.3.1–3.3.3, the three above mentioned
appliances remain scheduled mostly to the same slots of the day, under the sameα
parametrisation. While, in some days of some weeks, individual loads of any one of
these appliances may be anticipated or delayed by up to two slots, a small variation of
the corresponding preferences of the residents is often observed. This justiﬁes similar
performance according to the resident discomfort criterion.
Concerning the weekly electricity bill, the gap between the performance of the
ideal allocation and the CRH, RH and DA strategies is more noticeable, in particular
for α = 0. When the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill is considered, i.e.
for α∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1}, the typical allocation is shown to be outperformed by the
10https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 83
CRH, RH, DA and ideal allocation. However, forα = 0, when just the minimisation
of the resident discomfort is considered, the typical allocation matches its competing
strategies in terms of the weekly electricity bill.
The worst performance according to the resident discomfort criterion is registered
for the typical appliance allocation, even forα = 1, a weight for which this criterion
is not considered. As previously argued, no clear operation pattern is shown in the
historical appliance usage records, collected for each one of the 30 weeks. Moreover,
the absence of information concerning the preferred operation times for each appli-
ance motivated the estimation of these periods from the available one-year historical
records. The implications of not having clear patterns of the appliance operations,
and the absence of information concerning the preferred operation times, are twofold.
First, if no clear operation pattern is identiﬁed, the estimation procedure proposed
in (Pamulapati et al., 2020) results in multiple day slots with higher preferences of
residents. As each appliance is only operated during few slots of the day, an overall
high resident discomfort value is, inevitably, obtained. Second, the observed appli-
ance operation over each of the 30 weeks may not correspond to a preferred operation
setting. The observed load scheduling could be due to a suboptimal behaviour of the
residents, with no particular intention of minimising their discomfort. Moreover, the
scheduling of some loads to periods of higher discomfort could have also been carried
out due to unexpected events, such as late home arrival after work.
The absence of information about the preferences of the residents motivated a
compromise between the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill and the resident
discomfort, by settingα = 0.5. Nonetheless, in real-world scenarios, inquiries should
be submitted to household residents, so that a reliableα weight could, instead, be
selected. In Figures 3.9 and 3.10, the performance according to the weekly electricity
bill and the resident discomfort is, respectively, presented for the CRH, RH, DA, ideal
allocation and typical appliance operation, over the 30 weeks and assumingα = 0.5.
Moreover, in Figures 3.11–3.15 the operation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and
dishwasher machines is presented for this scenario.
For the novel CRH proposal, the average weekly electricity bill over the 30 weeks
is 24.26 c.u., with 24.42 c.u. registered for the traditional RH, 24.37 c.u. for the DA,
22.38 c.u. for the ideal allocation, and 26.79 c.u. for the typical appliance operation.
Concerning the resident discomfort, an average performance of 0.88 is registered for
the CRH, traditional RH, DA and the ideal allocation, while an average resident
discomfort of 0.96 is registered for the typical appliance operation. For the chosenα,
the typical appliance operation is consistently outperformed, in terms of the weekly
electricity bill and the resident discomfort, in each week, by all the other strategies.
Conversely, forα = 0.5, the ideal allocation outperforms all other strategies in terms
of the electricity bill, in every single week. As for the CRH, RH and DA approaches,
no one is shown to consistently outperform the others, according to both the weekly
electricity bill and the resident discomfort. In the case of the resident discomfort, no
diﬀerence is found between any of the three above mentioned strategies, in any one
of the 30 weeks. As for the weekly electricity bill, the CRH strategy outperforms the
other two by a small margin of no more than 0.16 currency unit, over all weeks.
The computational burden of the CRH, traditional RH and DA strategies is also
assessed. In Figure 3.16, the computation time of the CRH, RH and DA strategies is
presented in box-and-whisker plots, for each day of the horizon and all ﬁveα weights.
The computation time is retrieved using thestatistics command of the SCIP solver.
For each day of the horizon, the allocation of the controllable appliances is shown
to be carried out very fast, in a matter of few seconds. The smallest computation
time is registered for the DA strategy which, in any day of the horizon, is determined

almost instantly. In the case of the novel CRH approach, Figure 3.16 shows that the

In the prototype smart home case study considered in this work, the daily appli-

the horizon. Taking into consideration the performed discretisation of the scheduling
horizon into hourly slots, a 1 h computation time limit is imposed for the daily appli-

is carried out in few seconds, well below the 1 h computation time limit.

Figure 3.10: Resident discomfort for CRH, RH, DA and ideal
scheduling over the 30 weeks, considering α = 0 .5. Image taken
from (Leitão et al., 2021), under the Creative Commons 4.0 license12.
improvement in both the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort.
In light of this discussion, it is argued that adopting a compressive receding hori-
zon, in detriment of smaller or larger, ﬁxed-length, horizons, is more aligned with the
weekly appliance usage. In summary, this statement is supported by two points. The
ﬁrst is concerned with the absence of optional appliance scheduling in the individual
problem instances. As for the second, it has to do with the fact that no additional
constraints need to be speciﬁed in the daily optimisation problem instances, in order
to address the aforementioned optional load scheduling. The reader is directed to
Appendix A.3, where the complete formulation of the MILP optimisation problem in-
stances, considered at each new day of the week under the traditional RH approach, is
provided. As presented in this Appendix, the optional allocation of some controllable
appliances requires the speciﬁcation of extra constraints.
3.4 Synthesis
This chapter addressed the problem of week-ahead scheduling of domestic electrical
appliances, in a four-resident smart home prototype. The allocation of the control-
lable appliances was carried out by deterministic optimisation-based techniques. For
any given week, a complete clothes washing and tumble dryer operation cycle, daily
dishwasher machine operation, and the operation of an electrical pump system, used
to ﬁll an adjacent water tank with non-potable groundwater, were managed.

Day of the week
Figure 3.16: Controllable appliance computation time for each day
of the horizon. Image taken from (Leitão et al., 2020), under the
Creative Commons 4.0 license13.
A CRH-based approach was proposed for the week-ahead scheduling of the control-
lable domestic appliances. In this approach, forecasts of the local PV microgeneration
and proﬁles of non-controllable power and non-potable water demand are exploited
to plan the operation times of the controllable appliances. The daily controllable ap-
pliance rescheduling was promoted, under a compressive rolling horizon. In the ﬁrst
day, the operation of the appliances was scheduled over the entire week, by solving
an instance of a MILP problem. Only the operations planned for the ﬁrst day were
implemented, and the process was, subsequently, repeated for the next day. In the
second day, a six-day horizon was considered, obtained by shifting the ﬁrst day of
the horizon and maintaining its last day unchanged. The CRH-based controllable
appliance allocation was employed over 30 diﬀerent weeks, taking as the scheduling
objective a weighted sum of the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort.
The CRH-based appliance operation was found to be similar to that of the tradi-
tional RH and DA, in particular regarding the operation of the dishwasher and the
electrical pump system. These two appliances were mainly scheduled for oﬀ-peak pe-
riods, exploiting cheaper tariﬀs. The competitive performance of the CRH with that
of traditional RH and DA allocation, according to the weekly electricity bill and the
resident discomfort, was reported. For the CRH, RH and DA strategies, scheduling
of the controllable appliances was carried out based on a single realisation of the non-
controllable demand and the PV microgeneration, representing a possible real-world
scenario for a typical family. Oﬀ-peak shifting of the operation of the controllable ap-
pliances was observed, to which contributed an overestimation of the non-controllable
demand, in its considered realisation. In the case of the electrical pump system, the
overestimated non-controllable demand inﬂuenced its operation frequency and inten-
sity, which, ultimately, resulted in overﬂows of the water tank. Such overﬂows were
addressed by a simple, but eﬀective, pump pruning strategy, which discarded the ex-
cessive pump ﬂows when the tank is full, and carried out additional tank inﬂows if its
13https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 92
Figure 3.17: Histogram of cumulative electricity bill diﬀerence be-
tween CRH and ideal schedules. Coloured horizontal and vertical lines
are drawn for the 90th percentile of this distribution. Image taken
from (Leitão et al., 2020), under the Creative Commons 4.0 license14.
storage dropped below its minimum capacity. The proposed pump pruning strategy
was successful in avoiding water tank overﬂows, in all weeks and strategies. Further-
more, the resulting smaller and less frequent pump ﬂow rates contributed to a further
reduction of the household power demand and the weekly electricity bill.
CRH-based clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher operation was also
aligned with the ideal allocation, for which knowledge of the future demand and mi-
crogeneration was exploited for the optimal scheduling of the controllable appliances.
The main diﬀerence between these two strategies is the management of the electri-
cal pump system. Under the ideal allocation, smaller operation intensity was noted
for the pump system, pumping only the strictly necessary volumes that ensured the
minimum tank volume capacity. In 90% of the simulations, the weekly electricity bill
of the ideal allocation was not surpassed by the CRH-based scheduling by more than
2.54 c.u., whereas a negligible gap in the resident discomfort of 0.013 was observed.
The gaps of both the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort performance
are considered acceptable, given the fact that the future demand and generation can-
not be perfectly forecasted. Finally, the typical appliance operation carried out by
the residents was also assessed, being consistently outperformed in both criteria, by
the novel CRH proposal and other strategies, in all 30 weeks.
In comparison to the traditional RH and DA frameworks, the fact that the alloca-
tion of the appliances not required to operate every day is considered across diﬀerent
horizons, before making a decision, constitutes an intrinsic advantage of the CRH
framework. Moreover, the presented simulation results showed very fast appliance
scheduling time, not only for the novel CRH approach, but also for its traditional RH
and DA counterparts. Indeed, for the three strategies, the daily appliance allocation
was carried out in a matter of few seconds, well below the 1 h limit imposed for the
daily appliance operation rescheduling.

between CRH and ideal schedules. Coloured horizontal and vertical

In this chapter, the uncertainty in the future non-controllable demand and gener-
ation is explicitly removed from each scheduling problem, and replaced with single-
point forecasts. While simpler, lower-dimension and less computationally demanding
instances are obtained, an underlying dependency on the accuracy of the point fore-
casts is undeniable. Proper risk assessment and contingency planning are hindered
by the absence of additional uncertainty information, limiting the accommodation of
severe uncertainty realisations at both demand or supply-sides of the grid.
Over the last decade, the incorporation of probabilistic forecasts within decision-
making mechanisms such as stochastic programming, has received growing attention
in the literature (see Section 2.3.3). This integration has been predominantly moti-
vated to address the limitations pointed out in the previous paragraph. Unlike the
approaches considered in this chapter, stochastic programming frameworks adopt a
probabilisticmodellingofthesourcesofuncertaintyintheproblem, inordertoprovide
guarantees concerning the optimality and feasibility of the scheduling solutions. The
high computational load, due to the scenario-based approximation, constitutes their
main drawback. Even though similar and small (few seconds) CRH, traditional RH
and DA scheduling computation time was veriﬁed, the progressively smaller schedul-
ing horizons of the proposed CRH strategy may considerably reduce its computation
requirements, under stochastic programming-based formulations.
Thenextchaptersarededicatedtotheapplicationofstochasticprogrammingtech-
niques to the above described household appliance scheduling problem. In Chapter 4,
probabilistic modelling of the main sources of uncertainty in a smart home, namely
the non-controllable demand and the PV microgeneration, is covered in detail. On its
turn, in Chapter 5, the CRH-based scheduling approach is extended into a stochas-
tic programming variant, which explicitly addresses these problem uncertainties via
probabilistic modelling. The key results of this chapter are as follows:
15https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 3. Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 94
• Novel CRH optimisation-based strategy for week-ahead home controllable ap-
pliance scheduling. The novel strategy addresses the limitations of the standard
RH and DA scheduling variants. These limitations comprehend the inadequacy
of the scheduling horizon to the typical weekly appliance operation patterns. By
ﬁxing the last day of the horizon, the controllable appliances are always planned
over all of the remaining days of the current week. No optional operation of
the controllable appliances, resulting from covering only part of the scheduling
horizon, is considered. The controllable appliances are scheduled at the start
of each new day of the horizon, considering the updated demand proﬁles and
the generation forecasts. Appliance rescheduling is carried out, to address the
degradation of local PV generation models and other unexpected events.
• CRH-based appliance allocation, and consequently the weekly electricity bill and
the resident discomfort, is shown to be similar to that of the traditional RH and
DA. Anacceptablegap, in terms oftheweeklyelectricitybill, to theperformance
of the ideal allocation is observed. More precisely, in 90% of the simulations, the
CRH-based weekly electricity bill did not exceeded that of the ideal allocation
by more than 2.54 currency units. Indeed, taking into consideration that perfect
forecasts of the future non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration are not
possible, this gap is considered acceptable. Moreover, a negligible gap, in terms
of the resident discomfort, between the CRH and the ideal allocation strategies
was also registered. This further supported the claim of improved allocation
performance of the novel CRH strategy. Finally, a comparison of controllable
appliance operation times, determined with both the novel CRH strategy and
the typical appliance operation, clearly showed improvements in the CRH-based
weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort.
• The uncertainty in non-controllable demand and power generation was explicitly
discarded from the underlying scheduling problems. This hinders the subsequent
adoption of risk assessment and contingency planning measures, as they are de-
pendent on a single uncertainty realisation, in this case a representative proﬁle.
Considering the recent literature practices, the explicit integration of the uncer-
tainty models within the scheduling strategy has been identiﬁed as the primary
direction of future work. thus, it is explored in the upcoming chapters.

95
Chapter 4
Probabilistic Demand and
Generation Modelling
In Chapter 3, the problem of home electrical appliance scheduling was addressed via
a CRH optimisation-based strategy. The appliance operation times were determined
based on household non-controllable (electricity and water) demand proﬁles and fore-
casts of the local PV microgeneration. The non-controllable demand proﬁles were
generated based on historical records of the selected household, and represent a possi-
ble real-world scenario of intense non-controllable demand. This assumption of intense
non-controllable demand is deliberately carried out to promote the robustness of the
underlying appliance schedules, with respect to the non-controllable demand. Despite
the competitive performance with state of the art traditional RH and DA strate-
gies, and near-ideal electricity bill performance, the CRH-based schedule solutions
are highly tuned for the speciﬁc proﬁles provided. Thus, the versatility of any risk
assessment and contingency planning measures adopted by the HEMS is rather small.
This is because the HEMS-based controllable appliance allocation is dependent on the
individual realisation of the household non-controllable power demand and generation,
in this case the representative demand proﬁles and generation forecasts.
In Section 2.3.3, uncertainty-aware decision-making is introduced in the context of
smarthomeenergymanagementandappliancescheduling. Inthissection, thebaseline
load demand, appliance operation needs, appliance consumption, local microgenera-
tion, and utility grid prices are highlighted as the ﬁve main sources of uncertainty
in energy management and appliance scheduling problems. The explicit modelling
of uncertain information, and its integration in scheduling mechanisms, is also em-
phasized in this context. Mathematical optimisation-based strategies are found to
be particularly suited for explicit modelling of problem uncertainties, and also for its
integration in existing scheduling frameworks. Within mathematical optimisation-
based approaches, stochastic programming and robust optimisation stand out for
their decision-making support in the presence of uncertain information. While ro-
bust optimisation is only focused on worst-case uncertainty realisations, stochastic
programming exploits all possible realisations, when making optimal decisions. In
this approach, decisions are made for the expected value of the objective function of
the problem. Probabilistic models of the uncertainties are also exploited to determine
this expected value, and provide probabilistic guarantees on solution feasibility.
In this chapter, probabilistic non-parametric GP regression-based models are de-
veloped to describe household non-controllable power demand and PV microgenera-
tion, based on historical records of the smart home, presented in Section 3.1.1. The
developed GP-based models are intended to be posteriorly integrated in a stochastic
programming-based appliance scheduling framework, which is covered in Chapter 5.
A stochastic programming optimisation-based framework is chosen to address control-
lable appliance scheduling problems, rather than a robust optimisation alternative, in

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 96
order to favour optimality over robustness of determined appliance operation solu-
tions, against extreme non-controllable demand and generation realisations. In the
case of the appliance scheduling problem addressed in Chapter 3, it is argued that the
robustness of the reported scheduling solutions does not assume a critical role as in
other contexts, such as in the management of the grid infrastructure. Furthermore, by
exploiting all possible uncertainty realisations, morewell-rounded solutions, with bet-
ter performance in the presence of multiple realisations, can be obtained by stochastic
programming scheduling frameworks.
The reasons for describing the household non-controllable power demand and PV
microgeneration via GP-based frameworks are threefold. First, the growing popular-
ity of this technique in the uncertainty-aware decision-making literature (Hong and
Fan, 2016; Yang et al., 2018; Sharifzadeh et al., 2019). Second, the absence of prior
distribution assumptions, due to the non-parametric nature of GP models. Third, the
ability of GP models to predict the full posterior probability distribution function,
which is not supported by state of the art alternatives like QR, for which only predic-
tion intervals (PIs) are estimated (Cox, 1975; Heskes, 1996). Moreover, for stochastic
programming frameworks, the estimation of the full posterior probability distribution
function constitutes a requirement. In this chapter, diﬀerent composite GP covariance
function alternatives are proposed and evaluated, motivated by common household
demand and generation habits. The predictive performance of the GP models is com-
pared against that of random forest-based QR, via deterministic and probabilistic
metrics. The main contributions of this chapter are summarised as follows:
• Development of probabilistic GP regression-based models to describe demand
from non-controllable sources and PV microgeneration, of a smart home en-
vironment prototype. The probabilistic models are developed based on the
historical demand and generation records. These records are collected over a
two-year period, for a typical family of four members. To the best of the au-
thor knowledge, the simultaneous probabilistic modelling of these two sources is
under-represented in the literature, to which this chapter makes a contribution.
• Novelcovariancefunctionalternatives areproposed fortheGPregressionmodels
developed to describe the smart home load demand and the local power genera-
tion. The novelty in the proposed covariance functions lies within their periodic
components, in the form of cosine functions, combined with other, more conven-
tional, radial basis function (RBF) and rational quadratic (RQ) functions. The
proposal of these covariance functions is motivated by the common household
demand and generation habits.
• The predictive performance of the developed GP models is compared with that
of the probabilistic QR methods. The performance of the GP models is eval-
uated according to both deterministic and probabilistic metrics, assessing the
sharpness and reliability of the computed forecasts. Superior predictive perfor-
mance of the developed GP models, in comparison to their QR alternatives, is
clearly shown for both non-controllable demand and microgeneration.
The remainder of this chapter is organised as follows. In Section 4.1, a summary of
the literature on building load demand and generation forecasting is presented. In this
section, a theoretical background on the state of the art GP and QR techniques is also
provided. In Section 4.2, a description of the collected historical aggregate demand
and generation datasets is presented, along with the probabilistic forecasting method-
ology adopted throughout this chapter. Five main steps compose this methodology,
namely the data pre-processing and input representation, GP covariance functions,
performance criteria, CV-based model evaluation, and the heuristic-based selection

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 97
of GP and QR conﬁgurations with superior predictive performance. In Section 4.3,
CV results are discussed for the GP and QR models. Distinct conﬁgurations of each
predictive technique, with the best overall performance, are selected for further eval-
uation in a separate test dataset. The underlying predictive modelling results are
presented and compared against published literature works, in which the same tech-
niques were used to model demand and power generation data from smart homes.
Finally, a consolidated overview of this chapter is provided in Section 4.4, where the
main contributions and possible future work directions are highlighted.
4.1 Related Work
In this section, the literature on building demand and generation forecasting is sum-
marised. Single point and probabilistic-based forecasting strategies are discussed in
Section 4.1.1, being the most popular and trending techniques highlighted in the
context of uncertainty-aware decision-making. Following the probabilistic forecasting
literature, GP and QR are highlighted for probabilistic modelling and forecasting of
building demand and generation. As a result, in-depth theoretical background on
these two techniques is provided, respectively, in Sections 4.1.2 and 4.1.3.
4.1.1 Building Demand and Generation Forecasting
For many years, power grid decision-makers have relied on single point forecasts of the
expected aggregate customer demand, in order to plan grid operation (Hong and Fan,
2016). Extensive research into single point forecasting of building demand and renew-
able power generation has, therefore, been carried out, see e.g. (Hong and Fan, 2016;
Hippert et al., 2001; McSharry et al., 2005; van der Meer et al., 2018b; Sharifzadeh
et al., 2019). In the load demand literature, forecasting is reported to be primar-
ily carried out based on historical data, either alone or exploiting additional forecast
information regarding weather or ambient temperature, just to name out a few. In
contrast, two distinct trends are identiﬁed in the generation forecasting literature. On
the one hand, the power output of diﬀerent generators has been predicted in the same
way as the demand, i.e. directly from historical generation and forecast information.
On the other hand, indirect forecasting has also been carried out. In this approach,
e.g., weather-related information such as the global horizontal irradiance or the wind
speed, is initially predicted. Subsequently, available generator models, e.g. of wind
turbines or PV panels, are exploited to estimate the corresponding power output.
With respect to the single point forecasts, data-driven methods are highlighted
for both demand and generation data (van der Meer et al., 2018b; Sharifzadeh et al.,
2019). ANNs, namely multilayer perceptron (Zhang et al., 2018a; Moon et al., 2018),
deep belief networks (Dedinec et al., 2016; Zhang et al., 2019), CNNs (Wang et al.,
2017;Zahidetal.,2019;Huangetal.,2020), extremelearningmachines(Hossainetal.,
2017; Hu and Chen, 2018), LSTM and other recurrent ANN architectures (Qing and
Niu, 2018; Wang et al., 2020), have emerged among diﬀerent data-driven techniques.
OthernotedmethodsincludeSVMs(Hong,2011;Chenetal.,2017,2018b;Zahidetal.,
2019) and regression trees (Troncoso et al., 2015). Hybrid approaches, combining
Gaussian and Morlet wavelet time-series decomposition with deep CNN and LSTM
models, have also been considered (Wang et al., 2017; Liu et al., 2018b).
From a decision-making perspective, the most notorious drawback of the single
point forecast is the limited conveyed process information, in the form of a single
forecast of the expected value. Indeed, making decisions considering only a given
concrete uncertainty realisation, e.g. expected grid demand at a given hour, threatens

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 98
the performance and the safety if diﬀerent realisations are observed in practice (Leitão
et al., 2020). Moreover, as any forecast is inherently erroneous, error quantiﬁcation
is also extremely important, in particular to support the adoption of proper risk
management measures (Salinas et al., 2020).
In probabilistic forecasting, future state is modelled by either PDF estimates or
PIs, determined for a given conﬁdence level. The probabilistic forecasts can be ob-
tained mainly in two ways, namely (i) relying on purely probabilistic methods, and
(ii) modifying existing point forecast techniques (Hong and Fan, 2016). On the one
hand, probabilistic forecasting is speciﬁcally targeted in QR (Ben Taieb et al., 2016;
Liu et al., 2017) or GP regression (van der Meer et al., 2018a; Yang et al., 2018).
On the other hand, forecast post-processing and technique enhancements are encom-
passed in the second group. Forecast post-processing comprehends techniques such as
probabilistic modelling of residuals, or combination of multiple single point forecasts,
e.g. via scenario-based techniques. Finally, enhancements of existing techniques are
often aimed at replacing point forecasts with PIs, among which ANNs are a popular
choice (Sharifzadeh et al., 2019).
In (van der Meer et al., 2018b), parametric forecasting techniques are distinguished
from their non-parametric counterparts. Parametric techniques assume that the deter-
ministic forecasts, or its errors, are modelled by an a priori known family of probability
distributions (Xie et al., 2017). On the other hand, no such assumptions are made
by the non-parametric techniques. Therefore, in non-parametric modelling, both the
probability distribution function and its underlying parameters are estimated from
available data. Within the relevant literature of non-parametric probabilistic load de-
mand and renewable power generation forecasting, QR (Wang et al., 2019; González
Ordiano et al., 2020), GP regression (Shepero et al., 2018; Guan et al., 2021), gradient
boosting (Kumari and Toshniwal, 2021; Venkat et al., 2021) and kernel density esti-
mation (Arora and Taylor, 2016; He et al., 2016) are highlighted. The literature on
probabilistic load and generation forecast is proliﬁc in univariate methods, for which
aggregate demand or generation loads are estimated (De Gooijer and Hyndman, 2006;
Mashlakov et al., 2021). In univariate probabilistic forecasting, modelling and predic-
tion of a single variable, denoting the demand or the generation, is considered. In this
situation, correlations with other variables are ignored, which can penalise modelling
performance, particularly in the presence of complex temporal, spatial, and cross-
lagged correlations, such as in power systems (Lenzi et al., 2018). These shortcomings
have motivated recent research on multivariate methods. In (Bracale et al., 2020b;
Xing et al., 2020), multivariate QR models are studied for two-step cooperative short-
term probabilistic forecasting of multiple domestic loads. In (Bracale et al., 2020a),
an extension of the multivariate model of (Bracale et al., 2020b) is proposed to simul-
taneously forecast active and reactive power of a given industrial load. More recently,
deep learning-based techniques have also been applied to multivariate forecasting of
electricity load, generation and grid prices in power systems (Mashlakov et al., 2021).
Thus, it is evident that considerable research attention has been devoted, not
only to the development of new techniques, but also to the tuning of existing ones to
new datasets. Despite the contributions stemming from these eﬀorts, in (Sharifzadeh
et al., 2019), the lack of a globally accepted framework for consistent comparison and
evaluation of existing techniques is identiﬁed. The authors of this work attempt to
improve the lack of comparison between diﬀerent techniques by performing an ex-
haustive comparison between three popular techniques, namely ANN, SVM and GP
regression. The fact that only deterministic metrics are considered does not allow for
an adequate evaluation of the GP regression-based forecasts, due to its probabilistic

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 99
nature. Indeed, it would have been more adequate to consider criteria such as the con-
tinuous ranked probability score (CRPS) (Gneiting and Katzfuss, 2014), supporting
the comparison of both deterministic and probabilistic forecasts. In the literature, QR
and GP regression models have shown promising results in probabilistic forecasting of
power demand and generation, see e.g. (Hong and Fan, 2016; Sharifzadeh et al., 2019).
With respect to GPs, their high process modelling ﬂexibility constitutes a recurring
highlighted advantage, owed to the chosen covariance function (Antunes et al., 2017).
Within the GP-based demand and generation forecasting literature works, the same
RBF and Matérn 3 and 5 covariance functions are often considered. Nonetheless, the
expectation of somewhat periodic behaviour in both demand and generation, in par-
ticular at household level, motivates research into alternative GP covariance functions,
such as composite structures with periodic components.
4.1.2 Gaussian Processes
In Deﬁnition 4.1.1, a formal deﬁnition of a Gaussian Process is presented.
Deﬁnition 4.1.1.(Rasmussen and Williams, 2006) For any setX, a Gaussian Pro-
cess onX is deﬁned as a collection of random variables{f (x) : x∈X} such that
∀n∈ N,∀x1,..., xn∈X , the vector(f (x1),...,f (xn)) is multivariate Gaussian.
In GP-based frameworks, the possibility of erroneous and noisy measurements
of process inputs or outputs is assumed. In GP regression problems, noisy process
output readings are usually assumed, whereas process inputs can be assumed to be
measured with or without noise. Let(xi,yi) be pairs of input vectors and noisy output
measurements of a given process, withxi∈ Rd denoting the input vector,yi∈ R the
noisy output, d is the dimension of each input vector, andi = 1,...,n . Process
outputs are described asyi =f (xi) +ϵ, fori = 1,...,n , withf (xi) representing the
true process output andϵ∼N

Under a GP-based framework,yi can be explained by including latent variables
f (xi), for i = 1,...,n . The complete collection of process outputs, i.e.y1,...,y n,
cannot be explained by a single Gaussian distribution. Instead, each valuef (xi), and
consequentlyyi, is modelled by a separate Gaussian distribution, fori = 1,...,n .
GPs generalise the Gaussian probability distribution in a function space, passing
through a set of observations, and supporting Bayesian inference. A GP f(x) is
completely speciﬁed by its mean functionm (x) and covariance function or kernel
k (x, x′), deﬁned in (4.1), wherex and x′ representd-dimensional GP input vectors.

The mean functionm (x) of a GP is a function which maps index pointsx to real
values. For notation simplicity and mathematical convenience, the mean function is
commonly assumed zero (Rasmussen and Williams, 2006). Assuming zero mean noisy
observations, the resulting GP is expressed asf (x)∼GP (0,k (x, x′)). For nonzero
mean data, a zero-mean GPf′ (x)∼GP (0,k (x, x′)), is deﬁned by considering points
f(x) to be obtained by mean data subtraction, withf′(x) = f(x)−m(x). The ker-
nel has a key role in GP modelling, specifying a prior distribution over functions, and
determining the properties of GP generalisation. GP kernels are often mistakenly con-
sidered to specify the similarity between any pair of objects, when in fact they specify
the similarity between two values of a function evaluated on each object (Duvenaud,

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 100
2014). In the remainder of this document, both covariance function and kernel terms
will be used interchangeably when referring tok (x, x′) of a GP.
Diﬀerent covariance functions make distinct assumptions concerning the underly-
ing process, namely with respect to stationarity or periodicity. The speciﬁcation of a
number of parameters, often referred to as GP hyperparametersθ, is also required,
strongly inﬂuencing the quality of the GP models. The values of the hyperparameters
are learnt from a training dataset, consisting of(xi,yi) pairs, fori = 1,...,n . The
values of the hyperparameters are determined in (4.2), by maximising the natural
logarithm of the marginal likelihood of observed output datay = (y1,...,y n), given
training inputs ˆ x= (x1,..., xn).

Likewise for hyperparameter tuning, noisy or noise-free observations can be considered

space of functions is estimated. To this end, a prior distribution of the training and
test outputs is deﬁned, and subsequently restricted to contain only the functions that
agree with the observed data points. Probabilistically, this restriction corresponds to
conditioning the joint prior distribution on gathered observations. The process output
forecasts f∗ = (f (x∗

In (4.3), (4.5), (4.7) and (4.9), the inversion of matrices of sizen×n is required.
In (Rasmussen and Williams, 2006), this is carried out via Cholesky decomposition,
due to its faster implementation and good approximation of the inverse matrices.
Nonetheless, this remains a major weakness of GP-based modelling, due to the cubic
time complexity,O
(
n3)
, of this operation.
Approximate or sparse GPs are considered to address the high computational
burden of matrix inversions in GP-based modelling. Instead of considering all training
data samples, in sparse GPs, the process to be modelled is only described by a small
set of inducing variables. In short, instead of considering pairs(xi,yi), fori = 1,...,n ,
only pairs(xj,yj) are considered, withj = 1,...,k andk usually considerably smaller
than n (Das et al., 2017; Leibfried et al., 2021). Finally, extensions to GPs have also
been proposed, in order to support the modelling of multiple, and correlated, process
outputs. The resulting extended GPs take the form of multi-output GPs, where
the speciﬁed prior distribution does not explain a set of functions, but rather the
correlation between process outputs (Álvarez and Lawrence, 2011; Liu et al., 2018a).
4.1.3 Quantile Regression
LetY be a real-valued random variable, with conditional probability density function
given byfY (y) =P (Y =y|X =x), and conditional cumulative distribution function
given byΘY (Y =y|X =x) =P (Y ≤y|X =x), deﬁned for a given predictorX =x.
Forα∈ [0, 1] and a givenX =x, theα-quantileQα;Y (x) of Y is deﬁned in (4.10) as
the smallest realisationY = y whose conditional probability distribution function is
greater or equal toα.
Qα;Y (x) = inf{y : ΘY (Y =y|X =x)≥α} (4.10)

QR approaches are categorised into parametric and non-parametric. Within non-
parametric quantile regression, quantile smoothing splines (Koenker et al., 1994) and
tree-based methods (Meinshausen, 2006; Zhang et al., 2018b) are highlighted. In this
chapter, random forest-based quantile regression, described in (Meinshausen, 2006)
and based on the standard random forest algorithm (Breiman, 2001), is considered.

4.2 Methodology
In this section, the methodology followed to model the residential demand and PV mi-
crogeneration is presented. This section starts with a brief description of the collected
historical datasets, preliminary exploratory data analysis and the main preprocessing
steps. Subsequently, the development of GP and QR models is discussed, covering
alternatives for input variables and GP covariance functions. The ﬁdelity of GP-
based and QR-based demand and PV generation forecasts is assessed via determin-
istic and probabilistic performance metrics, evaluated within a variant of time-series
CV. Finally, the procedure adopted for the selection of individual GP and QR model
conﬁgurations is presented.
4.2.1 Exploratory Data Analysis
The same single-family smart home case study presented in Section 3.1.1 is herein
considered. The non-controllable household power demand is described by real-world
historical aggregate records available in the Pecan Street Inc. Dataport dataset1,
collected for a single-family home, over a two-year period and on an hourly basis.
In the absence of historical generation records for the selected home, the PV panel
generation output is estimated as in Chapter 3. At any given time instant, the PV
panel generation output is estimated based on models of the PV panel system (Widen,
2011)andsolarradiation( Parametrization Model C)(Iqbal,1983), alongwithweather
forecast and cloud coverage data (Holmgren et al., 2018), and a deterministic cloud
cover model (Larson et al., 2016). As such, a two-year time-series of estimated micro
PV panel generation is obtained, considering a PV area of 10 m2, with an inclination
of 35 degrees towards the equator.
In Figures 4.1 and 4.2, the relative frequency histograms of the collected historical
demand and estimated PV generation are, respectively, presented. The relative fre-
quency histograms are drawn in blue, and superimposed with lognormal PDF plots,
drawn in orange. These plots are estimated using thescipy.stats.lognorm.ﬁtfunction
of the Pythonscipy library. A lognormal distribution with shapes = 1.22, location
µ = 0 and scaleσ = 0.38 is estimated for the power demand, whereas a distribution
with shapes = 3.49, locationµ = 0 and scaleσ = 0 is estimated for the PV micro-
generation. In Figure 4.1, asymmetric and positively skewed demand data (drawn in
blue) is clearly showed. As such, for this data source, a lognormal shape is strongly
suggested by the estimated PDF (same plot, drawn in orange). On the other hand, in
Figure 4.2, the generation data is shown to be substantially more skewed towards the
origin (drawn in blue). This is justiﬁed by the absence of PV generation after sunset.
The lognormal shape suggested for the distribution of historical power demand
records motivates a logarithm normalisation as in (Shepero et al., 2018), so that
Gaussian distributed data is obtained in the transformed space. Letτ be a lognormal
random variable modelling the household power demand. If τ ∼ LN(µ,σ ), then
υ = ln(τ)∼N (µ,σ ), whereln denotes the natural logarithm. Given the fact that
residential demand is allowed to be zero at any given hour, a value for which the
1https://dataport.cloud, accessed on: 06 September 2021.
