Figure 4.1: Relative frequency histogram of historical power demand
data (drawn in blue), superimposed with the probability density func-
tion of a lognormal distribution (drawn in orange).
natural logarithm is not deﬁned, a constant increment ofϕ = 0.001 kWh is added
to every dataset record. In short, “lognormalised” household power demand¯υ is
computedas ¯υ =ln(τ+ϕ), whereτ ishouseholdpowerdemand, ln denotesthenatural
logarithm, ¯υ∼N (¯µ, ¯σ) and ϕ = 0.001 kWh. Concerning the PV microgeneration,
while its lognormality is not as strongly suggested as for household demand, the same
normalisation is motivated by its strong skewness, as shown in Figure 4.2.
In Figures 4.3 and 4.4, the relative frequency histograms of the normalised house-
hold demand and PV generation are, respectively, presented. The relative frequency
histograms are drawn in blue, and superimposed with Gaussian PDF plots. These
plots are estimated using thescipy.stats.norm.ﬁtfunction of thescipy Python library.
A Gaussian distribution with meanµ =−0.96 and standard deviationσ = 1.21 is
estimated for normalised power demand, whereas a distribution with meanµ =−4.04
and standard deviationσ = 2.99 is estimated for the normalised PV generation.
From Figure 4.3, a bell shape is resembled from the normalised historical res-
idential power demand, albeit with some outliers for positive normalised demand.
Conversely, a considerably worse lognormal ﬁt is suggested for PV generation. In
Figure 4.4, a skewed histogram is presented for the PV microgeneration, supporting
the preponderance of null values which hinders the Gaussian distribution ﬁt.
Finally, a possible statistical relationship between the residential power demand
and the PV microgeneration is also investigated. If a meaningful relationship between
the two data sources is found, ﬁtting of only a single regression model to one of the
sources, e.g. power demand, may be considered, rather than the ﬁtting of two separate
models. In this case, the underlying relationship between the two data sources can
be exploited to derive forecasts of the other variable. Given the high computational
burden of GP model training, due to the inversion of squared matrices, this approach
would allow a considerable drop in computation costs.
In this work, the statistical relationship between the household demand and the
PV microgeneration records is investigated via the Pearson (Rodgers and Nicewan-
der, 1988) and the Spearman (Lovie, 1995) correlation statistical tests. While only
linear relationships can be detected by the Pearson correlation statistical test, more

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 105
Figure 4.2: Relative frequency histogram of historical power genera-
tion data (drawn in blue), superimposed with the probability density
function of a lognormal distribution (drawn in orange).
expressive relationships can be identiﬁed by the Spearman correlation test. Moreover,
the latter test is particularly well-suited in the cases where this relationship is de-
scribed by a monotonic, i.e. non-increasing or non-decreasing, function. The Pearson
and Spearman correlations are evaluated, respectively, viascipy.stats.pearsonr and
scipy.stats.spearmanr functions of the Pythonscipy library. Poor correlation coeﬃ-
cients, ofaround 0.14, areregisteredforbothstatisticaltests. Theseresultssuggestthe
lack of a meaningful linear or monotonic relationship between the historical demand
and the PV generation records. In light of these results, individual GP regression
models are developed separately for the two data sources.
4.2.2 Data Preparation
Autoregressive GP and QR models are developed to describe the collected historical
residential power demand and PV microgeneration. The developed autoregressive
models rely on past time-series records, or lags, and explanatory input variables,
namely the prediction timestamp and consecutive time-series value diﬀerences. In
Table 4.1, explanatory input variables considered in the development of individual
GP and QR models are presented, whereut represents autoregressive model input at
hour t, ¯υt log-transformed demand or generation record at hourt, ∆¯υt = ¯υt− ¯υt−1,
and T the forecast timestamp (Unix time) of hourt.
The autoregressive time lags presented in the Table 4.1 are determined based on
an analysis of the plot of the partial autocorrelation function (PACF) of each data
source, i.e. power demand and PV generation (Box et al., 1994). In Figure 4.5,
PACF plots are presented for the household demand and PV generation data, for
an arbitrarily maximum number of 100 lags. Concerning the power demand, strong
partial autocorrelation is suggested for lags 1 and 2, being only local low correlation
peaks registered afterwards, with a periodicity of about 24 lags. This is somewhat
to be expected, given the recurring behaviour patterns of the daily demand. On the
other hand, a diﬀerent PACF plot is presented for the PV generation data. Signiﬁcant
variationsandanegativecorrelationforlags3to17isshown. Thesedistinctvariations
in the partial autocorrelation support distinct day-time and night-time generation.

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 106
Figure 4.3: Relative frequency histogram of normalised power de-
mand (drawn in blue), superimposed with Gaussian probability den-
sity function (drawn in orange).
The partial autocorrelation also decreases considerably for lags greater than one day
(24 hours). Autoregressive models of orders 2, 4, 6, 12 and 24 are chosen for developed
GP and QR models and presented in Table 4.1. Autoregressive models of order 2 are
chosen due to stronger partial autocorrelation up to 2 lags in both data sources.
Moreover, the strong autocorrelation in the 12 initial lags also motivated this choice.
On the other hand, the fact that low partial autocorrelation is observed, for both data
sources, for lags greater than one day, motivated the choice of 24 past records as the
limit for the autoregressive model order. Finally, autoregressive orders 4 and 6 are
also considered as intermediate values between autoregressive orders 2 and 12.
4.2.3 Covariance Function
Covariance functions, or kernels, are chosen based on speciﬁc assumptions regarding
the behaviour of the underlying process. While a single kernel can suﬃce to express
simpler processes, multiple kernels may need to be combined, in order to express more
complex behaviour. Among the kernel combination techniques, kernel addition and
multiplication are highlighted (Rasmussen and Williams, 2006). In the remainder of
this chapter,k1(x, x′) +k2(x, x′) is used to represent the sum of kernelsk1(x, x′) and
k2(x, x′), whereask1(x, x′)k2(x, x′) represents their product.
RBF and Matérn kernels, in particular Matérn 3 and 5, are the two most used co-
variance functions in GP time-series regression (Roberts et al., 2013; Duvenaud, 2014;
Swastanto, 2016; Shepero et al., 2018; van der Meer et al., 2018a). Moreover, RBF
kernels are thede-facto default kernel for GP modelling (Duvenaud, 2014). In addi-
tion to RBF and Matérn, RQ kernels are also referred in the above cited works. As
RBF and Matérn are local kernels, they tend to generalise poorly when extrapolating
over longer horizons, in particular Matérn kernels. This has conditioned the applica-
tion of Martérn kernels to long-term modelling problems (Wilson and Adams, 2013).
Moreover, as RBF and Matérn kernels are aperiodic, they are not recommended to
model periodic process behaviour. Instead, trigonometric functions, such as the cosine
and sine functions, have been considered (Rasmussen and Williams, 2006; MacKay,
1998). Nevertheless, to the best of the author knowledge, trigonometric kernels have

not yet been considered for residential power demand and PV generation, despite a
seemingly cyclical behaviour being expected in these two data sources.
In order to approximate the expected periodic behaviour of demand and power
generation, a composite kernel framework is considered. In addition to the standalone
RBF or RQ kernel conﬁgurations, the product of each of these kernels with a cosine
kernel is also considered. Moreover, the sum of another RBF or RQ kernel to this
composite structure is also contemplated. Extra RBF or RQ kernels are considered
in order to model residuals and small data variance. Composite kernels considered
in this work are presented in Table 4.2. For notation simplicity, letkCOS(x, x′) =

denote the cosine kernel, with|·| representing the vector magnitude.
The motivations for using these kernel structures are threefold. The ﬁrst motivation
is the widespread application of RBF kernels. Second, is the ability of RQ kernels
to model data varying at diﬀerent scales, through an inﬁnite sum of RBF kernels
with diﬀerent values for the lengthscale parameter. Third, is the fact that periodic
components of historical records are expected.

ARD (RQ) is parametrised by varianceσ, lengthscalel and
relative weightα. The subscriptARD denotes the automatic relevance determination
(ARD) variants of the respective kernels (Liu et al., 2020). ARD kernels diﬀer from
conventional, non-ARD, counterparts by ﬁtting individual lengthscale parametersl =
(l1,...,l m) to each input dimension. The value of eachlj, forj = 1...,m , is inversely
proportional to the contribution of that dimension to the regression problem. As such,
in ARD kernels, inputs from more relevant dimensions are allowed to have greater
inﬂuence in the forecasts of the model. Likewise, dimensions with contribution below
a given threshold can also be discarded. Although ARD kernel variants can improve
the accuracy of the process modelling, the resulting additional computational load
is non-neglectable. Another potential limitation of ARD kernel structures are their
overﬁtting risks. Finally, the contribution of each input dimension to the underlying
regression problem, provided by the ARD kernels, can also be regarded as a feature
selection mechanism.
4.2.4 Metrics
The performance of GP-based forecasts of both data sources is assessed using de-
terministic and probabilistic metrics. The deterministic metrics include the mean
absolute percentage error (MAPE) (Swastanto, 2016), presented in (4.15), and the
normalised root mean square error (NRMSE) (van der Meer et al., 2018a), shown
in (4.16).

4.2.5 Model Checking
Several GP and QR model conﬁgurations are assessed for the forecasting of electricity
load and PV generation, by means of forward chaining (FC), a k-fold CV variant for
sequential data. Similarly to the traditional CV, in FC, the original dataset is split
into train and test subsets. While in the traditional CV a random shuﬄe is performed
priortodatasplitting, inFCthesequentialorderofthedataispreserved. Thetraining
data is divided intok groups, or folds, of equal size, for which the sample order remains
unchanged. For each conﬁguration of the GP and QR models, the training is carried
out iteratively, as shown in Figure 4.6. In the ﬁrst iteration, each GP or QR model
is trained with the ﬁrst fold and evaluated on samples belonging to the second fold
(comprising the validation set). Next, the same models are trained using data from
the ﬁrst and second folds, and validated on the third fold. The procedure is repeated
until each GP and QR model conﬁguration is validated on the last fold of the training
data. Subsequently, the average performance of each GP and QR conﬁguration in
each evaluation set is determined. One GP and one QR conﬁguration with superior
performance in the training data are identiﬁed, and evaluated on the test set records.

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 111

Figure 4.6: Illustration of the forward chaining procedure for5 folds.
The choice for a CV framework, in detriment of a train-validation-test split, is
justiﬁed by a more robust estimation of the model accuracy over the training data.
Even for large datasets, it is argued that assessing the performance of the model over
diﬀerent folds maximises the amount of training data used. The resulting estimation
of the accuracy of each conﬁguration over the training data is stronger.
In this chapter, GP and QR models are trained using the ﬁrst year of historical
data, under the above described FC framework. A total of 10 folds,η = 95%, and
quantilesq = 0.01,..., 0.99 are considered. GP and QR conﬁgurations presenting, on
average, superior performance are subsequently compared against each other in the
second year of historical records. All GP models are implemented in Python 3.7.5,
using gpﬂowv1.5.0 (De G. Matthews et al., 2017) andTensorFlow v1.14.0 (Abadi
et al., 2015) libraries. As for QR, the implementation of its predictive models is based
on random forest-based quantile regression (Meinshausen, 2006), using the skgar-
den 2 Python library, fork = 1000 estimators. All computations are carried out in
a Ubuntu 16.04 machine, equipped with a dual 2.2GHz Intel Xeon Silver 4214 CPU
with 16.5MB cache, 504 GB RAM, and three 16 GB RAM NVIDIA Quadro P500
graphics processing units (GPUs).
4.2.6 Model Selection
The selection of the individual GP and QR models to explain the historical residential
power demand and PV generation records is carried out according to four heuristics.
Before presenting the heuristics rationale for model selection, the concept of kernel
stability order, denoted asOκ, must be introduced. Kernel stability order is here
deﬁned as the number of input conﬁgurations for which a GP model, with a given
covariance function, can be successfully ﬁtted to allΦ training data folds. The selec-
tion of the individual GP and QR models to explain the residential demand and PV
generation is, therefore, carried out according to the following heuristics:
1. Probabilisticmetricsarefavouredoverdeterministicones. Thisisjustiﬁedbythe
intrinsic probabilistic nature of the residential load and generation forecasting
approaches. TheperformanceoftheGPandQRmodelsissortedinthefollowing
order: PICP maximisation is taken as the primary criterion, followed by the
2https://scikit-garden.github.io/, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 112
minimisation of the PINAW, pinball loss function, Winkler score, MAPE and
NRMSE, in this order. The precedence of higher PICP performance over lower
PINAW scores is justiﬁed by the need of probabilistic models cover a higher
percentage of the target points, even at the cost of wider PIs. After PICP, the
preference for lower PINAW over the remaining probabilistic metrics is justiﬁed
to privilege narrower PIs. As stated, the PICP can be skewed due to wider
PIs, without corresponding to improved modelling. Two reasons justify the
preference for lower values of the pinball loss function metric, in detriment of
lower Winkler scores. First, the Winkler score combines both the PI coverage
and width. Second, the pinball loss function has been speciﬁcally designed for
quantile forecasts, which are considered in this work. Finally, the preference for
lower MAPE scores over the NRMSE metric is justiﬁed by the greater eﬀect of
larger errors on NRMSE.
2. Lower complexity models are favoured, i.e. models with fewer explanatory input
variables, for a given kernel. This point is justiﬁed by the so-called curse of
dimensionality dilemma (Bellman, 1956).
3. Model conﬁgurations for which a forecast timestamp is not provided are pre-
ferred. This is supported by the higher stability and superior performance of
kernelsk1
ARD andk2
ARD, for all metrics under consideration. Ask1
ARD andk2
ARD
are local kernels, the speciﬁcation of a forecast timestamp could contribute to a
convergence of model forecasts to the mean function value, in particular when
forecasting far away from the training points.
4. Kernels with higher stability order are preferred, due to their increased ﬂexibility
in ﬁtting data, and smaller sensitivity to embedded information. It should be
mentioned that this particular heuristic is not applicable to QR models, as they
do not rely on kernels.
4.3 Results
This section covers the application of the FC framework to GP-based and QR-based
probabilistic forecasting of power demand and PV generation. As stated, a collection
of two-year historical demand records, available in the Pecan Street Inc. Dataport
dataset, is considered. Concerning the PV generation, two-year records are estimated
from PV panel models and weather forecast data. In Section 4.3.1, the FC results
over the train data are presented for GP-based and QR-based techniques. In Sec-
tion 4.3.2, one GP and QR conﬁguration, with superior performance over the training
data, is selected and further evaluated on test data. Finally, in Section 4.3.3, a brief
comparison of the presented results with those reported in the literature, for the same
approaches and data sources, is carried out.
4.3.1 Model Checking
In this section, the application of the FC framework to the collected train data is
presented for the GP and QR techniques.
GP Model Checking

For both the residential power demand and the PV generation, an inverse relation-

in particular for the PICP. Lower PICP performance is noted to coincide with higher
PINAW and Winkler scores, and therefore narrower PIs. On the other hand, the GP
conﬁgurations with better PICP performance are also shown to have inferior PINAW
and Winkler scores, and therefore corresponding to wider PIs. It is also shown that
inferior PICP performance coincides with worse scores of the deterministic metrics,
i.e. MAPE and NRMSE, and vice-versa. One exception is noted for the conﬁguration
19-1 (inputu19

ARD). For this conﬁguration, worse MAPE and NRMSE
scores are shown, despite higher PICP scores also being registered. A closer inspection
on the forecasts provided by some of these models showed that they do not follow the
general shape of the data. This is further corroborated by higher MAPE and NRMSE
scores, suggesting that the high PICP scores do not correspond to high quality process
modelling. In light of these ﬁndings, a careful inspection of the shape of computed
forecasts is recommended, in order to avoid the favouring of low quality models.
Concerning the residential demand, improved PICP and pinball performance is not
exclusively achieved by considering wider PIs, as lower MAPE and NRMSE scores are
also found for the same conﬁgurations. Despite superior process modelling, suggested
by better performance according to the PICP, pinball loss, MAPE and the NRMSE
metrics, thewidthofthePIsstillneedstobeaccounted, asgreaterforecastuncertainty
is suggested. As for the PV generation, the best PICP performances do not always

framework did not improved the performance and stability of the kernelsk1

the cyclical or periodic components to the power demand and PV generation. In this
case, alternative, more complex, periodic kernels can be exploited in order to capture
the recurring nature of the demand and generation proﬁles. As such, it seems plausible
to consider a GP model with eitherk1

and PV generation. In Figures 4.7 to 4.10,k2

are presented for the residential demand, according to the deterministic and proba-
bilistic criteria discussed in Section 4.2.4. Likewise, in Figures 4.15 to 4.18, the mean
and standard deviation of each QR conﬁguration is presented for the PV generation,
according to the deterministic and probabilistic criteria discussed in Section 4.2.4. In

perior performance. Likewise for GP-based modelling (Section 4.3.1), the contents of
Figures 4.11 to 4.18 demonstrate that, for the two data sources, no QR conﬁguration
consistently outperforms its counterparts for all performance metrics.
For QR-based modelling of the power demand data, low variability of the PICP
performance is observed, which remains conﬁned to the [0.96,0.98] interval. On the
other hand, for QR-based modelling of the PV generation data, a greater variabil-
ity in PCIP scores is observed, varying in the interval [0.61,0.99]. In Figures 4.11
and 4.12, poor prediction capability of QR models is shown for the power demand
data. This is justiﬁed by the fact that high PICP scores with low variability have been

reported, along with worse performance according to the other metrics. Indeed, the
reported higher PINAW scores correspond to wider PIs and high forecast uncertainty.
Furthermore, the high magnitude of the MAPE and NRMSE performance clearly
demonstrates that the QR median forecasts are far away from the target historical
demand values. Taking all of the above into account, QR models produce highly un-
certain median forecasts of household power demand, and for this reason QR cannot
accurately model this data source.
In the case of PV generation, in Figures 4.15 to 4.18, MAPE and NRMSE scores
are reported to be orders of magnitude worse than the corresponding scores obtained
for the GP models. As for the pinball loss and the Winkler score, overall higher, and
therefore worse, scores have also been registered for QR-based forecasting of the PV
generation data. Nevertheless, a smaller diﬀerence to the corresponding GP-based
performance is registered, in comparison to that of MAPE and NRMSE metrics.
As for PINAW and PICP, higher PICP performance is accompanied by wider PIs,
and therefore higher PINAW scores. On the other hand, lower PICP coincides with
narrower PIs, and, therefore, lower PINAW scores. Poor QR-based model quality is
shown for PV generation data, being considerably inferior to that of the corresponding
GP regression models. This is justiﬁed by the contents of Figures 4.15 to 4.18, and
a posterior comparison against the corresponding GP-based modelling results. As
shown in these ﬁgures, signiﬁcantly high MAPE scores are observed, regardless of the
performance registered for other metrics, in particular wider or narrower PIs, and
lower or higher PICP scores. In this situation, the reported high PICP score is solely
the result of extremely wide PIs, potentially covering almost the entire range of the
predictor variable, instead of resulting from improved model quality. Likewise, lower
PICP scores are the result of narrower PIs, covering a smaller percentage of the range
of values of the predictor variable.
Taking into account the heuristics presented in Section 4.2.6, the QR-based model
conﬁgurations with superior PICP performance are favoured. For the power demand
data, PICP above 0.97 comprises the higher recorded scores for this metric, which
encompasses many QR conﬁgurations. As previously discussed for the QR-based
demand modelling, higher PICP coincides with worse PINAW performance, in this
case varying in the [0.85,0.9] interval. As for the remaining of the performance metrics,
no single conﬁguration is shown to consistently outperform all others. Taking into
consideration the expected poor performance of the QR-based modelling, for both
data sources, the second deﬁned heuristic justiﬁes the choice for the explanatory
input variableu3
t, based on fewer variables. For this explanatory input, high PICP
performance, greater than 0.97, is registered for the QR model conﬁgurations 3-4
and 3-8. Similar PINAW scores are reported for these two conﬁgurations, although

4.3.2 Best Model Evaluation
In this section, the predictive performance of the selected GP and QR models is as-
sessed on the test dataset, which contains the second year of the historical demand
readings and PV generation estimates. In Table 4.6, the performance on the test
dataset is presented for the selected GP and QR models, for the two data sources.
Superior performance, of either GP or QR models, is shown in bold for each metric
considered. Similar performance of the MAPE, NRMSE and PICP metrics is observed
for GP-based modelling of both data sources. Nevertheless, slightly improved predic-
tion accuracy and narrower PIs are shown, on average, for the GP-based modelling of
PV generation data.

In Figure 4.19, GP model forecasts for the power demand and PV generation are
presented, for the last month of the test data. The GP posterior distribution mean is
drawn in a solid blue line, while the target demand and generation are represented in
solid blue dots, and 95% PIs are drawn in shaded regions. Figure 4.19 (b) supports
the claims of improved PV generation modelling via the GP regression-based models,
in comparison to the GP-based demand modelling. The support for this claim is
threefold. First, the computed PIs are shown to cover almost all of the target points.
Second, narrower PIs, in comparison to those shown in Figure 4.19 (a), are computed.
Third, the average GP forecasts remain closer to the target points. An outlier in
the normalised demand forecast is also identiﬁed, near December17th. It is further
noticed that, as the horizon advances, the average power demand forecasts begin to
consistently surpass the corresponding target points, albeit still within computed PIs.
Concerning the QR-based modelling, the selected models are found incapable of
explaining and extrapolating the demand or generation data. This claim is justiﬁed
by the performance registered for both data sources (Table 4.6) and the plotted model
forecasts (Figure 4.20). In comparison to the GP-based performance for each data
source, MAPE and NRMSE scores have been signiﬁcantly aggravated. High MAPE
and NRMSE scores express median forecasts falling too far from the target records.
Median QR forecasts are further shown to remain mostly unchanged and, unlike in
GP-based modelling, fail to follow the target points (Figure 4.20). The pinball loss,
Winkler and PINAW scores are also greatly heightened, pinpointing unreliable and

(a)
(b)
Figure 4.19: Normalised demand (a) and generation (b) forecasts of

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 132
poor overall probabilistic forecast performance. Regarding the PINAW metric, large
PIs and highly uncertain forecasts are reported, in particular for PV generation. As a
result, the range of values taken by the normalised records is almost entirely covered
by computed PIs, which results in higher PCIP performance, but not necessarily in
high quality data modelling and extrapolation.
In comparison to QR, the higher computation burden of GP models is rewarded by
more accurate and certain forecasts. In addition to improved modelling, the estima-
tion of the full posterior distribution makes GPs more suitable to support uncertainty-
aware decision-making. In this context, GP regression is also shown to satisfy the un-
derlying probability distribution assumptions of stochastic programming, despite the
higher computational burden stemming from the inversion of the covariance matrix,
and the estimation of the full posterior distribution. Alternative QR implementations
can, nonetheless, be considered, e.g. gradient boosting regression (Friedman, 2001).
4.3.3 Related Literature Comparison
In this subsection, the performance of the reported probabilistic modelling of the
household power demand and generation is brieﬂy contextualised within that in the
literature. It is noted that, in the works cited below, diverse data sources are consid-
ered, collected at diﬀerent sampling frequencies, over distinct time periods and geo-
graphically distant locations. Thus, even though probabilistic forecasting of household
demand and generation is covered in these works, signiﬁcantly diﬀerent dynamics can
be expressed, which may not be optimally modelled by the same technique.
In (Sharifzadeh et al., 2019; Liu et al., 2017; González Ordiano et al., 2020),
unnormalised metrics such as the mean squared error (MSE), pinball loss and Winkler
scores are exclusively considered. As diﬀerent historical record databases are used in
each work, their comparison to the results reported in Sections 4.3.1 and 4.3.2 is more
diﬃcult. Concerning the GP regression, diﬀerent covariance functions are evaluated
in (Sharifzadeh et al., 2019), namely the Matérn 3 and 5, with and without ARD
variants. Low MSE scores are reported for forecasting of both data sources. Regarding
the width of the PIs, although appropriate metrics such as PINAW are not adopted, a
visual inspection of presented wind and solar power generation forecast plots suggest
that the PIs are narrower than those reported in this chapter. In the case of (Liu
et al., 2017; González Ordiano et al., 2020), lower pinball loss, higher Winkler scores,
and narrower PIs are reported, the latter suggesting higher forecast conﬁdence.
In (Ben Taieb et al., 2016; Wang et al., 2019), evaluation of the probabilistic fore-
casts is exclusively carried out using CRPS. As this metric has not been considered in
this work, due to the absence of the estimation of the full posterior distribution in QR,
a proper comparison to our work cannot be performed. On the other hand, in (van der
Meer et al., 2018a; Yang et al., 2018; Shepero et al., 2018; Guan et al., 2021; Bracale
et al., 2020b,a), the deterministic and probabilistic metrics described in Section 4.2.4,
namely the PICP, PINAW, pinball loss and MAPE, are adopted. In (van der Meer
et al., 2018a; Yang et al., 2018; Shepero et al., 2018; Guan et al., 2021), diﬀerent
explanatory input variables and covariance function alternatives, namely featuring
ARD and non-ARD Matérn 3, 5 and other periodic composite structures, are studied
for the GP-based modelling of demand and generation data. Lower PINAW scores
are registered in (van der Meer et al., 2018a; Yang et al., 2018; Shepero et al., 2018),
suggesting narrower PIs and higher forecast conﬁdence than for the GP models de-
veloped in this chapter. With respect to the PICP, higher scores are reported for the
models developed in this chapter, around 0.94, whereas a PCIP performance in the
range [0.85,0.9] is reported in the previous references. For the deterministic metrics,

superior MAPE performance is reported in (Shepero et al., 2018), dropping to about
half of the MAPE scores presented in Sections 4.3.1 and 4.3.2.
Finally, in (Bracale et al., 2020b), QR is carried out using a decision tree ensemble-
based approach similar to theskgarden implementation used in this chapter. Superior
quality of the QR modelling is shown in (Bracale et al., 2020b; Xing et al., 2020;
Bracale et al., 2020a), with narrower PIs being observed, consequently suggesting
smaller forecast uncertainty. Nevertheless, with respect to the PICP performance,
or the forecast period coverage probability in the case of (Xing et al., 2020), similar
performance to the references listed above is shown in this chapter.
4.4 Synthesis
Inthischapter, probabilisticmodellingofresidentialpowerdemandandPVgeneration
was carried out, considering frameworks based on GP and QR. The same smart home
case study presented and discussed in Chapter 3 was revisited, exploiting historical
non-controllable demand and generation records to develop predictive models.
Autoregressive GP regression models, comprising distinct explanatory input vari-
ables and covariance function alternatives, were developed, motivated by related ap-
plication works from the literature, and the expected recurrent demand and generation
behaviour. A variety of distinct GP and QR models were developed to describe each
data source. Overall, a total of 20 explanatory input variable alternatives were con-
sidered for both GP and QR. In the case of GP, 8 distinct covariance functions were
also proposed, whereas 8 combinations of regression tree parameters were considered
for QR models. The predictive performance of the developed GP and QR models was
evaluated over a two-year period, considering both deterministic and probabilistic
performance criteria, and a CV variant which preserved the sample order.
GP and QR model selection was carried out for each data source separately, sup-
ported by heuristics comprehending the metric type, complexity and kernel stability
order. A GP model with an RQ kernel, taking as input the past day of hourly records,
was chosen to describe the residential demand. On its turn, the power generation was
described by a GP model with a RBF kernel and the past day of hourly generation
records. Concerning QR, the household demand is described by a model parametrised
by θi =
(
θdimen
i = 0.2,θsplit
i = 0.2
)
, fori = 1,...,k and k = 1000. This model takes
as input the past two hours of hourly demand records and the forecast timestamp. As
for the PV generation, a QR model parametrised byθi =
(
θdimen
i = 0.3,θsplit
i = 0.1
)
,
for i = 1,...,k and k = 1000, was considered. This model takes as input the past
four hours of hourly generation records and the forecast timestamp.
Within the GP-based modelling, the superiority of the RBF and RQ kernels was
clearly shown by their higher forecast accuracy. Moreover, for these kernels, no nu-
merical instability preventing the GP hyperparameter tuning was observed. No peri-
odic composite kernel structure has been proven capable of describing the historical
datasets of the residential demand or the PV generation. The selected GP models
outperformed their QR counterparts on the test dataset, capturing the dynamics of
the data and suggesting higher forecast certainty, in particular for PV generation.
Despite the higher computational load of GP models, their superior model qual-
ity is shown to make up for this limitation. Furthermore, the estimation of the full
posterior distribution can also prove useful in decision-making tasks performed un-
der uncertain and incomplete information. Although registered for diﬀerent data,
narrower PIs, accompanied by high PICP scores, have been reported in the related
literature for GP and QR models. These results motivate further eﬀorts to decrease

Chapter 4. Probabilistic Demand and Generation Modelling 135
the forecast uncertainty of the predictive models developed in this chapter. How-
ever, further research on model improvement is deferred to future work iterations, in
particular pursuing alternative data preparation and normalisation procedures, along
with other GP covariance and mean functions. As previously stated, the next chapter
builds upon the herein presented contributions. Both selected GP models are inte-
grated within a stochastic programming variant of the CRH-based approach proposed
in Chapter 3. The problem uncertainties in household appliance scheduling are ex-
plicitly addressed in the resulting framework, and supported by the developed GP
models. The main results of this chapter are summarised as follows:
• Probabilistic, i.e. lognormal, modelling of smart home non-controllable demand
and PV microgeneration. Simultaneous modelling of these two sources is re-
ported to be under-represented in the smart home-related literature. Here,
this research gap was addressed, considering probabilistic modelling of non-
controllable power demand and microgeneration, via GP regression and QR.
• Superior predictive performance of RBF and RQ kernels, in particular for the
power generation data, is reported. Motivated by the expected recurring pat-
terns in domestic load demand and power generation, the superiority of the
novel periodic covariance functions was predicted. As stated, the periodic com-
ponents, in the form of a cosine function combined with standard covariance
functions, were considered. The new covariance functions were strongly aﬀected
by numerical instabilities, preventing the GP model training in most cases. In
the few cases where GP model training was successful, better predictive perfor-
mance was achieved with standard RBF and RQ covariance functions.
• GP-based probabilistic modelling is shown to consistently outperform the QR
alternatives for both non-controllable demand and power generation. The supe-
riority of GP modelling is shown not only for the original training dataset, but
also in a separate test dataset, on which GP and QR conﬁgurations with the
best overall performance were further evaluated.
• In the next chapter, the selected GP models are exploited for household appli-
ance scheduling, in the presence of uncertainty in the household non-controllable
demand and power generation. The role of GPs in these problems is to forecast
probabilitydistributionfunctionsthatdescribethenon-controllabledemandand
themicrogenerationoveragivenhorizon. Thesemodelswillprovideanadequate
probabilistic modelling basis support for the appliance scheduling.

137
Chapter 5
Stochastic Demand-Side Smart
Home Load Management
In Chapter 3, a CRH mathematical optimisation-based framework is proposed to ad-
dress the problem of week-ahead household appliance scheduling, for DSM electrical
energy rationalisation. In this framework, the household appliances are scheduled
based on single-valued realisations of the non-controllable demand and PV micro-
generation. Explicit modelling of the uncertain non-controllable demand was carried
out via representative proﬁles, whereas the uncertain microgeneration is modelled via
single-point forecasts. There, it is noted that the absence of additional uncertainty
information hinders the accommodation of severe realisations, as the allocation is
ﬁtted to the speciﬁc proﬁles and forecasts provided. This includes the lack of opti-
mality and feasibility guarantees in the presence of diﬀerent uncertainty realisations,
i.e. non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration.
InSection2.3.3, robustoptimisationandstochasticprogrammingaredistinguished
for decision-making in the presence of uncertain information, providing optimality and
feasibility guarantees within certain conﬁdence levels. On the one hand, robust opti-
misation obtains a solution that performs adequately in the presence of uncertainty
realisations with worse impact on the optimisation problem. On the other hand, in
stochastic programming, problem uncertainties are modelled according to given prob-
ability distributions. As a result, in stochastic programming, optimal decisions are
made with the goal of minimising the expected value of the objective function of the
problem. In stochastic programming problems, probabilistic-based modelling of the
uncertainties in the problem is also often exploited in the formulation of probabilis-
tic constraints, when solution feasibility depends on the uncertainty realisations. In
these cases, rather than imposing that the desired constraint should always be met, a
minimum threshold on the probability of meeting the constraint is deﬁned. In com-
parison to stochastic programming, robust optimisation solutions are generally more
conservative, which justiﬁes the preference for this technique in problems where the
solution feasibility and reliability are more critical (Gorissen et al., 2015). Within
residential appliance scheduling, guarantees on the feasibility and reliability of the
scheduling solutions, albeit important, are not as critical as in other contexts, e.g. in
the management of electrical power grids. For this reason, the higher conservatism of
robust optimisation solutions is often the reason for the wider adoption of stochastic
programming in HEMS appliance scheduling.
This chapter consolidates the contributions of Chapters 3 and 4. A scenario-based
stochastic compressive receding horizon (SB-SCRH) framework is proposed for house-
hold appliance scheduling in the presence of uncertain non-controllable demand and
PV microgeneration. The proposed framework comprises a stochastic programming
extension to the CRH framework presented in Chapter 3. In SB-SCRH, the uncer-
tainty concerning the non-controllable demand and the PV microgeneration over the

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 138
scheduling horizon is explicitly incorporated in the daily mathematical optimisation
problem instances, through stochastic programming techniques. The expected value
of the objective function, approximated via concrete non-controllable demand and
generation realisations or scenarios (Shapiro and Philpott, 2007), is minimised at the
start of each new day of the horizon. Probabilistic constraints are also imposed, in
order to provide feasibility thresholds in the presence of any uncertainty realisation.
Within the scope of this chapter, non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration
scenarios are obtained based on the probabilistic modelling of these two sources, car-
ried out in Chapter 4 and Section 3.1.1. The prototype of the single-family smart
home environment discussed in Chapter 3 is revisited, being the same controllable
appliance speciﬁcations and operation needs considered.
Following the validation of the CRH optimisation-based strategy presented in
Chapter 3, here, the proposed SB-SCRH strategy is compared against a total of four
alternative strategies: (i) typical appliance allocation, (ii) ideal scheduling, and (iii)
DA and (iv) traditional RH variants of the novel SB-SCRH proposal. Appliance
scheduling is carried out over the same set of 30 weeks considered in Chapter 3, cov-
ering a one-year period. For each week and strategy, the obtained appliance schedules
are evaluated according to the weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort criteria.
Motivated by the observed high computational burden of the scenario-based allocation
strategies, a scenario reduction technique is employed to prune the original, larger,
set of uncertainty realisations. A total of ﬁve scenario sets of smaller cardinality
are obtained. For each one of these scenario sets, controllable appliances are sched-
uled separately with each one of the ﬁve strategy alternatives mentioned above. The
impact of the scenario reduction techniques within the studied household appliance
scheduling problem is assessed, for each strategy, by comparing the appliance alloca-
tion schedules obtained with the original scenario set, and those resulting from each
reduced scenario set. Finally, the integration of the proposed SB-SCRH framework
within the prototype smart home is addressed. An extended SB-SCRH strategy is pro-
posed, in which the household demand and generation are continuously monitored,
and the operation of the controllable appliances is rescheduled upon the detection of
any constraint violation. The contributions of this chapter are summarised as follows:
• New SB-SCRH strategy for one week-ahead scheduling of residential appliances.
In this strategy, the operation of the controllable appliances is rescheduled at the
start of each new day of the week, considering newly-generated realisations of
the non-controllable demand and the microgeneration. The daily rescheduling
is carried out to address the degradation of the probabilistic models, and to
accommodate unexpected events, such as additional controllable load operation
outside of their typical usage.
• Comparison of the SB-SCRH strategy against the DA and traditional RH vari-
ants, following the state of the art in smart home appliance scheduling. The
performance of the planned appliance operation is assessed separately for each
week, according to the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort cri-
teria. The novel SB-SCRH strategy is validated for week-ahead scheduling of
smart home appliances. Competitive performance with that of the DA and RH
variants is shown for SB-SCRH, reﬂected in similar appliance operation times,
and weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort performance.
• Comparison of the SB-SCRH strategy with the ideal allocation and the typical
appliance operation. A gap to the ideal allocation, according to the weekly
electricitybillcriterion, isidentiﬁed. Thisgapisconsideredacceptable, giventhe
inability to predict the future demand and generation without error. As for the

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 139
resident discomfort, no signiﬁcant diﬀerence in performance is shown between
the novel SB-SCRH strategy and the ideal allocation. Finally, the SB-SCRH
appliance operation is shown to outperform the typical appliance operation,
according to both the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort criteria.
• The application of scenario reduction heuristics, which pruned the original set
of uncertainty realisations of its most redundant elements, proved eﬀective. The
application of these heuristics to the SB-SCRH, and its traditional RH and DA
variants, resulted in similar appliance operation times, weekly electricity bill and
resident discomfort, at a fraction of the computational load.
• Proposal of an extended SB-SCRH strategy, which addresses its deployment
within smart homes, such as the prototype case study considered throughout
this document. Noted enhancements of this strategy, in comparison to the orig-
inal SB-SCRH proposal, include: (i) continuous monitoring of the household
demand, generation and environment; and (ii) rescheduling of the controllable
appliance operation, carried out at any moment during the day, upon the de-
tection of a constraint violation.
The remainder of this chapter is organised as follows. Section 5.1 discusses the
appliance scheduling methodology, covering both the prototype smart home and the
novel allocation strategy. Section 5.2 presents a formal deﬁnition of the MILP stochas-
tic programming optimisation problems instantiated in the proposed SB-SCRH ap-
proach. In Section 5.3, the SB-SCRH-based appliance operation times are discussed
and compared against those from the DA and traditional RH variants, along with
those from the ideal allocation and typical appliance operation. In Section 5.4, the
application of the scenario reduction heuristics, aiming to relieve the high computa-
tional burden of scenario-based scheduling, is covered. In addition, the underlying
computational load and appliance schedules are discussed for ﬁve scenario sets of
smaller cardinality. For each of these scenario sets, controllable appliances are sched-
uled with the novel SB-SCRH strategy, and its DA and standard RH variants. In
Section 5.5, the integration of the proposed SB-SCRH framework within smart home
environments is discussed. An extended SB-SCRH strategy is proposed, whose main
features include the continuous monitoring of the household environment, and appli-
ance rescheduling upon the detection of any constraint violation. Finally, the main
ﬁndings of this chapter are summarised in Section 5.6.
5.1 Methodology
In this chapter, the household appliance scheduling is carried out for the same proto-
type smart home environment described in Section 3.1.1. A four-resident smart home,
with varying needs for power and non-potable water, is considered. The household is
equipped with a set of PV panels, exploited for local PV microgeneration, a battery
storage system, and an external water tank used to store non-potable groundwater
for later domestic supply. The storage of the water tank is ensured by an electrical
pump system and, likewise in Chapter 3, the water is exploited for domestic clothes
washing and toilet ﬂush supply. The operation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer,
dishwasher and electrical pump system is managed by a HEMS, following the mod-
elling of the load cycle and operation needs carried out in Chapter 3. The separation
between the controllable and non-controllable sources of demand is also maintained.
A probabilistic modelling of the non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration is
considered. This contrasts with the approach followed in Chapter 3, where conserva-
tive demand proﬁles and generation forecasts were adopted. A SB-SCRH strategy is

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 140
herein proposed for week-ahead scheduling of residential appliances, and outlined in
Algorithm 2. The probabilistic modelling of the non-controllable demand and gener-
ation is explicitly considered, in order to promote uncertainty-aware decision-making.
Algorithm 2Scenario-Based Stochastic Compressive Receding Horizon (SB-SCRH)
for eachday of the weekdo
1. Deﬁne the optimisation horizon (day – Sunday)
2. Generate the non-controllable demand (power and non-potable water) and the PV
microgeneration scenarios
3. Instantiate the stochastic programming MILP problem formulation (Section 5.2)
4. Solve the stochastic programming MILP instance
5. Retrieve the appliance schedules for the ﬁrst day of the horizon
6. Implement the ﬁrst day of controllable appliance operation
end for
The novel SB-SCRH strategy shares the motivation of its CRH-based counterpart
proposed in Chapter 3. It includes the weekly usage trends of the domestic appliances,
andtheliteratureunder-representationoftheCRH-basedstrategies. Foreachnewday
of the horizon, stochastic programming MILP optimisation problems are instantiated,
instead of the deterministic counterparts considered in the original CRH strategy
(Algorithm 1). Given their stochastic programming nature, a weighted sum of the
weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort criteria is no longer minimised in
the daily optimisation problem instances. Instead, the expected value of the objective
function, which is approximated through concrete scenarios of the non-controllable
demand and generation, is minimised in each instance of the optimisation problem.
5.2 Optimisation Problem Formulation
Under the SB-SCRH-based strategy outlined in Algorithm 2, the controllable appli-
ances are scheduled at the beginning of each new day of the week, by solving an
instance of a stochastic programming non-preemptive machine scheduling problem.
While the job completion time is highlighted as the most common uncertainty source
in problems of this nature, the uncertain household demand and generation is, instead,
considered in this work. The uncertainty in the household demand stems from the
usage of the non-controllable electrical equipment and the unpredicted toilet ﬂushes.
On the other hand, the uncertainty in the PV microgeneration emerges from weather-
related generation output variability. Following the probabilistic modelling discussed
in Chapter 4, the non-controllable power demand and PV microgeneration are mod-
elled, for each instant of the scheduling horizon, via separate lognormal probability
distributions. On its turn, the non-controllable non-potable water demand, resulting
from uncertain toilet ﬂush events, is described, at each instant, by distinct Poisson
probability distributions, as in Chapter 3 (see Table 3.1).
For each new day of the horizon, almost all of the parameters, variables, objective
and constraints are shared by all the instances of the optimisation problems considered
within the SB-SCRH strategy. Only diﬀerences on the values of certain parameters,
e.g. the length of the scheduling horizon, are noted. A discretisation of the time
domain is, once again, carried out. A total ofT hourly slots, numbered from 1 to
T, are considered, with theith slot covering the period from houri− 1 to hour i.
Finally, a distinction between scalars, vectors, matrices and tensors is considered,
with x representing a scalar,y a vector,ˆ za matrix, and¯ wa tensor.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 141
5.2.1 Parameters
For each daily optimisation problem instance, most of the parameters deﬁned in Sec-
tion 3.2.1 and Table 3.2 are maintained. Few exceptions are, nonetheless, noted.
They comprise the speciﬁcation of the scenarios of the non-controllable household de-
mand and PV microgeneration, along with additional parameters associated with the
constraints imposed over the contracted power and the tank volume.
Concerning the non-controllable demand and generation, the parameterNs speci-
ﬁes the number of scenarios to be considered. The parameter matricesˆEhome, ˆF and
ˆPV, of sizeT×Ns, are deﬁned, replacing the corresponding vectors speciﬁed in Sec-
tion 3.2.1. As such, the elementsEhome
is , withi = 1,...,T and s = 1,...,N s, denote
the non-controllable household power demand, expressed in kW, at the start of sloti
of the horizon, and for scenarios. Similarly, the elementsFis, withi = 1,...,T and
s = 1,...,N s, denote the non-controllable household toilet ﬂush events, expressed in
ﬂush/h, at the start of sloti of the horizon, and for scenarios. Finally, the elements
PVis, with i = 1,...,T and s = 1,...,N s, denote the local PV microgeneration,
expressed in kW, at the start of sloti of the horizon, and for scenarios. The value of
each elementEhome
is ,Fis and PVis, withi = 1,...,T ands = 1,...,N s, is determined
based on a probabilistic modelling of each source, following Table 3.1 and Chapter 4.
With respect to the contracted power and tank volume, the original constraints de-
ﬁned in (3.13) and (3.22) are replaced by probabilistic alternatives. This is motivated
by the explicit modelling of the uncertain non-controllable demand. The parameters
δC = δL = δU = 0.95 are also deﬁned to impose lower bounds for each probabilistic
constraint. As such,δC imposes a lower bound for the probabilistic constraint of the
contracted power. In turn, the parametersδL and δU impose lower bounds, respec-
tively, for the probabilistic constraints specifying the minimum and maximum volume
of the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank. Finally, in order to derive
deterministic equivalent models of the probabilistic constraints, percentile values of
the inverse cumulative distribution function (CDF) of the uncertain power and non-
potable water demand are also provided. As such, the parameter vectorΘ−1
Ehome, of
lengthT, represents the value of the inverse CDF, at pointδC, for the non-controllable
power demand. Each elementΘ−1

5.2.2 Variables
Atotalof 11T optimisationproblemdecisionvariablesareconsideredforeachinstance
of the optimisation problem, withT denoting the horizon length parameter. These
decision variables are presented in the listing that follows.
1. Binary variable vectorsw, t, d, of lengthT, which describe, respectively, the op-
eration of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines. Elements
wi, ti and di take the value of 1 if the corresponding appliance is executing at
time sloti (starting at sloti− 1 and ﬁnishing at sloti, due to the performed
discretisation of the time domain), and 0 otherwise, withi = 1,...,T .
2. Vector q, of lengthT, whose elementsqi∈ R+
0 represent the electrical pump
system ﬂow rate at sloti of the horizon, withi = 1,...,T .
3. Binary variable vectorssw, st, sd, of lengthT, which describe, respectively, the
slot of the horizon in which a new clothes washing, tumble dryer or dishwasher
load is started. Each vector elementswi,sti,sdi∈{ 0, 1}, takes the value 1 if a
new corresponding load starts at sloti, and 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
4. Matrix ˆV, of sizeT×Ns, whose elementsVis∈ R+
0 model the volume of non-
potable water stored in the tank at the end of sloti, and for scenarios, with
i = 1,...,T and s = 1,...,N s.
5. Vector SOC, of lengthT, whose elements SOCi describe the battery system
SOC at the end of sloti of the horizon, fori = 1,...,T .
6. Tensor ¯ e∈ RT×Ns×Ns, whose elementseijk represent the domestic electricity
balance at slot i, for the non-controllable power demand scenarioj and mi-
crogeneration scenariok, withi = 1,...,T , j = 1,...,N s and k = 1,...,N s.
The domestic electricity balance is deﬁned, for each slot of the horizon, as the
diﬀerence between the household demand and generation.

ijk,e−
ijk∈ R+
0 de-

5.2.3 Criteria
In the SB-SCRH-based allocation, the expected value of the weighted sum of the
weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort, deﬁned in (5.1), is optimised for
each new day of the horizon.
E (c) = E [αEB + (1−α)RD] (5.1)
where E(·) is taken with respect to the probability distributions representing the
problem uncertainties, i.e. the PV microgeneration and the non-controllable power
and non-potable water demand. The weekly electricity bill is deﬁned as in (3.6),
considering hourly slots, along with lognormal random variables describing the non-
controllable demand and the PV microgeneration. As for the resident discomfort, it
is deﬁned as in (3.7). Considering that the resident discomfort is not inﬂuenced by
any source of uncertainty, the objective function deﬁned in (5.1) is further developed
into (5.2).
E (c) =α E (EB) + (1−α) RD (5.2)
In Appendix B.1, a rationale to derive an analytical expression forE (EB) in (5.2)
is presented, being shown that the derivation of such an expression is not possible.
Thus, an approximation to E (EB) is required, relying on concrete scenarios of the
non-controllable household demand and the PV microgeneration. In other words, a
scenario-based approximation forE (EB) is performed and presented in (5.3).

In (5.2), a weighted sum of two conﬂicting criteria, namely the expected electricity bill
and the resident discomfort, is considered. Following Chapter 3, amax-min normali-
sation of each criteria is performed, to make the resulting objective function insensitive
to individual criterion scaling (Marler and Arora, 2005). This normalisation computes
y = x−min(x)
max(x)−min(x), wherex is the value of the original criterion, max(x) and min(x)
its upper and lower bounds, respectively, andy is deﬁned in the range [0, 1].

upper bounds for the expected household electricity bill, derived in Appendix B.2, are

dryer is imposed in (5.13). According to this constraint, a new tumble dryer load
starts in the slot immediately after the end of a clothes washing load. Moreover,
as one or none washer and dryer loads are scheduled in the current horizon, this
precedence is only deﬁned in the problem instances which consider the allocation
of these machines. In order to impose this precedence, the parameterτw is used
to turn the constraint on or oﬀ, accordingly.

10. The volume of non-potable groundwater stored in the tank is modelled as
in (Ocampo-Martínez et al., 2013). This volume is computed, at the end of each
sloti of the horizon, by taking the diﬀerence between the pump ﬂow rate and the
domestic demand, withi = 1,...,T . In this chapter, the non-controllable toilet
ﬂushes are modelled for each sloti through appropriate Poisson distributions
Fi∼P o (λi), withi = 1,...,T . The parametersλi are retrieved from Table 3.1.
Moreover, the corresponding toilet ﬂush realisationsFis, withi = 1,...,T and
s = 1,...,N s, are drawn from the respective Poisson distributions. The non-
potable water volume stored in the tank at the end of each slot of the horizon

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 150

Finally, it should be mentioned that, in (5.7), both the normalised expected weekly

app, and the resident discomfort,RD, are unitless and deﬁned in

the expected weekly electricity bill in the objective function of the corresponding

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 152
optimisationprobleminstance. Conversely, 1−αreﬂectstherelativeimportanceofthe
resident discomfort in the same objective function, of the same optimisation problem
instance. When lower values of the α weight parameter are considered, a greater
importance is given for the minimisation of the resident discomfort. Conversely, when
higherα values are considered, a stronger importance is assigned to the minimisation
of the expected weekly electricity bill.
5.3 Scenario-Based Appliance Allocation
Similarly to Chapter 3, the week-ahead scheduling of four domestic controllable appli-
ances, namely, the clothes washing, tumble dryer, dishwasher and the electrical pump
system, is considered, within the smart home environment and appliance operation
needs described in Sections 3.1.1 and 5.1. Moreover, the appliance allocation is car-
ried out over the same 30 weeks considered in Section 3.3, which cover a one-year
period, from March 2018 to February 2019. For each week, the controllable appli-
ances are scheduled using the SB-SCRH strategy presented in the Algorithm 2. The
traditional RH and DA allocation variants are also considered, being their choice mo-
tivated by their prevalence in the HEMS literature. While SB-SCRH explicitly follows
Algorithm 2, few modiﬁcations have been considered in the traditional RH and DA
allocation variants, so as to account for the appropriate lengths of the scheduling
horizon of each strategy.
A scenario-based stochastic receding horizon (SB-SRH) strategy is herein proposed
for traditional RH-based scheduling. Unlike in SB-SCRH, a seven-day horizon is
considered at the start of each new day of the horizon. As such, scheduling horizons
of equal length are considered. Likewise in SB-SCRH, only the ﬁrst day of the planned
appliance operation is implemented. A complete formulation of the daily instances
of the stochastic programming MILP optimisation problems, considered within the
SB-SRH strategy, is presented in Appendix B.4. Concerning the DA allocation, a
scenario-based stochastic day-ahead (SB-SDA) strategy is considered. In SB-SDA,
the controllable appliances are scheduled at the start of each new day of the week,
over a single-day horizon. Unlike mentioned above for the SB-SCRH and SB-SRH
strategies, all planned appliance allocation is implemented in SB-SDA. A complete
formulation of the daily instances of the optimisation problems considered within the
SB-SDA strategy is presented in Appendix B.5.
In this section, the allocation of the controllable appliances is carried out at the
start of each new day of each week, and for a total ofNs = 25 independent realisations
of the PV microgeneration and the non-controllable household power and non-potable
water demand. In stochastic programming optimisation problems, it is common to
consider a high number of scenarios, in the order of hundreds of even thousands.
This preference for more scenarios is motivated by an improved approximation of the
expected value of the objective function. In the scope of this work, limitations in
memory and computation time have motivated the choice for a smaller number of
scenarios for each optimisation problem instantiated. As an example, in each instance
of the optimisation problems, a total of3×T×Ns×Ns variables are deﬁned to model
the domestic electricity balance (i.e., the balance, along with its positive and negative
values for each slot of the horizon). As such, for a one-week horizon of hourly slots,
i.e. T = 168, andNs = 25, a total of315000 decision variables are deﬁned, just to
model the domestic electricity balance.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 153
In all three strategies, i.e. SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA, the scenario gen-
eration is carried out via Monte Carlo sampling, exploiting the probabilistic mod-
elling of the non-controllable demand and microgeneration presented in Table 3.1 and
in Chapter 4, for each slot of the horizon. In this procedure, the individual prob-
ability distributions are obtained, for each slot of the horizon, as follows. In the
case of the non-controllable power demand and microgeneration, for each week, sepa-
rate GP regression models are estimated for each one of the two uncertainty sources.
For each week, individual GP models are estimated, considering one-year historical
records – e.g., for the week starting on 05/03/2018, one GP model is trained with
non-controllable demand data from 05/03/2017 to 04/03/2018, whereas another GP
model is trained with microgeneration data from the same period. Once both GP
models have been trained, the GP inference is carried out to predict the probability
distributions that describe the non-controllable demand and the microgeneration in
each slot of the horizon. As for the non-controllable non-potable water demand, the
probability distributions describing this uncertainty source at each slot of the horizon
are explicitly characterised by Poisson distributions, parametrised according to Ta-
ble 3.1. Once the individual probability distributions are known for each slot of the
horizon, their inverse CDF can also be determined. Let, e.g., ln
