Regarding the speciﬁcation of the resident discomfort criterion, the same approach
followed in Chapter 3, and originally proposed in (Pamulapati et al., 2020), is adopted.
For each one of the 30 weeks, the preferences of the residents concerning the operation
times of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines are modelled
using one-year historical appliance usage records. Finally, for each week, the same
ﬁve household electricity billα weight values, adopted in Chapter 3, are considered:
(i) strict minimisation of the resident discomfort (α = 0); (ii) strict minimisation of
the expected weekly electricity bill (α = 1); (iii) equal criteria importance (α = 0.5);
(iv) higher minimisation of the resident discomfort, without completely disregarding
the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill (α = 0.25); and (v) higher
minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill, without completely disregarding
the minimisation of the resident discomfort (α = 0.75). All optimisation problems,
instantiated in every day of every week, and for each strategy, are solved using SCIP
solver v6.0.0 (Gleixner et al., 2018a,b), running on a Ubuntu 16.04 machine equipped
with 504 GB RAM, and a dual 2.2GHz Intel Xeon Silver 4214 CPU with 16.5MB
cache. As stated in Chapter 4, the GP inference is run in three 16GB RAM NVIDIA
Quadro P500 GPUs, installed in the same machine.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 154
5.3.1 Scenario-Based Stochastic Compressive Receding Horizon
The proposed SB-SCRH strategy is employed for the week-ahead scheduling of the
controllable appliances, for each of the 30 weeks. As discussed above, the instances of
thedailySB-SCRHoptimisationproblemsrelyonthescenariosofthenon-controllable
household demand and PV microgeneration. For each week, the appliance scheduling
is carried out for each expected electricity bill weight, i.e.α∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1}.
For α = 0, the allocation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher
machines is aligned with periods of higher resident preferences. In the case of the dish-
washer machine, it is mainly operated in the same periods reported in Section 3.3.1,
with a predominant operation during slots 3 and 4 of each day of the week, which cor-
responds to the oﬀ-peak billing period between 2 am and 4 am. Few exceptions in the
operation of this appliance are observed in some days, in which the start of this load is
anticipated by one slot. Concerning the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines,
they are also scheduled for the periods of higher resident preferences. In the case of the
clothes washing machine, it is mainly operated in slots 16 and 17 of the corresponding
day, i.e. from 3 pm to 5 pm. On its turn, the tumble dryer machine is operated from
slot 18 to 21 of the same day, i.e. from 5 pm to 9 pm. In some weeks, the allocation of
the single clothes washing and tumble dryer loads is, instead, scheduled for nighttime
periods, being operated from slot 1 to 6, i.e. from 0 am to 6 am. Despite being mostly
scheduled for the same slots of the day reported in Section 3.3.1 for the CRH strategy,
in SB-SCRH scheduling, the clothes washing and tumble dryer loads are operated in
diﬀerent days of the week. While the overestimation of the non-controllable demand
is no longer carried out in this chapter, the high probabilistic constraint thresholds
imposed under the SB-SCRH scheduling are responsible for the observed shifting to-
wards oﬀ-peak billing periods. Regarding the electrical pump system, its operation
does not inﬂuence the minimisation of the resident discomfort. Thus, this appliance
is scheduled with the sole goal of ensuring that the probabilistic water tank volume
and contracted power requirements are satisﬁed, given the generated non-controllable
power and non-potable water demand scenarios. Under the SB-SCRH strategy, the
operation of the electrical pump system is shown to diﬀer from its CRH counterpart
reported in Section 3.3.1. For CRH-based allocation, clothes washing loads and some,
more frequent, toilet ﬂushes are preceded by an intense and prolonged operation of the
electrical pump system. On the other hand, under the proposed SB-SCRH strategy,
the electrical pump system is operated more frequently, and at a higher ﬂow rate, but
during shorter periods of time. Nevertheless, one particularly prolonged operation is
observed in the fourth day of most weeks. In this day, the electrical pump system is
operated at half ﬂow rate for about 10 consecutive slots.
Forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, the operation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and
dishwasher machines remains similar to that reported forα = 0. As such, the dish-
washer machine is mainly operated during slots 3 and 4 of each day of the horizon,
i.e. from 2 am to 4 am. On the other hand, the clothes washing and tumble dryer
loads remain predominantly operated during on-peak billing periods, from slots 16
to 21, i.e. from 3 pm to 9 pm. The operation of these appliances is similar to that
reported in Section 3.3.1 for the CRH optimisation-based strategy. Few diﬀerences
between the two strategies are observed, for the same week andα weight. In the
case of the dishwasher machine, the reported diﬀerences comprise the anticipation of
some daily operations by one or two slots. On the other hand, in some weeks, the
clothes washing and tumble dryer loads are scheduled for diﬀerent days of the week
and, in some cases, anticipated to slots 1–6, i.e. from 0 am to 6 am. Regarding the
electrical pump system, similar operation is reported forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, being

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 155
operated predominantly at nighttime, exploiting oﬀ-peak billing tariﬀs to reduce the
weekly electricity bill. Nonetheless, noon and early afternoon operation of the elec-
trical pump system has also been reported. The operation of this equipment during
more expensive tariﬀs is only carried out in the same days of the full clothes washing
and tumble dryer loads. Such on-peak operation of the pump system is only carried
out for a short period of time, with the goal of restoring the non-potable water volume
supplied to the clothes washing machine. The operation of the electrical pump system
is also reported to be less frequent and intense on the last days of the week. Likewise
for the CRH-based scheduling, smoother operation of the electrical pump system is
responsible for a decrease in the volume of the non-potable water stored in the tank
at later slots of the week. In comparison to this strategy, tank storage remains closer
to its minimum capacity, in the last few slots of the week.
Lastly, forα = 1, the resident discomfort is no longer minimised. In this case, the
controllable appliances are scheduled with the sole purpose of minimising the expected
electricity bill, in which the operation of all controllable appliances is shifted towards
the oﬀ-peak billing periods. Two exceptions are identiﬁed, including few daytime
operations of the dishwasher and the electrical pump system. The on-peak, daytime,
operation of the dishwasher machine is motivated by PV microgeneration surpluses.
These surpluses are estimated based on the non-controllable demand and generation
scenarios. As for the pump system, forα = 1, its operation during the on-peak billing
periods is only carried out when strictly necessary to ensure the probabilistic tank
volume constraints imposed in (5.23). It must be noted that, under the Monte Carlo-
based scenario generation, the non-controllable demand is no longer overestimated. As
a result, higher and more prolonged local PV microgeneration surpluses are estimated.
Thisalsopromotesareductioningridpurchases, allowingsomecontrollableloadswith
shorterprogramdurationanddemand, suchastheelectricalpumpandthedishwasher,
to be substantially, or even entirely, supplied with local microgeneration. Rather than
being exploited for self-consumption, these generation surpluses could have, instead,
been reinjected into the grid. In this case, the above reported oﬀ-peak dishwasher
and pump system operation would have been maintained. However, considering that
the incentives for the energy reinjection are inferior to the oﬀ-peak billing tariﬀs,
i.e. Bi > S,∀i = 1,...,T , a greater reduction in the weekly electricity bill can be
achieved by means of self-consumption. In comparison to the smallerα weights, the
pump system is also reported to be operated less frequently, and at lower ﬂow rate,
throughout the entire week. Such operation is scheduled due to the greater relative
importance attributed to the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill.
For eachα weight and week, the operation of the controllable appliances planned
with the SB-SCRH strategy is subsequently simulated, considering the actual non-
controllable demand and PV microgeneration. The tank pruning strategy proposed
in Section 3.3.1 is reimplemented, in order to avoid underﬂows or overﬂows of the tank,
resulting from more or less intense real non-potable water demand. In Figure 5.1, two
distinct plots are presented for the week with higher demand andα = 0.5. They
comprise the power demand resulting from the controllable appliance operation, and
thenon-potablewatervolumestoredinthetank, registeredundertheabovementioned
pump pruning strategy. The reader is, once again, recalled of the proportionality
between the operation setting of the electrical pump system and its underlying power
demand. It can be inferred from Figure 5.1 that the volume of non-potable water
stored in the tank remains within its capacity bounds, throughout the entire week.
Moreover, compared to the CRH-based strategy, the reported smaller tank volumes
support both the lower pump ﬂow rates and a reduction in the weekly electricity bill.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 156
Figure 5.1: SB-SCRH controllable power demand (top) and tank
volume (bottom) under pump operation pruning, for the week with
higher demand andα = 0.5.
Unlike what was observed for the water tank, the management of the battery sys-
tem follows what is reported for the CRH optimisation-based strategy. In Figure 5.2,
the management of the battery system is illustrated for the same week and scenario.
About one complete battery charge and discharge cycle is carried out per day of the
horizon. In these daily cycles, the battery charging takes advantage of the cheaper
nightly tariﬀs, further contributing to the reduction of the weekly electricity bill. Once
again, the battery is not charged in the last day of the week, as this is the last day of
the horizon. Forα∈{ 0.25; 0.75; 1}, a similar management of the battery system is
performed, consisting of daily charging and discharging. Nevertheless, forα = 0, the
battery is only partially charged and discharged during the ﬁrst four days of the week.
From the fourth day onwards, it is no longer used, and its storage remains ﬁxed at its
initial value. This diverges from what was reported for the CRH strategy, considering
the sameα weight (see Section 3.3.1), according to which the battery system is not
exploited throughout the horizon. It is also noted that, forα = 0, the management
of the battery system under both the CRH and SB-SCRH strategies minimises the
resident discomfort. Nevertheless, these are not the only two optimal solutions. As
the minimisation of the electricity bill is not considered forα = 0, and the operation
of the battery system has no inﬂuence on the resident discomfort, any other feasbile
management of the battery would also be optimal.
5.3.2 Scenario-Based Stochastic Receding Horizon
The traditional RH variant of the scenario-based controllable appliance scheduling
follows the same trends reported in Section 5.3.1 for the SB-SCRH strategy.
Forα∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher are
operated during the respective periods of higher resident preferences. In the case of the
washer and dryer machines, noon and afternoon operation, coinciding with the more

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 157
Figure 5.2: SB-SCRHbatterySOC,fortheweekwithhigherdemand
and α = 0.5.
expensive electricity tariﬀs, is reported. Concerning the dishwasher, higher resident
preferencesareregisteredfornighttimeperiods, duringwhichcheaperelectricitytariﬀs
are in place. Forα = 0, the electrical pump system is operated with the sole goal of
ensuring the probabilistic contracted power and tank volume bound requirements. As
such, higher operation freedom is given to the electrical pump system, due to the fact
that, in this case, the resident discomfort is the only criterion considered, which is not
inﬂuenced by the operation of this equipment. Asα increases, so does the relative
importance of minimising the expected weekly electricity bill. A shift in the operation
of the electrical pump system towards the oﬀ-peak periods is, therefore, promoted,
along with an overall reduction of its ﬂow rate.
Conversely, forα = 1, only the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity
bill is considered. In this case, the estimated local PV microgeneration surpluses are
fully exploited for self-consumption. When both the controllable and non-controllable
power demand cannot be totally ensured by the estimated PV generation surpluses,
some or all of the controllable loads are shifted towards oﬀ-peak billing periods, in
order to further reduce the electricity bill. Unlike reported in Section 3.3.2 for tradi-
tional RH-based scheduling, no overnight operation of the clothes washing and tumble
dryer is observed. Moreover, as pointed out in Section 3.3.2, the optional allocation
of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines, during the second week, is only
observed for lowerα weights. As noted in the mentioned section, the decision to
schedule these two loads during the second week is entirely dependent on the value
of theα parameter. As such, for lowerα, the smaller contribution assigned to the
expected weekly electricity bill means that the resulting increase in the value of this
criterion, due to the scheduling of the washer and dryer loads in the second week, is
inferior to the increase in the resident discomfort, due to not scheduling these loads.
Thus, in these cases, it is more advantageous to plan these loads one week in advance.
The operation of the controllable appliances, planned with the SB-SRH strategy,
is simulated over all of the 30 weeks considered. In some of these weeks, the volume
of non-potable water stored in the tank is shown to surpass its maximum capacity.
The resulting tank overﬂow requires the implementation of the pump pruning strategy
introduced in Chapter 3. The application of this strategy is shown capable of avoiding

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 158
violations of the imposed tank volume constraints. As far as the management of the
battery system is concerned, it is again performed distinctively forα = 0 and all other
α weights. Forα = 0, the battery charging and discharging follows a diﬀerent pattern
than that reported in Section 5.3.1, under the SB-SCRH-based scheduling. An initial
full battery charge is carried out during the ﬁrst day of the week. In the remainder of
the week, the battery system is progressively discharged, albeit accompanied by small
partial charges. For all the otherα weights, similar SB-SRH, SB-SCRH and CRH-
based battery management is reported. Daily charging and discharging cycles are
observed, in which the battery is charged at nighttime, taking advantage of cheaper
tariﬀs. Likewise noted in Section 3.3.2 for the CRH and RH strategies, in the SB-SRH
variant, the battery is charged in the last day of the week, whereas in SB-SCRH it
is not. As explained in Section 3.3.2, this behaviour is justiﬁed by the equal-length
horizons considered in the standard RH-based strategies, which cover portions of two
distinct weeks. By charging the battery system in the seventh day of the horizon, the
SB-SRH-based schedules account for the power demand at the start of the next week.
5.3.3 Scenario-Based Stochastic Day-Ahead
Following the DA-based scheduling results presented in Section 3.3.3, the main dif-
ference between the SB-SDA and the two previously discussed strategies, namely the
SB-SCRH and the SB-SRH, concerns the operation of the clothes washing and tumble
dryer machines. As previously stated, in all DA allocation strategies, the scheduling
of these loads is optional in every day of the week. As a result, and as observed in
Section 3.3.3, the weekly loads of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines are
predominantly scheduled for the ﬁrst or the last days of the week. The choice of the
day of the week in which these machines are scheduled is dependent on the relative
importance assigned to the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill.
Therefore, for α∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, the allocation of the clothes washing and
tumble dryer machines is favoured in the ﬁrst day of the week, in all 30 weeks con-
sidered. Within the ﬁrst day of the week, these two appliances remain scheduled for
slots with higher resident preferences, contributing to the minimisation of the resident
discomfort. The clothes washing machine is operated during the slots 16 and 17 of the
day, i.e. from 3 pm to 5 pm, while the tumble dryer is operated from slots 18 to 21, i.e.
from 5 pm to 9 pm. Few exceptions are observed in some weeks, in the form of oﬀ-peak
washer and dryer allocations, carried out between slots 2 and 7 of the day, i.e. from
1 am to 7 am. Oﬀ-peak allocation of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines,
during the ﬁrst day of the week, is more frequent forα = 0.75. This is justiﬁed by the
higher relative importance assigned to the minimisation of the expected weekly elec-
tricity bill. Forα = 0.75, the operation of the washer and the dryer machines is shifted
away from the slots of higher resident preferences. With respect to the dishwasher
machine, for α∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75} and across all 30 weeks, the allocation of this
appliance matches higher resident preferences and oﬀ-peak billing slots. This way, the
operation of the dishwasher machine simultaneously contributes to the minimisation
of both the expected weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort. The planned
operation of the dishwasher follows that reported in Section 5.3.1 for the SB-SCRH
strategy. Thus, the dishwasher is predominantly operated during slots 3 and 4 of each
day of the week, i.e. from 2 am to 4 am. With regards to the electrical pump system,
a slight higher ﬂow rate is observed, in all 30 weeks and for the above mentionedα
weights, prior to the start of a new washer load. Moreover, the electrical pump system
is also predominantly operated at the end of each day of the week. This is performed
to reﬁll the tank with non-potable groundwater, ensuring enough volume for domestic

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 159
supply during the next day. Finally, the fact that, forα∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, the
washer is scheduled for the ﬁrst day of the week, means that higher pump system
ﬂow rate is also observed during the ﬁrst slots of this day. This is to replenish the
consumption of non-potable water of the clothes washing machine.
On the other hand, for α = 1, appliances are only allocated to minimise the
expected weekly electricity bill. The fact that the resident discomfort is no longer
considered, and the scheduling horizon only covers a one-day period, promotes a de-
lay in the allocation of the weekly washer and dryer loads. As such, in all 30 weeks,
the washer and dryer are operated in the last day of the week, forα = 1. Moreover,
a general trend towards oﬀ-peak billing shifting is reported for all controllable loads.
Thus, in addition to being delayed to the last day of the week, the weekly washer and
dryer loads are also carried out during the ﬁrst slots of the day, during which cheaper
tariﬀs are in place. As for the dishwasher machine, the fact that its periods of higher
resident preferences coincide with oﬀ-peak billing tariﬀs allows the minimisation of
both criteria, despite only the expected weekly electricity bill being explicitly consid-
ered for this value of theα weight. In few days of some weeks, the dishwasher machine
is operated in slots 1 and 2, from 0 am to 2 am, whereas in other days it is delayed
to slots 23 and 24, from 10 pm to 0 am. The fact that the PV microgeneration is
non-existent during the oﬀ-peak billing periods means that there is no impact to the
weekly electricity bill, resulting from scheduling a given appliance in diﬀerent oﬀ-peak
billing periods. Indeed, shifting, e.g., the dishwasher across diﬀerent oﬀ-peak periods
results in the same energy purchases from the same source, i.e. the grid, and sub-
jected to the same tariﬀs. Regarding the electrical pump system, a strategy similar
to that reported for SB-SCRH, in Section 5.3.1, is observed. Likewise reported for
smaller α weights, the only exception concerns the higher ﬂow rate of the electrical
pump system, prior to the weekly clothes washing load, which, in this case, always
takes place in the last day of the week. During the ﬁrst six days of the week, heavy
oﬀ-peak pump operation, characterised by prolonged high ﬂow rates, is promoted to
reﬁll the tank. Forα = 1, the on-peak pump operation is more scarce, being only
carried out as a last resort, in order to ensure the minimum non-potable water tank
volume storage for domestic supply.
In the above paragraphs, no substantial diﬀerence in the operation of the pump
system is reported between the three scenario-based strategies, namely the SB-SCRH,
SB-SRH and the SB-SDA. Even though higher ﬂow rates occur in the SB-SDA-based
allocation, prior to the weekly operation of the clothes washing machine, the resulting
tank inﬂows are not signiﬁcant enough to have a considerable impact in the tank
storage. Regarding the battery system, similarities to its management under the SB-
SCRH and SB-SRH strategies are reported in SB-SDA forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1}. For
theseα weights, one full charging and discharging cycle is performed at each day of the
week. The battery system is charged during the ﬁrst or last slots of the day, while it
is progressively discharged throughout the day. Unlike in SB-SRH, the battery is not
charged at the end of the last day of the week, given that the subsequent days are not
contemplated in the scheduling horizon. However, forα = 0, the local microstorage
is not exploited by the HEMS. Thus, for thisα weight, the battery is neither charged
nor discharged during the entire week, keeping its SOC level at its initial value.
5.3.4 Scenario-Based Load Deferment Analysis
The previous subsections discussed the operation of the controllable appliances deter-
mined by each scenario-based technique, i.e. SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA. It was
argued that no signiﬁcant diﬀerences in the management of the controllable appliances

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 160
are observed between the three strategies. For the same relative importance of the
weekly electricity bill, expressed in the value of theα weight parameter, the control-
lable appliances are scheduled for the same periods of the day and, in some cases, even
in the same slots. Similarities to the proﬁle-based allocation covered in Chapter 3 are
also noted. Despite the similar appliance management, the overall smaller ﬂow rate of
the electrical pump system, throughout the horizon, is reported for the scenario-based
allocation. The reasons for this operation are twofold. First, in comparison to the
representative proﬁles considered in Chapter 3, no overestimation of the household
demand is performed by the generated non-potable water demand realisations. Sec-
ond, the operation of the controllable appliances is planned on the basis of multiple
realisations, rather than a single one. This results inwell-rounded schedules, i.e. with
better performance in the presence of a larger variety of realisations.
When considering lowerα weights, the minimisation of the resident discomfort is
privileged over the expected weekly electricity bill. In these cases, the operation of the
clothes washing and tumble dryer machines is shifted towards afternoon periods, while
nighttimedishwasherallocationiscarriedout. Concerningtheelectricalpumpsystem,
as its operation does not inﬂuence the resident discomfort, it is freely operated with
the sole purpose of ensuring the minimum and maximum non-potable water storage
volume bounds for the tank, with a given probability. On the other hand, under higher
α weights, controllable appliances are shifted towards the oﬀ-peak billing periods, to
minimise the expected weekly electricity bill. For the dishwasher machine, there are
no signiﬁcant diﬀerences in the operation schedules, as the periods of lower resident
discomfort coincide with oﬀ-peak tariﬀs. Additionally, the clothes washing, tumble
dryer and electrical pump system machines are shifted towards oﬀ-peak tariﬀs.
In Figure 5.3, the performance of the scenario-based, proﬁle-based, ideal and the
typical allocation is presented, for the ﬁve diﬀerentα weights and the week with
higher demand, according to the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort
criteria. The proﬁle-based, ideal and the typical allocation are obtained as reported
in Chapter 3. The reader is reminded that the typical allocation is retrieved directly
from the historical appliance usage records. As such, its performance is not inﬂuenced
by any particularα weight. Nevertheless, it remains presented for eachα weight, for
easier comparison against the remaining strategies.
In Figure 5.3, similar weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort performance is
observed for the proﬁle-based and the scenario-based strategies, across allα weights.
The reader is reminded that, as stated in Section 3.3.6, the resident discomfort cri-
terion is only inﬂuenced by the slot of the day in which the corresponding load is
operated. Thus, scheduling the same appliance to the same slots of the day, across
diﬀerent days of each week, results in the same resident discomfort. This is supported
by the above reported controllable appliance scheduling to almost the same slots of
the day, for the three scenario-based strategies considered.
For the selected week, for which the highest demand is registered, none of the
strategies clearly and consistently outperform its counterparts. Only marginal dif-
ferences in the performance of the two criteria are noted. As an example, slight
worse resident discomfort is registered for the scenario-based allocation, in compari-
son to the proﬁle-based strategies. In its turn, the weekly electricity bill is marginally
lower for the scenario-based allocation, in comparison to its proﬁle-based counter-
parts. Moreover, forα = 0.5, scenario-based allocation surpasses the ideal allocation
strategy, according to the weekly electricity bill criterion. It should be noted that,
for α = 0.5, the ideal allocation provides a lower bound for the weighted sum of the
weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort, rather than for each individual cri-
terion. Therefore, this strategy still outperforms the scenario-based allocation, in the

As in Chapter 3, the lack of information on the preferred operation times for

aforementioned improvements of the scenario-based allocation are not very signiﬁcant

in some weeks, in comparison to their proﬁle-based counterparts. Likewise for the
weekly electricity bill, the performance diﬀerence is negligible in the context of resi-

tumble dryer and dishwasher to similar operation times to those of the ideal alloca-
tion. In light of these results, the most notorious diﬀerence between these two groups
of strategies concerns the operation of the electrical pump system. Moreover, the fact
that overall similar appliance management and competitive scheduling performance
are achieved for scenario-based strategies, in comparison to ideal allocation, validates
their application on the prototype smart home.
One major drawback and limitation of the scenario-based strategies herein pro-
posed is their underlying computation costs. Indeed, for SB-SCRH, SB-SRH, and
even SB-SDA strategies, a very high computational burden is observed, making their
implementation infeasible in practice. In Figure 5.9, the computation time for the
allocation of controllable appliances using the proﬁle-based and the scenario-based
strategies is presented, for each day of the week, and in logarithmic-scale box-and-
whisker plots. The computation time is retrieved using thestatistics command pro-
vided by the SCIP solver. Substantial diﬀerences in the computation times are clearly
shown in Figure 5.9, between the proﬁle-based and the scenario-based strategies. The
longer computation time of the scenario-based strategies is explained by the higher
number of scenarios considered, for which additional parameters, decision variables
and constraints are speciﬁed. As a result, higher-dimensionality stochastic program-
ming MILP problems are instantiated at the start of each day of the horizon, with
high-value requirements for the computation time and memory.
Day of the week

In any given day of the horizon, the proﬁle-based allocation of the controllable
appliances is determined very quickly, taking up to 12 seconds (around 2.5 in the
logarithmic scale). For some days of the horizon, the DA-based allocation is solved
almost instantly, being its computation time expressed by a negative value in the
logarithmic scale. Regarding the scenario-based allocation, longer computation time
is observed for all strategies of this type, being more signiﬁcant for the SB-SCRH and
SB-SRH. For any day of the horizon, the SB-SDA allocation takes anywhere from
12 seconds to around 2.5 minutes (ranging from 2.5 to 5 in the logarithmic scale).
Furthermore, the SB-SCRH and SB-SRH strategies are much slower. For any day
of the horizon, most appliance allocation carried out through the SB-SRH strategy
takes between 50 minutes and 6 hours, as shown in Figure 5.9 by the interquantile
range of the computation time distribution of this strategy. In terms of the SB-SCRH
scheduling, similar computation time is observed in the ﬁrst days of the weeks, with
a decreasing trend being identiﬁed from the fourth day onwards. This decreasing
trend in the computation time is expected, and justiﬁed by the shrinking horizon of
this strategy. In the last day of each week, as just a single-day scheduling horizon is
considered in SB-SCRH, the distribution of its computation time resembles that of the
SB-SDA strategy. This trend is also observed for CRH, the proﬁle-based equivalent
to the SB-SCRH strategy, albeit at a smaller scale. With regards to the proﬁle-based
strategies, a similar behaviour is observed. The traditional RH strategy presents the
longest computation time, which remains somewhat constant throughout the week.
This is justiﬁed by the fact that equal-length horizons are considered for each day
of the week. On the other hand, the smallest computation time is registered for DA
allocation, due to the fact that it considers the shortest scheduling horizon.
In Figure 5.10, the total computation time for the proﬁle-based and scenario-
based controllable appliance scheduling is presented, in logarithm scale, over the 30
weeks and forα = 0.5. This ﬁgure corroborates the above argued gap, in terms of
computational load, between proﬁle-based and scenario-based strategies. It can also
be inferred that SB-SCRH and SB-SRH are by far the two most demanding strategies.
The total computation time, in any week, for the SB-SCRH and SB-SRH strategies
is shown to range from 16 h to 45 h. Given the high computational burden, the
application of these two strategies, in any case study, is deemed infeasible. Indeed, in
both the proﬁle-based and scenario-based strategies, the controllable load scheduling
is carried out before the start of each new day of the week. Considering the week
starting at 05/03/2018, the ﬁrst iteration of both Algorithms 1 and 2 is performed
at slot 23 on 04/03/2018. Likewise, in the second day of the same week, the second
iteration of Algorithms 1 and 2 is performed at slot 23 on day 05/03/2018, and so
on until the last day of the week. In order to ensure that the planned appliance
schedules are available in the ﬁrst slot of the corresponding horizons, and considering
the discretisation of the scheduling horizon into hourly slots, a computation time
limit of 1 h is imposed on each iteration of Algorithms 1 and 2. In the proﬁle-based
strategies, each iteration of Algorithm 1 comprises the creation of demand proﬁles, PV
generation forecast retrieval and appliance allocation by solving a MILP optimisation
problem instance. As demonstrated in Figures 5.9 and 5.10, this runs in a matter of
few seconds, respecting the computation time limitations.
When applying the rationale in the previous paragraph to the SB-SCRH and
SB-SRH strategies, the imposed computation time limits are not respected. In the
SB-SCRH and SB-SRH strategies, each iteration of Algorithm 2 includes the following
three steps: (i) GP-based inference, to estimate the parameters of each distribution
modelling thenon-controllable power demandand thePV generation; (ii) Monte Carlo
sampling-based scenario generation; and (iii) solution of an instance of a stochastic

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 168
Figure 5.10: Total computation time for controllable appliance
scheduling in proﬁle-based and scenario-based strategies, presented in
logarithm scale, over the 30 weeks, for equal-weighting criteria.
programming MILP, based on a large number of scenarios. As veriﬁed in Figures 5.9
and 5.10, the established limit of 1 h has been greatly surpassed. In order to address
the high computation costs of the scenario-based approaches, three distinct paths have
been identiﬁed, which will be discussed in the remainder of this subsection.
One possible way to remove the high computational load of scenario-based strate-
gies consists in discarding these approaches. In this case, appliance allocation would
be ensured by proﬁle-based strategies. The main limitation of this choice is the lack of
uncertainty-aware decision-making, as discussed in Chapter 3. This has implications
is terms of feasibility guarantees and scheduling bias towards extreme non-controllable
demand or other events taken as the representative proﬁles. Moreover, as shown in
Figures 5.3, 5.4, and 5.5, the scenario-based allocation is more competitive than its
proﬁle-based counterparts, under the weekly electricity bill criterion.
The second alternative corresponds to the anticipation of the scenario-based allo-
cation, so that the underlying instances of the stochastic programming problems can
be solved before the beginning of each new day of the horizon. Taking into considera-
tion the computation times reported in Figure 5.9, in some days of the week, the daily
SB-SCRH and SB-SRH allocation would need be anticipated by up to 6 h. This antic-
ipation has a strong impact in the GP-based inference, and the corresponding scenario
generation. As presented in Chapter 4, non-controllable household demand and PV
generation are described by autoregressive GP models. If the appliance scheduling is
anticipated, the GP inference and the scenario generation would need to be carried
out without all the required past demand and generation readings. Consequently, new

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 169
GP models would need to be developed, in which the non-controllable power demand
and PV generation are predicted, at a given time, based on past records older than the
established delay, i.e. 6 h in this case. Adding up to the higher computational burden
of training more GP models, their predictive performance can also be penalised, due
to the strong partial autocorrelation observed in Figure 4.5 for small lags.
Finally, a third generically more adequate alternative addresses the main source of
the high computational load of scenario-based strategies. This source also embraces
one of the main challenges of stochastic programming (Römisch, 2009): while a large
number of scenarios is required to ensure a good approximation of the expected value
of the objective function, the resulting high computation cost motivates a choice of
fewer scenarios. In light of this dilemma, research on reduction techniques has been
carried out in the literature. In short, scenario reduction proposes to remove the most
redundant elements from a given set of scenarios. By doing so, the computational
burden of scenario-based stochastic programming optimisation problem instances can
be relieved, without decreasing the quality of the approximations of the expected value
of the objective function (Dupačová et al., 2003; Heitsch and Römisch, 2003).
Concerning the ﬁrst above discussed alternative, adverse implications are raised
by the disregard for explicit uncertainty modelling and the integration within the
decision-making framework. The resulting bias towards the extreme events, penalis-
ing the solution optimality and feasibility guarantees, is considered to outweigh the
observed reduction in the computational load. As for the second alternative, antici-
pating the allocation of the controllable appliances is simply not feasible in practice.
As argued above, the development and training of new predictive models is required,
in order to predict the future load or generation without considering the most recent
lags. In addition to the substantial additional computational burden stemming from
retraining GP models, their modelling performance is also likely to be decreased.
This aﬀects not only the resulting scenario-based approximation, but also the load
scheduling and their performance according to both criteria. On the other hand, the
motivations for scenario reduction are threefold. First, the scenario reduction ad-
dresses one of the main limitations of stochastic programming, in the form of the
high computation costs resulting from a large number of scenarios. Second, it sup-
ports uncertainty-aware decision-making, which is not available in the proﬁle-based
strategies. Third, for example, the authors of (Römisch, 2009) argued in favour of
the application of scenario reduction in detriment of the generation of fewer scenarios.
As pointed out in the cited work, the improved approximation of the value fo the
objective function is potentiated by the additional information contained in a larger
set of scenarios. Based on this discussion, the next section is entirely devoted to the
application of scenario reduction to SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA, discussing its
impact in the scheduling of residential controllable appliances.
5.4 Scenario Reduction
In this section, the 25 original realisations of the non-controllable household demand
and PV microgeneration are pruned of its most redundant elements, by means of a
scenario reduction technique. The problem of scenario reduction, and its role within
stochastic programming-based optimisation, has been extensively studied in the re-
lated literature for many years, see e.g. (Dupačová et al., 2003; Römisch, 2009; Va-
gropoulos et al., 2015). By itself, scenario reduction constitutes a combinatorial opti-
misation problem, in which a subset of a given cardinality is selected. The elements

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 170
belonging to the selected subset are such that their distance to the original, and larger,
set is minimal, according to a given distance metric.
Early proposals of scenario reduction heuristics date back to the beginning of
the 21st century. They mainly consisted of fast heuristics, such as forward selection
and backward reduction (Dupačová et al., 2003; Heitsch and Römisch, 2003). In
both heuristics, a scenario subset is iteratively selected. In forward selection, this
subset contains all scenarios to be retained, whereas in backward reduction, it contains
all scenarios to be discarded. In (Dupačová et al., 2003), promising results have
been shown for both approaches, with around 90% of the relative accuracy of the
original scenario tree being retained by the two techniques, after a 50% reduction in
the cardinality of the scenario set. Modiﬁcations to both heuristics have also been
proposed in (Heitsch and Römisch, 2003), namely in the form of fast forward selection
and simultaneous backward reduction heuristics. These improvements were registered
intermsofthecomputationaltimeandreductionaccuracy, inrelationtotherespective
original heuristics. In short, the fast forward selection proposes a more eﬃcient and
overall faster heuristic, whereas the simultaneous backward reduction improves the
accuracy of the scenario reduction, albeit at the cost of higher computational load.
Metaheuristic techniques, in particular PSO (Pappala et al., 2009; Xiong et al.,
2017;Lin etal., 2017), havealso been applied toscenario reduction problems, although
with less popularity than the alternatives discussed in the previous paragraph. In (Lin
et al., 2017), the high computation time requirements of metaheuristics, which have
been reported to dramatically increase with the number of the original scenarios and
the dimension of each scenario, are pointed out as the main reason for their limited
application. In addition to metaheuristics, clustering-based scenario reduction has
also been mentioned in (Lin et al., 2017), in which a variation of the K-means cluster-
ing algorithm is proposed for scenario reduction. This techniques has been compared
with the traditional backward reduction and PSO algorithms, with competitive ac-
curacy being obtained, in comparison to the baseline heuristics, in a fraction of the
computation time.
More recently, the application of data-driven-based techniques for both scenario
reduction and generation has also gained attention of practitioners and researchers.
For example, in (Vagropoulos et al., 2015), a feedforward ANN is developed for one
step-ahead forecasting of electrical loads. Load scenarios are subsequently generated
by adding Gaussian white noise to the electrical load forecasts. In (Chen et al., 2018),
a data-driven generative adversarial ANN is developed for model-free generation of
renewable wind and solar power output scenarios. Regarding the data-driven scenario
reduction, a CNN model has recently been developed for solar radiation data1. In
that work, the original scenario set is transformed into a 2-D image-like matrix, being
subsequentlyfedtotheproposedCNNarchitecture. Labelledreducedscenariosetsare
obtained via a heuristic search mechanism proposed in (Li et al., 2016), and taken as
target outputs during the training of the CNN model. In comparison to the heuristic
search-based technique, the competitive accuracy of the scenario reduction is shown
for the CNN model, obtained in a considerable fraction of the computation time.
In this section, the scenarios of the non-controllable load and microgeneration are
reduced via the traditional backward reduction heuristic. The choice for a traditional
heuristic is threefold. First, for its wide application in a variety of domains, which
consolidates and establishes the reduction accuracy of this method. Second, by reports
of low computation time, similar to its alternatives, for small-sized original scenario
sets. In the above cited literature, the computation time of the traditional heuristics
1https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.11486, accessed on: 06 September 2021.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 171
remains within few seconds, even when hundreds of scenarios are considered, see,
e.g., (Li et al., 2016; Lin et al., 2017). An exponential increase in computation time
only tends to be observed for several hundreds or thousands of scenarios. Considering
the original set of 25 scenarios generated in this work, a high computation time is
not expected for traditional heuristics. The third and last reason is the simplicity
of the implementation of this heuristic. Unlike, e.g., in data-driven methods, no
data annotation or specialised hardware, such as GPUs, are required to improve the
computation time or the reduction accuracy.
The traditional backward reduction heuristic considered in this work is outlined
in Algorithm 3, and implemented to determine the reduced subsets of the non-
controllable demand and microgeneration scenarios. Algorithm 3 follows the imple-
mentation in (Römisch, 2009), which in turn is derived from previous publications in
the literature (Dupačová et al., 2003; Heitsch and Römisch, 2003).
Algorithm 3Backward Reduction (Römisch, 2009)
Input:
Cardinality of the original scenario set:Ns
Original scenario set:ϵ ={ϵ1,...,ϵ Ns}
Probabilities of the original scenarios:P ={p1,...,p Ns}
Cardinality of the target reduced scenario set:n
Output:
Reduced scenario set:R ={R1,...,R n}
Probabilities of the reduced scenarios:Q ={q1,...,q n}
1. Initialise the set of scenarios to be discardedJ :=∅
2. Compute the distances between all pairs of scenarios according to distance metricD,

The Algorithm 3 is employed to reduce the original set of 25 scenarios into smaller
subsets, containing its 1, 5, 10, 15 and 20 most relevant elements. In this work, the
Euclidean distance metric is considered for the evaluation of the distance between
any pair of scenarios. For each reduced scenario set, a scenario-based allocation of
the controllable appliances is carried out as in Section 5.3. As such, the smart home
environment and appliance operation needs, originally described in Chapter 3, are
revisited. Moreover, the same set of 30 weeks is considered, and the controllable
appliances are scheduled over each week using the SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA
strategies, for each reduced scenario set. Finally, ﬁve household electricity bill weights
α∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1} are taken into account for each scheduling strategy, reduced
scenario set and week. The backward reduction heuristic is implemented in the C
programming language, compiled with GCC v7.5, taking as input the original set of
25 scenarios, generated in Section 5.3 for each week. The scenario reduction and the

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 172
corresponding optimisation problem instances, considered in each strategy, are solved
using the SCIP solver v6.0.0 (Gleixner et al., 2018a,b), running on an Ubuntu 16.04
machine equipped with 504 GB RAM, and a dual 2.2GHz Intel Xeon Silver 4214 CPU
with 16.5MB cache.
5.4.1 Reduced SB-SCRH
For each reduced scenario set of the non-controllable demand and microgeneration,
controllable appliances are scheduled through the SB-SCRH approach, over the 30
weeks, and considering the expected electricity bill weightsα∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1}.
For α = 0, the operation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher
machines follows a similar trend across all the reduced scenario sets. The operation
of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines is also similar to that
reported in Section 5.3.1, for the larger 25-scenario set. The three controllable ap-
pliances are operated during the slots of higher resident preferences. Thus, the daily
dishwasher loads are predominantly scheduled for slots 3 and 4, i.e. from 2 am to
4 am, of any given day of the week. On its turn, the weekly clothes washing and
tumble dryer weekly operation is carried out during the afternoon. In this case, the
washer is predominantly operated during slots 16 and 17 of the day, i.e. from 3 pm
to 5 pm, while the dryer is turned on from slots 18 to 21, i.e. from 5 pm to 9 pm, of
the same day. For all sets of reduced scenarios, the operation of the clothes washing
and tumble dryer machines is also anticipated for the ﬁrst slots of the chosen day of
some weeks. In these cases, the clothes washing machine is operated during the ﬁrst
and second slots of the day, i.e. from 0 am to 2 am, while the operation of the tumble
dryer is deferred to slots 3 to 6, i.e. from 2 am to 6 am, of the same day. In relation to
the electrical pump system, it is managed with the sole goal of ensuring the imposed
probabilistic bounds for the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank. This is
due to the fact that, forα = 0, the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity
bill is not considered, in any optimisation problem instance. For the reduced scenario
sets of cardinality 20, 15 and 10, the electrical pump system is operated more fre-
quently, and for shorter periods of time, than what was reported for the original 25
scenarios. Moreover, whenever the pump system is turned on, it is assigned a higher
ﬂow rate. Conversely, for reduced scenario sets of cardinality 1 and 5, the operation
of the electrical pump system is more similar to that reported in Section 3.3.1 for the
CRH-based allocation. For the reduced scenario sets of smaller cardinality, fewer, but
more prolonged pump system operation, is carried out. As the generated scenarios
do not overestimate the non-potable water demand as strongly as the representative
proﬁles computed in Section 3.3, lower ﬂow rates are generally observed. Neverthe-
less, in comparison to the original set of 25 scenarios, higher ﬂow rates are reported
during the ﬁrst half of the week. Furthermore, higher tank storage is observed dur-
ing this period, requiring the deployment of the pump pruning strategy proposed in
Chapter 3, to avoid the overﬂow of the tank. On the other hand, signiﬁcantly more
scarce operation of the electrical pump system is reported during the last day of the
week, for all reduced scenario sets. As a result, smaller non-potable water volumes are
stored in the tank at the end of the week, albeit still above the established minimum
storage bound. Lastly, the battery system is similarly managed for all scenario sets of
smaller cardinality. During the ﬁrst days of the week, the battery system is fully or
partially (around half capacity) charged. From the third day of the week onwards, it
is usually discharged until its initial SOC value is reached, i.e. SOC=0.5. After this
initial discharge, no additional charge or discharge of the battery is veriﬁed. Once
again, the reader is reminded that, forα = 0, alternative management strategies for

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 173
the battery system could have been obtained, resulting in the same scheduling per-
formance. As previously stated, this is because the battery system does not inﬂuence
the resident discomfort, which is the only scheduling goal considered forα = 0.
For α∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75} and all the reduced scenario sets, the clothes washing,
tumble dryer and dishwasher machines remain scheduled for slots of higher resident
preferences. The daily dishwasher machine loads are, thus, scheduled for the ﬁrst slots
of any given day, which also coincide with cheaper tariﬀs. On its turn, the afternoon
operation of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines is maintained, even for
α = 0.75, when the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill is privileged
over the resident discomfort. In light of these ﬁndings, it can be concluded that, for
the aboveα weights, and in particular forα = 0.75, the additional discomfort result-
ing from shifting the operation of the washer and dryer towards oﬀ-peak billing slots
is inferior to the additional bill resulting from scheduling these appliances during pe-
riods of higher resident preferences and grid tariﬀs. In comparison to the controllable
appliance allocation determined with the original set of 25 scenarios and proﬁle-based
strategies, in some weeks, the clothes washing and tumble dryer loads have also been
scheduled for diﬀerent days of the week, albeit having remained conﬁned to the same
slots of the day. Regarding the electrical pump system, for the same values of the
α weight, its operation remains similar across all reduced scenario sets. Compared
to its operation reported in the previous paragraph forα = 0, less frequent pump
system operation is observed for higherα weights. Moreover, the operation of the
electrical pump system is also shown to be more concentrated in time, taking ad-
vantage of cheaper grid tariﬀs to reduce the weekly electricity bill. Lower ﬂow rates
are, once again, observed for this appliance, during the last day of the horizon. This
contributes to a reduction of the volumes of non-potable water stored in the tank,
during this period. Nevertheless, the established minimum and maximum bounds
for the tank storage are respected across all weeks, for all reduced scenario sets and
α∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}. In Figure 5.11, the controllable power demand and tank vol-
ume are presented, for the week with higher demand,α = 0.5 and 5 scenarios. The
contents of this ﬁgure support the above discussed management of the controllable
appliances. As previously argued, no need for the pruning of the operation of the
pump is identiﬁed. In terms of the battery system, one daily complete charging and
discharging cycle is reported at nighttime, taking advantage of the cheaper tariﬀs to
reduce the weekly electricity bill. Figure 5.12 illustrates this behaviour for the week
with higher demand,α = 0.5 and for 5 scenarios.
Finally, forα = 1, the controllable loads are shifted towards oﬀ-peak billing peri-
ods, for all the reduced scenario sets. As argued in Section 5.3.1, this oﬀ-peak load
shifting is due to the fact that the expected weekly electricity bill is the sole min-
imisation goal. In this case, even though the washer and the dryer are not always
operated in the same days of the week as determined, for the same strategy, with the
original set of 25 scenarios, they remain assigned to oﬀ-peak billing slots. The same
oﬀ-peak allocation trend is reported for the dishwasher machine, with few exceptions
in some days of some weeks, where this load is operated during afternoon periods,
exploiting the self-consumption of PV generation and reducing the weekly electricity
bill. As argued in Section 5.3.1, the relatively small demand and program duration
of the dishwasher and the pump system make them suitable for exploiting periods of
higher microgeneration surplus. Regarding the electrical pump system, less frequent
and smaller ﬂow rates are observed, in comparison to its operation reported for lower
α weights. Even though smaller pump system ﬂow rates also contribute to lower non-
potable water volumes stored in the tank, the tank storage remains within the imposed
minimum and maximum capacity bounds, for each reduced scenario set. Finally, the

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 174
Figure 5.11: SB-SCRH controllable power demand (top) and tank
volume (bottom) under pump operation pruning, for the week with
higher demand, considering 5 scenarios andα = 0.5.
battery system is managed in a similar way to that presented forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}.
One complete charging and discharging cycle is reported for each day of the week,
with the battery being charged at nighttime to exploit cheaper electricity tariﬀs.
In conclusion, no signiﬁcant diﬀerences in the management of the controllable
appliances have been reported when fewer scenarios are considered. The four control-
lable appliances, namely the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines,
and the electrical pump system, have been assigned to mostly the same slots of the
horizon. Note that even the charging and discharging cycles of the battery system
remained similar to those reported for the same strategy and the original scenario
set. Nevertheless, the overestimation of the non-controllable power and non-potable
water demand is avoided thanks to its probabilistic modelling. In comparison to the
proﬁle-based strategies, lower pump system ﬂow rates are observed, reducing tank
storage and the weekly electricity bill.
5.4.2 Reduced SB-SRH
For each set of reduced non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration scenarios,
the controllable appliances are scheduled using the SB-SRH strategy, over all 30 weeks
and considering the expected electricity bill weightsα∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75; 1}. Overall,
the SB-SRH-based controllable appliance allocation is very similar to that determined
with the same strategy, for the original 25 scenarios.
For α = 0 , the controllable appliance scheduling is carried out exclusively to
minimise the resident discomfort, over each week. In this case, the three controllable
appliances whose operation directly inﬂuences the performance of this criterion, i.e.
the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines, are shifted towards slots
of maximum resident preferences. The weekly washer and dryer loads are, therefore,

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 175
Figure 5.12: SB-SCRH battery SOC, for the week with higher de-
mand, considering 5 scenarios andα = 0.5.
predominantly performed during afternoon periods, more precisely from slots 16 to
21 of the chosen day. As observed for SB-SCRH, the day of the week during which
these loads are performed may diﬀer, for the same week, in diﬀerent scenario sets
of diverse cardinalities. As for the dishwasher machine, it is operated at nighttime,
predominantly from 2 am to 4 am. Concerning the electrical pump system, as the
minimisationoftheexpectedweeklyelectricitybillisnotconsideredfor α = 0, noclear
operation pattern is noticed. Indeed, the operation of the pump system only ensures
the capacity bounds for the non-potable water volume stored in the tank. The absence
of the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill allows a second load of the
washer and the dryer to be planned over the second week. The scheduling of this
additional load requires higher ﬂow rates for the pump system during the last day
of the week, to accommodate the future demand of the extra clothes washing load.
Overall, no signiﬁcant diﬀerence in the volume of non-potable water stored in the
tank is observed, under the SB-SRH strategy, between the original and each reduced
scenario set. Lastly, diﬀerent management of the battery system is reported forα = 0,
in comparison to all otherα weight values considered. Following the operation of
the battery system reported in Section 5.3.2, a complete charge is performed during
the ﬁrst day of the week. Over the subsequent days, the battery is progressively
discharged, and partially charged from the second day of the week onwards. Over the
week, the battery system SOC levels are maintained at around SOC=0.6.
Forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5}, the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines are operated
during the afternoon, predominantly from slots 16 and 18 to 21 and 23, respectively, of
the selected day of the horizon. As for the daily dishwasher loads, these are performed
at nighttime, generally during slots 3 and 4 of each day of the week. The operation
trends of these three devices are maintained for all reduced scenario sets, and aligned
with their allocation reported in Section 5.3.2, for the original set of 25 scenarios. For
α = 0.75, changes in the operation of the clothes washing and tumble dryer machines
are noted, while the same nighttime schedule is assigned to the daily dishwasher loads.
In some weeks, a new washer load is performed during the ﬁrst slots of the day, i.e.
from 0 am to 2 am. Thus, the corresponding dryer load is scheduled from slot 3 to

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 176
6, i.e. from 2 am to 6 am. As for the electrical pump system and, consequently, the
non-potable water volume stored in the tank, no major diﬀerences are observed, for
the sameα weights, between the SB-SRH allocation with the original 25 scenarios,
and that determined for each reduced scenario set. In short, asα is increased, there
is a tendency for the electrical pump system to be operated during shorter periods of
time, with a lower ﬂow rate, and more intermittently. There is also no need to prune
the operation of the pump for all the reduced scenario sets, as the tank volumes are
maintained within the established bounds, in all 30 weeks. Regarding the battery sys-
tem, daily charging and discharging cycles are also observed forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75},
exploiting cheaper nighttime electricity for prolonged battery charging at a lower cost.
Finally, forα = 1, the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill is the
only driver of the controllable appliance allocation. As a result, for all 30 weeks and
reduced scenario sets, almost all controllable loads are shifted towards oﬀ-peak billing
periods. Few exceptions are, nonetheless, identiﬁed. These consist in sporadic af-
ternoon occurrences of dishwasher loads and electrical pump system tank inﬂows. It
should also be noted that the afternoon scheduling of these two operations is only per-
formed when the PV microgeneration surpluses are estimated, based on the diﬀerent
scenarios considered. Furthermore, in this case, the pump is operated with a small
ﬂow rate due, in general, to the low estimated PV microgeneration surpluses. Even
during oﬀ-peak billing slots, a tendency for lower pump system ﬂow rates, in com-
parison to those reported for lowerα weights, is observed. This is motivated by the
higher relative importance assigned to the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill.
Naturally, the non-potable water volumes stored in the tank are also smaller, although
remaining above the minimum capacity bound. In comparison to SB-SCRH-based ap-
pliance scheduling, higher tank storage is observed in the last day of the horizon. This
is due to the frequent operations of the pump, even at lower ﬂow rates, carried out to
ensure the domestic supply in the subsequent days. The overnight operation of the
clothes washing machine is also observed in some weeks, across the diﬀerent reduced
scenario sets. Eﬀorts to minimise the weekly electricity bill are also reﬂected by the
absence of the allocation of the optional loads of the washer and the dryer, during the
second week. Lastly, the daily charging and discharging cycles of the battery system
are also observed forα = 1, and across all the reduced scenario sets. Likewise for the
water tank, the battery system is charged in the last day of the week, in order to ac-
count for the domestic supply in the ﬁrst day of the subsequent week. As only a single
week is considered in the SB-SCRH allocation, both water tank inﬂows and battery
charging are not carried out in the last slots of the horizon, further contributing to
the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill.
5.4.3 Reduced SB-SDA
For each reduced scenario set, the day-ahead allocation of the controllable appliances
is shown to follow the same operation times determined with the same strategy, for
the original 25 scenarios. As such, the weekly clothes washing and tumble dryer loads
are predominantly planned for the ﬁrst or last days of the horizon. The decision to
schedule their operation for one of these two alternatives is conditioned by the relative
importance assigned to the minimisation of the expected weekly electricity bill, herein
modelled by the value assigned to theα weight parameter.
Forα∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, the weekly clothes washing and tumble dryer loads are
scheduled for the ﬁrst day of the week in almost all 30 weeks, and for all reduced
scenario sets. In the few cases for which a full load of clothes is not washed and
dried in the ﬁrst day, it is guaranteed to be performed in the second day. For all

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 177
reduced scenario sets, the planned operations of the washer and the dryer, over the 30
weeks, are aligned with the slots of higher resident preferences. As such, the afternoon
allocation of these two appliances is carried out, from slot 16 to 21 of the chosen day,
i.e. from 3 pm to 9 pm. Concerning the dishwasher machine, its operation also follows
the preferred residents slots, being scheduled for slots 3 and 4 of each day of the week,
i.e. from 2 am to 4 am. This dishwasher operation is observed for all days of all 30
weeks, α∈{ 0; 0.25; 0.5; 0.75} and all reduced scenario sets. Regarding the electrical
pump system, it is predominantly operated at the end of each day of the week. Its
operation targets reﬁlls of the tank with groundwater, ensuring that there is enough
non-potable water volumes for domestic supply in the next day. Moreover, as observed
for the original 25 scenarios and the DA-based strategy, nighttime tank reﬁlls coincide
with cheaper electricity tariﬀs. Likewise reported in Section 5.3.3, higher ﬂow rates
are assigned to the pump during the ﬁrst day of the week. Such operation of the pump
system takes place before the scheduled clothes washing machine load. This is carried
out to ensure the domestic supply of non-potable water for this load. Furthermore, as
theα weights grow, smaller ﬂow rates are observed, seeking to supply only the strictly
necessary non-potable water volume to ensure the domestic supply and the feasibility
of the tank bounds. Overall, for each reduced scenario set, the management of the
electrical pump system is not shown to be signiﬁcantly diﬀerent from that carried out
for the original 25 scenarios. As a result, identical non-potable water tank volume
storage is reported, for each week and reduced scenario set. Most importantly, the
volume of non-potable water stored in the tank is maintained within the established
lower and upper bounds, in all SB-SDA simulations. As such, both the excessive
pump pruning and the emergency tank reﬁlls have not been carried out. Finally, the
management of the battery system is distinct forα = 0 and α∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}.
For α = 0, local microstorage is not exploited, being the battery SOC ﬁxed at its
initial value throughout the entire week. On the other hand, the daily charging and
discharging of the battery, characteristic of SB-SCRH and SB-SRH-based battery
management, is reported forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}. For the ﬁrst day of the week, the
battery is charged during the ﬁrst slots of the day, during which lower grid tariﬀs are
registered. Subsequently, its discharging is progressively carried out throughout the
afternoon period. From the second day of the week onwards, charging of the battery is
shifted to the last slots of each day, allowing its discharging to take place throughout
the entire day, in particular amid on-peak grid tariﬀs.
Conversely to the management of the controllable appliances reported in the pre-
vious paragraph, forα = 1, the expected electricity bill is the only criterion subject
to minimisation, resulting in a shift in the operation of the controllable appliances,
towards oﬀ-peak billing slots. As such, the weekly loads of the clothes washing and
tumble dryer machines are deferred to the last day of the week, in which they are pre-
dominantly carried out from slots 2 to 7, i.e. from 1 am to 7 am. In some weeks, and
for all reduced scenario sets, the weekly operation of the washer and the dryer is noted
to be anticipated or delayed by one slot, albeit remaining scheduled for the last day
of the week. As for the dishwasher, the absence of the resident discomfort criterion is
responsible for shifting its operation to diﬀerent slots than those reported for smaller
α. Nevertheless, the planned dishwasher loads have remained conﬁned to oﬀ-peak
billing tariﬀ slots. Considering that, within the scope of this problem, the electrical
power is the only resource of interest for the dishwasher, there is no direct impact in
the weekly electricity bill with the shifting of this load. Indeed, as the PV microgen-
eration is non-existent during oﬀ-peak billing periods, the same amount of power is
purchased from the grid, and billed at the same rate. Regarding the electrical pump
system, preferable operation during oﬀ-peak billing slots is reported, in particular for

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 178
prolonged tank reﬁlls, under high ﬂow rate. On-peak pump operation is, therefore,
more scarce, and commonly carried out as a last resort to ensure the feasibility of
the tank storage bounds and domestic supply. Alternatively, on-peak pump operation
can also be performed to exploit the estimated microgeneration surpluses. Likewise
observed for smallerα weights, higher pump system ﬂow rate is reported before the
weekly clothes washing load. Overall, the volume of non-potable water stored in the
tank is shown to remain within the established lower and upper bounds. As a result,
the pump pruning strategy, originally introduced in Chapter 3, is not deployed in any
day of any week, regardless of the number of scenarios considered. Finally, similarities
in the management of the battery system to that reported forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}
are noted. The battery is charged at the beginning of the ﬁrst day of the week, and
in the last slots of all subsequent days of the week, maximising grid purchases during
cheaper tariﬀ slots. Subsequently, it is progressively discharged from the2nd to the7th
days of the week, avoiding or reducing grid purchases during on-peak billing periods.
Overall, the reduced scenario sets of diﬀerent cardinalities, considered in this and
previous subsections, have promoted a competitive scheduling with that determined
for the original 25 scenarios. In most weeks, ﬁxing the strategy and the weight of
the expected weekly electricity bill criterion results in exactly the same, or equiv-
alent, controllable appliance schedules. The reported simulation results corroborate
the existence of multiple redundant non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration
realisations. Indeed, it is shown that, by considering just a few of the most informative
scenarios, essentially the same schedules can be obtained. As argued in Section 5.3.4,
the large number of realisations originally considered is responsible for the substan-
tial computational burden of the underlying stochastic programming-based allocation.
Supported by the previous subsections, a similar controllable load allocation can be
achieved considering only the most representative scenarios, and therefore at a fraction
of the computation cost.
5.4.4 Scenario Reduction Load Deferment Analysis
From the contents of the previous subsections, it is argued that, for the same strategy
and α weights, discarding the most redundant scenarios leads to similar controllable
appliance schedules and, consequently, similar performance for the weekly electricity
bill and the resident discomfort. Moreover, given that fewer non-controllable demand
and generation scenarios are considered, the results reported in terms of schedules and
performance can be obtained at a fraction of the computational burden. Figures 5.13
to 5.15, support this argument, presenting, for each scenario-based strategy, the per-
formance according to the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort criteria,
for the week with higher demand, all scenario sets andα weights.
For the sameα weight, similar weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort per-
formance is shown for all scenario sets. Small diﬀerences are observed in the values of
either one or both criteria, in particular worst performance with the electricity bill is
veriﬁed when only one scenario is considered. Nevertheless, such observed diﬀerences
are marginal, in particular for the weekly electricity bill, where it is only incremented
by few cents of the currency unit. Following the ﬁndings presented in Sections 3.3.6
and 5.3.4, the minimisation of the resident discomfort is privileged for lower values of
α, whereas the minimisation of the weekly electricity bill is privileged for higherα.
Moreover, the diﬀerences in the resident discomfort performance, among all reduced
scenario sets, are more noticeable for higherα, in particular forα = 1. Similarly,
diﬀerences in the weekly electricity bill performance, among all reduced scenario sets,
are more noticeable for lowerα weights. It can be inferred from Figures 5.13 to 5.15

that none of the strategies clearly and consistently outperforms all the others. Never-
theless, the fact that equivalent bill and resident discomfort performance is achieved
with a signiﬁcantly small number of scenarios, namely 1 or 5, constitutes a very im-
portant outcome. By relying on fewer scenarios, the underlying computational load
is, as previously argued, expected to drop signiﬁcantly, without a corresponding de-
terioration in the scheduling performance. Nevertheless, it should be noted that only
the week with the higher electricity demand is covered in Figures 5.13 to 5.15. In
order to assess the application of the SB-SCRH and SB-SRH strategies to real-world
scenarios, the impact of the scenario reduction on both criteria and the computational
burden of the scheduling strategies must be investigated over all 30 weeks considered.
Following Chapter 3 and Section 5.3.4, equal importance of each criterion is as-
sumed for in-depth assessment of the impact of scenario reduction on appliance sched-
ules, and its corresponding performance. The main motivation for choosingα = 0.5
relies on the absence of information on the preferences of the residents. In Figures 5.16
to 5.18, the performance according to both criteria is presented for the scenario-based
strategies, over all 30 weeks andα = 0.5.
Concerning the weekly electricity bill, no signiﬁcant diﬀerence in the average bill is
shown for each strategy in Figures 5.16 to 5.18. For the three strategies, reducing the
number of scenarios up to 10 realisations is only responsible for a marginal increase of
up to 0.04 c.u. in the average weekly electricity bill. Even though this increase is not
the same for all strategies, in both this case study and most residential environments,
it has no importance in practice. Overall, higher weekly electricity bill is observed for
fewer scenarios, in particular for a single realisation, remaining somewhat consistent
over the 30 weeks, and for the three strategies. Nevertheless, even for few realisations,
the proﬁle-based strategies presented in Chapter 3 are outperformed by their scenario-
based counterparts presented in this chapter. For the resident discomfort, the same
average value is obtained for all reduced scenario sets and strategies, which suggests
that greater resident discomfort is not achieved by considering fewer scenarios. In gen-
eral, the similar scheduling performance, resulting from pruning the original scenario
set, in particular up to 10 elements, is supported by the ﬁgures presented above. These

results validate the application of the scenario reduction heuristics for the scheduling

In light of the results presented above, it becomes imperative to assess the impact
on the computation costs, resulting from considering fewer scenarios in controllable
appliance scheduling. In Figures 5.19 to 5.21, the computation time is presented,
for each day of the week, in box-and-whisker plots with a logarithmic scale, respec-
tively for the SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA strategies. In these ﬁgures, the daily
computation time covers the scenario reduction and the solution of the optimisation
problem instances. The computation time required to solve each optimisation problem
instance is retrieved using thestatistics command available in the SCIP solver. As for
scenario reduction, its underlying computation time is determined using theclock_-
gettime function available in the standard library of the C programming language.
Day of the week
Figure 5.19: SB-SCRH controllable appliance computation time,
presented in logarithm scale, for each day of the horizon.
In Figures 5.19 to 5.21, the impact on the computing time, resulting from consid-
ering fewer scenarios in the appliance scheduling, is clearly shown. For the SB-SCRH
and SB-SRH strategies, a 40% reduction in the number of scenarios, from 25 to 15, is
accompanied by a signiﬁcant decrease in computation time, from up to 6 hours to only
18 minutes. Recalling the content of Figures 5.13 to 5.17, no signiﬁcant diﬀerence in
the scheduling performance is registered between the two scenario sets, for diﬀerent
α weights and over the course of all 30 weeks. Further reduction of the number of
scenarios is responsible for an even more signiﬁcant relief of the computational load,
with a small penalisation on the scheduling performance. A larger interquartile range
(IQR) computation time is shown for 5 scenarios over all days of the horizon and
for the various strategies, encompassing that of 10 scenarios and, in some cases, even
that of 15 scenarios. Smaller computational burden is, naturally, registered when
only a single scenario is considered. In this case, each underlying daily scenario re-
duction problem, and the corresponding optimisation problem instance, are solved in
few seconds. In addition, the same resident discomfort performance is registered with
marginally higher weekly electricity bill, amounting up to 2 extra c.u. in some weeks.

Day of the week
