sented in logarithm scale, for each day of the horizon.
Day of the week
Figure 5.21: SB-SDA controllable appliance computation time, pre-
sented in logarithm scale, for each day of the horizon.
Essentially, an important outcome of this section is the veriﬁcation of similar
scheduling performance achieved at a signiﬁcantly lower computation cost. This is
particularly important for the SB-SCRH and SB-SRH strategies, and their applica-
tion to the considered smart home prototype. In this case study, a higher-cardinality
reduced scenario set whose computation time does not surpass the imposed 1 h time
limit can, therefore, be considered to schedule controllable appliances at the start of
each day of the week. Throughout each day, the controllable load operation, non-
controllable demand and microgeneration can also be continuously monitored. Mod-
iﬁcations to the SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA strategies are, therefore, required
to ensure the monitoring of the environment. In addition, if, at any given slot, prede-
ﬁned load rescheduling criteria are met, a fast load rescheduling can be carried out,

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 186
in order to restore constraint feasibility. The next section exploits the ideas discussed
above, within the smart home considered throughout this work. Modiﬁcations to the
novel SB-SCRH strategy, outlined in Algorithm 2, are proposed, in order to ensure
the smart home environment monitoring and controllable load rescheduling.
5.5 Smart Home Scenario-Based Allocation
This section builds upon the ﬁndings of the previous sections, addressing the integra-
tion of the new SB-SCRH scheduling strategy into the prototype smart home. This
case study is, therefore, revisited, along with the set of appliances to be managed
and their respective individual weekly operation needs. Following Algorithm 2, the
SB-SCRH-based appliance scheduling is carried out at the start of each new day of
the week. In addition to the daily dishwasher loads and the weekly operation of the
clothes washing and tumble dryer machines, extra load operations may be considered
atanygivendayoftheweek. Theseextraloadscanresultfromsocialgatheringevents,
hosted by the family. In these events, the family is joined by guests for dinner and
posterior entertainment, with some visitors sleeping in a guest room. The fact that a
more elaborate dinner is prepared and served to more people than usual, aﬀects the
daily dishwasher loads in two ways. First, the usual daily dishwasher load is required
to ﬁnish, at the latest, by 7 pm. This anticipation is promoted to ensure that dishes
are washed in time for dinner. Second, after the meal, one additional dishwasher load
is carried out, to wash the dishes used during dinner. In order to reduce the impact
of this extra operation on the dishwasher loads in the subsequent days of the horizon,
the extra load is performed in the same day as the event, being its allocation available
from 9 pm to 0 am. Additionally, in the day after the event, one extra clothes washing
and tumble dryer load is scheduled, in order to wash sheets, towels and other laundry
of the guests. These extra loads can only be scheduled from 9 am onwards, to ensure
that the guests are already awake.
In Algorithm 2, the controllable load operation planned at the beginning of each
day of the week remains ﬁxed, regardless of the actual non-controllable demand and
PV microgeneration observed throughout the day. Considering that only imperfect
forecasts of the demand and generation can be obtained, it is not possible to guar-
antee that the planned operation of the controllable appliances will always respect
all operational constraints, in particular the contracted power and the minimum and
maximum limits of the tank capacity. In order to address this limitation, the smart
home demand and PV microgeneration are continuously monitored, over the course
of each day of the week. If, at any given slot, the aforementioned constraints are
not respected, a speciﬁed trigger is deployed, and the operation of the controllable
appliances is rescheduled in a smaller horizon, covering the period from the current
slot to the end of the week. The goal of this rescheduling is to restore the feasibility
of the appliance operation as fast as possible. Nevertheless, other unforeseen events,
relevant to the environment considered, can also be included.
In the described smart home scenario, distinct computation time limits are consid-
ered for the controllable appliance allocation. As discussed in Section 5.3.4, assuming
that the non-controllable demand and generation are known at the start of each slot,
and ﬁxed throughout the duration of the respective slot, the daily load scheduling is
carried out in the last slot of the previous day. A one-slot computation time limit is
imposed to ensure that the planned operation is available at the start of each day.
On the other hand, mid-day rescheduling requires signiﬁcantly faster allocation, in
the order of a few seconds. Motivations for faster mid-day rescheduling include the

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 187
minimisation of the duration of the infeasibility, and the impact on the operation of
the controllable appliances. In order to address the computation time limitations,
an extension to the SB-SCRH strategy is proposed in Section 5.5.1. This strategy
promotes faster mid-day rescheduling, by considering fewer scenarios in the respective
stochastic programming MILP instances. To this end, the heuristics that guide the
selection of fewer scenarios are described in Section 5.5.2. Following the explanation
of the load coordination strategy and the heuristics for the selection of the number
of scenarios, the results of the allocation of the controllable appliances are presented
in Section 5.5.3. Finally, an alternative multistage scenario-based framework, and its
application within the smart home case study, are brieﬂy discussed in Section 5.5.4.
5.5.1 Load Coordination Strategy
The extended SB-SCRH strategy, proposed in this section for smart home scenario-
based allocation, is outlined in Algorithm 4. This strategy extends Algorithm 2,
accounting for continuous monitoring of the non-controllable demand and local mi-
crogeneration, and the subsequent mid-day controllable appliance rescheduling.
Algorithm 4Smart Home Scenario-Based Allocation
for eachday of the weekdo
1. Deﬁne the optimisation horizon (day – Sunday)
2. Retrieve the current status of social gathering events
3. Generate non-controllable (power and non-potable water) demand and PV microgen-
eration scenarios
4. Determine reduced scenario sets of smaller cardinality for non-controllable (power and
non-potable water) demand and PV microgeneration realisations generated in step 3
5. Instantiate the stochastic programming MILP problem formulation (Appendix B.6)
6. Solve the stochastic programming MILP instance of step 5
7. Retrieve the appliance schedules for the ﬁrst day of the horizon
8. for eachslot of the ﬁrst day of the horizondo
8.1 Implement the controllable appliance operation for the current slot
8.2 if Rescheduling triggerthen
8.3 Deﬁne the rescheduling optimisation horizon (current slot – Sunday)
8.4 Retrieve non-controllable (power and non-potable water) demand and PV
microgeneration scenario sets of smaller cardinality, obtained in step 4
8.5 Instantiate the stochastic programming MILP problem formulation (Ap-
pendix B.7), considering the rescheduling horizon deﬁned in step 8.3
8.6 Solve the stochastic programming MILP instance of step 8.5
8.7 Retrieve and implement the appliance operation for the current slot
8.8 Generate non-controllable (power and non-potable water) demand and PV
microgeneration scenarios over the remaining horizon (next slot – Sunday)
8.9 Determine the reduced scenario sets of smaller cardinality for non-controllable
(power and non-potable water) demand and PV microgeneration realisations
generated in step 8.8
8.10 Instantiate the stochastic programming MILP problem formulation (Ap-
pendix B.7), considering the remaining horizon (next slot – Sunday)
8.11 Solve the stochastic programming MILP instance of step 8.10
8.12 Retrieve the controllable appliance operation for the remainder of current day
8.13 end if
9. end for
end for

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 188
Likewise the CRH and SB-SCRH strategies, the residential load scheduling is
initially carried out at the beginning of each new day of the week (step 6 of Algo-
rithm 4). The residential load scheduling is carried out by solving an instance of a
stochastic programming MILP problem. This instance is strongly based on the formu-
lation presented in Section 5.2 for SB-SCRH-based scheduling, comprising appropriate
modiﬁcations to account for the extra loads of the controllable appliance. A reduced
scenario set with appropriate cardinality is also considered, based on the computation
time limitations. An in-depth discussion of the heuristics that guide the selection of
the fewest scenarios is performed in Section 5.5.2.
Throughout each day of the week, monitoring of the controllable and the non-
controllable loads, along with the microgeneration, is guaranteed by the HEMS. If, at
any slot, mid-day adjustments of the controllable appliance operation are needed, e.g.
in the sequel of a constraint violation due to extreme non-controllable demand, the
rescheduling procedure is triggered. In step 8 of Algorithm 4, two distinct instances of
the stochastic programming problems are solved, under updated scenarios of the non-
controllable demand and generation. In the ﬁrst instance (step 8.6 of Algorithm 4),
a fast HEMS reaction is necessary, since the constraint violation is only detected in
the same slot it occurs. As the infeasibility cannot be previously detected, this ﬁrst
rescheduling procedure must be carried out in a matter of a few seconds, in order to
quickly restore the feasibility of the imposed constraints. The choice of fewer scenarios
in the mid-day rescheduling is justiﬁed by the computation time requirements.
Once the identiﬁed feasibility issue has been resolved, i.e. after solving the instance
in step 8.6 of Algorithm 4, the controllable appliance operation in the remaining of
the horizon is further readjusted to exploit the updated non-controllable demand and
generation information. In this second rescheduling problem, the computation can
occupy the entire length of the current slot, i.e. one hour in this case. During this
slot, updated GP-based non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration realisations
aregenerated, andsubsequentlyreducedtothesamecardinalityconsideredinstep4of
Algorithm 4. Lastly, once the respective rescheduling optimisation problem instance is
solved (step 8.11 of Algorithm 4), the controllable appliance operation determined for
the remainder of the current day is retrieved and implemented at each slot, advancing
the HEMS monitoring to the next slot of the current day.
In steps 8.3 to 8.6 of Algorithm 4, the ﬁrst controllable appliance rescheduling
could have been solely determined for the current slot, rather than for the remainder
of the week. The additional beneﬁt previously discussed of pondering the appliance
operation over diﬀerent alternatives, before committing to a decision, justiﬁes the
choice for rescheduling over the remainder of the week. Moreover, the fact that, in
steps 8.3–8.6 of Algorithm 4, the controllable appliance operation is scheduled over
the entire horizon, could also put into question the need for the second rescheduling
problem. As demonstrated in Section 5.4, considering fewer scenarios results in more
signiﬁcant penalisations of the scheduling performance. Furthermore, in general, less
adaptable operation times are obtained from fewer scenarios, in particular when only
a single scenario is considered (Shapiro and Philpott, 2007). As such, after the initial
and fast appliance rescheduling, an overall more adaptable and robust allocation is
sought in steps 8.8–8.11 of Algorithm 4. To do so, updated non-controllable demand
andgenerationrealisationsareobtained, basedonrecentreadingsandalargerscenario
set, being the set of the same cardinality as that considered in the daily allocation of
the appliances (step 6 of Algorithm 4).
A complete formulation of the daily stochastic programming MILP problem in-
stances solved in step 6 of Algorithm 4 is presented in Appendix B.6. Furthermore, a

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 189
mathematical formulation of both instances of the rescheduling stochastic program-
ming MILP problems, solved in steps 8.6 and 8.11 of Algorithm 4, is presented in
Appendix B.7. It must be noted that this formulation constitutes an extension of
that in Appendix B.6. For the sake of simplicity of the implementation of the un-
derlying mathematical programming models, the mid-day rescheduling is performed
considering the same scheduling horizon, i.e. current day–Sunday. At any given slot,
for which rescheduling is required, the operation of the controllable appliances, imple-
mented prior to the current slot, is maintained. An additional parameterκ is deﬁned
in the instances of the MILP rescheduling problems, describing the current slot in
the ﬁrst day of the horizon, from which the operation of the controllable appliances
is scheduled. Additional constraints are imposed to specify the operation of the con-
trollable loads from the ﬁrst slot to slotκ− 1. The non-controllable demand and PV
microgeneration are also modelled separately for the ﬁrstκ slots, during which it is
known, and for all subsequent slots, where distinct scenarios are adopted.
The fact that, in both instances of the rescheduling MILP problems, the con-
trollable appliances are scheduled over diﬀerent horizons requires an appropriate
parametrisation of the formulations of the respective optimisation problems, described
in Appendix B.7. For illustrative purposes, consider that controllable appliances must
be rescheduled at sloti of the ﬁrst day of a given week, withi = 1,...,T . The ﬁrst
rescheduling problem (step 8.6 of Algorithm 4) is instantiated considering κ = i.
Moreover, the historical non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration are pro-
vided from the start of the horizon to slotκ. As for the remainder of the horizon, i.e.
from slotκ +1 toT, the previously generated non-controllable demand and microgen-
eration scenarios are considered. Finally, additional constraints covering the operation
of the controllable appliances from the beginning of the horizon to slotκ− 1 are also
imposed. On the other hand, the second rescheduling problem (step 8.11 of Algo-
rithm 4) is instantiated consideringκ =i, along with the historical non-controllable
demand and microgeneration, from the beginning of the horizon to slotκ, and the
newly generated non-controllable demand and microgeneration scenarios, covering the
period from slot κ + 1 to T. Additional constraints, covering controllable appliance
operation from the beginning of the horizon to slotκ, are also imposed.
5.5.2 Reduced Scenario Selection
The selection of the number of scenarios to be considered for each instance of the
stochastic programming MILP problem in Algorithm 4 is carried out according to
four heuristics. Before presenting the rationale of these heuristics, some considera-
tions regarding the box-and-whisker plots are performed. In Figures 5.19 to 5.21, the
quantilesLw =Q1− 1.5IQR andUw =Q3 + 1.5IQR are represented, respectively, in
the bottom and top whiskers. In these expressions,Q1 and Q3 denote, respectively,
the ﬁrst and third quartiles of the corresponding distribution of the computation time,
while IQR=Q3−Q1. Any data pointx /∈ [Lw,Uw] is often taken as an outlier, and
represented with a rhombus-shaped marker. It should also be pointed out that the
outlier detection literature is proliﬁc, see e.g. (Gil et al., 2019; Boukerche et al., 2020),
and therefore more appropriate and robust methodologies have been proposed. Nev-
ertheless, the IQR-based outlier detection is still popular in tasks where accurate and
robust outlier detection is not a requisite. The choice of the cardinality of the reduced
scenario sets is, therefore, supported on the following heuristics:
1. The scenarios with smaller computation time are privileged. In order to avoid
skewing in this criterion due to the presence of outliers, the computation time
is truncated at quantileUw, for each strategy and day of the week.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 190
2. For each problem instance, adequate computation time limits are imposed, af-
fecting the selection of the number of reduced scenarios. While a unitary time
slot limit is imposed for problem instances solved in steps 6 and 8.11 of Algo-
rithm 4, a faster allocation, in the order of a few seconds, is instead needed in
step 8.6 of Algorithm 4. The motivations for faster allocation in this case are
to minimise the duration of the scheduling infeasibility, and its impact on the
operation of the controllable appliances.
3. Scenario sets whose computation time has lower variance, represented by the
height of each box in the box-and-whisker plots, are privileged. In these plots,
the distributions of the computation time that cover a wider range of values
are represented by taller boxes. Intuitively, when considering a new problem
instance, higher uncertainty regarding its computation time is suggested for
scenario sets with taller box plots. Conversely, lower uncertainty regarding the
time needed to solve a given problem instance is suggested by shorter box plots.
4. Scenario sets with higher cardinality are privileged. The motivation for this
heuristic stems from (Shapiro and Philpott, 2007). In that work, it is argued
in favour of the increased adaptability of solutions of scenario-based stochastic
programming problems, when more scenarios are considered. Moreover, this ar-
gument is particularly highlighted when considering solutions determined based
on a single realisation of the uncertainties of the problem.
Taking into account these heuristics, all reduced scenario sets considered in Sec-
tion 5.4 are ordered based on their computation time, truncated at quantileUw. As
such, smaller computation time is registered for the unitary scenario set, followed by
the sets of 10, 5, 15 and, ﬁnally, 20 scenarios. According to the second heuristic, the
unitary scenario set is the only one complying with the lower computation time limit
imposed for the problem instances established in step 8.6 of Algorithm 4. As a result,
only a single uncertainty realisation is considered in the ﬁrst rescheduling problem.
On the other hand, in Section 5.4, the scenario sets of cardinality 1, 10, 5 and 15 were
shown to fulﬁl the unitary time slot limits imposed in steps 6 and 8.11 of Algorithm 4.
Within the four selected alternatives, the sets with 1, 10 and 15 scenarios stand out for
their lower computation time variance, across diﬀerent days of the week. As per the
ﬁnal heuristic, a higher number of scenarios is favoured to promote a more adaptable
operation of the controllable appliances. This rationale justiﬁes the choice of sets with
15 scenarios in problem instances considered in steps 6 and 8.11 of Algorithm 4.
5.5.3 Smart Home Appliance Scheduling
The extended SB-SCRH strategy outlined in Algorithm 4 is employed for the schedul-
ing of the controllable loads in the prototype smart home presented in Section 3.1.1,
over the course of the same 30 weeks considered in Sections 3.3, 5.3 and 5.4. For il-
lustrative purposes, it is assumed that the occurrence of unpredicted social gathering
events is described by a Uniform distribution. Moreover, in each one of the ﬁrst six
days of any given week, it is assumed that one social gathering event has a20% prob-
ability of occurring. Considering the fact that each unforeseen social gathering event
requires extra controllable load operations in two distinct days, its occurrence in the
last day of the week is not allowed. Indeed, if such event took place in the last day of
the week, one extra clothes washing and tumble dryer load would have to be scheduled
in the ﬁrst day of the following week, which is not covered in the scheduling horizon of
Algorithm 4. In practice, this could be considered by including additional parameters
in the optimisation problem instances deﬁned in step 6 of Algorithm 4, similarly to
the overnight controllable appliance operation. For the sake of simplicity, this is not

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 191
taken into account in this illustrative example. It is also assumed that the unforeseen
social gathering events are independent over distinct days of the week, and have a
minimum delay of one day between their occurrences. In the absence of information
about the preferences of the residents,α = 0.5 is assumed for all optimisation problem
instances considered in every week. It should also be noted that, the same previously
considered probabilistic constraint bounds are adopted, i.e.δC =δL =δU = 0.95.
For each day of every week, an initial set of 25 realisations of the non-controllable
(power and non-potable water) demand and PV microgeneration are generated via
Monte Carlo sampling (see Section 5.3). The scenario generation is supported by
the probabilistic modelling of the non-controllable demand and microgeneration, dis-
cussed in Chapter 4 and Table 3.1. The 25 realisations are subsequently pruned out
of their most redundant elements by the backward reduction heuristic described in
Algorithm 3, obtaining scenario sets of cardinality 1 and 15. All optimisation problem
instances are solved using SCIP solver v6.0.0 (Gleixner et al., 2018a,b), running on a
Ubuntu 16.04 machine equipped with 504 GB RAM, and a dual 2.2GHz Intel Xeon
Silver 4214 CPU with 16.5MB cache. As stated in Chapter 4, the deﬁnition of the
GP inference-based scenarios for the power generation and non-controllable demand
is performed on three 16GB RAM NVIDIA Quadro P500 GPUs.
In all 30 weeks, the operation of the controllable appliances is aligned with the
schedules reported in Section 5.4.1 for the same set of 15 scenarios. The clothes
washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher loads are predominantly scheduled for periods
of higher resident preferences. The operation of the clothes washing and tumble dryer
machines in the early afternoon is maintained, whereas the daily dishwasher loads are
performed in the ﬁrst slots of each day of the week, exploiting cheaper grid tariﬀs
to further reduce the weekly electricity bill. As for the electrical pump system, it is
predominantly operated during nighttime. Despite the high probabilistic constraint
bounds imposed, some violations of the non-potable water volume stored in the tank
and of the contracted power limit have been identiﬁed, in a few slots of some weeks.
This is due to the higher power and non-potable water demand, contributing to the
increase of the supply pressure on the grid and the tank. The sources for the observed
higher demand are twofold. First, the occurrence of more extreme non-controllable
power and non-potable water demand. Second, the existence of extra controllable
appliance loads, carried out in response to unforeseen social gathering events.
Over the course of the 30 weeks, the proposed extension for the SB-SCRH strategy
proved to be eﬀective in restoring the feasibility of the contracted power and tank vol-
ume requirements. In Figure 5.22, the application of the extended SB-SCRH strategy
is demonstrated in the week starting on 03/09/2018, and consideringα = 0.5. In
the top subplot of this ﬁgure, the controllable power demand is presented for each
appliance, over the entire week. Additionally, in the bottom subplot of this ﬁgure, the
overall household demand, including both controllable and non-controllable sources,
is presented for the same period. A red horizontal line is also included in the bottom
subplot, representing the contracted power limit. In this week, the usual clothes
washing and tumble dryer loads are operated in the ﬁrst day of the week. In the
second day of the week, a social gathering event is hosted by the family. The occur-
rence of this event is responsible for adjustments in the operation of the controllable
appliances, to accommodate the extra dishwasher, clothes washing and tumble dryer
loads. Thus, in the second day of the week, the usual daily dishwasher load is an-
ticipated to the ﬁrst slots of the day, allowing the respective extra dishwasher load
to be performed at the end of the second day. At the start of the third day of the
week, the supplementary clothes washing load is originally scheduled for slots 15 and
16 of the week, i.e. slots 63 and 64 of the horizon. Given that, during these slots,

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 192
Figure 5.22: Controllable power demand (top) and overall household
demand (bottom), under the extended SB-SCRH allocation, for the
week starting on 03/09/2018, forα = 0.5.
the non-controllable demand is greater than the corresponding generated scenarios,
maintaining the originally scheduled clothes washing and tumble dryer loads would
result in a violation of the contracted power limit. Thus, the operation of this load is
delayed, in order to avoid a violation of this limit (see Figure 5.23).
In this example, the robustness of the controllable appliance schedules is increased
through the monitoring of the household demand and rescheduling of the operation
of the appliances, proposed within Algorithm 4. As demonstrated in Figure 5.22, the
proposed extension to the SB-SCRH strategy was successful in avoiding constraint
violations, by rescheduling controllable loads when needed. It should be mentioned
that schedule feasibility cannot be guaranteed by Algorithm 4, for any possible non-
controllable demand or generation. Recalling the extra clothes washing and tumble
dryer loads discussed in the previous paragraph, if the contracted power limit had only
been surpassed when the tumble dryer load was already operating, e.g. in slot 67 or
68 of the horizon, the resulting constraint violation could not have been avoided. The
reason that prevents the recovery of the contracted power feasibility, in the case of the
previous example, is the adopted modelling of controllable appliances. In this work,
all controllable loads are modelled as uninterruptible loads, which means that the
on-going controllable appliance operation cannot be delayed, interrupted or stopped.
Returning to the previous example, the tumble dryer is the only controllable appliance
being operated from slots 66 to 69. In this case, if a contracted power violation is
identiﬁed during any of these slots, it cannot be prevented, as the operation of this
appliance cannot be stopped.
As ﬁnal considerations, reference can be made to alternative frameworks whose de-
velopment aims at sequential decision-making under uncertainty. Within such frame-
works, multistage stochastic programming strategies have been highlighted, in topics

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 193
Figure 5.23: Controllable power demand (top) and overall household
demand (bottom), under the extended SB-SCRH allocation, between
slots 60 and 72 of the week starting on 03/09/2018, forα = 0.5.
such as energy resource management, for many years (Pereira and Pinto, 1991; Kall-
rath et al., 2009; Watson and Woodruﬀ, 2011; Şahin et al., 2020). It should be noted
that the integration of multi-stage stochastic programming strategies within the pro-
totype smart home will be discussed in the next section.
5.5.4 Multistage Stochastic Programming-based Scheduling
In Section 2.3.3, stochastic programming is discussed, among other alternative tech-
niques, suchasrobustoptimisationandchance-constrainedprogramming, fordecision-
making in the presence of uncertain information. In stochastic programming, a prob-
abilistic modelling of the problem uncertainties is assumed, and the optimisation
procedure is carried out for the expected value of the objective function. Within
stochastic programming, two-stage formulations are very popular (Wang et al., 2018;
Mohammadi Rad and Barforoushi, 2020; Kim et al., 2021). Two-stage stochastic
programming problems assume that, at any given moment, some decisions are made
only based on the information available up to the current moment. These decisions
comprise the so-called ﬁrst-stage decisions. On the other hand, the second-stage de-
cisions are postponed until after a state observation has been made, at which point
the problem uncertainties are unveiled. In (2.4), a classical formulation of two-stage
linear stochastic programming problems is presented.
Naturally, the two-stage stochastic programming problems can be extended. In
particular, inmanyreal-worldproblems, uncertaintyisnotentirelyrevealedfromasin-
gle state observation, but rather from multiple observations, carried out sequentially
in time. In a multistage stochastic programming problem, the problem uncertainty is
assumed to be revealed gradually over time, in a total ofT periods. Consequently, the
problem decisions are also made iteratively, with decisions in each staget depending

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 194
only on the history up to staget, wheret = 1,...,T . The resulting decision-making
process has the following form (Shapiro et al., 2014):
decision (x1)→ observation(ϵ2)→ decision (x2)→
... → observation(ϵT)→ decision (xT)
where the decision vector at timet is denoted by xt, and the data vector at time
t is denoted byϵt, for t = 1,...,T . Concerning the data vectorϵt, it represents a
sequence of random variables, each following a given probability distribution.
Following the notation of Section 2.3.3, each uncertainty observation is deﬁned
as a realisation of the corresponding random variable vectors, and denoted by˜ϵt,
for t = 1,...,T . In (5.29), a formulation of the multi-stage stochastic programming
problem is presented (Shapiro et al., 2014).

x2∈χ2(x1,ϵ2)
f2 (x2,ϵ 2) + Eϵ3

··· + EϵT
[

fT (xT,ϵ T)
]]]
(5.29)
where fi denotes the objective function at each timei, withi = 1,...,T .
In (5.29), expectations Eϵi are taken with respect to the conditional distribution
of ϵi at stagei, i.e. Eϵi [·] = Eϵi [·|˜ϵi−1], fori = 2,...,T . Moreover, the expectations
Emathbfϵi are assumed well-deﬁned and ﬁnite for allx∈χ, and withi = 2,...,T . Fol-
lowing the Monte Carlo sampling-based approximation of the expectation, presented
in (2.2), and the SAA method described in Section 2.3.3, a scenario-based approxima-
tion of the multi-stage stochastic programming problem (5.29) is performed in (5.30).

Recalling the prototype smart home used in this work, three distinct sources of
uncertainty have been considered in this chapter, namely, the PV microgeneration
and non-controllable power and non-potable water demand. Moreover, a discreti-

resulted in the speciﬁcation of each uncertainty source at each slot of the horizon.

Replacing (5.31) in (5.30), and taking into account the high number of slots con-
sidered in this case study, a very high number of terms is deﬁned in (5.30). The
computational burden of the multistage stochastic programming problems is usually
very high, increasing exponentially with the number of problem stages (Şahin et al.,
2020). The Lagrangian relaxation of non-anticipativity constraints (Collado et al.,
2012), along with stochastic dual dynamic programming (SDDP) (Pereira and Pinto,
1991) have been proposed as exact solution methods for multistage stochastic pro-
gramming problems. Nevertheless, their application in this prototype case study is
not feasible, as their individual assumptions are not always veriﬁed. For example, in
the case of SDDP, stagewise independency is assumed, i.e.P (ϵj|ϵj−1) = P (ϵj), for
j = 1,...,T . Considering the GPR-based modelling of the non-controllable power
demand and PV microgeneration, this assumption cannot be veriﬁed. On the other
hand, the Lagrangian relaxation of the non-anticipativity constraints requires com-
plete recourse, i.e. individual recourse problems must always be feasible. Given that
the contracted power and tank volume constraints are aﬀected by the realisations
of the non-controllable demand, the feasibility of the underlying recourse problems
cannot be guaranteed for all possible realisations. As a result, the application of
both methods is not suitable for the prototype smart home herein addressed. Even if
these assumptions had been met, the high computational load associated with solving
the multistage stochastic programming problems would also render their integration
within the prototype case study to be infeasible in practice.
5.6 Synthesis
This chapter addressed the limitations, identiﬁed in Chapter 3, for the novel CRH
optimisation-based strategy, proposed for the week-ahead scheduling of smart home
appliances. As noted in Chapter 3, limited preventive measures of risk management
and contingency planning were provided in the original CRH proposal. This limited
adoption of preventive measures was due to the absence of the explicit modelling of
the individual problem uncertainties. In the CRH strategy, controllable appliances
are scheduled using single-point forecasts of the non-controllable demand and micro-
generation, the former in the form of the representative proﬁles.
Here, the mentioned limitations were addressed by integrating the probabilistic
modelling of the non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration within the orig-
inal CRH strategy. Therefore, a SB-SCRH strategy was proposed to address the
week-ahead smart home appliance scheduling problems. The main points that dis-
tinguish the SB-SCRH and CRH strategies are fourfold. First, the representative
demand proﬁles and PV microgeneration forecasts are replaced by lognormal and
Poisson random variables, and their respective realisations. Second, the controllable
appliances are scheduled with the goal of minimising the expected value of a weighted
sum of the normalised weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort criteria. Third, a
scenario-based approximation of the above mentioned expectation is performed, sup-
ported by random variables that describe the uncertainties of the problem, i.e. the
non-controllable demand and generation. Fourth, a probabilistic reformulation of the
contracted power and tank storage constraints is carried out, also motivated by the
probabilistic-based uncertainty modelling.
The SB-SCRH appliance allocation was carried out in the same prototype smart
home case study, and over the same set of 30 weeks, as considered in Chapter 3. A

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 196
total of 25 realisations of non-controllable demand and PV microgeneration were gen-
erated through a Monte Carlo sampling-based procedure. In each week, the SB-SCRH
appliance allocation was compared with the traditional RH and DA variants, consid-
ering ﬁve distinct weights for the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort
criteria. Similar controllable appliance operation was reported for the three strategies,
namely SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA, for each individual weight considered:
• For α = 0, the daily dishwasher load is predominantly operated from 2 am
to 4 am in both SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA. In all three strategies, the
weekly clothes washing load is scheduled from 3 pm to 5 pm of a given day, with
the corresponding tumble dryer load scheduled from 5 pm to 9 pm of the same
day. In the case of the SB-SDA strategy, the weekly clothes washing and tumble
dryer loads are mainly carried out during the ﬁrst day of each week. Concerning
the electrical pump system, a frequent operation throughout the entire day is
noted, characterised by high ﬂow rates promoted during short periods of time.
Higher operation frequency is reported for the electrical pump system when
fewer scenarios are considered. As for the battery system, in the SB-SCRH
strategy, a partial battery charge is promoted during the ﬁrst four days of the
week, after which its SOC drops to the initial value and is no longer used. On
the other hand, the SB-SRH strategy promotes and initial full charge of the
battery during the ﬁrst day of the week, followed by a progressive discharging,
while the SB-SDA strategy does not exploit the battery system.
• Forα∈{ 0.25; 0.5; 0.75}, the daily dishwasher load is predominantly operated
from 2 am to 4 am. In some days of some weeks, the daily dishwasher load
is anticipated by one or two slots, while the operation of the clothes washing
and the tumble dryer machines remains predominantly scheduled from 3 pm to
9 pm. In few of the weeks, the SB-SCRH strategy shifts the operation of these
appliances from 0 am to 6 am. The electrical pump system is predominantly
operated at nighttime, with high ﬂow rate although, in some days of some weeks,
the SB-SCRH and SB-SRH strategies also plan its operation during noon and
afternoon periods. Moreover, in all the three strategies, smaller ﬂow rates of the
pump system are observed for higherα weight values, in particular forα = 0.75.
Moreover, for the SB-SDA strategy, higherα weight values also promote a shift
in the operation of the pump towards the last day of the week. Finally, a
complete daily charging and discharging of the battery system is considered,
with the former carried out at nighttime and the later during the day.
• For α = 1, the operations of the clothes washing, tumble dryer, dishwasher
and the electrical pump system are scheduled for oﬀ-peak, nighttime, periods.
Very few exceptions are noted, in few days of some weeks, for the dishwasher
and pump system. In these cases, the two appliances are sporadically operated
during noon and afternoon periods, to exploit predicted PV microgeneration
surpluses for self-consumption. In the case of the SB-SRH, no clothes washing
and tumble dryer loads are scheduled for the portions of the second week covered
in each daily scheduling horizon while, for the SB-SDA strategy, the weekly load
of these two appliances is deferred to the last day of each week. Finally, a single
complete daily charging and discharging of the battery system is considered,
with the former carried out at nighttime, and the later throughout the day.
A superior performance of the SB-SCRH was observed, in relation to the respective
CRH counterpart, in terms of the weekly electricity bill. Regarding the resident
discomfort, no performance deterioration was reported. As a result, the SB-SCRH
was shown capable to further shrink the gap to the ideal allocation, according to the

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 197
weekly electricity bill. Despite being able to address uncertainty in domestic demand
and generation, a signiﬁcantly high computational burden was reported for the SB-
SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA strategies. In some of the days, the computation time
of the daily appliance allocation was recorded to grow up to 6 hours. This substantial
computation time has prevented the integration of these strategies in smart home
environments. This also motivated further research into the application of scenario
reduction heuristics, in order to relieve the observed computation cost.
Within the scope of this chapter, a backward reduction heuristic was considered
to remove the most redundant scenarios, generating reduced scenario sets of distinct
cardinalities, namely 1, 5, 10, 15 and 20. The SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA
allocations were repeated over the same 30 weeks and ﬁve weight values, to minimise
the same objective function. The controllable appliance operation obtained with the
scenario sets of smaller cardinality was shown to be similar to that obtained with the
original, higher cardinality, set of scenarios. These similarities were also veriﬁed in
the performance according to the weekly electricity bill and the resident discomfort
criteria. A marginally worse weekly electricity bill performance was registered when
fewer scenarios were considered. Despite a decrease in the electricity bill performance,
the CRH, RH and DA strategies were still clearly outperformed when fewer scenarios
were considered in the SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA strategies. With regard to
the computational burden, a substantial reduction was observed, in particular for sets
with 1 and 5 scenarios. Even when a higher number of scenarios was considered, the
underlying reduction in the computation time was still substantial. As an example,
a 40% reduction in the number of scenarios, from 25 to 15, enabled the computation
time of SB-SCRH and SB-SRH to drop from up to 6 hours to just 18 minutes. The
simulation results clearly showed that the high computational load of the stochastic
programming-based scheduling was addressed through scenario reduction.
Finally, two main shortcomings in the deployment of the SB-SCRH strategies
within smart home contexts were identiﬁed: (i) the lack of continuous monitoring of
the household demand and generation, and (ii) the absence of controllable appliance
rescheduling, in the case of violation of a constraint. A SB-SCRH extension was,
therefore, proposed to address these shortcomings. In this approach, at the start of
each new day of the week, the controllable appliances were scheduled over the current
horizon. Unlike in the original SB-SCRH proposal, fewer scenarios were considered for
the scheduling of the operation of the controllable appliances. This smaller cardinal-
ity scenario set was obtained by applying the presented scenario reduction heuristics.
The appliance operation planned for the ﬁrst day of the current horizon was, sub-
sequently, implemented. At each slot of the horizon, the household demand and
generation was monitored. If the violation of any imposed constraint was detected,
the operation of the controllable appliances, in the period from the current slot to
the end of the horizon, would be rescheduled. Further simulations were carried out to
validate the proposed extension for the SB-SCRH strategy. Unforeseen events, whose
occurrence required the scheduling of extra loads of the controllable appliances, were
also considered. All constraint violations were properly detected, and the subsequent
rescheduling was shown to be successful. The extra loads were also adequately sched-
uled without compromising the feasibility of their operation. Despite the successful
application of the proposed extended SB-SCRH strategy, it should be pointed out that
this strategy does not ensure the feasibility of controllable appliance operation for any
possible realisation of the non-controllable demand and generation. For example, due
to the herein considered uninterruptible modelling of the controllable appliances, if a
given contracted power violation could only be avoided by interrupting the operation
of one such appliance, then the schedule feasibility could not be restored.

Chapter 5. Stochastic Demand-Side Smart Home Load Management 198
The main results of this chapter are:
• Novel SB-SCRH strategy for week-ahead scheduling of smart home electrical
appliances. The proposed SB-SCRH strategy addresses the limitations of the
CRH-based allocation, identiﬁed in Chapter 3. The probabilistic modelling of
the non-controllable demand and generation, carried out in Chapter 4, is inte-
grated in a stochastic programming-based strategy. The daily allocation is per-
formed to minimise the expected value of the weighted sum of the normalised
weekly electricity bill and resident discomfort. An approximation to the value
of the objective function is computed using multiple realisations, or scenarios,
of the non-controllable demand and generation. The rescheduling of the con-
trollable appliances is carried out, considering the updated realisations of the
non-controllable demand and power generation, obtained at the start of each
new day of the horizon. The daily rescheduling also addresses model degrada-
tion and unexpected events.
• Comparison of the SB-SCRH strategy with the SB-SRH and SB-SDA variants,
followingthestateoftheartinsmarthomeappliancescheduling. Thescheduling
performance is assessed over a total of 30 weeks. The improvements of the
SB-SCRH, SB-SRH and SB-SDA over their CRH, RH and DA counterparts,
considered in Chapter 3, are shown. There is a smaller weekly electricity bill for
the SB-SCRH in all weeks, shrinking the gap to the ideal allocation according to
this criterion. As for resident discomfort, similar performance to that registered
in Chapter 3 for the proﬁle-based allocation is reported. The SB-SCRH strategy
also outperforms the typical allocation, according to both scheduling criteria.
• The improved performance of the SB-SCRH weekly electricity bill is due to
the probabilistic modelling of the non-controllable demand and generation, in-
tegrated within the proposed stochastic programming-based strategy. During
the appliance scheduling, multiple realisations of the demand and generation
are considered, privileging morewell-rounded operation, i.e. with better per-
formance in the presence of diﬀerent uncertainty realisations. Unlike what is
reported in Chapter 4 for the CRH strategy, no overestimation of the demand
or generation is deliberately performed.
• ScenarioreductiontechniquesweresuccessfullyemployedwithinthescopeofSB-
SCRH allocation, in order to relieve its high computation cost. In the simulation
results, there is a signiﬁcant reduction of the underlying computation time with
a marginal loss of the weekly electricity bill, without further penalisation of the
resident discomfort. For a larger set of uncertainty realisations, the daily SB-
SCRH appliance allocation took several hours. As a result, the application of
this strategy was not feasible in practice, as the computation time limitations
were not respected. The scenario reduction addressed this shortcoming, allowing
daily allocation in a few minutes, with marginal performance degradation.
• The limitations of the application of the SB-SCRH strategy in smart homes,
namely the lack of continuous monitoring of the environment and appliance
rescheduling upon constraint violation, were addressed by a proposal to extend
the SB-SCRH strategy. Within this extension, unforeseen events, whose occur-
rence required additional load scheduling, were also considered. Further simula-
tions validated the proposed SB-SCRH extension, with all constraint violations
being properly detected, and the subsequent appliance rescheduling carried out
successfully. Extra controllable loads were also scheduled without compromising
the feasibility of the operation of the domestic controllable appliances.

199
Chapter 6
Conclusion
CPSs are perfectly framed within the on-going digital revolution of the21st century.
This revolution is characterised by the proliferation of embedded electrical devices
within the existing physical environments. These devices present many beneﬁts to
its users, ranging from two-way wired and wireless communication capabilities, to
computational power, supporting increasingly complex and demanding computations,
and even intelligent and real-time decision-making.
The ubiquity of this new-generation of electrical devices makes CPSs a very broad
class of systems. Even though, in the early years after their initial proposal, these
systems were predominantly associated with industrial applications, recently, they
have been consistently discussed in alternative contexts. The energy sector, and more
precisely the management of the power grid infrastructure, is one notable example. In
this sector, the advent of the smart grid paradigm shift has called for a new generation
of electrical devices, required to support the continuous and adaptive communication
and supervisory control of the grid infrastructure.
Despite the recent breakthroughs, a number of challenges to the realisation of
a holistic smart grid still needs to be addressed. They range from (i) the need to
collect, store and process large volumes of data across the grid; (ii) the requirement of
making decisions in a short time limit, and in the presence of uncertain and incomplete
information; (iii) the need for a proper integration of electricity generated from RESs,
namely the need to address the high generation output variability, as well as other
challenges concerned with the distribution, placement and storage of the generators
and electrical power. Enframed in the context of the smart grid digital revolution,
this research work deals with the rationalisation of the use of the electrical energy in
residential buildings, via a week-ahead scheduling of the household appliances. To this
end, an adaptive optimisation-based framework for week-ahead appliance scheduling
was proposed. It relies on a CRH methodology, probabilistic modelling of demand
and generation records, and decision-making in the presence of uncertain information.
Competitive performance according to the weekly electricity bill and the resident
discomfort criteria, in comparison to state of the art strategies based on the standard
receding horizon and day-ahead scheduling, was shown over 30 separate weeks. Even
in the presence of more extreme and unpredicted demand, the successful allocation of
all controllable loads, and the satisfaction of all imposed constraints, were observed.
6.1 Reﬂection on Research Problem and Questions
This research is primarily focused on the problem of coordinating the demand-side
and supply-side grid entities, for the purposes of improving the rationalisation of
the electrical energy in new-generation smart homes. The focus of this research was
posteriorly formulated, in Section 1.2, as the general research question. Following
Section 2.2.2, the two predominant strategies to improve the rationalisation of the

Chapter 6. Conclusion 200
building electrical energy propose to either shift the operation of the controllable
appliancess over time, or carry out building interventions and acquisition of more
energy-eﬃcient devices. Following the previously identiﬁed literature trends, an ap-
proach based on consumption shifting principles was adopted, being its choice moti-
vated by the need to perform cheaper and less intrusive interventions. The general
research question was posteriorly segmented into four more detailed research ques-
tions. In the remainder of this section, each research question is revisited, and the
corresponding eﬀorts to address them are discussed.
Research Question 1: How should smart home electrical appliance operation
be managed, in order to promote energy rationalisation, while addressing operational
constraints and maintaining resident comfort within acceptable levels?
In this work, smart home electrical appliances are managed via mathematical
optimisation-based consumption shifting techniques. The appliance scheduling was
formulated as an instance of a non-preemptive machine scheduling problem. Alter-
native strategies, such as metaheuristics and machine learning methods, in particular
RL, could have also been considered. The main advantages of mathematical optimi-
sation, in comparison to these alternatives, include (i) smaller computational burden,
(ii) optimality and feasibility guarantees, and (iii) lack of dependency on specialised
hardware, such as GPUs. In Chapter 3, a purely deterministic strategy was adopted,
comprising the ﬁrst contribution of this research (see Section 1.3.1). Considering that
the household demand and generation, over any given horizon, cannot be determined
with perfect accuracy, a conservative approach was adopted, in the form of representa-
tive demand and generation proﬁles which deliberately overestimated the household
non-controllable demand demand. The proposed CRH strategy was validated for
week-ahead household appliance scheduling, and compared against the standard RH
and DA strategies. Modiﬁcations to this strategy were proposed in Chapter 5, includ-
ing probabilistic modelling of non-controllable demand and microgeneration.
Research Question 2:How to properly model the diﬀerent sources of uncertainty
related to the electrical energy needs of a smart home?
This work comprehends uncertainties in household non-controllable (power and
non-potable water) demand and microgeneration, modelling them by separate log-
normal random variables, for each slot of the scheduling horizon. The underlying
parametrisation of the probability distribution describing each random variable is ob-
tained by means of GP regression-based inference. For each data source, a GP-based
methodology was developed, comprehending the speciﬁcation of autoregressive models
with diﬀerent explanatory input variables and covariance functions, and the speciﬁ-
cation of evaluation metrics (see Section 4.2).
Research Question 3:How to integrate uncertainty sources within state of the
art energy rationalisation mechanisms?
UncertaintysourceswereintegratedwithintheproposedCRHapproachviastochas-
tic programming techniques, originating a SB-SCRH framework. In this framework,
the optimisation of the expected value of the objective function of interest (see Sec-
tion5.2.3)isminimised, relyingonrandomvariablestodescribethehouseholddemand
and microgeneration at each slot of the horizon. In comparison to other mathematical
optimisation techniques, such as robust optimisation, stochastic programming oﬀers
better performance in the presence of diﬀerent uncertainty realisations. Probabilistic
constraints, imposing a threshold on the probability of meeting the desired restriction,
are imposed for the contracted power (Section 5.2.4, constraint 5.11) and water tank

Chapter 6. Conclusion 201
bounds (Section 5.2.4, constraint 5.22). Computer simulations validated the proposed
SB-SCRH strategy for the week-ahead scheduling of smart home controllable appli-
ances. Competitive performance with that of the standard RH and DA variants was
registered, for a number of uncertainty sets of diﬀerent cardinalities. The application
of scenario reduction heuristics produced a signiﬁcant reduction in the underlying
computation time, while registering a negligible performance gap.
Research Question 4: How to manage diﬀerent electrical appliances, monitor
household demand and operational needs, and ensure a proper coordination with the
smart grid? Moreover, how can time-varying constraints and other unexpected events,
aﬀecting the management of smart home electrical appliances, be taken into consider-
ation?
A SB-SCRH-based approach was proposed for week-ahead scheduling of household
electrical appliances in smart homes, in the presence of uncertain non-controllable
demand and generation (see Section 5.1, Algorithm 2). Despite its contributions
in uncertainty-aware decision-making, and competitive performance showed over the
course of 30 distinct weeks, the proposed framework was shown limited capability to
detect violations of the operational constraints throughout the day, and to schedule
extra loads of the controllable appliances. This motivated additional modiﬁcations to
the SB-SCRH strategy, presented in Algorithm 4, to enable the continuous monitoring
of the unexpected events, household demand and microgeneration. If, at any given
slot, an unexpected event – requiring the scheduling of extra controllable loads or
changes in previously deﬁned operation needs – is detected, a rescheduling procedure
is triggered. The simulation results presented showed that the proposed modiﬁcations
enabled appliance rescheduling, and the detection of all constraint violations.
6.2 Future Work
In the sequel of this research, interesting possibilities for future research directions
have been identiﬁed. In the remainder of this section, a total of ﬁve main ideas are
presented and discussed.
The ﬁrst direction of future work is concerned with the improvement of the prob-
abilistic non-controllable demand and generation models, developed in Chapter 4. As
stated in Section 4.4, additional GP covariance functions, in particular those with
periodic components, should be pursued. As previously stated in the aforementioned
chapter, the interest in periodic functions is mainly motivated by recurring patterns,
expected to be observed in demand and generation records. In addition, alternative
data preparation and normalisation procedures must also be investigated, as they can
also promote improvements in the performance of the developed predictive models.
The second direction is motivated by the adopted set of controllable appliances.
A total of four controllable electrical appliances, namely a clothes washing, tumble
dryer, dishwasher and an electrical pump, were considered. Additional appliances,
such as HVAC, can also be contemplated, which not only contributes to the increase
of the portion of the household power demand subjected to HEMS management, but
also does not have a predeﬁned number of operation slots. Appliances such as the
HVAC operate to ensure a given comfort level, meaning that there is a greater free-
dom in the length and frequency of their operation. As such, a compromise between
the comfort level and the remaining criteria, e.g. electricity cost, can be achieved.
Overall, a proper incorporation of these appliances within the proposed scheduling

Chapter 6. Conclusion 202
framework requires information from three main sources. They are, (i) a mathe-
matical model of the appliance, e.g. the HVAC system; (ii) a model describing the
dynamics of the environment with respect to the physical quantities of interest, e.g.
indoor temperature, taking into consideration the operation of the appliance; (iii) ad-
ditional environment information, complementing the previously mentioned sources,
e.g. detailed information of the individual rooms where HVAC indoor units are in-
stalled, including the outdoor temperature, solar radiation, other electrical appliances
in the room, thermal insulation, room occupancy, just to name out a few. While
linear models describing the operation of HVAC systems have been reported in the
literature, see e.g. (Bianchini et al., 2019), information concerning the remaining two
sources is more diﬃcult to obtain. Moreover, for the prototype of the smart home
considered in this work, and the historical demand records, no such information could
be collected. Specialised software could also be considered, to simulate the operation
of HVAC devices within smart homes. Nevertheless, a proper integration with the
mathematical optimisation-based framework developed in this work is still required.
Following this last idea, the third direction of future work proposes to explore
alternative techniques for controllable load shifting. More precisely, the adoption of
RL should not be discarded, despite the advantages of mathematical optimisation
techniques discussed in Section 6.1. Indeed, the growing popularity of RL, not only
for smart home consumption shifting, but also for other machine scheduling problems,
is undeniable. In the upcoming years, further research in these techniques is expected,
which may contribute to make RL more competitive with mathematical optimisation.
The fourth direction of future work refers to the extension of the proposed frame-
work to problems of larger dimension, e.g. considering the management of a given
section of the grid. In this sense, a strategy similar to SB-SCRH could be investigated,
in order to coordinate the demand from multiple customers, with minimum costs to
each customer and the grid. Moreover, power generation from renewable sources could
also be considered, both locally at each household, and at a larger scale, complement-
ing the generation from fossil fuel-based sources, carried out at the grid-level.
Finally, theﬁfthandlastdirectionoffutureworkenvisionstoapplythisstrategyto
the scheduling of other machines, incorporated in environments other than residential
homes. Notwithstanding that the scheduling strategies covered in this dissertation
were mainly focused on the residential environments and electrical appliances, the
proposed adaptive supervision principles can be generalised to other environments and
applications, encompassedwithinthescopeofCPSs. Thisisthecase, e.g., ofindustrial
applications, where the adaptive supervisory techniques of CPSs are required to ensure
a proper operation of highly complex non-linear dynamic systems.

203
Appendix A
Demand-Side Load Management
The purpose of this Appendix is threefold. First, it presents a detailed treatment of
the mathematical expressions deﬁned for the MILP instances considered under the
CRH framework proposed in Chapter 3. This mathematical treatment includes the
derivation of lower and upper bounds for the household electricity bill (Section A.1)
and the resident discomfort (Section A.2) criteria. Second, a detailed formulation
of the MILP optimisation problem instances considered in the traditional RH-based
variant of the scheduling strategy is provided in Section A.3. This formulation is
considered in Section 3.3.2, as an alternative to the CRH-based week-ahead domestic
appliance scheduling. Finally, a detailed formulation of the MILP optimisation prob-
lem instances, considered in the DA-based scheduling strategy variant, is provided in
Section A.4. Likewise the standard RH counterpart, the DA-based appliance schedul-
ing is considered in Section 3.3.3 as an alternative to the CRH-based week-ahead
scheduling of the domestic appliances.
A.1 Household Electricity Bill Criterion Bounds
Prior to the derivation of the bounds for the household electricity bill, few consider-
ations concerning the domestic electricity balance are presented. As per (3.11), the
electricity balance is deﬁned, for each slot of the horizon, as the diﬀerence between
the household demand and PV microgeneration. According to (3.6), in order to derive
a linear expression for the electricity bill criterion, the domestic electricity balance at
each slot must be split into its positive and negative values, respectivelye+
i and e−
i .
From this variable splitting, additional constraints are speciﬁed in (3.10).

A.3 Traditional Receding Horizon Problem Formulation
This section presents a complete formulation of the instances of the MILP problems
consideredatthestartofeachnewdayofthehorizon, underthetraditionalRHvariant
of the CRH scheduling strategy, introduced in Chapter 3. The formulation herein
discussed follows that of Section 3.2, with few changes concerning the parameters of
the problem instances, the decision variables and the operation constraints.
A.3.1 Parameters
In the traditional RH variant, few parameter changes, in comparison to those deﬁned
in Section 3.2.1, are noted and presented in the listing that follows. All parameters
of the daily optimisation problem instances, deﬁned for the traditional RH strategy
variant, are summarised in Table A.1.
1. The parameter df, representing the last day of the horizon in the instances
of the CRH-based optimisation problems, is replaced by the parametersdﬁrst
f
and dsecond
f . For each new day of the horizon, the last day of the ﬁrst week is
represented bydﬁrst
f , whereas the last day of the portion of the second week,
covered in the current horizon, is represented bydsecond
f . Likewise for the CRH-
based scheduling strategy,dﬁrst
f = 7 for all week days, whereasdsecond
f =d0 + 6.
In the ﬁrst day of the horizon,dﬁrst
f = dsecond
f = 7 whereas, e.g. in the second
daydﬁrst
f = 7 and dsecond
f = 8.

Appendix A. Demand-Side Load Management 207
2. The length of each scheduling horizon remains represented by the parameterT,
expressed in number of slots. In traditional RH-based scheduling, this parame-
ters takes the constant valueT = 168, as equal-length horizons are considered
for each new day of the horizon. An additional parameterTﬁrstis introduced
to describe the number of slots, from the start of the horizon, belonging to the
ﬁrst week. As such,Tﬁrst=

by each horizon. This parameter is similar todﬁrst
w , which imposes this delay
from the start of the horizon. Likewise fordﬁrst
w , the delay imposed bydsecond
w is
also expressed in days, resulting indsecond
w = 3.

A.3.2 Variables
The optimisation problem considers the variables presented in the following listing.
1. Binary variable vectors w, t and d, of length T, describe, respectively, the
operation status of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines
at each slot of the horizon. The elements in positioni of each of these vectors
takethevalue1ifthecorrespondingapplianceisturnedonduringslot i(starting
at sloti− 1 and ﬁnishing at sloti, due to the performed discretisation of the
time domain), and 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
2. The vector q, of length T, describes the pump system ﬂow throughout the
horizon. The elementsqi, withi = 1,...,T describe the ﬂow rate of the pump
system at sloti of the horizon.
3. Binary vectors sw, st, sd, of lengthT, describe, respectively, the start of the
operation of a a clothes washing machine, tumble dryer or a dishwasher. The
elements in the positioni of each vector take the value 1 if a new load of the
corresponding appliance starts at sloti, and 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
4. Vector V, of lengthT, represents the volume of non-potable water stored in the
tank at the end of each slot of the horizon.
5. Vector SOC, of lengthT, describes the battery system SOC at the end of each
slot of the horizon.
6. Vector e, of lengthT, represents the domestic electricity balance at the end of
each slot of the horizon.
7. Vectors e+, e–, of lengthT, of non-negative variablese+
i ,e−
i ∈ R+
0 , whose ele-
ments model, respectively, the positive and the negative values of the electricity
balance at each sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon.
8. Vectors pbc and pbd, of length T, describe, respectively, the battery system
charging and discharging power throughout the horizon.

Appendix A. Demand-Side Load Management 209
9. Binary vectorbc, of lengthT, describe the battery system status at each slot of
the horizon. The element in positioni takes the value 1 if the battery system is
being charged during sloti of the horizon, and 0 otherwise, withi = 1,...,T .
10. Auxiliary real-valued variable vectors rbc and rbd, of length T, describe the
battery system charging and discharging ratio at each slot of the horizon. The
elements in positioni take the value 1 if the battery system is being charged or
discharged during sloti, and 0 otherwise, withi = 1,...,T .
11. Auxiliary vectorsrbcbc and rbdbc, of lengthT, model, respectively, the problem
variable productsrbc
i ·bc
i and rbd
i ·bc
i at sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon.
12. Auxiliary binary variables τsecond
w ,τ second
t ∈ {0, 1}, which take the value 1 if
a clothes washing and a tumble dryer load, respectively, are scheduled during
the portion of the second week covered in the current horizon, and 0 otherwise.
It is noted that, as the second week is only partially covered in the scheduling
horizons considered at each day, the operation of the clothes washing and tumble
dryer machines during this period is not mandatory.
13. Auxiliary binary variable vectors τ sw and τ st, of length T, are introduced
to model the productsswi·τsecond
w and sti·τsecond
t , respectively, for each slot
i = 1,...,T of the horizon.

MILP problems. For the sake of clarity and completeness, the entire formulation of
the optimisation problem is presented. As such, the objective function in (A.11) is
minimised by the traditional RH variants of the scheduling strategy.

A.4 Day-Ahead Allocation Problem Formulation
This section presents a complete formulation of the MILP problem instances consid-
ered for each new day of the horizon under the DA variant of the CRH scheduling
strategy, introduced in Chapter 3. The formulation herein discussed follows that of
Section 3.2, with few changes concerning the parameters of the problem, the decision
variables and the operation constraints.
A.4.1 Parameters
In DA allocation, the same set of parameters adopted in CRH-based allocation, and
described in Table 3.2, is considered. Naturally, as scheduling horizons of smaller
length are considered in DA-based scheduling, diﬀerences in the parameters of the
optimisation instances are still noted, and highlighted in the listing that follows.
1. The parameterdf, which describes the last day of the horizon, is assigned the
same value of parameterd0. As a single-day horizon is considered, the ﬁrst and
last days of the horizon are the same, i.e.d0 =df.
2. The parameterT, which describes the number of slots in the horizon, is assigned
a smaller value. While its value remains given byT = (df−d0 + 1)·h, the fact
that d0 =df results inT =h, for all days of the horizon.
3. The parametersτw andτt no longer describe the need to wash and dry a load of
clothes in the current horizon. Instead, they specify the availability to schedule
a given load of these appliances in the current horizon. While, in CRH-based
allocation, τw andτt require or prevent the scheduling of these two machines, in
the herein considered DA strategy, their allocation is not mandatory.
A.4.2 Variables
In the DA-based allocation, the same problem variables described in Section 3.2.2 for
the CRH-based allocation are adopted. Additional problem variables are considered in
the DA-based instances of the daily MILP optimisation problems, which are presented
in the listing that follows. These additional problem variables are deﬁned with the
goal of modelling the optional allocation of the clothes washing and tumble dryer
machines in the current horizon.
1. Auxiliary binary variableτopt
w ∈{ 0, 1}, which takes the value 1 if one clothes
washing and tumble dryer load is scheduled in the current horizon, and 0 oth-
erwise.

The DA-based allocation is carried out considering the same weekly electricity bill

MILP problems. For the sake of clarity and completeness, the entire formulation of
the optimisation problem is presented. As such, the objective function in (A.32) is
minimised by DA allocation.

Appendix B
Stochastic Demand-Side Load
Management
A total of seven outcomes are revisited in this Appendix. First, an analytical expres-
sion of the objective function of the instances of the optimisation problems considered
within the SB-SCRH strategy, proposed in Chapter 5, is derived in Section B.1. Sec-
ond, lower and upper bounds for the weekly electricity bill criterion considered in the
SB-SCRH-based appliance scheduling are derived in Section B.2. Third, a mathe-
matical treatment of the probabilistic contracted power and tank volume constraints,
imposedineachinstanceoftheoptimisationproblemsconsideredwithintheSB-SCRH
strategy, is presented in Section B.3. The forth contribution comprises a detailed for-
mulation of the instances of the stochastic programming MILP problems considered
within the SB-SRH variant of the SB-SCRH scheduling strategy, and is presented in
Section B.4. The ﬁfth contribution corresponds to a detailed formulation of the in-
stances of the stochastic programming MILP problems considered within the SB-SDA
variant of the SB-SCRH scheduling strategy, and is presented in Section B.5. Finally,
Sections B.6 and B.7 present a detailed formulation of the mathematical optimisation
problems considered under a variant of the SB-SCRH strategy, extended to address
the integration of this strategy in smart home environments. As such, a formulation
of stochastic programming MILPs instantiated at the start of each day of the horizon
is presented in Section B.6, whereas a mathematical formulation of the rescheduling
problems is presented in Section B.7.
B.1 Objective Function
In this section, the rationale carried out to derive an analytical expression for the ob-
jective function of the instances of the daily stochastic programming MILP problems,
considered in Chapter 5 for the novel SB-SCRH strategy, is covered. Following the
deﬁnition of the objective function in (5.2), a weighted sum of the expected value of
the electricity bill and the resident discomfort criteria is considered for each new opti-
misation problem instance. In (5.2), the expected value of the electricity bill is com-
puted with respect to the random variables modelling the uncertain non-controllable
household power demand and the microgeneration at each slot of the horizon.
Revisiting the weekly electricity bill criterion, deﬁned in (3.5) as a piecewise func-
tion, the ﬁrst branch of (3.5) is evaluated whenei≥ 0. Considering the domestic
electricity balance deﬁned in (3.11), the ﬁrst branch of (3.5) is evaluated when:

It is not possible for the two integrals in (B.7) and (B.8) to be analytically computed.
Thus, an expression for the expected value of the weekly electricity bill cannot be
derived. In light of these ﬁndings, this expected value needs to be approximated.
In the scope of this work, and considering the probabilistic modelling of the non-
controllablehouseholddemandandgeneration, thisapproximationiscarriedoutbased
on concrete realisations of these uncertainty sources.
B.2 Appliance Scheduling Criteria Bounds
In this section, the lower and upper bounds for the expected weekly household elec-
tricity bill are derived. The expected weekly electricity bill is taken as one of the two
optimisation criteria of the daily SB-SCRH stochastic MILP instances, considered in
Chapter 5. This section is strongly based on Appendix A.1, given the similarities
between the household weekly electricity bill criterion considered under both CRH
(Chapter 3) and SB-SCRH (Chapter 5) approaches. Concerning the resident discom-
fort, the second optimisation objective considered within the SB-SCRH strategy, the
lower and upper bounds have already been derived in Appendix A.2.

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 222

B.3 Probabilistic Constraints
In this section, deterministic equivalent models to the probabilistic constraints con-
cerning the household contracted power and the water tank bounds, imposed under
the SB-SCRH scheduling strategy discussed in Chapter 5, are derived.

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 225
B.3.1 Contracted Power Probabilistic Constraint
Facing uncertain non-controllable household demand, the household contracted power
limit is imposed via the probabilistic constraint (5.11). This constraint is deﬁned such
that, for each sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon, the household power demand does not
surpass the negotiated limit, with at least a probability ofδC. In (5.11), the non-
controllable household power demand is modelled, at each sloti = 1,...,T of the

B.3.2 Water Tank Lower Bound
Facing the uncertain non-controllable non-potable water demand, in the form of the
uncertain toilet ﬂush events, the limits on the volume of water stored in the tank
are imposed via the probabilistic constraint (5.22). This constraint is deﬁned such
that, for each sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon, the volume of non-potable water stored
in the tank remains within the established lower and upper bounds, with at least
probabilitiesδL andδU, respectively. In(5.21), thevolumeofnon-potablewaterstored
in the tank is modelled, at each sloti, considering Poisson-based random toilet ﬂush
events, i.e. Fi∼P o (λi), withλi retrieved from Table 3.1, fori = 1,...,T . Taking
into consideration the computation of the volume of non-potable water stored in the
tank, and the probabilistic modelling of the toilet ﬂush events, the constraint (5.22)
is replaced with (B.19).

B.4 Traditional Receding Horizon Problem Formulation
In this section, a complete formulation of the MILP problem instances, considered for
each new day of the horizon under the SB-SRH scheduling strategy, is presented. This
formulation follows that presented in Section 5.2 for the SB-SCRH strategy, with few
changes in the parameters of the problem, decision variables and constraints.
B.4.1 Parameters
Regarding the parameters of each instance of the optimisation problems, while the
overwhelming majority remains unchanged, few changes are noted and presented in
the listing that follows. Problem parameters are summarised in Table B.1.
1. The parameterdf, representing the last day of the horizon in the CRH-based
optimisation problem instances, is replaced by the parametersdﬁrst
f anddsecond
f .
For each new day of the horizon, the last day of the ﬁrst week is represented
by dﬁrst
f , whereas the last day of the portion of the second week, covered in
the current horizon, is denoted bydsecond
f . Likewise the CRH-based scheduling

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 228
strategy,dﬁrst
f = 7 for all days of the week, whereasdsecond
f =d0 + 6. In the ﬁrst
day of the horizon,dﬁrst
f = dsecond
f = 7, whereas, e.g. in the second day of the
horizon, dﬁrst
f = 7 and dsecond
f = 8.
2. The number of slots in the current horizon remains represented by the parame-
ter T, also expressed in number of slots. In the traditional RH-based strategy,
scheduling horizons of equal length are considered for each new day of the hori-
zon, resulting inT = 168 for all optimisation problem instances. An additional
parameter Tﬁrstis introduced to describe the number of slots, from the start of
the horizon, of the ﬁrst week. As such,Tﬁrst=
(
dﬁrst
f −d0 + 1
)
·h.
3. An additional parameterdsecond
w is considered to impose a delay between the
scheduling of the clothes washing machine in the two independent weeks con-
tained within the current horizon. This parameter is similar todﬁrst
w , imposing
a number of days, from the start of the second week, without the operation of
the clothes washing or tumble dryer machines. Likewisedﬁrst
w ,dsecond
w = 3 in the
daily instances of the optimisation problems.
