B.4.2 Variables
The optimisation problem considers the variables presented in the following listing:
1. Binary variable vectors w, t, d, of length T, which describe, respectively, the
operation of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines. The
elements wi,ti and di take the value of 1 if the corresponding appliance is ex-
ecuting at time sloti (starting at sloti− 1 and ﬁnishing at sloti, due to the
performed discretisation of the time domain), and a value 0 otherwise, with
i = 1,...,T .
2. Vector q, of lengthT, of elementsqi∈ R+
0 , withi = 1,...,T , which describe
the ﬂow rate of the pump system at each slot of the horizon.
3. Binary variable vectorssw, st, sd, of lengthT, which describe, respectively, the
start of a new clothes washing, tumble dryer or dishwasher load. Each vector

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 230
element swi,sti,sdi∈{ 0, 1} takes the value 1 if a new corresponding load starts
at sloti, and 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
4. Matrix ˆV, of sizeT×Ns, whose elementsVis∈ R model the volume of non-
potable water stored in the tank at the end of sloti, and for non-controllable
non-potable water demand scenarios, fori = 1,...,T and s = 1,...,N s.
5. Vector SOC, of lengthT, whose elements SOCi describe the battery system
SOC value at the end of each slot of the horizoni = 1,...,T .
6. Tensor ¯ e∈ RT×Ns×Ns, whose elementseijk represent the domestic electricity
balance, deﬁned as the diﬀerence between the demand and the generation, at
slot i of the horizon, for the non-controllable power demand scenarioj and
microgeneration scenariok, withi = 1,...,T , j = 1,...,N s and k = 1,...,N s.
7. Tensors ¯e+, ¯e–ofdimension T×Ns×Ns, ofnon-negativevariables. Theelements
e+
ijk describe the positive values of the electricity balance at sloti of the horizon,
for the non-controllable power demand scenarioj and microgeneration scenario
k, while the elementse−
ijk describe the negative values of the electricity balance,
at sloti of the horizon, for the non-controllable power demand scenarioj and
microgeneration scenariok, withi = 1,...,T , j = 1,...,N s and k = 1,...,N s.
8. Vectors pbc and pbd, of lengthT, whose elementspbc
i and pbd
i describe, respec-
tively, the battery system charging and discharging power at each sloti of the
horizon, fori = 1,...,T .
9. Binary vectorbc, of lengthT, whose elementsbc
i take the value 1 if the battery
system is being charged at sloti of the horizon, and 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
10. Auxiliary real-valued variable vectorsrbc and rbd, of lengthT, whose elements
rbc
i ,rbd
i represent, respectively, the battery system charging and discharging ratio
at each sloti of the horizon, withi = 1,...,T .
11. Auxiliaryvectors rbcbc and rbdbc, oflengthT, whoseelementsrbcbc
i,rbdbc
i model
products rbc
i ·bc
i and rbd
i ·bc
i, at each sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon.
12. Auxiliary binary variablesτsecond
w ,τ second
t ∈{ 0, 1} are deﬁned, taking the value 1
if a load of the washer and the dryer is scheduled during the second week portion
of the current horizon. It is noted that, as the second week is only partially
covered in each scheduling horizon, the scheduling of this load is optional.
13. Auxiliary binary variable vectors τsw and τst, of length T. The elements
τswi,τ sti, withi = 1,...,T , model the productswi·τsecond
w and ti·τsecond
t .
B.4.3 Objective
The traditional RH-based scheduling is carried out considering the same conﬂicting
criteria as in its SB-SCRH counterpart (Chapter 5). Thus, the daily MILP problem
instances minimise the weighted sum of the normalised expected electricity bill and
residentdiscomfortcriteria, deﬁnedin(B.22). Forthesakeofclarityandcompleteness,
the entire optimisation problem formulation is presented.

B.5 Day-Ahead Allocation Problem Formulation
In this section, a complete formulation of the MILP problem instances considered for
each new day of the horizon, under the SB-SDA scheduling strategy, is presented. The
formulation herein discussed follows that presented in Section 5.2, with few changes
in some parameters of the problem, decision variables and constraints.
B.5.1 Parameters
In the SB-SDA controllable appliance allocation, the same set of parameters adopted
in the novel SB-SCRH strategy proposal, and described in Table 5.1, is considered.
As smaller scheduling horizons are considered in the day-ahead horizon, diﬀerences in
the value of some parameters are noted, and highlighted in the listing that follows.
1. The parameterdf, describing the last day of the horizon, is herein assigned the
same value of parameterd0. As a single-day horizon is considered, the ﬁrst and
last days of the horizons correspond to the same day.
2. The paarameterT, describing the number of slots in the current horizon, is also
assigned a smaller value. While its value remains given byT = (df−d0 + 1)·h,
the fact thatd0 =df results inT =h, for all days of the horizon.
3. The parametersτw and τt no longer describe the need to wash and dry a load
of clothes in the current horizon. Instead, they specify the availability of the
scheduling of one load of the clothes washing or tumble dryer machines, in the
current horizon. While in the CRH strategy,τw and τt require or prevent the
scheduling of these two loads in the current horizon, in the SB-SDA strategy, it
is not mandatory to schedule these loads in the current horizon.
B.5.2 Variables
In the SB-SDA scheduling strategy, the same set of decision variables considered in
Section 5.2.2 for the novel SB-SCRH strategy are adopted. Additional variables are
also adopted, deﬁned with the goal of modelling the optional allocation of the clothes
washing and tumble dryer loads in the current horizon. These additional decision
variables are presented in the listing that follows.
1. Auxiliary binary variableτopt
w ∈{ 0, 1}, taking the value 1 if one clothes washing
and tumble dryer load is scheduled in the current horizon, and 0 otherwise.
2. Auxiliary vectors τ sw and τ st, of lengthT, whose binary variables model the
products swi·τopt
w and sti·τopt
w for each sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon.

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 236
B.5.3 Objective
The same two conﬂicting criteria, i.e. the normalised expected electricity bill and
the resident discomfort, adopted in SB-SCRH scheduling are again considered for the
day-ahead allocation variant herein presented. The daily stochastic programming-
based optimisation problem instances minimise the weighted sum of these two criteria,
expressed in (B.43). For the sake of clarity and completeness, the entire formulation
of each optimisation problem is presented.

B.6 Smart Home Scenario-Based Daily Problem Formu-
lation
In this section, a complete formulation of the MILP problem instances considered at
each new day of the horizon, under the proposed extension of the SB-SCRH strategy
for the smart home allocation, is presented. The formulation herein discussed is
considered within the scope of Section 5.5.1, being instantiated for each new day of
the horizon according to the step 5 of Algorithm 4.

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 241
B.6.1 Parameters
In the proposed extension of the SB-SCRH scheduling strategy, all SB-SCRH param-
eters deﬁned in Section 5.2.1 are adopted. In order to model the supplementary loads
of the washer, dryer and the dishwasher, performed in the sequel of the unpredicted
social gathering events, the following additional parameters are speciﬁed:
1. The parameters τextra
w ,τ extra
t ∈ {0, 1, 2} represent, respectively, the allocation
of the supplementary clothes washing and tumble dryer loads, carried out in
the sequel of unpredicted social gathering events. Upon the observation of
the unpredicted event, in step 2 of Algorithm 4, these parameters are set to
τextra
w = τextra
t = 2, in the optimisation problem instance of the current day.
This parametrisation is adopted in order to signal the supplementary allocation
of the clothes washing and tumble dryer loads in the second day of the horizon.
Subsequently, in the optimisation problem instance considered in the next day
of the horizon, both parameters are assignedτextra
w = τextra
t = 1. In this case,
the scheduling of the supplementary clothes washing and tumble dryer loads is
carried out during the ﬁrst day of the horizon. Finally, when the supplementary
scheduling of the washer and the dryer is not considered,τextra
w =τextra
t = 0.
2. The parameter τextra
d ∈{ 0, 1}, represents the allocation of the supplementary
dishwasher load, in the sequel of the unpredicted social gathering events. Upon
the observation of the unpredicted event, in step 2 of Algorithm 4, this param-
eter is set toτextra
d = 1, in the optimisation problem instance of the current
day. This parametrisation is adopted to signal the supplementary allocation
of the dishwasher loads in the ﬁrst day of the horizon. Conversely, when the
supplementary dishwasher allocation is not considered,τextra
d = 0.
3. The parameterssﬁrst
w,extra = 10 and sstart
d,extra = 22 describe, respectively, the ﬁrst
slot in any given day of the horizon from which the supplementary allocation of
the clothes washing and dishwasher machines can be carried out.
4. The parameterslast
d = 17restricts the usual allocation of the dishwasher machine
in the ﬁrst day of the horizon, provided an unpredicted social gathering event
has occurred. When the occurrence of such event is observed, this parameter
deﬁnes the last slot in the ﬁrst day of the horizon in which the usual operation
of the dishwasher machine can start.
B.6.2 Variables
In the proposed extension to the SB-SCRH scheduling strategy, the same decision
variables speciﬁed in Section 5.2.2 for the SB-SCRH strategy are adopted. As such, a
total of 11T decision variables are considered and detailed in the listing that follows,
with T denoting the horizon length parameter.
1. Binary variable vectors w, t, d, of length T, which describe, respectively, the
operationoftheclotheswashing, tumbledryerandthedishwasher. Theelements
wi,ti and di take the value of 1 if the corresponding appliance is executing at
time sloti (starting at sloti− 1 and ﬁnishing at sloti, due to the performed
discretisation of the time domain), and a value 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
2. Vector q, of lengthT, of elementsqi∈ R+
0 , which describe the ﬂow rate of the
electrical pump system at each sloti = 1,...,T of the horizon.
3. Binary variable vectorssw, st and sd, of lengthT, which deﬁne, respectively,
the slot of the horizon in which a new clothes washing, tumble dryer or dish-
washer load starts. Each elementswi,sti,sdi∈{ 0, 1} takes the value 1 if a new
corresponding load starts at sloti, and 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 242
4. Matrix ˆV, of sizeT×Ns, whose elementsVis∈ R model the volume of non-

toilet ﬂush scenarioFs, withi = 1,...,T and s = 1,...,N s.
5. Vector SOC, of lengthT, whose elements SOCi describe the battery system
SOC value at the end of each slot of the horizon, withi = 1,...,T .
6. Tensor ¯ e∈ RT×Ns×Ns, whose elementseijk represent the domestic electricity
balance at slot i, non-controllable power demand scenarioEhome
ij and micro-

B.6.3 Objective
The same two conﬂicting criteria, i.e. the normalised expected electricity bill and
the resident discomfort, adopted in SB-SCRH scheduling are again considered for
this variant. As such, the operation times of the controllable appliances times are
determined by minimising the objective function deﬁned in (B.66).

B.7 Smart Home Scenario-Based Rescheduling Problem
Formulation
This section presents a complete formulation of the MILP problem instances consid-
ered in the appliance rescheduling during any given day of the horizon, under the
proposed extension of the SB-SCRH strategy for smart home allocation. These MILP
optimisation problems are instantiated when, at any given slot of the horizon, the
established contracted power limit is violated or the volume of non-potable water
stored in the tank does not respect its minimum and maximum capacity bounds.
When the operational constraints are not respected, the feasibility of the scheduling
solutions is restored by rescheduling their operation in the remainder of the horizon.
The formulation herein presented is considered within the scope of Section 5.5.1, be-
ing instantiated in steps 8.5 and 8.10 of Algorithm 4. As stated in Section 5.5.1, the
formulations of the optimisation problems covered in this section constitute an exten-
sion of the formulation presented in Appendix B.6, which is instantiated at the start
of each new day of the horizon. For the sake of the simplicity of the implementation
of the mathematical programming models, the scheduling horizon is maintained in all
the appliance rescheduling carried out during the day, i.e. current day–Sunday.

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 250
In the ﬁrst rescheduling problem instance, i.e. step 8.5 of Algorithm 4, the control-
lable appliance operation implemented from the start of the horizon to the previous
slot is imposed as additional constraints of the optimisation problem. Concerning
the second rescheduling optimisation problem instance, i.e. step 8.10 of Algorithm 4,
additional constraints are included, specifying the planned controllable appliance op-
eration, from the start of the horizon to the current slot. Finally, the local PV
microgeneration and the non-controllable power and non-potable water demand are
modelled separately in the following two periods: (i) from the start of the horizon to
thecurrentslot; and(ii)fromthenextslottotheendofthehorizon. Intheﬁrstperiod,
the PV microgeneration and the non-controllable demand are represented by histor-
ical records. On the other hand, from the current horizon onwards, a scenario-based
modelling of the PV microgeneration and the non-controllable demand modelling is
adopted, following the original SB-SCRH strategy (see Section 5.2).
B.7.1 Parameters
Within the controllable appliance rescheduling, all the parameters deﬁned in Ap-
pendix B.6.1 are adopted. Few exceptions are noted, including the matrices repre-
senting the non-controllable power and the non-potable water demand scenarios, and
the PV microgeneration scenarios, i.e.ˆEhome, ˆF, and ˆPV. These matrices have been
renamed to ˆEhome,stoch, ˆFstoch, and ˆPV
stoch
, respectively. This is carried out to
clearly distinguish the parameters representing the historical demand and the gener-
ation records, from those representing scenarios that model their forecasts over the
remainder of the horizon. Finally, the length of the parameter vectorsΘ−1
Ehome, Θ−1
L ,
and Θ−1
U has also been reduced. In order to ensure that the appliance schedules imple-
mented prior to the start of the current slot is included in the determined scheduling
solutions, the following additional parameters are deﬁned:
1. Parameter κ, identifying the current slot in the ﬁrst day of the horizon.
2. Vectors Ehome,past, Fpast, and PVpast, of length κ. These vectors represent,
respectively, the historical recorded non-controllable power demand, the non-
controllable non-potable water demand, and the PV microgeneration, from slot
1 toκ, of the ﬁrst day of the horizon. Each vector elementEhome,past

B.7.2 Variables
Similarly to the parameters of the controllable appliance rescheduling problems, all
the decision variables deﬁned in Appendix B.6.2 for the daily appliance scheduling
are, once again, considered. Few exceptions are noted, concerning the matrix ˆV
and the tensors¯ e, ¯e+ and ¯e-. The matrix ˆV, originally representing the non-potable
water volume stored in the tank, has been renamed toˆVstoch. This matrix has size
(T− κ)×Ns, and contains, for each realisation of the non-controllable non-potable
water demand, the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank, from slotκ + 1to
slotT. One additional vectorVpast, of lengthκ, is deﬁned. It represents the volume
of non-potable water stored in the tank in the ﬁrstκ slots of the horizon. Regarding
the domestic electricity balance, separate problem variables are deﬁned to describe the
domestic electricity balance in the ﬁrstκ slots of the horizon, and in subsequent slots.
As such, the vectorsepast, epast,+ and epast,-, of length κ, are deﬁned to describe,
respectively, the domestic electricity balance, and its positive and negative values, in
the ﬁrstκ slots of the horizon. Finally, the tensors¯ estoch,¯ esotch,+ and ¯ estoch,- of size
(T− κ)×Ns are deﬁned, to describe, respectively, the domestic electricity balance,
and its positive and negative values from slotκ + 1 to slotT of the horizon, for each
scenario of the non-controllable power demand and PV microgeneration. For the sake
of clarity, all optimisation problem variables are presented in the listing that follows:
1. Vectors w, t, d of binary variables, of lengthT, describe, respectively, the op-
eration of the clothes washing, tumble dryer and dishwasher machines. The
elementswi,ti,di take the value of 1 if the corresponding appliance is executing
at slot i (starting at slot i− 1 and ﬁnishing at sloti, due to the performed
discretisation of the time domain), and a value 0 otherwise, fori = 1,...,T .
2. Vector q, of lengthT, whose elementsqi∈ R+

dishwasher machines, respectively.
4. Vector Vpast, of length κ, whose elementsV past

B.7.3 Objective
The same two conﬂicting criteria, i.e. the normalised expected weekly electricity bill
and the resident discomfort, adopted in the original SB-SCRH scheduling strategy, are
again considered. The resulting optimisation problem instances, whose solution yields
the controllable appliance operation times, minimise the objective function deﬁned
in (B.94). In comparison to the formulations presented in the previous sections, the
normalised expected weekly electricity bill criterion is computed separately for the
ﬁrstκ slots of the horizon, and all other slots. In the ﬁrstκ slots, it is computed
by adding the electricity bill in each slot, while in the remainder of the horizon, a
scenario-based approximation of the expected weekly electricity bill is computed. In
light of this change, in (B.94), this criterion is renamed fromEB∗
app to EB∗
alt.

11. The modelling of the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank is carried
out separately for the ﬁrstκ slots, and for the remaining slots of the horizon.
In both cases, the volume of non-potable water stored in the tank is modelled
according to (Ocampo-Martínez et al., 2013). At the end of each slot of the
horizon, it is computed by taking the diﬀerence between the tank inﬂows and
its outﬂows. In the ﬁrstκ slots of the horizon, the volume of non-potable water

Appendix B. Stochastic Demand-Side Load Management 259
stored in the tank is described by (3.23). Moreover, upper and lower bounds
for this volume are also imposed. On the other hand, from slotκ + 1 to T of
the horizon, the referred volume is described at the end of each slot, according
to (5.21). In this constraint, it is considered thatV0 =V past
κ .
The probabilistic modelling of the non-controllable toilet ﬂush events is adopted
fromslot κ+1toslot T ofthehorizon, following Fi∼P o (λi)fori = κ+1,...,T ,
with λi retrieved from Table 3.1. This probabilistic modelling motivated the
volume of non-potable water stored in the tank, at the end of each slot of the
horizon, to also be modelled separately for each non-controllable toilet ﬂush
scenario Fis, withi = κ + 1,...,T and s = 1,...,N s, as described in (5.21).
Likewise for the constraint on the household contracted power, the probabilistic
constraints are imposed in (B.118) to regulate the tank storage.
